=== sys-01 | C in C++: arrays, structs, pointers and printf
--- teach
In Computer Organization you saw memory as a long row of numbered bytes, and a CPU that loads and stores them. The C++ courses gave you vectors, strings and classes that hide those bytes behind a friendly face. This course takes the face off. You will write code the way C programmers do: fixed arrays, plain structs, raw pointers, and `printf`. It is the style of operating system kernels, device drivers, databases and flight software, and after this course you will be able to read and write that code.

### Why C, and why inside C++

**C** is the language C++ grew out of. It has almost no hidden machinery: every line turns into a few machine instructions you could predict, and every byte of memory is where you put it. That is why the [[Linux kernel|c-everywhere]], most embedded firmware, SQLite and the Python interpreter are written in it.

Almost every C program is also a C++ program. So in this course you write C++ that uses only the C part of the language: **C style**. You compile it as C++, which lets the checks call your functions, but you leave out everything C does not have.

| C++ gives you | C style uses instead |
|---|---|
| `std::vector`, `std::array` | a fixed array, or memory from `malloc` (later lessons) |
| `std::string` | an array of `char` ending in a zero byte (the strings lesson) |
| references `int&` | pointers `int*` |
| classes with methods | structs holding data, and functions that take a pointer to one |
| `std::cout << x` | `printf("%d", x)` |
| exceptions, `std::optional` | a return value that says whether it worked |

### Arrays: a row of boxes

An **array** is a fixed number of values of one type, stored side by side in memory with no gaps. The size is part of the type and never changes.

```cpp fragment
int burns[4] = {12, 30, 7, 45};   // four ints, one after another
burns[2] = 8;                     // index 2 is the third box
```

The boxes are numbered from 0, so `burns` has `burns[0]` to `burns[3]`. There is no `burns[4]`.

`sizeof` gives the size of a type or an object in bytes. On the machines in this course an `int` is 4 bytes, so `sizeof burns` is 16. Dividing the array's size by one element's size gives the number of elements:

```cpp fragment
int count = sizeof burns / sizeof burns[0];   // 16 / 4 = 4
```

**Why this is exact:** the elements have no gaps between them, so an array of n elements of type T takes exactly n × `sizeof(T)` bytes. Dividing by `sizeof(T)` gives back n. The same fact explains why indexing is fast: element i lives at the array's start address plus i × `sizeof(T)`, one multiplication and one addition whatever the size of the array. So reading `burns[i]` costs O(1).

Three things arrays cannot do, which surprise people coming from `std::vector`:

- **They do not check the index.** `burns[4]` compiles, and reads or writes whatever bytes happen to come next. This is **[[undefined behaviour|ub-first]]**.
- **They cannot be assigned or returned.** `a = b;` for two arrays does not compile. You copy them element by element.
- **They forget their size when passed to a function.** A function that takes an array really receives the address of its first element. Next lesson shows exactly why. For now, the rule is: always pass the length as a second argument.

```cpp fragment
int largest(const int* values, int n) {   // the array, and how many there are
    int best = values[0];
    for (int i = 1; i < n; i++) {
        if (values[i] > best) best = values[i];
    }
    return best;
}
```

Here `const int*` means "the address of ints this function only reads". Calling `largest(burns, 4)` passes the start of `burns`.

### Structs without classes

A **struct** groups named values into one object. In C style a struct holds only data: no methods, no private parts, no constructors.

```cpp fragment
struct Tank {
    int id;
    double litres;
};

Tank main_tank = {3, 812.5};                    // fields in order
Tank spare = {.id = 4, .litres = 40.0};         // named: a designated initialiser
Tank fleet[3] = {{1, 90.0}, {2, 75.5}, {3, 0.0}};
```

The second form names each field, which is a [[designated initialiser|designated]]. C++ accepts it only with the fields in the order they are declared.

Instead of methods, C writes functions that take a pointer to the struct. Following a pointer and then picking a field is so common that it has its own operator, the arrow: `t->litres` means exactly `(*t).litres`, "the `litres` field of the struct `t` points at".

```cpp fragment
void drain(Tank* t, double amount) {
    t->litres -= amount;
    if (t->litres < 0) t->litres = 0;
}
```

Why a pointer and not the struct itself? Passing a struct by value copies every byte of it, which costs time proportional to its size, and changes made inside the function change only the copy. A pointer is one address, whatever the struct's size, and it lets the function change the caller's struct. When the function only reads, write `const Tank*`, so both the compiler and the reader know nothing changes.

### Returning more than one thing

C has no references, no exceptions and no `std::optional`. The C habit is: **return a status, and write the results through pointers** the caller passes in. Pointers used this way are called **out-parameters**.

```cpp fragment
bool digit_value(char c, int* out) {
    if (c < '0' || c > '9') return false;   // not a digit: *out is left alone
    *out = c - '0';
    return true;
}
```

The caller writes `int d; if (digit_value('7', &d)) ...`. The promise "on failure, the outputs are left alone" is part of the function's contract, and callers rely on it. Say it in the comment, and keep it.

### printf: formatted output

`printf`, from the header `<cstdio>`, prints a **format string**. Each `%` in it starts a **conversion specification**: a placeholder that says how to print the next argument.

| specifier | argument type | example output |
|---|---|---|
| `%d` | `int` | `-42` |
| `%u`, `%x` | `unsigned int`, printed in decimal or hex | `42`, `2a` |
| `%lld` | `long long` | `9000000000` |
| `%zu` | `size_t`, the type `sizeof` gives | `16` |
| `%f`, `%e` | `double` | `3.141593`, `3.141593e+00` |
| `%c`, `%s` | a character; a text (a `const char*`) | `A`, `orbit` |
| `%p` | a pointer, as `void*` | `0x7ffd4a10` |
| `%%` | nothing: prints a `%` sign | `%` |

Between the `%` and the letter you can put a **width** (the smallest number of characters to use), a **precision** after a dot (for `%f`, the digits after the decimal point), and **flags**: `-` pads on the right instead of the left, `0` pads with zeros, `+` always shows the sign.

```cpp
#include <cstdio>

int main() {
    int orbit = 7;
    double apogee = 402.4567;
    printf("orbit %d\n", orbit);          // orbit 7
    printf("[%5d]\n", orbit);             // [    7]   width 5
    printf("[%-5d]\n", orbit);            // [7    ]   pad on the right
    printf("[%03d]\n", orbit);            // [007]     pad with zeros
    printf("%.1f km\n", apogee);          // 402.5 km  one digit after the point
    printf("%+.2f\n", -apogee);           // -402.46
    printf("%s is %d%% done\n", "burn", 80);   // burn is 80% done
    return 0;
}
```

**The types must match.** `printf` is a [[variadic function|variadic]]: it takes any number of arguments, and the arguments carry no labels saying what type they are. `printf` trusts the format string. If you write `%d` and pass a `double`, it reads the wrong bytes from the wrong place: undefined behaviour, and in practice garbage. The compiler warns about this when the format is a literal, so read your warnings.

### snprintf: printing into a buffer

Often you want the text in memory rather than on the screen: to send it, store it or check it. `snprintf(buf, size, format, ...)` formats exactly like `printf`, but into the `char` array `buf`, and it never writes more than `size` bytes.

A C text ends with a zero byte, written `'\0'`, which marks where it stops (the strings lesson is all about this). So `snprintf` writes at most `size - 1` characters and then the zero byte. Its return value is the length the **whole** text needs, not counting the zero byte, even if it did not all fit.

```cpp
#include <cstdio>

int main() {
    char line[8];
    int need = snprintf(line, sizeof line, "fuel %d%%", 100);
    printf("%s|%d\n", line, need);   // fuel 10|9
    return 0;
}
```

"fuel 100%" is 9 characters. The buffer holds 8 bytes, so 7 characters fit, plus the zero byte. The return value, 9, tells you it was cut short.

**Why the test `need >= size` detects exactly the cut-short case.** The whole text fits when its `need` characters and the zero byte fit in `size` bytes, that is when need + 1 ≤ size, or need < size. So it was cut short exactly when need ≥ size. (A negative return means an encoding error, which cannot happen with the plain formats in this course.)

With `size` equal to 0, `snprintf` writes nothing at all, and still returns the length. Programs use that to ask "how big a buffer do I need?".

**Cost:** `printf` and `snprintf` read the format once, left to right, and do a fixed amount of work per character they produce: O(length of the format + length of the output).

### Where it is used

- The interfaces between programs and the operating system are C functions with C structs: every language, Python and C++ included, calls them underneath.
- Flight software on spacecraft is mostly C, written under strict rules such as NASA JPL's [[Power of Ten|power-of-ten]]: fixed-size arrays, simple structs, every return value checked.
- `snprintf` builds log lines, file names and telemetry messages in systems with no `std::string`.

**Watch out:** `sizeof` on something passed into a function. Inside `int largest(const int* values, int n)`, `sizeof values` is the size of a pointer (8 bytes on a PC, 4 in the browser), not of the caller's array. People write `sizeof values / sizeof values[0]` in a function, get 2 or 1 or 0, and wonder why their loop stops early. The array's size is only known where the array itself is declared. Pass the length.

::: context c-everywhere Where C runs
Dennis Ritchie created C at Bell Labs around 1972 to rewrite the Unix operating system, which until then was written in assembly. Its descendants are everywhere: the Linux, Windows and macOS kernels are mostly C; so are Git, SQLite, the Python interpreter and the firmware in cars, satellites and washing machines. Bjarne Stroustrup started C++ in 1979 as "C with classes", which is why nearly every C program still compiles as C++.
:::

::: context ub-first Undefined behaviour
The C and C++ standards list some mistakes, such as reading past the end of an array, as undefined behaviour: the standard makes no promise at all about what happens. The program might crash, print nonsense, or work perfectly until the day it matters. The compiler is even allowed to assume these mistakes never happen and optimise on that assumption. A later lesson of this course is devoted to it. For now: an index outside the array is a bug even if the program seems fine.
:::

::: context designated Naming the fields
Designated initialisers came into C in 1999, and into C++ only in C++20. C lets you name the fields in any order and even skip around; C++ requires the order in which the struct declares them, and does not allow mixing named and unnamed fields. Any field you leave out is set to zero. They make big configuration structs much safer to fill in, because each value sits next to the name it belongs to.
:::

::: context variadic Functions that take any number of arguments
`printf` is declared as `int printf(const char* format, ...);`, where the three dots mean "any number of further arguments, of any type". The caller pushes the values; `printf` pulls them out one by one, and only the format string tells it what type to pull next. Small types are widened first: a `char` or `short` arrives as an `int`, and a `float` arrives as a `double`, which is why `%f` works for both. This is also why a wrong specifier cannot be caught at run time.
:::

::: context power-of-ten Ten rules for code that must not fail
In 2006 Gerard Holzmann of NASA's Jet Propulsion Laboratory published ten rules for safety-critical C. Among them: no recursion, every loop has a fixed upper bound, no memory allocation after start-up, check the return value of every function that has one, and compile with every warning turned on. Code for the Mars rovers was written under rules like these. Several lessons in this course return to them, because each rule answers a classic C bug.
:::
--- task
The starter declares a struct for one sensor reading:

```cpp fragment
struct Reading {
    int sensor;
    double value;
};
```

Write three functions. No `main`.

- `int count_above(const Reading* rs, int n, double limit)`: how many of the `n` readings in the array `rs` have a `value` strictly greater than `limit`.
- `bool value_range(const Reading* rs, int n, double* lo, double* hi)`: if `n` is 0, return `false` and leave `*lo` and `*hi` unchanged. Otherwise write the smallest `value` into `*lo`, the largest into `*hi`, and return `true`.
- `int format_reading(char* buf, size_t size, const Reading* r)`: write the reading into `buf` with `snprintf`, as the letter `S`, the sensor number padded with zeros to at least 2 digits, one space, and the value with its sign always shown and exactly 2 digits after the point. Return what `snprintf` returns. So sensor 7 with value 12.5 gives `S07 +12.50`, and sensor 123 with value −0.004 gives `S123 -0.00`.
--- starter
#include <cstdio>

struct Reading {
    int sensor;
    double value;
};

int count_above(const Reading* rs, int n, double limit) {
    return 0;
}

bool value_range(const Reading* rs, int n, double* lo, double* hi) {
    return false;
}

int format_reading(char* buf, size_t size, const Reading* r) {
    return 0;
}
--- solution
#include <cstdio>

struct Reading {
    int sensor;
    double value;
};

int count_above(const Reading* rs, int n, double limit) {
    int count = 0;
    for (int i = 0; i < n; i++) {
        if (rs[i].value > limit) count++;
    }
    return count;
}

bool value_range(const Reading* rs, int n, double* lo, double* hi) {
    if (n == 0) return false;
    double smallest = rs[0].value;
    double largest = rs[0].value;
    for (int i = 1; i < n; i++) {
        if (rs[i].value < smallest) smallest = rs[i].value;
        if (rs[i].value > largest) largest = rs[i].value;
    }
    *lo = smallest;
    *hi = largest;
    return true;
}

int format_reading(char* buf, size_t size, const Reading* r) {
    return snprintf(buf, size, "S%02d %+.2f", r->sensor, r->value);
}
--- hint
`rs[i].value` is the value of reading `i`: index the array, then pick the field with a dot. Inside `format_reading`, `r` is a pointer, so its fields are `r->sensor` and `r->value`.
--- hint
For `value_range`, handle `n == 0` first. Then start both the smallest and the largest at the first reading's value, compare every other reading, and write through `*lo` and `*hi` only at the end.
--- hint
The format for `format_reading` is `"S%02d %+.2f"`: `%02d` pads the sensor with zeros to width 2, and `%+.2f` shows the sign and two decimals.
--- check test | count_above counts strictly greater values
[] { Reading rs[5] = {{1, 3.0}, {2, 7.5}, {3, 7.0}, {4, 12.0}, {5, -1.0}}; return count_above(rs, 5, 7.0) == 2; }()
--- check test | count_above of no readings is 0
[] { Reading rs[1] = {{1, 99.0}}; return count_above(rs, 0, 0.0) == 0; }()
--- check test | count_above with negative limits and values
[] { Reading rs[4] = {{1, -5.0}, {2, -2.5}, {3, -2.5}, {4, 0.0}}; return count_above(rs, 4, -3.0) == 3 && count_above(rs, 4, 0.0) == 0; }()
--- check test | count_above looks only at the first n readings
[] { Reading rs[4] = {{1, 9.0}, {2, 9.0}, {3, 9.0}, {4, 9.0}}; return count_above(rs, 2, 1.0) == 2; }()
--- check test | value_range finds the smallest and largest
[] { Reading rs[5] = {{1, 3.5}, {2, -7.25}, {3, 10.0}, {4, 0.0}, {5, 9.99}}; double lo = 0, hi = 0; bool ok = value_range(rs, 5, &lo, &hi); return ok && lo == -7.25 && hi == 10.0; }()
--- check test | value_range with one reading
[] { Reading rs[1] = {{8, 4.5}}; double lo = 0, hi = 0; return value_range(rs, 1, &lo, &hi) && lo == 4.5 && hi == 4.5; }()
--- check test | value_range with all values negative
[] { Reading rs[3] = {{1, -3.0}, {2, -1.0}, {3, -2.0}}; double lo = 0, hi = 0; return value_range(rs, 3, &lo, &hi) && lo == -3.0 && hi == -1.0; }()
--- check test | value_range of no readings returns false and leaves both outputs alone
[] { Reading rs[1] = {{1, 5.0}}; double lo = 111.0, hi = 222.0; bool ok = value_range(rs, 0, &lo, &hi); return !ok && lo == 111.0 && hi == 222.0; }()
--- check test | format_reading pads and signs: S07 +12.50
[] { Reading r = {7, 12.5}; char buf[32]; int n = format_reading(buf, sizeof buf, &r); return std::string(buf) == "S07 +12.50" && n == 10; }()
--- check test | format_reading with a 3-digit sensor and a tiny negative value
[] { Reading r = {123, -0.004}; char buf[32]; format_reading(buf, sizeof buf, &r); return std::string(buf) == "S123 -0.00"; }()
--- check test | format_reading with sensor 0 and a large value
[] { Reading r = {0, 1234.567}; char buf[32]; format_reading(buf, sizeof buf, &r); return std::string(buf) == "S00 +1234.57"; }()
--- check test | format_reading into a small buffer: cut short, still ends in a zero byte, returns the full length
[] { Reading r = {7, 12.5}; char buf[6] = "#####"; int n = format_reading(buf, sizeof buf, &r); return std::string(buf) == "S07 +" && n == 10; }()

+++ practice | The mass that flies
--- task
A spacecraft's parts list is an array of this struct:

```cpp fragment
struct Part {
    int id;
    int mass_g;
    bool spare;
};
```

Spare parts stay on the ground. Write `int flight_mass(const Part* parts, int n)` that returns the total `mass_g` of the parts whose `spare` is `false`. With `n` equal to 0 it returns 0. No `main`.

For example, parts `{1, 500, false}`, `{2, 120, true}` and `{3, 80, false}` give `580`.
--- starter
struct Part {
    int id;
    int mass_g;
    bool spare;
};

int flight_mass(const Part* parts, int n) {
    return 0;
}
--- solution
struct Part {
    int id;
    int mass_g;
    bool spare;
};

int flight_mass(const Part* parts, int n) {
    int total = 0;
    for (int i = 0; i < n; i++) {
        if (!parts[i].spare) total += parts[i].mass_g;
    }
    return total;
}
--- hint
Loop `i` from 0 while `i < n`, and look at `parts[i].spare` and `parts[i].mass_g`.
--- hint
Add a part's mass to a running total only when `!parts[i].spare`.
--- check test | The example: 580
[] { Part p[3] = {{1, 500, false}, {2, 120, true}, {3, 80, false}}; return flight_mass(p, 3) == 580; }()
--- check test | No parts weigh nothing
[] { Part p[1] = {{1, 700, false}}; return flight_mass(p, 0) == 0; }()
--- check test | All spares weigh nothing
[] { Part p[2] = {{1, 10, true}, {2, 20, true}}; return flight_mass(p, 2) == 0; }()
--- check test | Only the first n parts count
[] { Part p[4] = {{1, 1, false}, {2, 2, false}, {3, 4, false}, {4, 8, false}}; return flight_mass(p, 3) == 7; }()

+++ practice | Mission clock
--- task
Write two functions. No `main`.

- `void split_time(int total, int* h, int* m, int* s)`: `total` is a number of seconds, 0 or more. Write the whole hours into `*h`, the minutes left over (0 to 59) into `*m`, and the seconds left over (0 to 59) into `*s`.
- `int format_clock(char* buf, size_t size, int total)`: use `split_time`, then write `T+` followed by the hours, minutes and seconds, each padded with zeros to at least 2 digits and separated by colons. Return what `snprintf` returns.

So `format_clock` of 3723 writes `T+01:02:03`, of 0 writes `T+00:00:00`, and of 360000 (100 hours) writes `T+100:00:00`.
--- starter
#include <cstdio>

void split_time(int total, int* h, int* m, int* s) {
}

int format_clock(char* buf, size_t size, int total) {
    return 0;
}
--- solution
#include <cstdio>

void split_time(int total, int* h, int* m, int* s) {
    *h = total / 3600;
    *m = total % 3600 / 60;
    *s = total % 60;
}

int format_clock(char* buf, size_t size, int total) {
    int h, m, s;
    split_time(total, &h, &m, &s);
    return snprintf(buf, size, "T+%02d:%02d:%02d", h, m, s);
}
--- hint
There are 3600 seconds in an hour. Integer division `total / 3600` gives the hours, and `%` gives what is left over.
--- hint
In `format_clock`, declare `int h, m, s;` and pass their addresses: `split_time(total, &h, &m, &s);`. Then one `snprintf` with `%02d` three times.
--- check test | split_time of 3723 is 1 h 2 min 3 s
[] { int h = -1, m = -1, s = -1; split_time(3723, &h, &m, &s); return h == 1 && m == 2 && s == 3; }()
--- check test | split_time of 0
[] { int h = -1, m = -1, s = -1; split_time(0, &h, &m, &s); return h == 0 && m == 0 && s == 0; }()
--- check test | split_time of 3599 and of 86400
[] { int h, m, s; split_time(3599, &h, &m, &s); bool a = h == 0 && m == 59 && s == 59; split_time(86400, &h, &m, &s); return a && h == 24 && m == 0 && s == 0; }()
--- check test | format_clock of 3723 is T+01:02:03
[] { char b[32]; int n = format_clock(b, sizeof b, 3723); return std::string(b) == "T+01:02:03" && n == 10; }()
--- check test | format_clock of 0 and of 59
[] { char b[32]; format_clock(b, sizeof b, 0); std::string a = b; format_clock(b, sizeof b, 59); return a == "T+00:00:00" && std::string(b) == "T+00:00:59"; }()
--- check test | 100 hours widen the hours field
[] { char b[32]; int n = format_clock(b, sizeof b, 360000); return std::string(b) == "T+100:00:00" && n == 11; }()

+++ practice | A parts table
--- task
Write a whole program, with `main`, that prints this table of pumps exactly:

```text
ID  NAME       TEMP  OK
 1  feed      +21.5  yes
 2  oxidiser  -40.2  no
12  purge      +3.0  yes
```

Store the pumps in an array of this struct, with the values shown, and print every row with **one** `printf` call inside a loop:

```cpp fragment
struct Pump {
    int id;
    const char* name;
    double temp;
    bool ok;
};
```

The columns: the id right-aligned in width 2, two spaces, the name left-aligned in width 8, one space, the temperature with its sign and 1 decimal right-aligned in width 6, two spaces, then `yes` or `no`. Print the header line as it is.
--- starter
#include <cstdio>

int main() {
    printf("ID  NAME       TEMP  OK\n");
    return 0;
}
--- solution
#include <cstdio>

struct Pump {
    int id;
    const char* name;
    double temp;
    bool ok;
};

int main() {
    Pump pumps[3] = {
        {1, "feed", 21.5, true},
        {2, "oxidiser", -40.2, false},
        {12, "purge", 3.0, true},
    };
    int count = sizeof pumps / sizeof pumps[0];
    printf("ID  NAME       TEMP  OK\n");
    for (int i = 0; i < count; i++) {
        printf("%2d  %-8s %+6.1f  %s\n", pumps[i].id, pumps[i].name, pumps[i].temp, pumps[i].ok ? "yes" : "no");
    }
    return 0;
}
--- hint
Widths go between the `%` and the letter: `%2d` right-aligns in 2 characters, and `%-8s` left-aligns a text in 8.
--- hint
The temperature is `%+6.1f`: always a sign, 1 decimal, at least 6 characters wide. For the last column, pass `pumps[i].ok ? "yes" : "no"` to a `%s`.
--- hint
The row format is `"%2d  %-8s %+6.1f  %s\n"`: count the spaces between the columns against the table.
--- check output | Prints the table exactly
ID  NAME       TEMP  OK
 1  feed      +21.5  yes
 2  oxidiser  -40.2  no
12  purge      +3.0  yes
--- check source | Prints the rows from a loop
\bfor\s*\(
--- check source | Uses printf widths, not hand-typed spaces
%-8s

+++ practice | Did it fit?
--- task
Write `bool format_label(char* buf, size_t size, int id, double volts)` that writes `id=` and the id, a space, `v=` and the voltage with exactly 3 decimals into `buf`, using `snprintf`. So id 42 and 3.3 volts give `id=42 v=3.300`. No `main`.

Return `true` only if the whole text fitted in `buf` together with its ending zero byte, and `false` if it was cut short. When `size` is 0, nothing may be written to `buf` at all, and the answer is `false`.

For example, `id=42 v=3.300` is 13 characters, so it fits in a buffer of 14 bytes but not in one of 13.
--- starter
#include <cstdio>

bool format_label(char* buf, size_t size, int id, double volts) {
    snprintf(buf, size, "id=%d v=%.3f", id, volts);
    return true;
}
--- solution
#include <cstdio>

bool format_label(char* buf, size_t size, int id, double volts) {
    int need = snprintf(buf, size, "id=%d v=%.3f", id, volts);
    return need >= 0 && (size_t)need < size;
}
--- hint
`snprintf` returns the length the whole text needs, without the zero byte, even when it did not fit.
--- hint
The text fits when that length plus 1 is at most `size`, that is when the length is strictly less than `size`. Compare as unsigned: `(size_t)need < size`.
--- check test | Fits in a big buffer
[] { char b[64]; bool ok = format_label(b, sizeof b, 42, 3.3); return ok && std::string(b) == "id=42 v=3.300"; }()
--- check test | Exactly 14 bytes fit 13 characters and the zero byte
[] { char b[14]; bool ok = format_label(b, 14, 42, 3.3); return ok && std::string(b) == "id=42 v=3.300"; }()
--- check test | 13 bytes: cut short, and still ends in a zero byte
[] { char b[13]; bool ok = format_label(b, 13, 42, 3.3); return !ok && std::string(b) == "id=42 v=3.30"; }()
--- check test | Size 0 writes nothing and returns false
[] { char b[4] = {'x', 'y', 'z', 0}; bool ok = format_label(b, 0, 7, 1.0); return !ok && std::string(b) == "xyz"; }()
--- check test | Negative values and longer ids
[] { char b[64]; bool ok = format_label(b, sizeof b, -12345, -0.0005); return ok && std::string(b) == "id=-12345 v=-0.001"; }()
--- check test | Size 1 holds only the zero byte
[] { char b[2] = {'q', 0}; bool ok = format_label(b, 1, 1, 1.0); return !ok && b[0] == 0; }()

+++ practice | Debug: the average of two
--- task
`mean` should return the average of the first `n` values in the array `values`, and 0.0 when `n` is 0. It returns the wrong answer for almost every array: `mean` of `{2.0, 4.0, 9.0}` with `n` 3 should be 5.0.

Find the bug and fix it. No `main`.
--- starter
double mean(const double values[], int n) {
    if (n == 0) return 0.0;
    int count = sizeof(values) / sizeof(values[0]);
    double total = 0.0;
    for (int i = 0; i < count; i++) {
        total += values[i];
    }
    return total / count;
}
--- solution
double mean(const double values[], int n) {
    if (n == 0) return 0.0;
    double total = 0.0;
    for (int i = 0; i < n; i++) {
        total += values[i];
    }
    return total / n;
}
--- hint
Inside a function, `values` is not an array at all, even though the parameter is written `const double values[]`. What is `sizeof(values)` there?
--- hint
The parameter is really a pointer, so `sizeof(values)` is the size of a pointer. The caller already tells you how many values there are: use `n` for the loop and for the division.
--- check test | mean of {2, 4, 9} is 5
[] { double v[3] = {2.0, 4.0, 9.0}; return mean(v, 3) == 5.0; }()
--- check test | mean of no values is 0
[] { double v[1] = {8.0}; return mean(v, 0) == 0.0; }()
--- check test | mean of one value
[] { double v[1] = {-3.5}; return mean(v, 1) == -3.5; }()
--- check test | Only the first n values count
[] { double v[6] = {1.0, 2.0, 3.0, 4.0, 100.0, 100.0}; return mean(v, 4) == 2.5; }()

+++ practice | The sensor with the widest swing
--- task
A telemetry log is an array of samples:

```cpp fragment
struct Sample {
    int sensor;
    double v;
};
```

Sensors are numbered 0 to 7, and they appear in any order. A sensor's **swing** is its largest value minus its smallest value; a sensor with one sample has swing 0.

Write two functions. No `main`.

- `int widest_sensor(const Sample* s, int n, double* swing)`: return the number of the sensor with the largest swing, and write that swing into `*swing`. On a tie, return the lowest sensor number. Only sensors that appear in the log count. If `n` is 0, return -1 and leave `*swing` unchanged.
- `int swing_report(char* buf, size_t size, const Sample* s, int n)`: write `sensor 3 swing 4.25` (the swing with 2 decimals), or `no data` when `n` is 0. Return what `snprintf` returns.

For example, samples `{2, 1.0}`, `{5, 3.0}`, `{2, 4.5}` and `{5, 2.0}` give sensor 2 with swing 3.5.
--- starter
#include <cstdio>

struct Sample {
    int sensor;
    double v;
};

int widest_sensor(const Sample* s, int n, double* swing) {
    return -1;
}

int swing_report(char* buf, size_t size, const Sample* s, int n) {
    return snprintf(buf, size, "no data");
}
--- solution
#include <cstdio>

struct Sample {
    int sensor;
    double v;
};

int widest_sensor(const Sample* s, int n, double* swing) {
    double lo[8];
    double hi[8];
    bool seen[8] = {};
    for (int i = 0; i < n; i++) {
        int k = s[i].sensor;
        if (!seen[k]) {
            seen[k] = true;
            lo[k] = s[i].v;
            hi[k] = s[i].v;
        } else {
            if (s[i].v < lo[k]) lo[k] = s[i].v;
            if (s[i].v > hi[k]) hi[k] = s[i].v;
        }
    }
    int best = -1;
    for (int k = 0; k < 8; k++) {
        if (seen[k] && (best < 0 || hi[k] - lo[k] > hi[best] - lo[best])) best = k;
    }
    if (best >= 0) *swing = hi[best] - lo[best];
    return best;
}

int swing_report(char* buf, size_t size, const Sample* s, int n) {
    double swing = 0.0;
    int k = widest_sensor(s, n, &swing);
    if (k < 0) return snprintf(buf, size, "no data");
    return snprintf(buf, size, "sensor %d swing %.2f", k, swing);
}
--- hint
Keep three arrays of 8, one slot per sensor: the smallest value, the largest value, and whether the sensor has been seen. `bool seen[8] = {};` starts every slot at `false`.
--- hint
The first sample of a sensor sets both its smallest and largest value. After the loop, walk sensors 0 to 7 in order and keep the first one whose swing is strictly larger than the best so far: strictly, so ties keep the lower number.
--- hint
`swing_report` calls `widest_sensor` with the address of a local `double`, then chooses between two `snprintf` formats.
--- check test | The example: sensor 2, swing 3.5
[] { Sample s[4] = {{2, 1.0}, {5, 3.0}, {2, 4.5}, {5, 2.0}}; double w = 0; int k = widest_sensor(s, 4, &w); return k == 2 && w == 3.5; }()
--- check test | An empty log returns -1 and leaves the swing alone
[] { Sample s[1] = {{3, 1.0}}; double w = 77.0; int k = widest_sensor(s, 0, &w); return k == -1 && w == 77.0; }()
--- check test | One sample: swing 0 for that sensor
[] { Sample s[1] = {{6, -2.0}}; double w = 5.0; int k = widest_sensor(s, 1, &w); return k == 6 && w == 0.0; }()
--- check test | Ties go to the lowest sensor number
[] { Sample s[4] = {{7, 0.0}, {7, 2.0}, {4, 10.0}, {4, 12.0}}; double w = 0; int k = widest_sensor(s, 4, &w); return k == 4 && w == 2.0; }()
--- check test | Only negative values
[] { Sample s[4] = {{0, -9.0}, {1, -1.0}, {0, -3.0}, {1, -1.5}}; double w = 0; int k = widest_sensor(s, 4, &w); return k == 0 && w == 6.0; }()
--- check test | swing_report of the example
[] { Sample s[4] = {{2, 1.0}, {5, 3.0}, {2, 4.5}, {5, 2.0}}; char b[64]; swing_report(b, sizeof b, s, 4); return std::string(b) == "sensor 2 swing 3.50"; }()
--- check test | swing_report of an empty log
[] { Sample s[1] = {{0, 0.0}}; char b[64]; int n = swing_report(b, sizeof b, s, 0); return std::string(b) == "no data" && n == 7; }()

+++ question | Why the count is exact
--- ask
For `double temps[7];`, why does `sizeof temps / sizeof temps[0]` give exactly 7?
--- choice
Every array stores its length in a hidden header, and `sizeof` reads it.
--- choice correct
The elements sit side by side with no gaps, so the array is exactly 7 × `sizeof(double)` bytes, and dividing by one element's size gives 7 back.
--- choice
Because a `double` is 8 bytes on every machine, and 56 / 8 is 7.
--- why
An array of n elements of type T takes exactly n × `sizeof(T)` bytes, whatever T is, so the division always gives n. It only works where the array itself is in scope: inside a function that received it, the name is a pointer.

+++ question | What snprintf returns
--- ask
What is `n` after these lines?

```cpp fragment
char b[6];
int n = snprintf(b, sizeof b, "T+%d", 12345);
```
--- answer
7
--- why
The whole text "T+12345" is 7 characters. `snprintf` writes only 5 of them, "T+123", plus the terminator into the 6-byte buffer, but it returns the length the whole text needs. Since 7 ≥ 6, the caller can tell it was cut short.

=== sys-02 | Pointer arithmetic: strides, ranges and one past the end
--- teach
Last lesson you passed arrays to functions as a pointer and a length, and I promised to explain why an array forgets its size on the way in. This lesson does that, and much more: it shows how C walks through memory by doing arithmetic on addresses. This is how `memcpy`, every C string function and every C++ iterator work underneath.

### Adding to a pointer

A pointer holds an address, and C lets you add a whole number to it. The rule that makes this useful: **adding 1 to a pointer moves it to the next element, not the next byte.** If `p` has type `T*`, then `p + k` is the address `k × sizeof(T)` bytes further on. The compiler does the multiplication for you, because it knows the type.

Picture a street where every house is 8 metres wide. "Three houses along" means 24 metres. The pointer's type is the house width.

```cpp
#include <cstdio>

int main() {
    double temps[3] = {20.5, 21.0, 19.75};
    double* p = temps;                 // points at temps[0]
    printf("%.2f %.2f\n", *p, *(p + 2));
    printf("%td elements apart\n", (p + 2) - p);
    printf("%td bytes apart\n", (char*)(p + 2) - (char*)p);
    return 0;
}
```

It prints `20.50 19.75`, then `2 elements apart`, then `16 bytes apart`. The last line casts both pointers to `char*` first. A `char` is 1 byte by definition, so on `char*` pointers the arithmetic counts bytes. Casting like this is how C code views any memory as raw bytes.

Moving backwards works too: `p - 1` is the element before, and `++p` and `--p` step one element either way.

### Indexing is pointer arithmetic

Here is the definition C uses for square brackets: **`a[i]` means exactly `*(a + i)`**, "move `i` elements from `a`, then follow". There is no separate indexing operation underneath. That is why indexing works on a pointer just as it does on an array, and why it cannot check bounds: it is only an addition and a load.

### Arrays decay to pointers

Why does `double* p = temps;` compile at all, when `temps` is an array? Because of a rule called **array-to-pointer decay**: when you use an array's name in almost any expression, it turns into a pointer to its first element. So `temps` becomes `&temps[0]`.

Only a few places keep the real array: `sizeof temps` (the whole array's size, 24 bytes), `&temps` (the address of the whole array), and initialising a `char` array from a text in quotes.

A function parameter is not one of those places. That is the answer to last lesson's puzzle: `void f(double values[])` is just another way to write `void f(double* values)`. The array decays when you call `f`, and only the address arrives. The size stays behind with the caller.

### The distance between two pointers

Subtracting two pointers into the same array gives the number of elements between them, not bytes. Its type is **[[ptrdiff_t|ptrdiff]]**, a signed whole number big enough for any distance in memory, printed with `%td`.

```cpp fragment
const int* first = counts;        // counts is an array of 10 ints
const int* seventh = counts + 6;
ptrdiff_t gap = seventh - first;  // 6
```

This is only defined when both pointers point into the **same** array (or just past its end, below). Subtracting pointers into two different arrays is undefined behaviour, because the language makes no promise about where different objects sit.

### One past the end

C makes one special promise: for an array of n elements, the address `a + n`, **one past the end**, is a valid pointer. You may compute it, compare with it and subtract with it. You may **never follow it**, because there is no element there.

That promise exists so that a range can be described by two pointers, a **begin** and an **end**, where end points one past the last element. This is a **half-open range**, written [begin, end): begin is included, end is not.

```cpp fragment
long long total = 0;
for (const int* q = counts; q != counts + 10; ++q) {
    total += *q;
}
```

Half-open ranges are the convention for good reasons, and it is worth knowing them:

- **The length is `end - begin`**, with no "+ 1" to forget.
- **The empty range is `begin == end`**, and the loop above runs zero times on it with no special case.
- **Splitting is clean.** Any `m` between them splits [begin, end) into [begin, m) and [m, end), which share no element and miss none.

**Why the loop is correct.** Take the invariant "before each test of `q != end`, `total` is the sum of the elements in [begin, q)".

- **At the start** `q` is `begin`, so [begin, q) is empty and `total` is 0. True.
- **Each pass** adds `*q` and then moves `q` on by one, so the range [begin, q) grows by exactly the element just added. Still true.
- **It stops**, because `end - q` is a whole number that drops by 1 every pass, starting from n ≥ 0. When `q == end`, the invariant says `total` is the sum of [begin, end): every element.

Notice that the loop only ever compares `q` with `end`, and follows `q` only when `q != end`. So it never follows the one-past-the-end pointer.

The C++ standard library copied this design: `v.begin()` and `v.end()` of a vector are exactly such a pair, and every algorithm in `<algorithm>` takes [[half-open ranges|dijkstra-zero]].

### Before the beginning is not allowed

The promise is only for one past the **end**. The address `a - 1`, before the first element, is undefined behaviour even to compute, whether or not you follow it. This bites loops that walk backwards:

```cpp fragment
// Wrong: when q reaches counts, --q makes counts - 1
for (const int* q = counts + 9; q >= counts; --q) { /* ... */ }

// Right: step back first, then use
for (const int* q = counts + 10; q != counts; ) {
    --q;
    /* use *q */
}
```

### Strides: walking a table

A **2D array** such as `int grid[3][4]` (3 rows of 4) is stored in **[[row-major order|row-major]]**: row 0's four elements, then row 1's, then row 2's, all in one block. So `grid[r][c]` is element number r × 4 + c of that block.

**Why:** before row r come r full rows of 4 elements each, which is r × 4 elements. Inside its row, the element sits c places along. Nothing else comes in between, since arrays have no gaps.

A row-major table is often passed around as a plain pointer to its first element plus the number of columns, and then the formula does the indexing. The number of elements you jump to reach the next item you want is the **stride**. Walking along a row has stride 1. Walking **down a column** has stride equal to the number of columns:

```cpp fragment
// print column 2 of a table with `rows` rows and 5 columns
const float* q = table + 2;
for (int r = 0; r < rows; r++) {
    printf("%.1f\n", *q);
    q += 5;                       // the stride: one whole row
}
```

Strides are everywhere in systems code. A sound card delivers stereo samples interleaved, left, right, left, right, so one channel has stride 2. An image in memory often has rows padded to a multiple of 64 bytes, so the stride between rows, called the **pitch**, is larger than the width.

### Copying bytes: memcpy

`memcpy(dst, src, n)`, from `<cstring>`, copies `n` **bytes** from `src` to `dst`. It takes `void*` pointers, which have no element type, so it cannot scale anything for you. To copy `k` ints you must say `k * sizeof(int)` bytes. The two areas must not overlap; `memmove` is the version that allows it.

### What it costs

Every pointer operation here, adding, subtracting, comparing, following, is O(1): a machine addition, subtraction or load. A walk over a range of n elements is Θ(n).

But not every O(1) access costs the same time. Memory reaches the CPU in [[cache lines|stride-cache]] of 64 bytes. A stride-1 walk over ints uses all 16 ints of each line it loads. A walk down a column of a wide table uses one element per line and throws the rest away, so it can be several times slower for the same number of elements.

### Where it is used

- `memcpy`, `memset`, `strlen` and the rest of the C library are loops over pointer ranges.
- C++ iterators are pointers made general: a vector's iterator is often just a pointer inside.
- Image processing, audio, and telemetry frames that interleave many channels are all stride arithmetic.
- Operating systems hand out buffers as a start address and a length, and drivers walk them this way.

**Watch out:** mixing up elements and bytes. `p + 4` on an `int*` moves 16 bytes, not 4. And the byte-counting functions go the other way: `memcpy(dst, src, 10)` copies 10 **bytes**, which is only two and a half ints. Whenever a number crosses between the two worlds, multiply or divide by `sizeof` on purpose.

::: context ptrdiff A type for distances
`ptrdiff_t` comes from the header `<cstddef>` and is signed, because a distance can be negative: `first - seventh` is -6. Its partner `size_t`, the type `sizeof` gives, is unsigned and holds sizes. On a 64-bit PC both are 64 bits wide. In the browser, where your code runs as 32-bit WebAssembly, both are 32 bits, and so is every pointer. Code that stores a pointer difference in an `int` works on both only while the distance fits.
:::

::: context dijkstra-zero Why counting starts at zero
In 1982 Edsger Dijkstra wrote a one-page note, "Why numbering should start at zero". He compared four ways to write the numbers 2 to 12 as a range and argued for 2 ≤ i < 13: the lower bound included, the upper bound excluded. The length is then the difference, adjacent ranges meet without overlap, and a range starting at the smallest natural number needs no bound below it. Start the numbering at 0 and the n elements are exactly 0 ≤ i < n. C, Python's slices and C++'s iterators all follow this.
:::

::: context row-major Rows first, or columns first
C, C++ and Python's NumPy (by default) store tables row by row: row-major. Fortran, MATLAB and many linear algebra libraries store them column by column: column-major. Neither is better; what matters is walking in the order the data is stored. A program that sums a big C table column by column does the same arithmetic as one that sums it row by row, but touches memory with a large stride, and can be several times slower. Mixing the two conventions when passing a table between languages silently transposes it.
:::

::: context stride-cache Why strides cost time
A cache loads memory in fixed blocks called lines, 64 bytes on most CPUs. The picture shows 16 ints per line. Walking with stride 1 uses every int in a line before moving to the next line. Walking with a stride of 16 ints or more uses one int per line, so every access may miss the cache and wait for main memory, which takes roughly 100 times longer than a cache hit.

```svg

  <text x="10" y="20" font-size="12" fill="#1f2a44">stride 1: every box of each line used</text>

  </g>
  <g stroke="#1f2a44" fill="#8fb8f0">
    <rect x="190" y="30" width="20" height="20"/><rect x="210" y="30" width="20" height="20"/><rect x="230" y="30" width="20" height="20"/><rect x="250" y="30" width="20" height="20"/>
    <rect x="270" y="30" width="20" height="20"/><rect x="290" y="30" width="20" height="20"/><rect x="310" y="30" width="20" height="20"/><rect x="330" y="30" width="20" height="20"/>

    <rect x="90" y="100" width="20" height="20"/><rect x="110" y="100" width="20" height="20"/><rect x="130" y="100" width="20" height="20"/><rect x="150" y="100" width="20" height="20"/>
  </g>
  <g stroke="#1f2a44" fill="#e6e9ef">
    <rect x="190" y="100" width="20" height="20"/><rect x="210" y="100" width="20" height="20"/><rect x="230" y="100" width="20" height="20"/><rect x="250" y="100" width="20" height="20"/>
    <rect x="270" y="100" width="20" height="20"/><rect x="290" y="100" width="20" height="20"/><rect x="310" y="100" width="20" height="20"/><rect x="330" y="100" width="20" height="20"/>

  <text x="180" y="143" font-size="11" fill="#6c7a93" text-anchor="middle">each group of 8 boxes stands for one cache line</text>
```
