[] { int e[1] = {0}; return peak_stack(e, 0) == 0; }()
--- check test | A return with nothing active: -1
[] { int e[3] = {8, 0, 0}; int f[1] = {0}; return peak_stack(e, 3) == -1 && peak_stack(f, 1) == -1; }()
--- check test | Calls still active at the end are fine
[] { int e[3] = {10, 20, 30}; return peak_stack(e, 3) == 60; }()
--- check test | The peak is not the end
[] { int e[8] = {100, 50, 0, 0, 20, 20, 20, 0}; return peak_stack(e, 8) == 150; }()
--- check test | Deep recursion, 120 frames of 48 bytes, then all return
[] { int e[256]; int n = 0; for (int i = 0; i < 120; i++) e[n++] = 48; for (int i = 0; i < 120; i++) e[n++] = 0; return peak_stack(e, n) == 5760; }()

+++ practice | Handles that know when they dangle
--- task
A raw pointer cannot tell you that its object is gone. Game engines and flight software often hand out **handles** instead: a slot number plus a **generation**, a counter that goes up every time the slot is freed. A handle whose generation no longer matches its slot is stale, and can be detected. The starter declares:

```cpp fragment
struct Pool {
    bool used[8];
    int gen[8];
};

struct Handle {
    int slot;
    int gen;
};
```

Write four functions. No `main`.

- `void pool_init(Pool* p)`: every slot free, every generation 0.
- `Handle pool_acquire(Pool* p)`: take the **lowest-numbered** free slot, mark it used, and return `{slot, its current generation}`. If every slot is used, return `{-1, 0}`.
- `bool pool_valid(const Pool* p, Handle h)`: `true` only if `h.slot` is from 0 to 7, that slot is in use, and its generation equals `h.gen`.
- `void pool_release(Pool* p, Handle h)`: if `h` is valid, free its slot and add 1 to the slot's generation. If `h` is not valid, do nothing at all, so releasing the same handle twice is harmless.

So after acquiring handle A in slot 0, releasing it, and acquiring handle B (slot 0 again, generation 1), A is no longer valid but B is.
--- starter
struct Pool {
    bool used[8];
    int gen[8];
};

struct Handle {
    int slot;
    int gen;
};

void pool_init(Pool* p) {
}

Handle pool_acquire(Pool* p) {
    Handle h = {-1, 0};
    return h;
}

bool pool_valid(const Pool* p, Handle h) {
    return true;
}

void pool_release(Pool* p, Handle h) {
}
--- solution
struct Pool {
    bool used[8];
    int gen[8];
};

struct Handle {
    int slot;
    int gen;
};

void pool_init(Pool* p) {
    for (int i = 0; i < 8; i++) {
        p->used[i] = false;
        p->gen[i] = 0;
    }
}

Handle pool_acquire(Pool* p) {
    for (int i = 0; i < 8; i++) {
        if (!p->used[i]) {
            p->used[i] = true;
            Handle h = {i, p->gen[i]};
            return h;
        }
    }
    Handle none = {-1, 0};
    return none;
}

bool pool_valid(const Pool* p, Handle h) {
    return h.slot >= 0 && h.slot < 8 && p->used[h.slot] && p->gen[h.slot] == h.gen;
}

void pool_release(Pool* p, Handle h) {
    if (!pool_valid(p, h)) return;
    p->used[h.slot] = false;
    p->gen[h.slot]++;
}
--- hint
`pool_acquire` scans the slots from 0 upwards and takes the first one that is not used.
--- hint
In `pool_valid`, check the slot's range **first**, before you use it as an index: `&&` stops at the first false part, so the array is never read out of range.
--- hint
`pool_release` starts with `if (!pool_valid(p, h)) return;`. Then the generation bump is what makes every older handle to that slot stale.
--- check test | Acquire takes the lowest free slots, generation 0
[] { Pool p; pool_init(&p); Handle a = pool_acquire(&p); Handle b = pool_acquire(&p); return a.slot == 0 && a.gen == 0 && b.slot == 1 && pool_valid(&p, a) && pool_valid(&p, b); }()
--- check test | A released handle is stale, and the reused slot gets a new generation
[] { Pool p; pool_init(&p); Handle a = pool_acquire(&p); pool_release(&p, a); Handle b = pool_acquire(&p); return b.slot == 0 && b.gen == 1 && !pool_valid(&p, a) && pool_valid(&p, b); }()
--- check test | Releasing twice is harmless
[] { Pool p; pool_init(&p); Handle a = pool_acquire(&p); pool_release(&p, a); pool_release(&p, a); Handle b = pool_acquire(&p); return b.gen == 1 && pool_valid(&p, b); }()
--- check test | A stale handle cannot release the slot's new owner
[] { Pool p; pool_init(&p); Handle a = pool_acquire(&p); pool_release(&p, a); Handle b = pool_acquire(&p); pool_release(&p, a); return pool_valid(&p, b); }()
--- check test | A full pool returns slot -1
[] { Pool p; pool_init(&p); for (int i = 0; i < 8; i++) pool_acquire(&p); Handle h = pool_acquire(&p); return h.slot == -1 && !pool_valid(&p, h); }()
--- check test | Out-of-range slots are never valid
[] { Pool p; pool_init(&p); pool_acquire(&p); Handle x = {8, 0}; Handle y = {-3, 0}; Handle z = {100000, 0}; pool_release(&p, x); return !pool_valid(&p, x) && !pool_valid(&p, y) && !pool_valid(&p, z); }()
--- check test | A never-acquired slot is not valid
[] { Pool p; pool_init(&p); Handle h = {5, 0}; return !pool_valid(&p, h); }()
--- check test | Freeing a middle slot: it is the next one taken
[] { Pool p; pool_init(&p); Handle h[4]; for (int i = 0; i < 4; i++) h[i] = pool_acquire(&p); pool_release(&p, h[2]); Handle n = pool_acquire(&p); return n.slot == 2 && n.gen == 1; }()

+++ practice | Debug: both temperatures the same
--- task
`report(out, size, before, after)` should write a line like `21.5C -> 23.0C` into `out`, showing a temperature before and after a heater runs, each with one decimal and a `C`. Instead it writes the same temperature twice, such as `23.0C -> 23.0C`.

Find the bug and fix it, so that `report` gives the right line. You may change `format_temp` as well as `report`. No `main`.
--- starter
#include <cstdio>

const char* format_temp(double c) {
    static char buf[16];
    snprintf(buf, sizeof buf, "%.1fC", c);
    return buf;
}

void report(char* out, size_t size, double before, double after) {
    snprintf(out, size, "%s -> %s", format_temp(before), format_temp(after));
}
--- solution
#include <cstdio>

void format_temp(double c, char* buf, size_t size) {
    snprintf(buf, size, "%.1fC", c);
}

void report(char* out, size_t size, double before, double after) {
    char a[16];
    char b[16];
    format_temp(before, a, sizeof a);
    format_temp(after, b, sizeof b);
    snprintf(out, size, "%s -> %s", a, b);
}
--- hint
How many buffers does `format_temp` have, and how many results does `report` need alive at the same moment?
--- hint
Both calls return the same static buffer, so both `%s` arguments are one pointer, holding whichever text was written last. Give `format_temp` a buffer from its caller, and let `report` pass two different local buffers.
--- check test | 21.5 and 23 give 21.5C -> 23.0C
[] { char o[64]; report(o, sizeof o, 21.5, 23.0); return std::string(o) == "21.5C -> 23.0C"; }()
--- check test | Negative and rounded values
[] { char o[64]; report(o, sizeof o, -4.25, 100.04); return std::string(o) == "-4.2C -> 100.0C" || std::string(o) == "-4.3C -> 100.0C"; }()
--- check test | Two reports in a row
[] { char o[64], p[64]; report(o, sizeof o, 1.0, 2.0); report(p, sizeof p, 3.0, 4.0); return std::string(o) == "1.0C -> 2.0C" && std::string(p) == "3.0C -> 4.0C"; }()
--- check test | A small output buffer is cut short, not overflowed
[] { char o[8]; for (int i = 0; i < 8; i++) o[i] = '#'; report(o, 6, 10.0, 20.0); return std::string(o) == "10.0C" && o[6] == '#'; }()

+++ practice | The worst-case stack of a flight program
--- task
Flight software rules forbid recursion so that the deepest possible stack can be computed before launch. Compute it.

A program has `n` functions, numbered 0 to `n - 1` (at most 64). Function `i` has a stack frame of `frame[i]` bytes. There are `m` call edges: function `from[j]` may call function `to[j]`. The stack use of a chain of calls is the sum of the frame sizes of every function in the chain.

Write `int worst_stack(int n, const int* frame, const int* from, const int* to, int m, int root)` that returns the largest stack use of any chain of calls that starts at `root` (whose frame always counts). If some function reachable from `root` can call itself, directly or through other functions, there is no bound: return -1. Cycles among functions that `root` never reaches do not matter. No `main`.

For example, with frames `main` 64, `read` 32, `filter` 48, `log` 16, and calls main→read, main→filter, read→log, filter→log, the answer is 64 + 48 + 16 = 128.
--- starter
int worst_stack(int n, const int* frame, const int* from, const int* to, int m, int root) {
    int total = 0;
    for (int i = 0; i < n; i++) total += frame[i];
    return total;
}
--- solution
int depth_from(int f, int n, const int* frame, const int* from, const int* to, int m, int* state, int* best) {
    if (state[f] == 2) return best[f];
    if (state[f] == 1) return -1;
    state[f] = 1;
    int deepest = 0;
    for (int j = 0; j < m; j++) {
        if (from[j] != f) continue;
        int d = depth_from(to[j], n, frame, from, to, m, state, best);
        if (d < 0) return -1;
        if (d > deepest) deepest = d;
    }
    state[f] = 2;
    best[f] = frame[f] + deepest;
    return best[f];
}

int worst_stack(int n, const int* frame, const int* from, const int* to, int m, int root) {
    int state[64] = {};
    int best[64] = {};
    return depth_from(root, n, frame, from, to, m, state, best);
}
--- hint
The worst stack from a function is its own frame plus the worst stack of whichever function it calls that is deepest. That is a recursion over the call graph: a depth-first search, as in DSA.
--- hint
Remember each function's answer once it is finished, so shared callees such as `log` are computed once. Mark each function with a state: 0 not visited, 1 on the current path, 2 finished.
--- hint
Reaching a function that is in state 1 means you have walked around a cycle back onto the current path: return -1, and pass that -1 all the way up.
--- check test | The example: 128
[] { int fr[4] = {64, 32, 48, 16}; int a[4] = {0, 0, 1, 2}; int b[4] = {1, 2, 3, 3}; return worst_stack(4, fr, a, b, 4, 0) == 128; }()
--- check test | A function that calls nothing
[] { int fr[1] = {200}; int a[1] = {0}; int b[1] = {0}; return worst_stack(1, fr, a, b, 0, 0) == 200; }()
--- check test | Direct recursion gives -1
[] { int fr[2] = {10, 20}; int a[2] = {0, 1}; int b[2] = {1, 1}; return worst_stack(2, fr, a, b, 2, 0) == -1; }()
--- check test | Recursion through other functions gives -1
[] { int fr[4] = {8, 8, 8, 8}; int a[4] = {0, 1, 2, 3}; int b[4] = {1, 2, 3, 1}; return worst_stack(4, fr, a, b, 4, 0) == -1; }()
--- check test | A cycle root never reaches does not matter
[] { int fr[4] = {5, 7, 100, 100}; int a[3] = {0, 2, 3}; int b[3] = {1, 3, 2}; return worst_stack(4, fr, a, b, 3, 0) == 12; }()
--- check test | Starting from another root
[] { int fr[4] = {64, 32, 48, 16}; int a[4] = {0, 0, 1, 2}; int b[4] = {1, 2, 3, 3}; return worst_stack(4, fr, a, b, 4, 1) == 48 && worst_stack(4, fr, a, b, 4, 3) == 16; }()
--- check test | A long chain of 60 functions, each also calling the last
[] { int fr[60]; int a[120]; int b[120]; int m = 0; for (int i = 0; i < 60; i++) fr[i] = i + 1; for (int i = 0; i + 1 < 60; i++) { a[m] = i; b[m] = i + 1; m++; a[m] = i; b[m] = 59; m++; } return worst_stack(60, fr, a, b, m, 0) == 1830; }()
--- check test | A diamond: shared callees counted once per chain
[] { int fr[5] = {1, 10, 20, 30, 40}; int a[5] = {0, 0, 1, 2, 3}; int b[5] = {1, 2, 3, 3, 4}; return worst_stack(5, fr, a, b, 5, 0) == 91; }()

+++ question | Why one pointer runs the stack
--- ask
Why can the call stack be managed with a single stack pointer, when the heap needs a whole allocator?
--- choice
Because the compiler works out every frame's address before the program runs.
--- choice correct
Calls nest: a function returns before its caller does, so the frame being freed is always the newest one, and the live frames always form one block that ends at the stack pointer.
--- choice
Because the stack is small, so there is never much to keep track of.
--- choice
Because the operating system frees each frame when its function returns.
--- why
Nested lifetimes mean last in, first out. Heap blocks can be freed in any order, which leaves holes that have to be tracked; stack frames never leave holes, so one boundary is all there is to remember. Frame addresses depend on the path of calls at run time, so the compiler cannot fix them in advance.

+++ question | Rounding up to a multiple of 8
--- ask
What is `(n + 7) / 8 * 8` for `n = 17`, with whole-number division?
--- answer
24
--- why
17 + 7 = 24, 24 / 8 = 3, and 3 × 8 = 24: the smallest multiple of 8 that is at least 17. For n = 16 it gives 16 itself, since 23 / 8 is 2.

=== sys-05 | malloc and free: sizes, alignment, leaks and realloc
--- teach
Last lesson ended with three ways for a function to hand data back, and the third was the heap: memory that lives from the moment you ask for it until the moment you give it back, whatever functions start and end in between. This lesson is about the two functions that do the asking and the giving, `malloc` and `free`, their partner `realloc`, and the bugs that come from using them wrongly. In C there is no garbage collector and no destructor: every block you take, you must give back yourself.

### Asking for memory: malloc

`void* malloc(size_t n)`, from `<cstdlib>`, asks for a block of `n` bytes on the heap. It returns the address of the first byte, or a null pointer if it cannot find the memory.

`void*` is a pointer with no element type: "an address, of something". You convert it to the pointer type you want. C does this silently; C++ needs [[a cast|void-cast]]:

```cpp fragment
int count = 50;
double* samples = (double*)malloc(count * sizeof *samples);
if (samples == nullptr) {
    // no memory: report it, clean up, return an error
}
```

Read `sizeof *samples` as "the size of the thing `samples` points at", here the size of a `double`. It is the safe way to write the size: if you later change `samples` to a `float*`, the size changes with it.

Three facts about the block you get:

- **Its contents are garbage.** `malloc` does not clear the memory. Reading a byte before writing it gives whatever was there before.
- **It is aligned for any type.** The address is a multiple of the largest alignment any standard type needs, usually 16. So you may store `double`s, `long long`s or structs in it. More on alignment below.
- **It can fail.** On a desktop that is rare, but on a small embedded system it is ordinary. Always check for a null pointer.

`calloc(count, size)` asks for `count` elements of `size` bytes each, and **sets every byte to zero**. It also refuses safely when `count × size` is too big to represent, which `malloc(count * size)` does not, as you will see.

### Giving it back: free

`free(p)` gives the block that starts at `p` back to the heap. After that, the memory is no longer yours: `p` still holds the old address, but it is a dangling pointer, like the ones last lesson. A good habit is to set the pointer to null right after freeing it.

```cpp fragment
free(samples);
samples = nullptr;   // now a mistaken second free(samples) does nothing
```

`free(nullptr)` is allowed and does nothing, which keeps cleanup code simple.

The whole contract fits in one sentence: **every block from `malloc` must be passed to `free` exactly once, with the very address `malloc` returned.** Each way of breaking it has a name:

| mistake | what happens |
|---|---|
| never freeing a block | a **memory leak**: the block stays taken until the program ends |
| freeing it twice | a **double free**: undefined behaviour, often a crash inside `free` later |
| using it after freeing | a **use-after-free**: undefined behaviour; the memory may already belong to someone else |
| freeing an address `malloc` did not return | undefined behaviour: the middle of a block, a local variable, a string literal |

A leak happens most often when the **last pointer** to a block is lost: overwritten, or gone because the function holding it returned. Once no pointer holds the address, nothing can ever free the block.

Double frees and use-after-free are worse than leaks. The allocator keeps its own bookkeeping next to your blocks (two lessons from now you will write one), so writing into a freed block, or freeing it twice, damages that bookkeeping. The program may crash much later, somewhere unrelated, and attackers can [[exploit it|uaf-attacks]].

### Ownership

Since C will not free anything for you, every block needs an **owner**: the one piece of code responsible for freeing it. Write the owner down in the comments. Typical rules are:

- a function that returns a block from `malloc` hands ownership to its caller ("the caller must free the result");
- a struct that holds a pointer owns that block, and comes with a function that frees it;
- a function that is only lent a pointer must not free it.

### A size that overflows

Suppose a message says it holds `count` records of `size` bytes, and you compute `malloc(count * size)`. The multiplication is done in `size_t`, which is unsigned, so if the true product is too big it **wraps round**, silently, to a small number. `malloc` then succeeds with a tiny block, and the code that fills in `count` records writes far past its end. Real security holes have come from exactly this.

The check that catches it, for `count` greater than 0, is:

```cpp fragment
if (size > SIZE_MAX / count) { /* the product would not fit */ }
```

`SIZE_MAX`, from `<cstdint>`, is the largest `size_t` value: about 4.3 billion when `size_t` is 32 bits, as in ORBIT's browser build, and about 1.8 × 10¹⁹ when it is 64 bits, as on a typical laptop.

**Why the check is exact.** Let M be `SIZE_MAX`, c the count, and q the whole-number quotient M / c, so q is the largest whole number with c × q ≤ M. If size ≤ q, then c × size ≤ c × q ≤ M, so the product fits. If size ≥ q + 1, then c × size ≥ c × (q + 1) > M, because q + 1 is too big to be the quotient. So the product fits exactly when size ≤ q, and the check never multiplies, so it cannot overflow itself.

### Alignment

Every type has an **alignment**: its objects must start at an address that is a multiple of it. `alignof(T)` gives it. A `char` has alignment 1, an `int` usually 4, and a `double` 8. The reasons are in the hardware: a CPU reads memory in aligned chunks, so a `double` that straddled two chunks would need two reads, or would not be readable at all on some processors, and it could straddle two cache lines or even two pages.

`malloc` cannot know what you will store, so it returns addresses aligned for the most demanding standard type, the alignment of the type `max_align_t`: typically 16. That wastes a little memory on small blocks, and it is one reason why a thousand 3-byte `malloc`s take far more than 3,000 bytes.

### Resizing: realloc

`realloc(p, n)` changes the size of the block at `p` to `n` bytes, and returns the block's address afterwards. It keeps the old contents, up to the smaller of the two sizes. If there is room right after the block it may grow it where it is; otherwise it gets a new block, copies the contents over, frees the old one, and returns the new address. So after a successful `realloc`, **only the returned pointer is valid**. Any other pointer into the old block is dangling.

If it cannot get the memory, `realloc` returns a null pointer and **leaves the old block alone**: still valid, still holding your data, and still yours to free. That rule makes one very common line a bug:

```cpp fragment
buf = (char*)realloc(buf, bigger);   // wrong: on failure buf becomes null
```

On failure this overwrites the only pointer to the old block with null. The block leaks, and the data in it is lost. The fix is to put the result in a second variable, and replace the old pointer only when it is not null.

`realloc(nullptr, n)` is the same as `malloc(n)`, which means a growing buffer that starts out empty needs no special first case.

### Growing by doubling

A buffer that grows one element at a time by calling `realloc` for each new element may copy everything on every call: 1 + 2 + … + n copies in total, which is Θ(n²). The fix is the one from the dynamic arrays lesson of DSA: when it is full, **double** the capacity.

**Why doubling makes each append O(1) on average.** Start at capacity 4, say, and push n elements. A copy happens only when the buffer is full, at sizes 4, 8, 16, …, up to the last capacity below n. Those copies move 4 + 8 + … + 2^k elements, where 2^k < n. That sum is 2^(k+1) − 4, which is less than 2n. So n pushes cost O(n) copying in total: O(1) **amortised** per push. Growing by a fixed amount instead, say 16 more each time, still makes about n / 16 copies of up to n elements each, which is Θ(n²).

### Finding these bugs

Leaks and double frees are silent, so programmers use tools that watch every allocation. **Valgrind** runs the program on a simulated CPU and reports every leaked block and bad access. **AddressSanitizer** (`-fsanitize=address`) builds checks into the program. Many projects also wrap `malloc` and `free` in a **debug allocator** that counts live blocks and refuses to free an address it never handed out.

The checks in this lesson do exactly that. Several starters begin with a small **tracker**: three functions, `t_malloc`, `t_realloc` and `t_free`, that behave like `malloc`, `realloc` and `free` but keep a list of live blocks. `live_count` is the number of blocks allocated and not yet freed, and `bad_frees` counts attempts to free something that is not a live block (without actually freeing it, so the program survives to be graded). The checks can also tell the tracker to make one allocation fail, to test that your code copes. Use the tracker's functions wherever the task says so, and leave its code as it is.

### What it costs

`malloc` and `free` usually take tens of nanoseconds, but not a fixed amount: the allocator may have to search its free lists, or ask the operating system for more memory. Every block also carries a hidden header of 8 or 16 bytes in front of it, and is rounded up to the alignment. So many tiny blocks waste both time and memory, and programs that allocate in a hot loop are often sped up by allocating once, outside it.

### Where it is used

- Every growing structure in C is built on these functions: dynamic arrays, hash tables, trees, and the objects of the Python interpreter.
- Safety-critical code often forbids `malloc` after start-up (the third [[Power of Ten|rule-three]] rule): everything is allocated once at the beginning, so memory cannot run out mid-mission, and timing stays predictable.
- Servers and databases replace the standard allocator with faster ones, such as jemalloc and tcmalloc, without changing any other code, precisely because all allocation goes through these few functions.

**Watch out:** `sizeof` of the wrong thing. `malloc(count * sizeof(samples))` takes the size of the **pointer** (4 bytes in ORBIT's browser build, 8 on a typical laptop), not of a `double`, and `malloc(count)` forgets the element size entirely. Both compile, both give a block too small, and the program then writes past its end. Write `count * sizeof *samples`, and read it aloud.

::: context void-cast Why C++ needs the cast
In C, a `void*` converts to any other object pointer type without a cast, so C programmers write `double* p = malloc(n * sizeof *p);`, and many C style guides say not to cast, since a cast can hide the error of a missing `#include <stdlib.h>`. C++ is stricter: turning a `void*` into a `double*` might be a mistake, so it must be written out. In this course the code is compiled as C++, so every `malloc` result gets a cast.
:::

::: context uaf-attacks How use-after-free is exploited
After a block is freed, the allocator can hand the same memory to the next request. If an attacker can make the program allocate something of their own choosing there, the old dangling pointer now reads and writes the attacker's data, for example a function pointer they control. Use-after-free bugs in web browsers have been among the most exploited security holes of the last decade, which is one reason browsers and operating systems are adopting memory-safe languages such as Rust.
:::

::: context rule-three No allocation after start-up
Rule 3 of NASA JPL's "Power of Ten" says: do not use dynamic memory allocation after initialisation. Allocators can behave unpredictably in time, can fail when memory is fragmented, and are the source of leaks, double frees and use-after-free. Flight software therefore sizes its buffers for the worst case, allocates them all while starting up, and then only reuses them. The next lesson builds the kind of fixed pools such systems use instead of a general heap.
:::
--- task
Build a growable array of doubles in C, like a small `std::vector`. It owns its memory. The starter begins with the tracker; use `t_realloc` and `t_free` for all memory.

```cpp fragment
struct Series {
    double* data;   // the block, or nullptr when empty
    int size;       // values stored
    int cap;        // values the block can hold
};
```

Write four functions. No `main`.

- `void series_init(Series* s)`: an empty series with no block: `data` is `nullptr`, `size` and `cap` are 0.
- `bool series_push(Series* s, double v)`: add `v` at the end. When the block is full, grow `cap` to 4 if it was 0, and otherwise to double, with `t_realloc`. If that allocation fails, return `false` and leave the series **exactly as it was**, with its old block and values. Otherwise store the value and return `true`.
- `void series_free(Series* s)`: free the block and make the series empty again, as `series_init` does. Calling it twice in a row must be harmless.
- `bool series_copy(Series* dst, const Series* src)`: `dst` is an empty series. Make it an independent copy of `src`, with its own block of exactly `src->size` values (so `cap` equals `size`). Copying an empty series allocates nothing and returns `true`. If the allocation fails, return `false`, and `dst` stays empty.
--- starter
#include <cstdlib>
#include <cstring>

// ---- The tracker. The checks watch your blocks through it: use t_malloc,
// ---- t_realloc and t_free exactly like malloc, realloc and free. Do not change it.
static void* live[1024];
static int live_count = 0;   // blocks allocated and not yet freed
static int bad_frees = 0;    // frees of pointers that were not live blocks
static int alloc_calls = 0;  // successful allocations and reallocations
static int fail_after = -1;  // -1: never fail; k: k more succeed, then one fails
static size_t last_size = 0; // bytes asked for by the last successful allocation

static bool should_fail() {
    if (fail_after == 0) {
        fail_after = -1;
        return true;
    }
    if (fail_after > 0) fail_after--;
    return false;
}

static int find_live(void* p) {
    for (int i = 0; i < live_count; i++) {
        if (live[i] == p) return i;
    }
    return -1;
}

void* t_malloc(size_t n) {
    if (should_fail() || live_count == 1024) return nullptr;
    void* p = malloc(n == 0 ? 1 : n);
    if (p != nullptr) {
        live[live_count++] = p;
        alloc_calls++;
        last_size = n;
    }
    return p;
}

void t_free(void* p) {
    if (p == nullptr) return;
    int i = find_live(p);
    if (i < 0) {
        bad_frees++;
        return;
    }
    live[i] = live[--live_count];
    free(p);
}

void* t_realloc(void* p, size_t n) {
    if (p == nullptr) return t_malloc(n);
    int i = find_live(p);
    if (i < 0) {
        bad_frees++;
        return nullptr;
    }
    if (should_fail()) return nullptr;
    void* q = realloc(p, n == 0 ? 1 : n);
    if (q != nullptr) {
        live[i] = q;
        alloc_calls++;
        last_size = n;
    }
    return q;
}
// ---- End of the tracker.

struct Series {
    double* data;
    int size;
    int cap;
};

void series_init(Series* s) {
}

bool series_push(Series* s, double v) {
    return false;
}

void series_free(Series* s) {
}

bool series_copy(Series* dst, const Series* src) {
    return false;
}
--- solution
#include <cstdlib>
#include <cstring>

// ---- The tracker. The checks watch your blocks through it: use t_malloc,
// ---- t_realloc and t_free exactly like malloc, realloc and free. Do not change it.
static void* live[1024];
static int live_count = 0;   // blocks allocated and not yet freed
static int bad_frees = 0;    // frees of pointers that were not live blocks
static int alloc_calls = 0;  // successful allocations and reallocations
static int fail_after = -1;  // -1: never fail; k: k more succeed, then one fails
static size_t last_size = 0; // bytes asked for by the last successful allocation

static bool should_fail() {
    if (fail_after == 0) {
        fail_after = -1;
        return true;
    }
    if (fail_after > 0) fail_after--;
    return false;
}

static int find_live(void* p) {
    for (int i = 0; i < live_count; i++) {
        if (live[i] == p) return i;
    }
    return -1;
}

void* t_malloc(size_t n) {
    if (should_fail() || live_count == 1024) return nullptr;
    void* p = malloc(n == 0 ? 1 : n);
    if (p != nullptr) {
        live[live_count++] = p;
        alloc_calls++;
        last_size = n;
    }
    return p;
}

void t_free(void* p) {
    if (p == nullptr) return;
    int i = find_live(p);
    if (i < 0) {
        bad_frees++;
        return;
    }
    live[i] = live[--live_count];
    free(p);
}

void* t_realloc(void* p, size_t n) {
    if (p == nullptr) return t_malloc(n);
    int i = find_live(p);
    if (i < 0) {
        bad_frees++;
        return nullptr;
    }
    if (should_fail()) return nullptr;
    void* q = realloc(p, n == 0 ? 1 : n);
    if (q != nullptr) {
        live[i] = q;
        alloc_calls++;
        last_size = n;
    }
    return q;
}
// ---- End of the tracker.

struct Series {
    double* data;
    int size;
    int cap;
};

void series_init(Series* s) {
    s->data = nullptr;
    s->size = 0;
    s->cap = 0;
}

bool series_push(Series* s, double v) {
    if (s->size == s->cap) {
        int new_cap = s->cap == 0 ? 4 : s->cap * 2;
        double* bigger = (double*)t_realloc(s->data, new_cap * sizeof *bigger);
        if (bigger == nullptr) return false;
        s->data = bigger;
        s->cap = new_cap;
    }
    s->data[s->size] = v;
    s->size++;
    return true;
}

void series_free(Series* s) {
    t_free(s->data);
    series_init(s);
}

bool series_copy(Series* dst, const Series* src) {
    series_init(dst);
    if (src->size == 0) return true;
    double* block = (double*)t_malloc(src->size * sizeof *block);
    if (block == nullptr) return false;
    memcpy(block, src->data, src->size * sizeof *block);
    dst->data = block;
    dst->size = src->size;
    dst->cap = src->size;
    return true;
}
--- hint
In `series_push`, grow only when `size == cap`. Put the result of `t_realloc` in a new variable, and copy it into `s->data` (and update `cap`) only if it is not null. Since `t_realloc(nullptr, n)` acts like `t_malloc(n)`, the first push needs no special case.
--- hint
The size in bytes of `k` doubles is `k * sizeof(double)`. `series_free` can call `t_free(s->data)` even when it is `nullptr`, then reset the fields, which makes a second call harmless.
--- hint
`series_copy` allocates `src->size` doubles with `t_malloc`, copies them with `memcpy` (the byte count again), and fills in all three fields of `dst`.
--- check test | series_init makes an empty series
[] { Series s; s.data = (double*)1; s.size = 5; s.cap = 9; series_init(&s); return s.data == nullptr && s.size == 0 && s.cap == 0; }()
--- check test | Pushing 10 values keeps them in order, in one live block
[] { int before = live_count; Series s; series_init(&s); for (int i = 0; i < 10; i++) if (!series_push(&s, i * 1.5)) return false; bool ok = s.size == 10 && live_count == before + 1; for (int i = 0; i < 10; i++) ok = ok && s.data[i] == i * 1.5; series_free(&s); return ok; }()
--- check test | The capacity goes 4, 8, 16
[] { Series s; series_init(&s); int caps[17]; for (int i = 0; i < 17; i++) { series_push(&s, 0.0); caps[i] = s.cap; } bool ok = caps[0] == 4 && caps[3] == 4 && caps[4] == 8 && caps[7] == 8 && caps[8] == 16 && caps[15] == 16 && caps[16] == 32; series_free(&s); return ok; }()
--- check test | series_free gives the block back and empties the series
[] { int before = live_count; Series s; series_init(&s); series_push(&s, 1.0); series_push(&s, 2.0); series_free(&s); return live_count == before && s.data == nullptr && s.size == 0 && s.cap == 0; }()
--- check test | series_free twice is harmless, and never frees a bad pointer
[] { int bad = bad_frees; Series s; series_init(&s); series_push(&s, 1.0); series_free(&s); series_free(&s); Series e; series_init(&e); series_free(&e); return bad_frees == bad; }()
--- check test | A failed growth returns false and leaves the series exactly as it was
[] { int before = live_count; Series s; series_init(&s); for (int i = 0; i < 4; i++) series_push(&s, i + 0.25); double* old = s.data; fail_after = 0; bool pushed = series_push(&s, 99.0); bool ok = !pushed && s.data == old && s.size == 4 && s.cap == 4 && s.data[3] == 3.25 && live_count == before + 1; ok = ok && series_push(&s, 5.0) && s.size == 5 && s.data[4] == 5.0; series_free(&s); return ok && live_count == before; }()
--- check test | A failed first push leaves the series empty
[] { int before = live_count; Series s; series_init(&s); fail_after = 0; bool pushed = series_push(&s, 1.0); return !pushed && s.data == nullptr && s.size == 0 && s.cap == 0 && live_count == before; }()
--- check test | 100,000 pushes need only about 16 reallocations
[] { int calls = alloc_calls; Series s; series_init(&s); for (int i = 0; i < 100000; i++) if (!series_push(&s, i)) return false; bool ok = s.size == 100000 && s.data[99999] == 99999.0 && alloc_calls - calls <= 20; series_free(&s); return ok; }()
?? Growing by one element, or by a fixed amount, reallocates thousands of times. Double the capacity.
--- check test | series_copy makes an independent copy with cap equal to size
[] { int before = live_count; Series a; series_init(&a); for (int i = 0; i < 5; i++) series_push(&a, i * 2.0); Series b; bool ok = series_copy(&b, &a); ok = ok && b.size == 5 && b.cap == 5 && b.data != a.data && b.data[4] == 8.0 && live_count == before + 2; a.data[0] = -1.0; ok = ok && b.data[0] == 0.0; series_free(&a); series_free(&b); return ok && live_count == before; }()
--- check test | Copying an empty series allocates nothing
[] { int calls = alloc_calls; Series a; series_init(&a); Series b; b.data = nullptr; b.size = 0; b.cap = 0; bool ok = series_copy(&b, &a); return ok && b.data == nullptr && b.size == 0 && b.cap == 0 && alloc_calls == calls; }()
--- check test | A failed copy returns false and leaves the copy empty
[] { int before = live_count; Series a; series_init(&a); series_push(&a, 3.0); Series b; b.data = nullptr; b.size = 0; b.cap = 0; fail_after = 0; bool ok = !series_copy(&b, &a) && b.data == nullptr && b.size == 0 && live_count == before + 1; series_free(&a); return ok && live_count == before; }()

+++ practice | A range of numbers on the heap
--- task
The starter begins with the tracker from the lesson. Write `int* make_range(int lo, int hi)` that returns a new block, from `t_malloc`, holding the `hi - lo` whole numbers `lo`, `lo + 1`, …, `hi - 1`, in that order. The caller owns the block and frees it with `t_free`. No `main`.

If `hi <= lo` the range is empty: return `nullptr` without allocating anything. If the allocation fails, return `nullptr`.

For example, `make_range(3, 7)` returns a block holding `3, 4, 5, 6`.
--- starter
#include <cstdlib>

// ---- The tracker. The checks watch your blocks through it: use t_malloc,
// ---- t_realloc and t_free exactly like malloc, realloc and free. Do not change it.
static void* live[1024];
static int live_count = 0;   // blocks allocated and not yet freed
static int bad_frees = 0;    // frees of pointers that were not live blocks
static int alloc_calls = 0;  // successful allocations and reallocations
static int fail_after = -1;  // -1: never fail; k: k more succeed, then one fails
static size_t last_size = 0; // bytes asked for by the last successful allocation

static bool should_fail() {
    if (fail_after == 0) {
        fail_after = -1;
        return true;
    }
    if (fail_after > 0) fail_after--;
    return false;
}

static int find_live(void* p) {
    for (int i = 0; i < live_count; i++) {
        if (live[i] == p) return i;
    }
    return -1;
}

void* t_malloc(size_t n) {
    if (should_fail() || live_count == 1024) return nullptr;
    void* p = malloc(n == 0 ? 1 : n);
    if (p != nullptr) {
        live[live_count++] = p;
        alloc_calls++;
        last_size = n;
    }
    return p;
}

void t_free(void* p) {
    if (p == nullptr) return;
    int i = find_live(p);
    if (i < 0) {
        bad_frees++;
        return;
    }
    live[i] = live[--live_count];
    free(p);
}

void* t_realloc(void* p, size_t n) {
    if (p == nullptr) return t_malloc(n);
    int i = find_live(p);
    if (i < 0) {
        bad_frees++;
        return nullptr;
    }
    if (should_fail()) return nullptr;
    void* q = realloc(p, n == 0 ? 1 : n);
    if (q != nullptr) {
        live[i] = q;
        alloc_calls++;
        last_size = n;
    }
    return q;
}
// ---- End of the tracker.

int* make_range(int lo, int hi) {
    return nullptr;
}
--- solution
#include <cstdlib>

// ---- The tracker. The checks watch your blocks through it: use t_malloc,
// ---- t_realloc and t_free exactly like malloc, realloc and free. Do not change it.
static void* live[1024];
static int live_count = 0;   // blocks allocated and not yet freed
static int bad_frees = 0;    // frees of pointers that were not live blocks
static int alloc_calls = 0;  // successful allocations and reallocations
static int fail_after = -1;  // -1: never fail; k: k more succeed, then one fails
static size_t last_size = 0; // bytes asked for by the last successful allocation

static bool should_fail() {
    if (fail_after == 0) {
        fail_after = -1;
        return true;
    }
    if (fail_after > 0) fail_after--;
    return false;
}

static int find_live(void* p) {
    for (int i = 0; i < live_count; i++) {
        if (live[i] == p) return i;
    }
    return -1;
}

void* t_malloc(size_t n) {
    if (should_fail() || live_count == 1024) return nullptr;
    void* p = malloc(n == 0 ? 1 : n);
    if (p != nullptr) {
        live[live_count++] = p;
        alloc_calls++;
        last_size = n;
    }
    return p;
}

void t_free(void* p) {
    if (p == nullptr) return;
    int i = find_live(p);
    if (i < 0) {
        bad_frees++;
        return;
    }
    live[i] = live[--live_count];
    free(p);
}

void* t_realloc(void* p, size_t n) {
    if (p == nullptr) return t_malloc(n);
    int i = find_live(p);
    if (i < 0) {
        bad_frees++;
        return nullptr;
    }
    if (should_fail()) return nullptr;
    void* q = realloc(p, n == 0 ? 1 : n);
    if (q != nullptr) {
        live[i] = q;
        alloc_calls++;
        last_size = n;
    }
    return q;
}
// ---- End of the tracker.

int* make_range(int lo, int hi) {
    if (hi <= lo) return nullptr;
    int count = hi - lo;
    int* block = (int*)t_malloc(count * sizeof *block);
    if (block == nullptr) return nullptr;
    for (int i = 0; i < count; i++) block[i] = lo + i;
    return block;
}
--- hint
The block needs `(hi - lo) * sizeof(int)` bytes. Convert the `void*` that `t_malloc` returns with `(int*)`.
--- hint
Check `hi <= lo` before allocating anything, and check the result of `t_malloc` before writing into it.
--- check test | make_range(3, 7) holds 3, 4, 5, 6 in one block
[] { int before = live_count; int* r = make_range(3, 7); bool ok = r != nullptr && r[0] == 3 && r[1] == 4 && r[2] == 5 && r[3] == 6 && live_count == before + 1; t_free(r); return ok && live_count == before; }()
--- check test | An empty range allocates nothing
[] { int calls = alloc_calls; int* a = make_range(5, 5); int* b = make_range(9, 2); return a == nullptr && b == nullptr && alloc_calls == calls; }()
--- check test | Negative numbers
[] { int* r = make_range(-2, 1); bool ok = r != nullptr && r[0] == -2 && r[1] == -1 && r[2] == 0; t_free(r); return ok; }()
--- check test | A failed allocation returns nullptr
[] { int before = live_count; fail_after = 0; int* r = make_range(0, 10); return r == nullptr && live_count == before; }()
--- check test | 10,000 numbers
[] { int* r = make_range(0, 10000); bool ok = r != nullptr; for (int i = 0; ok && i < 10000; i++) ok = r[i] == i; t_free(r); return ok && bad_frees == 0; }()

+++ practice | Three strings joined into one block
--- task
The starter begins with the tracker from the lesson. Write `char* concat3(const char* a, const char* b, const char* c)` that returns a new string, in a block from `t_malloc`, holding `a`, then `b`, then `c`, and a terminator. Allocate **exactly** the bytes needed: the three lengths plus 1. The caller owns the result. You may use `strlen` and `memcpy` from `<cstring>`. No `main`.

If the allocation fails, return `nullptr`. For example, `concat3("ORB", "-", "7")` returns `ORB-7`, in a block of 6 bytes.
--- starter
