// ---- End of the tracker.

char* concat3(const char* a, const char* b, const char* c) {
    size_t la = strlen(a);
    size_t lb = strlen(b);
    size_t lc = strlen(c);
    char* out = (char*)t_malloc(la + lb + lc + 1);
    if (out == nullptr) return nullptr;
    memcpy(out, a, la);
    memcpy(out + la, b, lb);
    memcpy(out + la + lb, c, lc);
    out[la + lb + lc] = '\0';
    return out;
}
--- hint
Measure the three strings first. The block needs their total length plus 1 for the terminator.
--- hint
Copy each string's characters with `memcpy` to the right place, `out`, `out + la` and `out + la + lb`, then write the terminator at the very end.
--- check test | ORB, -, 7 gives ORB-7
[] { int before = live_count; char* s = concat3("ORB", "-", "7"); bool ok = s != nullptr && std::string(s) == "ORB-7" && live_count == before + 1; t_free(s); return ok && live_count == before; }()
--- check test | Empty strings
[] { char* s = concat3("", "", ""); char* t = concat3("", "mid", ""); bool ok = s != nullptr && s[0] == 0 && std::string(t) == "mid"; t_free(s); t_free(t); return ok; }()
--- check test | Allocates exactly the bytes needed
[] { int calls = alloc_calls; char* s = concat3("alpha", "beta", "gamma"); bool ok = std::string(s) == "alphabetagamma" && last_size == 15 && alloc_calls == calls + 1; t_free(s); char* t = concat3("ab", "c", ""); ok = ok && std::string(t) == "abc" && last_size == 4; t_free(t); return ok; }()
--- check test | A failed allocation returns nullptr and leaks nothing
[] { int before = live_count; fail_after = 0; char* s = concat3("x", "y", "z"); return s == nullptr && live_count == before; }()
--- check test | The inputs are not changed
[] { char a[] = "one"; char b[] = "two"; char c[] = "three"; char* s = concat3(a, b, c); bool ok = std::string(a) == "one" && std::string(b) == "two" && std::string(c) == "three" && std::string(s) == "onetwothree"; t_free(s); return ok; }()

+++ practice | A grid in two blocks
--- task
The starter begins with the tracker from the lesson. A table of `rows` × `cols` ints can be allocated so that it is used as `g[r][c]`, with only two blocks:

1. one block of `rows × cols` ints for all the values, row after row, every value set to 0;
2. one block of `rows` pointers, where pointer `r` points at the start of row `r` inside the first block.

Write two functions. No `main`.

- `int** grid_new(int rows, int cols)`: build the grid that way with `t_malloc` (rows and cols are at least 1) and return the block of row pointers. If **either** allocation fails, free whatever was already allocated and return `nullptr`, so nothing leaks.
- `void grid_free(int** g)`: free both blocks. `grid_free(nullptr)` does nothing.

The row-pointer block has type `int**`, a pointer to pointers to ints, so its size is `rows * sizeof(int*)`. Remember that a pointer is 4 bytes in ORBIT's browser build and 8 on a typical laptop: always write `sizeof(int*)`, never a number.
--- starter
#include <cstdlib>
#include <cstring>

// ---- The tracker. The checks watch your blocks through it: use t_malloc,
// ---- t_realloc and t_free exactly like malloc, realloc and free. Do not change it.
static void* live[1024];
static int live_count = 0;   // blocks allocated and not yet freed
static int bad_frees = 0;    // frees of pointers that were not live blocks
static int alloc_calls = 0;  // successful allocations and reallocations
static int fail_after = -1;  // -1: never fail; k: k more succeed, then one fails
static size_t last_size = 0; // bytes asked for by the last successful allocation

static bool should_fail() {
    if (fail_after == 0) {
        fail_after = -1;
        return true;
    }
    if (fail_after > 0) fail_after--;
    return false;
}

static int find_live(void* p) {
    for (int i = 0; i < live_count; i++) {
        if (live[i] == p) return i;
    }
    return -1;
}

void* t_malloc(size_t n) {
    if (should_fail() || live_count == 1024) return nullptr;
    void* p = malloc(n == 0 ? 1 : n);
    if (p != nullptr) {
        live[live_count++] = p;
        alloc_calls++;
        last_size = n;
    }
    return p;
}

void t_free(void* p) {
    if (p == nullptr) return;
    int i = find_live(p);
    if (i < 0) {
        bad_frees++;
        return;
    }
    live[i] = live[--live_count];
    free(p);
}

void* t_realloc(void* p, size_t n) {
    if (p == nullptr) return t_malloc(n);
    int i = find_live(p);
    if (i < 0) {
        bad_frees++;
        return nullptr;
    }
    if (should_fail()) return nullptr;
    void* q = realloc(p, n == 0 ? 1 : n);
    if (q != nullptr) {
        live[i] = q;
        alloc_calls++;
        last_size = n;
    }
    return q;
}
// ---- End of the tracker.

int** grid_new(int rows, int cols) {
    return nullptr;
}

void grid_free(int** g) {
}
--- solution
#include <cstdlib>
#include <cstring>

// ---- The tracker. The checks watch your blocks through it: use t_malloc,
// ---- t_realloc and t_free exactly like malloc, realloc and free. Do not change it.
static void* live[1024];
static int live_count = 0;   // blocks allocated and not yet freed
static int bad_frees = 0;    // frees of pointers that were not live blocks
static int alloc_calls = 0;  // successful allocations and reallocations
static int fail_after = -1;  // -1: never fail; k: k more succeed, then one fails
static size_t last_size = 0; // bytes asked for by the last successful allocation

static bool should_fail() {
    if (fail_after == 0) {
        fail_after = -1;
        return true;
    }
    if (fail_after > 0) fail_after--;
    return false;
}

static int find_live(void* p) {
    for (int i = 0; i < live_count; i++) {
        if (live[i] == p) return i;
    }
    return -1;
}

void* t_malloc(size_t n) {
    if (should_fail() || live_count == 1024) return nullptr;
    void* p = malloc(n == 0 ? 1 : n);
    if (p != nullptr) {
        live[live_count++] = p;
        alloc_calls++;
        last_size = n;
    }
    return p;
}

void t_free(void* p) {
    if (p == nullptr) return;
    int i = find_live(p);
    if (i < 0) {
        bad_frees++;
        return;
    }
    live[i] = live[--live_count];
    free(p);
}

void* t_realloc(void* p, size_t n) {
    if (p == nullptr) return t_malloc(n);
    int i = find_live(p);
    if (i < 0) {
        bad_frees++;
        return nullptr;
    }
    if (should_fail()) return nullptr;
    void* q = realloc(p, n == 0 ? 1 : n);
    if (q != nullptr) {
        live[i] = q;
        alloc_calls++;
        last_size = n;
    }
    return q;
}
// ---- End of the tracker.

int** grid_new(int rows, int cols) {
    int* cells = (int*)t_malloc(rows * cols * sizeof *cells);
    if (cells == nullptr) return nullptr;
    int** g = (int**)t_malloc(rows * sizeof *g);
    if (g == nullptr) {
        t_free(cells);
        return nullptr;
    }
    memset(cells, 0, rows * cols * sizeof *cells);
    for (int r = 0; r < rows; r++) g[r] = cells + r * cols;
    return g;
}

void grid_free(int** g) {
    if (g == nullptr) return;
    t_free(g[0]);
    t_free(g);
}
--- hint
Allocate the values first. Then allocate the row pointers, and if that fails, free the values block before returning `nullptr`.
--- hint
Row `r` starts `r * cols` ints into the values block: `g[r] = cells + r * cols`. `memset(cells, 0, bytes)` sets every byte to 0, which makes every int 0.
--- hint
In `grid_free`, `g[0]` is the start of the values block, so `t_free(g[0])` frees it. Free the values before the row pointers: once `g` is freed you may not read `g[0]` any more.
--- check test | A 3 × 4 grid: two live blocks, all zeros, rows back to back
[] { int before = live_count; int** g = grid_new(3, 4); bool ok = g != nullptr && live_count == before + 2 && g[1] == g[0] + 4 && g[2] == g[0] + 8; for (int r = 0; ok && r < 3; r++) for (int c = 0; c < 4; c++) ok = ok && g[r][c] == 0; grid_free(g); return ok && live_count == before; }()
--- check test | Values written as g[r][c] can be read back
[] { int** g = grid_new(5, 7); for (int r = 0; r < 5; r++) for (int c = 0; c < 7; c++) g[r][c] = r * 100 + c; bool ok = g[4][6] == 406 && g[0][0] == 0 && *(g[0] + 7) == 100; grid_free(g); return ok && bad_frees == 0; }()
--- check test | A 1 × 1 grid
[] { int before = live_count; int** g = grid_new(1, 1); bool ok = g != nullptr && g[0][0] == 0; grid_free(g); return ok && live_count == before; }()
--- check test | The first allocation fails: nullptr, nothing leaks
[] { int before = live_count; fail_after = 0; int** g = grid_new(2, 2); return g == nullptr && live_count == before; }()
--- check test | The second allocation fails: nullptr, nothing leaks
[] { int before = live_count; fail_after = 1; int** g = grid_new(2, 2); return g == nullptr && live_count == before && bad_frees == 0; }()
--- check test | grid_free(nullptr) does nothing
[] { int bad = bad_frees; grid_free(nullptr); return bad_frees == bad; }()

+++ practice | An array allocation that refuses to overflow
--- task
The starter begins with the tracker from the lesson. Write `void* alloc_zeroed(size_t count, size_t size)`, a safe `calloc`: it returns a block from `t_malloc` of `count × size` bytes, all set to 0. No `main`.

- If `count` or `size` is 0, return `nullptr` without allocating.
- If `count × size` would be larger than `SIZE_MAX`, the product cannot be represented: return `nullptr` **without calling `t_malloc`**. Detect this without doing the multiplication that might overflow.
- If the allocation fails, return `nullptr`.

`SIZE_MAX` is in `<cstdint>`. Its value differs between ORBIT's 32-bit build and a 64-bit laptop, so never write it as a number.
--- starter
#include <cstdint>
#include <cstdlib>
#include <cstring>

// ---- The tracker. The checks watch your blocks through it: use t_malloc,
// ---- t_realloc and t_free exactly like malloc, realloc and free. Do not change it.
static void* live[1024];
static int live_count = 0;   // blocks allocated and not yet freed
static int bad_frees = 0;    // frees of pointers that were not live blocks
static int alloc_calls = 0;  // successful allocations and reallocations
static int fail_after = -1;  // -1: never fail; k: k more succeed, then one fails
static size_t last_size = 0; // bytes asked for by the last successful allocation

static bool should_fail() {
    if (fail_after == 0) {
        fail_after = -1;
        return true;
    }
    if (fail_after > 0) fail_after--;
    return false;
}

static int find_live(void* p) {
    for (int i = 0; i < live_count; i++) {
        if (live[i] == p) return i;
    }
    return -1;
}

void* t_malloc(size_t n) {
    if (should_fail() || live_count == 1024) return nullptr;
    void* p = malloc(n == 0 ? 1 : n);
    if (p != nullptr) {
        live[live_count++] = p;
        alloc_calls++;
        last_size = n;
    }
    return p;
}

void t_free(void* p) {
    if (p == nullptr) return;
    int i = find_live(p);
    if (i < 0) {
        bad_frees++;
        return;
    }
    live[i] = live[--live_count];
    free(p);
}

void* t_realloc(void* p, size_t n) {
    if (p == nullptr) return t_malloc(n);
    int i = find_live(p);
    if (i < 0) {
        bad_frees++;
        return nullptr;
    }
    if (should_fail()) return nullptr;
    void* q = realloc(p, n == 0 ? 1 : n);
    if (q != nullptr) {
        live[i] = q;
        alloc_calls++;
        last_size = n;
    }
    return q;
}
// ---- End of the tracker.

void* alloc_zeroed(size_t count, size_t size) {
    return t_malloc(count * size);
}
--- solution
#include <cstdint>
#include <cstdlib>
#include <cstring>

// ---- The tracker. The checks watch your blocks through it: use t_malloc,
// ---- t_realloc and t_free exactly like malloc, realloc and free. Do not change it.
static void* live[1024];
static int live_count = 0;   // blocks allocated and not yet freed
static int bad_frees = 0;    // frees of pointers that were not live blocks
static int alloc_calls = 0;  // successful allocations and reallocations
static int fail_after = -1;  // -1: never fail; k: k more succeed, then one fails
static size_t last_size = 0; // bytes asked for by the last successful allocation

static bool should_fail() {
    if (fail_after == 0) {
        fail_after = -1;
        return true;
    }
    if (fail_after > 0) fail_after--;
    return false;
}

static int find_live(void* p) {
    for (int i = 0; i < live_count; i++) {
        if (live[i] == p) return i;
    }
    return -1;
}

void* t_malloc(size_t n) {
    if (should_fail() || live_count == 1024) return nullptr;
    void* p = malloc(n == 0 ? 1 : n);
    if (p != nullptr) {
        live[live_count++] = p;
        alloc_calls++;
        last_size = n;
    }
    return p;
}

void t_free(void* p) {
    if (p == nullptr) return;
    int i = find_live(p);
    if (i < 0) {
        bad_frees++;
        return;
    }
    live[i] = live[--live_count];
    free(p);
}

void* t_realloc(void* p, size_t n) {
    if (p == nullptr) return t_malloc(n);
    int i = find_live(p);
    if (i < 0) {
        bad_frees++;
        return nullptr;
    }
    if (should_fail()) return nullptr;
    void* q = realloc(p, n == 0 ? 1 : n);
    if (q != nullptr) {
        live[i] = q;
        alloc_calls++;
        last_size = n;
    }
    return q;
}
// ---- End of the tracker.

void* alloc_zeroed(size_t count, size_t size) {
    if (count == 0 || size == 0) return nullptr;
    if (size > SIZE_MAX / count) return nullptr;
    void* p = t_malloc(count * size);
    if (p == nullptr) return nullptr;
    memset(p, 0, count * size);
    return p;
}
--- hint
The product fits exactly when `size <= SIZE_MAX / count`, for `count` greater than 0. The division cannot overflow, so check that before multiplying.
--- hint
After a successful `t_malloc`, `memset(p, 0, count * size)` sets every byte to 0.
--- check test | 10 ints, all zero, one block
[] { int before = live_count; int* p = (int*)alloc_zeroed(10, sizeof(int)); bool ok = p != nullptr && live_count == before + 1; for (int i = 0; ok && i < 10; i++) ok = p[i] == 0; t_free(p); return ok && live_count == before; }()
--- check test | Zero count or zero size: nullptr, no allocation
[] { int calls = alloc_calls; return alloc_zeroed(0, 8) == nullptr && alloc_zeroed(8, 0) == nullptr && alloc_calls == calls; }()
--- check test | A product that overflows: nullptr, no allocation
[] { int calls = alloc_calls; size_t half = SIZE_MAX / 2 + 1; bool a = alloc_zeroed(half, 2) == nullptr; bool b = alloc_zeroed(2, half) == nullptr; bool c = alloc_zeroed(SIZE_MAX, SIZE_MAX) == nullptr; bool d = alloc_zeroed(SIZE_MAX / 3 + 1, 3) == nullptr; return a && b && c && d && alloc_calls == calls; }()
?? count * size wraps round to a small number. Check size > SIZE_MAX / count before multiplying.
--- check test | Memory that was used before still comes back zeroed
[] { unsigned char* a = (unsigned char*)t_malloc(64); memset(a, 0xAB, 64); t_free(a); unsigned char* b = (unsigned char*)alloc_zeroed(8, 8); bool ok = b != nullptr; for (int i = 0; ok && i < 64; i++) ok = b[i] == 0; t_free(b); return ok; }()
--- check test | A failed allocation returns nullptr
[] { int before = live_count; fail_after = 0; void* p = alloc_zeroed(4, 4); return p == nullptr && live_count == before; }()

+++ practice | Debug: the log that lost everything
--- task
The starter begins with the tracker from the lesson, then a growable log of ints. `log_append` should add a value, doubling the capacity when the block is full, and return `false` when it cannot get more memory, **keeping** the values it already has.

When an allocation fails, the log instead loses every value it held, and its block leaks: nothing points at it any more. Find the bug and fix it. No `main`.
--- starter
#include <cstdlib>

// ---- The tracker. The checks watch your blocks through it: use t_malloc,
// ---- t_realloc and t_free exactly like malloc, realloc and free. Do not change it.
static void* live[1024];
static int live_count = 0;   // blocks allocated and not yet freed
static int bad_frees = 0;    // frees of pointers that were not live blocks
static int alloc_calls = 0;  // successful allocations and reallocations
static int fail_after = -1;  // -1: never fail; k: k more succeed, then one fails
static size_t last_size = 0; // bytes asked for by the last successful allocation

static bool should_fail() {
    if (fail_after == 0) {
        fail_after = -1;
        return true;
    }
    if (fail_after > 0) fail_after--;
    return false;
}

static int find_live(void* p) {
    for (int i = 0; i < live_count; i++) {
        if (live[i] == p) return i;
    }
    return -1;
}

void* t_malloc(size_t n) {
    if (should_fail() || live_count == 1024) return nullptr;
    void* p = malloc(n == 0 ? 1 : n);
    if (p != nullptr) {
        live[live_count++] = p;
        alloc_calls++;
        last_size = n;
    }
    return p;
}

void t_free(void* p) {
    if (p == nullptr) return;
    int i = find_live(p);
    if (i < 0) {
        bad_frees++;
        return;
    }
    live[i] = live[--live_count];
    free(p);
}

void* t_realloc(void* p, size_t n) {
    if (p == nullptr) return t_malloc(n);
    int i = find_live(p);
    if (i < 0) {
        bad_frees++;
        return nullptr;
    }
    if (should_fail()) return nullptr;
    void* q = realloc(p, n == 0 ? 1 : n);
    if (q != nullptr) {
        live[i] = q;
        alloc_calls++;
        last_size = n;
    }
    return q;
}
// ---- End of the tracker.

struct Log {
    int* items;
    int size;
    int cap;
};

bool log_append(Log* log, int value) {
    if (log->size == log->cap) {
        int new_cap = log->cap == 0 ? 8 : log->cap * 2;
        log->items = (int*)t_realloc(log->items, new_cap * sizeof(int));
        if (log->items == nullptr) return false;
        log->cap = new_cap;
    }
    log->items[log->size] = value;
    log->size++;
    return true;
}
--- solution
#include <cstdlib>

// ---- The tracker. The checks watch your blocks through it: use t_malloc,
// ---- t_realloc and t_free exactly like malloc, realloc and free. Do not change it.
static void* live[1024];
static int live_count = 0;   // blocks allocated and not yet freed
static int bad_frees = 0;    // frees of pointers that were not live blocks
static int alloc_calls = 0;  // successful allocations and reallocations
static int fail_after = -1;  // -1: never fail; k: k more succeed, then one fails
static size_t last_size = 0; // bytes asked for by the last successful allocation

static bool should_fail() {
    if (fail_after == 0) {
        fail_after = -1;
        return true;
    }
    if (fail_after > 0) fail_after--;
    return false;
}

static int find_live(void* p) {
    for (int i = 0; i < live_count; i++) {
        if (live[i] == p) return i;
    }
    return -1;
}

void* t_malloc(size_t n) {
    if (should_fail() || live_count == 1024) return nullptr;
    void* p = malloc(n == 0 ? 1 : n);
    if (p != nullptr) {
        live[live_count++] = p;
        alloc_calls++;
        last_size = n;
    }
    return p;
}

void t_free(void* p) {
    if (p == nullptr) return;
    int i = find_live(p);
    if (i < 0) {
        bad_frees++;
        return;
    }
    live[i] = live[--live_count];
    free(p);
}

void* t_realloc(void* p, size_t n) {
    if (p == nullptr) return t_malloc(n);
    int i = find_live(p);
    if (i < 0) {
        bad_frees++;
        return nullptr;
    }
    if (should_fail()) return nullptr;
    void* q = realloc(p, n == 0 ? 1 : n);
    if (q != nullptr) {
        live[i] = q;
        alloc_calls++;
        last_size = n;
    }
    return q;
}
// ---- End of the tracker.

struct Log {
    int* items;
    int size;
    int cap;
};

bool log_append(Log* log, int value) {
    if (log->size == log->cap) {
        int new_cap = log->cap == 0 ? 8 : log->cap * 2;
        int* bigger = (int*)t_realloc(log->items, new_cap * sizeof(int));
        if (bigger == nullptr) return false;
        log->items = bigger;
        log->cap = new_cap;
    }
    log->items[log->size] = value;
    log->size++;
    return true;
}
--- hint
What does `realloc` return when it fails, and what happens to the old block in that case?
--- hint
On failure `t_realloc` returns null and leaves the old block alone, but the starter has already overwritten `log->items` with that null. Keep the result in a separate pointer, and assign it to `log->items` only when it is not null.
--- check test | Appending works and grows
[] { Log g = {nullptr, 0, 0}; for (int i = 0; i < 20; i++) if (!log_append(&g, i)) return false; bool ok = g.size == 20 && g.cap == 32 && g.items[19] == 19; t_free(g.items); return ok; }()
--- check test | A failed growth keeps every value and the block
[] { int before = live_count; Log g = {nullptr, 0, 0}; for (int i = 0; i < 8; i++) log_append(&g, i * 10); fail_after = 0; bool added = log_append(&g, 80); bool ok = !added && g.items != nullptr && g.size == 8 && g.cap == 8 && g.items[7] == 70 && live_count == before + 1; t_free(g.items); return ok && live_count == before; }()
--- check test | After a failure, the next append works
[] { Log g = {nullptr, 0, 0}; for (int i = 0; i < 8; i++) log_append(&g, i); fail_after = 0; log_append(&g, 100); bool ok = log_append(&g, 200) && g.size == 9 && g.items[8] == 200 && g.items[0] == 0; t_free(g.items); return ok; }()
--- check test | A failed first append leaves an empty log
[] { int before = live_count; Log g = {nullptr, 0, 0}; fail_after = 0; bool added = log_append(&g, 1); return !added && g.items == nullptr && g.size == 0 && g.cap == 0 && live_count == before; }()

+++ practice | A string builder for log lines
--- task
The starter begins with the tracker from the lesson. Build a growable string, the way C programs assemble long log messages:

```cpp fragment
struct Builder {
    char* buf;    // the block, holding a terminated string; nullptr before the first append
    size_t len;   // characters in the string, not counting the terminator
    size_t cap;   // bytes in the block
};
```

Write three functions. No `main`.

- `void sb_init(Builder* b)`: no block yet: `buf` is `nullptr`, `len` and `cap` are 0.
- `bool sb_append(Builder* b, const char* s)`: add `s` to the end. The block must hold `len + strlen(s) + 1` bytes. If it is too small, grow `cap` to 16 if it was 0, then keep doubling it until it is big enough, and resize **once** with `t_realloc`. If that fails, return `false` and leave the builder exactly as it was. Afterwards `buf` always holds a terminated string. Appending `""` to an empty builder must still create a block holding `""`.
- `char* sb_take(Builder* b)`: hand the string to the caller, who then owns it and frees it with `t_free`, and reset the builder to empty (as `sb_init`) without freeing anything. If the builder has no block, return a new block holding `""` (or `nullptr` if that allocation fails).

Appending must be amortised O(1) per character: a check appends 200,000 pieces and counts the reallocations.

For example, appending `"T+5 "`, `"burn "` and `"ok"` and then taking gives `T+5 burn ok`.
--- starter
#include <cstdlib>
#include <cstring>

// ---- The tracker. The checks watch your blocks through it: use t_malloc,
// ---- t_realloc and t_free exactly like malloc, realloc and free. Do not change it.
static void* live[1024];
static int live_count = 0;   // blocks allocated and not yet freed
static int bad_frees = 0;    // frees of pointers that were not live blocks
static int alloc_calls = 0;  // successful allocations and reallocations
static int fail_after = -1;  // -1: never fail; k: k more succeed, then one fails
static size_t last_size = 0; // bytes asked for by the last successful allocation

static bool should_fail() {
    if (fail_after == 0) {
        fail_after = -1;
        return true;
    }
    if (fail_after > 0) fail_after--;
    return false;
}

static int find_live(void* p) {
    for (int i = 0; i < live_count; i++) {
        if (live[i] == p) return i;
    }
    return -1;
}

void* t_malloc(size_t n) {
    if (should_fail() || live_count == 1024) return nullptr;
    void* p = malloc(n == 0 ? 1 : n);
    if (p != nullptr) {
        live[live_count++] = p;
        alloc_calls++;
        last_size = n;
    }
    return p;
}

void t_free(void* p) {
    if (p == nullptr) return;
    int i = find_live(p);
    if (i < 0) {
        bad_frees++;
        return;
    }
    live[i] = live[--live_count];
    free(p);
}

void* t_realloc(void* p, size_t n) {
    if (p == nullptr) return t_malloc(n);
    int i = find_live(p);
    if (i < 0) {
        bad_frees++;
        return nullptr;
    }
    if (should_fail()) return nullptr;
    void* q = realloc(p, n == 0 ? 1 : n);
    if (q != nullptr) {
        live[i] = q;
        alloc_calls++;
        last_size = n;
    }
    return q;
}
// ---- End of the tracker.

struct Builder {
    char* buf;
    size_t len;
    size_t cap;
};

void sb_init(Builder* b) {
}

bool sb_append(Builder* b, const char* s) {
    return false;
}

char* sb_take(Builder* b) {
    return nullptr;
}
--- solution
#include <cstdlib>
#include <cstring>

// ---- The tracker. The checks watch your blocks through it: use t_malloc,
// ---- t_realloc and t_free exactly like malloc, realloc and free. Do not change it.
static void* live[1024];
static int live_count = 0;   // blocks allocated and not yet freed
static int bad_frees = 0;    // frees of pointers that were not live blocks
static int alloc_calls = 0;  // successful allocations and reallocations
static int fail_after = -1;  // -1: never fail; k: k more succeed, then one fails
static size_t last_size = 0; // bytes asked for by the last successful allocation

static bool should_fail() {
    if (fail_after == 0) {
        fail_after = -1;
        return true;
    }
    if (fail_after > 0) fail_after--;
    return false;
}

static int find_live(void* p) {
    for (int i = 0; i < live_count; i++) {
        if (live[i] == p) return i;
    }
    return -1;
}

void* t_malloc(size_t n) {
    if (should_fail() || live_count == 1024) return nullptr;
    void* p = malloc(n == 0 ? 1 : n);
    if (p != nullptr) {
        live[live_count++] = p;
        alloc_calls++;
        last_size = n;
    }
    return p;
}

void t_free(void* p) {
    if (p == nullptr) return;
    int i = find_live(p);
    if (i < 0) {
        bad_frees++;
        return;
    }
    live[i] = live[--live_count];
    free(p);
}

void* t_realloc(void* p, size_t n) {
    if (p == nullptr) return t_malloc(n);
    int i = find_live(p);
    if (i < 0) {
        bad_frees++;
        return nullptr;
    }
    if (should_fail()) return nullptr;
    void* q = realloc(p, n == 0 ? 1 : n);
    if (q != nullptr) {
        live[i] = q;
        alloc_calls++;
        last_size = n;
    }
    return q;
}
// ---- End of the tracker.

struct Builder {
    char* buf;
    size_t len;
    size_t cap;
};

void sb_init(Builder* b) {
    b->buf = nullptr;
    b->len = 0;
    b->cap = 0;
}

bool sb_append(Builder* b, const char* s) {
    size_t add = strlen(s);
    size_t need = b->len + add + 1;
    if (need > b->cap) {
        size_t new_cap = b->cap == 0 ? 16 : b->cap;
        while (new_cap < need) new_cap *= 2;
        char* bigger = (char*)t_realloc(b->buf, new_cap);
        if (bigger == nullptr) return false;
        b->buf = bigger;
        b->cap = new_cap;
    }
    memcpy(b->buf + b->len, s, add + 1);
    b->len += add;
    return true;
}

char* sb_take(Builder* b) {
    char* out = b->buf;
    if (out == nullptr) {
        out = (char*)t_malloc(1);
        if (out == nullptr) return nullptr;
        out[0] = '\0';
    }
    sb_init(b);
    return out;
}
--- hint
Work out `need = len + strlen(s) + 1` first. Only when `need > cap` do you grow: start from 16 (or the old `cap`), double until it is at least `need`, then call `t_realloc` once, keeping the result in a separate pointer.
--- hint
Copy `strlen(s) + 1` bytes with `memcpy` to `buf + len`: that brings the terminator along. Then add the length to `len`.
--- hint
`sb_take` returns the current `buf` and forgets it by calling `sb_init`. Only when `buf` is `nullptr` does it need a new 1-byte block holding the terminator.
--- check test | The example: T+5 burn ok, in one block owned by the caller
[] { int before = live_count; Builder b; sb_init(&b); sb_append(&b, "T+5 "); sb_append(&b, "burn "); sb_append(&b, "ok"); char* s = sb_take(&b); bool ok = std::string(s) == "T+5 burn ok" && live_count == before + 1 && b.buf == nullptr && b.len == 0 && b.cap == 0; t_free(s); return ok && live_count == before; }()
--- check test | The first block is 16 bytes, then it doubles
[] { Builder b; sb_init(&b); sb_append(&b, "abc"); bool a = b.cap == 16 && b.len == 3; sb_append(&b, "0123456789ab"); bool c = b.cap == 16 && b.len == 15; sb_append(&b, "x"); bool d = b.cap == 32 && b.len == 16 && std::string(b.buf) == "abc0123456789abx"; t_free(sb_take(&b)); return a && c && d; }()
--- check test | One long append doubles several times, but reallocates once
[] { int calls = alloc_calls; Builder b; sb_init(&b); char big[100]; for (int i = 0; i < 99; i++) big[i] = 'z'; big[99] = 0; sb_append(&b, big); bool ok = b.cap == 128 && b.len == 99 && alloc_calls == calls + 1 && last_size == 128; t_free(sb_take(&b)); return ok; }()
--- check test | Appending the empty string still makes a string
[] { Builder b; sb_init(&b); bool ok = sb_append(&b, "") && b.buf != nullptr && b.buf[0] == 0 && b.len == 0; char* s = sb_take(&b); ok = ok && s[0] == 0; t_free(s); return ok; }()
--- check test | Taking from a builder with no block gives a new empty string
[] { int before = live_count; Builder b; sb_init(&b); char* s = sb_take(&b); bool ok = s != nullptr && s[0] == 0 && live_count == before + 1; t_free(s); return ok && live_count == before; }()
--- check test | A failed append leaves the builder exactly as it was
[] { int before = live_count; Builder b; sb_init(&b); sb_append(&b, "0123456789"); char* old = b.buf; fail_after = 0; bool added = sb_append(&b, "0123456789"); bool ok = !added && b.buf == old && b.len == 10 && b.cap == 16 && std::string(b.buf) == "0123456789"; t_free(sb_take(&b)); return ok && live_count == before; }()
--- check test | 200,000 appends: about 20 reallocations
[] { int calls = alloc_calls; Builder b; sb_init(&b); for (int i = 0; i < 200000; i++) if (!sb_append(&b, "ab,")) return false; bool ok = b.len == 600000 && b.buf[599999] == ',' && b.buf[600000] == 0 && alloc_calls - calls <= 20; t_free(sb_take(&b)); return ok && bad_frees == 0; }()
?? Growing to exactly the size needed reallocates on every append. Double the capacity.

+++ question | The realloc line
--- ask
Why is `buf = (char*)realloc(buf, bigger);` a bug?
--- choice
`realloc` always moves the block, so the old contents are lost.
--- choice correct
When `realloc` fails it returns a null pointer and leaves the old block alone, but this line has overwritten the only pointer to that block: it leaks, and its data is gone.
--- choice
`realloc` cannot make a block bigger, only smaller.
--- why
Keep the result in a second pointer, and replace `buf` only when it is not null. When `realloc` succeeds it may or may not move the block; either way, only the returned pointer is valid afterwards.

+++ question | Growing by a fixed amount
--- ask
A buffer starts empty and grows by 16 elements every time it is full, copying everything into the new block. What is the total copying for n pushes?
--- choice
Θ(n), the same as doubling.
--- choice correct
Θ(n²): there are about n / 16 growths, and the later ones each copy up to n elements.
--- choice
Θ(n log n).
--- why
The copies move 16 + 32 + 48 + … + n elements, an arithmetic series of about n / 16 terms, which is about n² / 32. Doubling makes the sizes a geometric series, 4 + 8 + … + 2ᵏ < 2n, so the total is Θ(n) and each push is amortised O(1).

+++ question | The overflow check on a toy machine
--- ask
On a toy machine where `SIZE_MAX` is 100, `alloc_zeroed(3, size)` checks `size > SIZE_MAX / 3`. What is the largest `size` that passes the check?
--- answer
33
--- why
100 / 3 is 33 in whole-number division. 3 × 33 = 99 fits, and 3 × 34 = 102 would not: exactly as the proof says, the product fits when size ≤ SIZE_MAX / count, and the check never has to multiply.

=== sys-06 | Writing an allocator: bump, free list, splitting and coalescing
--- teach
Last lesson you used `malloc` and `free` as a black box, and learned the rules: every block freed exactly once, never touched afterwards. This lesson opens the box. You will build an allocator yourself, first the simplest possible one, then a real free-list allocator of the kind that runs inside embedded operating systems. Once you have built one, the rules of the last lesson stop being arbitrary: you will see exactly what a double free breaks.

### What an allocator must do

An allocator is given one big region of memory, from the operating system (through [[calls such as mmap|os-memory]]) or from a fixed array. Its job:

- **hand out blocks** of the sizes asked for, which never overlap each other;
- **align** every block, so any type can be stored in it;
- **take blocks back**, and reuse that memory for later requests;
- do all this quickly, and without wasting much memory.

One thing it can never do is **move** a block that is handed out. The program holds pointers into it, and the allocator cannot find and fix them. That single restriction is why fragmentation, below, is a hard problem.

### The simplest allocator: a bump pointer

A **bump allocator** keeps one offset, `used`, into its region. To allocate `n` bytes, it rounds `used` up to the alignment, returns the address there, and moves `used` on by `n`. Freeing one block does nothing at all. Instead, the whole region is reset at once, when everything in it is finished.

It is the stack allocator from two lessons ago without the marks. Allocation is O(1) and a few instructions, there is no bookkeeping in memory, and nothing ever fragments. It is exactly right when many objects die together: everything built while handling one network request, or one frame of a game, or one pass of a compiler. It is exactly wrong when objects die one by one, because their memory is never reused.

### Headers: how free learns the size

`free(p)` receives only an address. To reuse the block, the allocator must know how big it is. The classic answer: store a small **header** just in front of every block, holding its size and whether it is in use. The address handed to the program, the **payload**, starts right after the header.

In this lesson's allocator the heap is an array of bytes and a header is 8 bytes:

```cpp fragment
struct Header {
    uint32_t size;   // payload bytes after this header, a multiple of 8
    uint32_t used;   // 1 if handed out, 0 if free
};
```

The fields are `uint32_t`, unsigned 32-bit whole numbers from `<cstdint>`, so the header is 8 bytes on every machine, including ORBIT's 32-bit browser build.

Blocks sit back to back and cover the whole heap: header, payload, header, payload, and so on. So from any header you can reach the next one: it starts `8 + size` bytes further on. Walking from offset 0 visits every block, used or free, in address order. This arrangement is called an **implicit free list**, since the free blocks are found by walking past all the others. The [[picture of the heap|heap-picture]] shows it.

Going from a payload back to its header is one step: the header is the 8 bytes just before it.

```cpp fragment
Header* h = (Header*)((unsigned char*)payload - sizeof(Header));
```

### Finding a block: first fit

To allocate, round the request up to a multiple of 8, then walk the blocks from the start and take the **first** free block whose payload is big enough. This is **first fit**. Two other classic policies are **best fit**, which walks every block and takes the smallest one that is big enough, and **next fit**, which starts each search where the last one ended.

### Splitting

The block first fit finds is often much bigger than the request. Handing out all of it would waste the rest. So the allocator **splits** it: the front part becomes the used block, of exactly the size asked for, and a new header is written right after it, turning the rest into a new free block.

Here is a free block with 64 payload bytes, at offset 0, and a request for 20 bytes. Rounded up, that is 24.

| | before | after splitting |
|---|---|---|
| block at offset 0 | free, size 64 | used, size 24 |
| new block at offset 8 + 24 = 32 | (part of the payload) | free, size 64 − 24 − 8 = 32 |

Both before and after, the blocks cover 8 + 64 = 72 bytes: 8 + 24 + 8 + 32 = 72. The new free block is 8 bytes smaller than you might expect, because its own header takes 8 bytes out of what was left.

A split is only worth making if the leftover can be a real block: a header plus at least 8 bytes of payload, 16 bytes in all. If less than that would be left, the allocator hands out the whole block, and the few extra bytes are wasted inside it.

### Freeing, and coalescing

To free a block, find its header and mark it free. But that alone is not enough. Picture four free 16-byte blocks side by side. Together they are 4 × (8 + 16) = 96 bytes of free memory, yet a request for 40 bytes fails, because no single block is big enough. This is **false fragmentation**.

The cure is **coalescing**: when a block is freed, merge it with its neighbours if they are free. Merging a free block with the free block after it adds the second block's header and payload to the first: `size += 8 + next->size`. The second header simply stops being a header, and becomes payload of the first. The same step merges a free previous block with the one just freed.

Finding the **next** block is easy: it is `8 + size` bytes on. Finding the **previous** one is not, because nothing points backwards. The simple way is to walk from the start of the heap, remembering the last block before the freed one. Real allocators avoid that walk with [[boundary tags|boundary-tags]]: a copy of the size at the end of each block as well, so the block before can be found in one step.

### Why the allocator is correct

An allocator is correct if it never hands out overlapping memory, and every payload it hands out is aligned. We prove it by three **invariants**, facts that hold after `init` and after every `alloc` and `free`:

1. **Tiling.** Walking from offset 0, stepping `8 + size` each time, lands exactly on the end of the heap. So the blocks cover the heap with no gaps and no overlaps.
2. **Alignment.** Every `size` is a multiple of 8. Since the heap starts at a multiple of 8 and every step is 8 + a multiple of 8, every header and every payload starts at a multiple of 8.
3. **No two free blocks are next to each other.**

- **After init** there is one free block covering the heap: all three hold.
- **Alloc** either marks one free block used, which changes no sizes, or splits it. A split replaces 8 + S bytes by 8 + need and 8 + (S − need − 8) bytes, which adds up to the same total, so tiling holds. Both new sizes are multiples of 8, because S and need are, and the remainder's size is at least 8, because the allocator only splits when S − need ≥ 16. The new free remainder sits between the block just marked used and the block that followed the original free block. That follower was not free, by invariant 3, since the original block was free. So the remainder has no free neighbour.
- **Free** marks one block free, which can only break invariant 3 between that block and its two neighbours. Coalescing merges exactly those. A merge keeps the total bytes the same, so tiling holds, and the merged size, size₁ + 8 + size₂, is still a multiple of 8. One merge on each side is enough: before the free, invariant 3 held, so a free previous block has no free block before it, and a free next block has no free block after it.

And no two used blocks overlap, because by tiling no two blocks of any kind overlap.

### What it costs

- **Bump allocator:** O(1) for every operation, and no memory spent on headers.
- **This free-list allocator:** allocation walks the blocks, O(B) for B blocks. Freeing merges with the next block in O(1), but finding the previous block by walking from the start is O(B). Boundary tags make freeing O(1).
- **Real allocators** such as [[dlmalloc|dlmalloc]] keep separate lists of free blocks by size, called **segregated free lists**, so most requests are found in O(1) without walking past used blocks at all.

Space is lost in two ways. **Internal fragmentation** is waste inside blocks: headers, rounding up to 8, and leftovers too small to split off. **External fragmentation** is free memory broken into pieces too small for the requests that arrive. No policy avoids it for every sequence of requests, and it is one reason safety-critical software avoids general heaps altogether.

### Where it is used

- **Embedded systems:** the FreeRTOS operating system offers several heaps. `heap_1` is a bump allocator that never frees, and `heap_4` is first fit with coalescing, the design of this lesson. They run on satellites, drones and medical devices.
- **The browser build of this course:** C++ compiled to WebAssembly usually gets its `malloc` from dlmalloc, an allocator built on exactly these headers, splitting and coalescing.
- **Operating system kernels** use a **buddy allocator** for pages of memory, and pools of fixed-size blocks (slab allocators) for their own small objects. You will build both kinds in the practice.

**Watch out:** header arithmetic. The three classic bugs are all an 8 in the wrong place: stepping to the next block by `size` instead of `8 + size`; giving the split-off remainder `S − need` bytes instead of `S − need − 8`; and returning the header's address instead of the payload's. Each one quietly makes two blocks overlap, so the program corrupts its own data long before anything crashes. When you write an allocator, write a checker that walks the heap and tests the invariants too, and run it after every operation while testing.

::: context os-memory Where the heap comes from
On Linux, an allocator gets memory from the kernel in large pieces. The old way is `brk`/`sbrk`, which moves the end of the heap region upwards. The modern way is `mmap`, which maps fresh pages anywhere in the address space; glibc uses it directly for very large requests, of 128 KB or more by default. Either way the kernel hands out whole pages of 4 KB, and the allocator carves them into the small blocks programs ask for. On a microcontroller with no operating system, the "region" is simply a big array.
:::

::: context heap-picture An implicit free list
Each block is a header followed by its payload. Headers are drawn dark; the numbers are payload sizes. Following `8 + size` from each header reaches the next one, so a walk from the left visits every block, used or free, until it reaches the end of the heap.

```svg

  <rect x="22" y="40" width="60" height="36" fill="#f2b880" stroke="#1f2a44"/>

  <rect x="94" y="40" width="90" height="36" fill="#c9e3c0" stroke="#1f2a44"/>
  <text x="139" y="63" font-size="11" text-anchor="middle" fill="#1f2a44">free 40</text>

  <rect x="240" y="40" width="12" height="36" fill="#1f2a44"/>

```
:::

::: context boundary-tags Knuth's boundary tags
Donald Knuth described boundary tags in 1973, in The Art of Computer Programming. Each block stores its size at both ends: in the header and in a footer. When a block is freed, the footer of the block just before it is the 8 bytes right in front of its header, so the previous block's size and state can be read in one step, with no walk. A common refinement drops the footer from used blocks and keeps one bit in the next header saying whether the previous block is free.
:::

::: context dlmalloc Doug Lea's malloc
Doug Lea wrote dlmalloc in 1987, and versions of it were the standard allocator in the GNU C library for years; glibc's current malloc, ptmalloc, grew from it. It uses boundary tags, bins of free blocks sorted by size, splitting and coalescing, and falls back to `mmap` for huge requests. It is small and portable, which is why WebAssembly toolchains such as Emscripten and the WASI C library still ship it today.
:::
--- task
Write a first-fit allocator with splitting and coalescing over a 4,096-byte heap. The starter declares:

```cpp fragment
const uint32_t HEAP_SIZE = 4096;
alignas(8) static unsigned char heap[HEAP_SIZE];

struct Header {
    uint32_t size;   // payload bytes after this header, a multiple of 8
    uint32_t used;   // 1 if handed out, 0 if free
};
```

Every block is an 8-byte header followed by `size` payload bytes, and the blocks lie back to back from offset 0 to the end of the heap. Write three functions. No `main`.

- `void heap_init()`: make the heap one free block, with its header at offset 0 and `size` 4096 − 8 = 4088.
- `void* heap_alloc(size_t n)`: if `n` is 0, or larger than the heap, return `nullptr`. Otherwise round `n` up to a multiple of 8, and find the **first** free block, walking from offset 0, whose `size` is at least that. If there is none, return `nullptr`. If the block's size minus the rounded request is at least 16, **split** it: the block keeps exactly the rounded size, and a new free block starts right after it with the rest minus 8 bytes for its header. Otherwise hand out the whole block. Mark the block used and return the address of its **payload**.
- `void heap_free(void* p)`: `nullptr` does nothing. Otherwise mark the block whose payload is at `p` free, then **coalesce**: merge it with the next block if that one is free, and with the previous block if that one is free.

For example, after `heap_init`, `heap_alloc(10)` returns `heap + 8`, and the heap is then a used block of size 16 at offset 0 followed by a free block of size 4064 at offset 24.
--- starter
#include <cstddef>
#include <cstdint>
#include <cstring>

const uint32_t HEAP_SIZE = 4096;
alignas(8) static unsigned char heap[HEAP_SIZE];

struct Header {
    uint32_t size;
    uint32_t used;
};

void heap_init() {
}

void* heap_alloc(size_t n) {
    return nullptr;
}

void heap_free(void* p) {
}
--- solution
#include <cstddef>
#include <cstdint>
#include <cstring>

const uint32_t HEAP_SIZE = 4096;
alignas(8) static unsigned char heap[HEAP_SIZE];

struct Header {
    uint32_t size;
    uint32_t used;
};

static Header* header_at(uint32_t offset) {
    return (Header*)(heap + offset);
}

void heap_init() {
    Header* h = header_at(0);
    h->size = HEAP_SIZE - sizeof(Header);
    h->used = 0;
}

void* heap_alloc(size_t n) {
    if (n == 0 || n > HEAP_SIZE) return nullptr;
    uint32_t need = (uint32_t)((n + 7) / 8 * 8);
    for (uint32_t off = 0; off < HEAP_SIZE; off += sizeof(Header) + header_at(off)->size) {
        Header* h = header_at(off);
        if (h->used || h->size < need) continue;
        if (h->size - need >= 16) {
            Header* rest = header_at(off + sizeof(Header) + need);
            rest->size = h->size - need - sizeof(Header);
            rest->used = 0;
            h->size = need;
        }
        h->used = 1;
        return heap + off + sizeof(Header);
    }
    return nullptr;
}

void heap_free(void* p) {
    if (p == nullptr) return;
    uint32_t off = (uint32_t)((unsigned char*)p - heap) - sizeof(Header);
    Header* h = header_at(off);
    h->used = 0;
    uint32_t next = off + sizeof(Header) + h->size;
    if (next < HEAP_SIZE && !header_at(next)->used) {
        h->size += sizeof(Header) + header_at(next)->size;
    }
    uint32_t prev = 0;
    bool found = false;
    for (uint32_t at = 0; at < off; at += sizeof(Header) + header_at(at)->size) {
        prev = at;
        found = true;
    }
    if (found && !header_at(prev)->used) {
        header_at(prev)->size += sizeof(Header) + h->size;
    }
}
--- hint
Write a small helper that turns an offset into a `Header*`: `(Header*)(heap + offset)`. The walk in `heap_alloc` starts at offset 0 and steps by `8 + size` until it reaches `HEAP_SIZE`.
--- hint
When splitting a free block of size S at offset `off` for a rounded request `need`: the new header goes at `off + 8 + need`, and gets size `S - need - 8`. The payload you return is at `heap + off + 8`.
--- hint
In `heap_free`, the header's offset is `p - heap - 8`. Merge with the next block first (it starts at `off + 8 + size`, if that is before the end). Then walk from 0 to find the last block that starts before `off`; if it is free, add `8 + size` of the freed block to it.
--- check test | heap_init makes one free block of 4088
[] { heap_init(); Header* h = (Header*)heap; return h->size == 4088 && h->used == 0; }()

"U" : "F") + std::to_string(h->size) + " "; off += 8 + h->size; } return o; }; return p == heap + 8 && lay() == "U16 F4064 "; }()

?? Walk the heap after each step: blocks must tile it exactly, sizes must be multiples of 8, and no two free blocks may touch.

+++ practice | A bump allocator with alignment
--- task
Write a bump allocator over memory the caller provides. The starter declares:

```cpp fragment
struct Arena {
    unsigned char* base;   // start of the memory
    size_t cap;            // its size in bytes
    size_t used;           // bytes used from the start, including padding
};
```

Write three functions. No `main`.

- `void arena_init(Arena* a, void* mem, size_t cap)`: use the `cap` bytes at `mem`, none used yet.
- `void* arena_alloc(Arena* a, size_t n, size_t align)`: `align` is a power of two (1, 2, 4, 8, 16, …). Return an address inside the arena, at or after `base + used`, that is the smallest such address that is a **multiple of `align`**, and move `used` to just past the `n` bytes that start there. If `n` is 0, or the padding plus `n` bytes do not fit in what is left, return `nullptr` and change nothing.
- `void arena_reset(Arena* a)`: everything is free again.

The address must be aligned, not the offset: `base` itself may not be a multiple of `align`. Convert the address to `uintptr_t` (from `<cstdint>`) to do arithmetic on it, and round up with `(x + align - 1) / align * align`.
--- starter
#include <cstddef>
#include <cstdint>

struct Arena {
    unsigned char* base;
    size_t cap;
    size_t used;
};

void arena_init(Arena* a, void* mem, size_t cap) {
    a->base = (unsigned char*)mem;
    a->cap = cap;
    a->used = 0;
}

void* arena_alloc(Arena* a, size_t n, size_t align) {
    void* p = a->base + a->used;
    a->used += n;
    return p;
}

void arena_reset(Arena* a) {
}
--- solution
#include <cstddef>
#include <cstdint>

struct Arena {
    unsigned char* base;
    size_t cap;
    size_t used;
};

void arena_init(Arena* a, void* mem, size_t cap) {
    a->base = (unsigned char*)mem;
    a->cap = cap;
    a->used = 0;
}

void* arena_alloc(Arena* a, size_t n, size_t align) {
    if (n == 0) return nullptr;
    uintptr_t here = (uintptr_t)(a->base + a->used);
    uintptr_t aligned = (here + align - 1) / align * align;
    size_t pad = aligned - here;
    size_t left = a->cap - a->used;
    if (pad > left || n > left - pad) return nullptr;
    a->used += pad + n;
    return a->base + (a->used - n);
}

void arena_reset(Arena* a) {
    a->used = 0;
}
--- hint
Compute `here`, the address of the first unused byte, as a `uintptr_t`. Round it up to a multiple of `align`; the difference is the padding to skip.
--- hint
It fits when the padding and `n` together are at most `cap - used`. Test `pad > left || n > left - pad` so that nothing can overflow. Then move `used` on by `pad + n`.
--- check test | Aligned requests from an aligned base follow each other
[] { alignas(16) static unsigned char mem[64]; Arena a; arena_init(&a, mem, 64); unsigned char* p = (unsigned char*)arena_alloc(&a, 5, 1); unsigned char* q = (unsigned char*)arena_alloc(&a, 8, 8); unsigned char* r = (unsigned char*)arena_alloc(&a, 2, 2); return p == mem && q == mem + 8 && r == mem + 16 && a.used == 18; }()
--- check test | A misaligned base: the address is aligned, not the offset
[] { alignas(16) static unsigned char mem[80]; Arena a; arena_init(&a, mem + 3, 64); unsigned char* p = (unsigned char*)arena_alloc(&a, 4, 16); return p == mem + 16 && a.used == 17 && ((uintptr_t)p % 16) == 0; }()
--- check test | Every alignment from 1 to 32
[] { alignas(32) static unsigned char mem[512]; Arena a; arena_init(&a, mem + 1, 500); for (size_t al = 1; al <= 32; al *= 2) { void* p = arena_alloc(&a, 3, al); if (p == nullptr || (uintptr_t)p % al != 0) return false; } return true; }()
--- check test | Exactly full works; one byte more does not, and changes nothing
[] { alignas(16) static unsigned char mem[32]; Arena a; arena_init(&a, mem, 32); void* p = arena_alloc(&a, 30, 1); void* q = arena_alloc(&a, 2, 1); size_t u = a.used; void* r = arena_alloc(&a, 1, 1); return p && q && r == nullptr && u == 32 && a.used == 32; }()
--- check test | Padding that does not fit returns nullptr and changes nothing
[] { alignas(16) static unsigned char mem[32]; Arena a; arena_init(&a, mem, 20); arena_alloc(&a, 1, 1); void* p = arena_alloc(&a, 8, 16); return p == nullptr && a.used == 1; }()
--- check test | Zero bytes, and huge sizes, return nullptr
[] { alignas(16) static unsigned char mem[32]; Arena a; arena_init(&a, mem, 32); arena_alloc(&a, 4, 4); return arena_alloc(&a, 0, 4) == nullptr && arena_alloc(&a, (size_t)-1, 1) == nullptr && arena_alloc(&a, (size_t)-8, 8) == nullptr && a.used == 4; }()
--- check test | arena_reset frees everything
[] { alignas(16) static unsigned char mem[32]; Arena a; arena_init(&a, mem, 32); arena_alloc(&a, 32, 1); arena_reset(&a); void* p = arena_alloc(&a, 16, 16); return a.used == 16 && p == mem; }()

+++ practice | A pool of fixed-size blocks
--- task
Flight software and kernels often replace a general heap with a **pool**: a region cut into blocks that are all the same size, so allocation can never fragment. The free blocks are kept in a linked list, and the links are stored **inside the free blocks themselves**: the first bytes of each free block hold a pointer to the next free block. No extra memory is needed for the list. The starter declares:

```cpp fragment
struct Pool {
    void* free_list;   // the first free block, or nullptr when none is left
    int free_count;    // how many blocks are free
};
```

Write three functions. No `main`.

- `void pool_init(Pool* p, void* mem, size_t block, int count)`: the region at `mem` holds `count` blocks of `block` bytes each, back to back. Link them all into the free list so that block 0 is first, then block 1, and so on, with the last block's link `nullptr`. (The checks pass a `block` that is a multiple of 8, so a pointer always fits and is aligned.)
- `void* pool_alloc(Pool* p)`: take the first block off the list and return it, or `nullptr` if the list is empty.
- `void pool_free(Pool* p, void* b)`: put block `b` back at the **front** of the list. `nullptr` does nothing.

Both `pool_alloc` and `pool_free` must be O(1). To read or write the link in a block `b`, treat `b` as a pointer to a pointer: `*(void**)b`.
--- starter
#include <cstddef>

struct Pool {
    void* free_list;
    int free_count;
};

void pool_init(Pool* p, void* mem, size_t block, int count) {
    p->free_list = nullptr;
    p->free_count = 0;
}

void* pool_alloc(Pool* p) {
    return nullptr;
}

void pool_free(Pool* p, void* b) {
}
--- solution
#include <cstddef>

struct Pool {
    void* free_list;
    int free_count;
};

void pool_init(Pool* p, void* mem, size_t block, int count) {
    unsigned char* base = (unsigned char*)mem;
    p->free_list = nullptr;
    for (int i = count - 1; i >= 0; i--) {
        void* b = base + (size_t)i * block;
        *(void**)b = p->free_list;
        p->free_list = b;
    }
    p->free_count = count;
}

void* pool_alloc(Pool* p) {
    void* b = p->free_list;
    if (b == nullptr) return nullptr;
    p->free_list = *(void**)b;
    p->free_count--;
    return b;
}

void pool_free(Pool* p, void* b) {
    if (b == nullptr) return;
    *(void**)b = p->free_list;
    p->free_list = b;
    p->free_count++;
}
--- hint
Pushing a block `b` onto the front of the list is two steps: store the current head in `b`'s link, `*(void**)b = p->free_list;`, then make `b` the head.
--- hint
To end up with block 0 at the front, push the blocks in reverse order, from the last one down to block 0. Block `i` starts at `base + i * block`.
--- hint
`pool_alloc` pops: remember the head, replace the head by the link stored inside it, and return the remembered block.
--- check test | Blocks come out in order 0, 1, 2, …
[] { alignas(16) static unsigned char mem[4 * 32]; Pool p; pool_init(&p, mem, 32, 4); void* a = pool_alloc(&p); void* b = pool_alloc(&p); void* c = pool_alloc(&p); void* d = pool_alloc(&p); return a == mem && b == mem + 32 && c == mem + 64 && d == mem + 96 && p.free_count == 0; }()
--- check test | An empty pool returns nullptr
[] { alignas(16) static unsigned char mem[2 * 16]; Pool p; pool_init(&p, mem, 16, 2); pool_alloc(&p); pool_alloc(&p); return pool_alloc(&p) == nullptr && p.free_count == 0; }()
--- check test | A freed block is the next one handed out
[] { alignas(16) static unsigned char mem[4 * 16]; Pool p; pool_init(&p, mem, 16, 4); void* a = pool_alloc(&p); void* b = pool_alloc(&p); pool_free(&p, a); void* c = pool_alloc(&p); return c == a && b == mem + 16 && p.free_count == 2; }()
--- check test | Freeing in any order, then taking all back, gives every block once
[] { alignas(16) static unsigned char mem[8 * 24]; Pool p; pool_init(&p, mem, 24, 8); void* b[8]; for (int i = 0; i < 8; i++) b[i] = pool_alloc(&p); int order[8] = {5, 2, 7, 0, 3, 6, 1, 4}; for (int i = 0; i < 8; i++) pool_free(&p, b[order[i]]); bool seen[8] = {}; for (int i = 0; i < 8; i++) { unsigned char* q = (unsigned char*)pool_alloc(&p); if (q == nullptr) return false; int k = (q - mem) / 24; if (seen[k]) return false; seen[k] = true; } return pool_alloc(&p) == nullptr; }()
--- check test | A pool of zero blocks is empty
[] { alignas(16) static unsigned char mem[16]; Pool p; pool_init(&p, mem, 16, 0); return pool_alloc(&p) == nullptr && p.free_count == 0; }()
--- check test | pool_free(nullptr) does nothing
[] { alignas(16) static unsigned char mem[2 * 16]; Pool p; pool_init(&p, mem, 16, 2); pool_free(&p, nullptr); return p.free_count == 2 && pool_alloc(&p) == mem; }()
--- check test | Users may fill whole blocks while they hold them
[] { alignas(16) static unsigned char mem[3 * 32]; Pool p; pool_init(&p, mem, 32, 3); unsigned char* a = (unsigned char*)pool_alloc(&p); unsigned char* b = (unsigned char*)pool_alloc(&p); for (int i = 0; i < 32; i++) { a[i] = 0xAA; b[i] = 0xBB; } pool_free(&p, a); unsigned char* c = (unsigned char*)pool_alloc(&p); unsigned char* d = (unsigned char*)pool_alloc(&p); return c == a && d == mem + 64 && b[31] == 0xBB && pool_alloc(&p) == nullptr; }()

+++ practice | A heap checker
--- task
An allocator is only as good as its invariants, so real allocators come with a checker that walks the heap and tests them. Write one for the heap layout of the lesson. The starter declares the same header:

```cpp fragment
struct Header {
    uint32_t size;   // payload bytes after this header
    uint32_t used;   // 1 if handed out, 0 if free
};
```

Write `bool heap_ok(const unsigned char* h, uint32_t total)`. The heap is the `total` bytes starting at `h` (a multiple of 8, and at least 8), with the first header at offset 0. Return `true` only if **all** of these hold, walking from offset 0 and stepping `8 + size` each time:

1. every header lies wholly inside the heap (its 8 bytes end at or before `total`);
2. every `size` is a multiple of 8 and at least 8;
3. every `used` is 0 or 1;
4. the walk ends **exactly** at `total`: the last block does not run past the end;
5. no two free blocks are next to each other.

Read each header with `memcpy` into a local `Header`, so the checker works whatever the bytes are. No `main`.
--- starter
#include <cstdint>
#include <cstring>

struct Header {
    uint32_t size;
    uint32_t used;
};

bool heap_ok(const unsigned char* h, uint32_t total) {
    return true;
}
--- solution
#include <cstdint>
#include <cstring>

struct Header {
    uint32_t size;
    uint32_t used;
};

bool heap_ok(const unsigned char* h, uint32_t total) {
    uint32_t off = 0;
    bool prev_free = false;
    while (off < total) {
        if (total - off < sizeof(Header)) return false;
        Header hd;
        memcpy(&hd, h + off, sizeof hd);
        if (hd.size % 8 != 0 || hd.size < 8) return false;
        if (hd.used > 1) return false;
        if (hd.size > total - off - sizeof(Header)) return false;
        if (!hd.used && prev_free) return false;
        prev_free = !hd.used;
        off += sizeof(Header) + hd.size;
    }
    return off == total;
}
--- hint
Walk with an offset starting at 0 while it is less than `total`. Before reading a header, make sure 8 bytes are left; then copy it out with `memcpy(&hd, h + off, sizeof hd)`.
--- hint
Before stepping on, make sure the payload fits: `size` must be at most `total - off - 8`. Written that way round, nothing can overflow even for a huge `size`.
--- hint
Keep a flag saying whether the previous block was free. A free block when the flag is set breaks rule 5.
--- check test | One free block covering the heap is fine
[] { alignas(8) unsigned char h[64] = {}; Header a = {56, 0}; memcpy(h, &a, 8); return heap_ok(h, 64); }()
--- check test | Used, free, used is fine
[] { alignas(8) unsigned char h[64] = {}; Header a = {8, 1}, b = {16, 0}, c = {16, 1}; memcpy(h, &a, 8); memcpy(h + 16, &b, 8); memcpy(h + 40, &c, 8); return heap_ok(h, 64); }()
--- check test | Two free blocks side by side are not
[] { alignas(8) unsigned char h[64] = {}; Header a = {8, 1}, b = {8, 0}, c = {24, 0}; memcpy(h, &a, 8); memcpy(h + 16, &b, 8); memcpy(h + 32, &c, 8); return !heap_ok(h, 64); }()
--- check test | A last block running past the end is not
[] { alignas(8) unsigned char h[64] = {}; Header a = {8, 1}, b = {48, 0}; memcpy(h, &a, 8); memcpy(h + 16, &b, 8); return !heap_ok(h, 64); }()
--- check test | A size that is not a multiple of 8, or is 0, is not
[] { alignas(8) unsigned char h[32] = {}; Header a = {12, 1}, b = {12, 1}; memcpy(h, &a, 8); memcpy(h + 20, &b, 4); bool x = !heap_ok(h, 32); Header z = {0, 1}; memcpy(h, &z, 8); return x && !heap_ok(h, 32); }()
--- check test | A used field other than 0 or 1 is not
[] { alignas(8) unsigned char h[32] = {}; Header a = {24, 7}; memcpy(h, &a, 8); return !heap_ok(h, 32); }()
--- check test | A huge size does not wrap round the walk
[] { alignas(8) unsigned char h[32] = {}; Header a = {8, 1}, b = {4294967288u, 1}; memcpy(h, &a, 8); memcpy(h + 16, &b, 8); return !heap_ok(h, 32); }()
--- check test | A heap of exactly one header and 8 bytes
[] { alignas(8) unsigned char h[16] = {}; Header a = {8, 1}; memcpy(h, &a, 8); return heap_ok(h, 16); }()

+++ practice | Free extents on a disk
--- task
File systems and operating systems often track free space as a sorted list of **extents**: runs of free units, each with a start and a length. The starter declares:

```cpp fragment
struct Extent {
    int start;
    int len;
};

struct FreeList {
    Extent ext[32];   // sorted by start; no two overlap or touch
    int count;
};
```

Write three functions. No `main`.

- `void fl_init(FreeList* f, int total)`: one free extent `{0, total}`. If `total` is 0 or less, the list is empty.
- `int fl_alloc(FreeList* f, int len)`: first fit. Find the extent with the smallest start whose length is at least `len`, take `len` units from its **front**, and return where they start. If that uses the whole extent, remove it from the list, keeping the rest in order. If `len` is 0 or less, or no extent is long enough, return -1 and change nothing.
- `void fl_free(FreeList* f, int start, int len)`: give back the run of `len` units at `start` (always a run that is currently allocated, so it overlaps nothing free). Insert it in order, then merge it with the extent just before it if that one ends exactly at `start`, and with the extent just after it if that one starts exactly at `start + len`. The list must stay sorted, with no two extents touching.

For example, from `{0, 100}`, allocating 10, 20 and 30 returns 0, 10 and 30, leaving `{60, 40}`. Freeing the run at 10 of length 20 gives `{10, 20}, {60, 40}`. Freeing the run at 30 of length 30 then merges everything after 10 into `{10, 90}`.
--- starter
struct Extent {
    int start;
    int len;
};

struct FreeList {
    Extent ext[32];
    int count;
};

void fl_init(FreeList* f, int total) {
    f->count = 0;
}

int fl_alloc(FreeList* f, int len) {
    return -1;
}

void fl_free(FreeList* f, int start, int len) {
}
--- solution
struct Extent {
    int start;
    int len;
};

struct FreeList {
    Extent ext[32];
    int count;
};

void fl_init(FreeList* f, int total) {
    f->count = 0;
    if (total > 0) {
        f->ext[0].start = 0;
        f->ext[0].len = total;
        f->count = 1;
    }
}

static void remove_at(FreeList* f, int i) {
    for (int j = i; j + 1 < f->count; j++) f->ext[j] = f->ext[j + 1];
    f->count--;
}

int fl_alloc(FreeList* f, int len) {
    if (len <= 0) return -1;
    for (int i = 0; i < f->count; i++) {
        Extent* e = &f->ext[i];
        if (e->len < len) continue;
        int at = e->start;
        e->start += len;
        e->len -= len;
        if (e->len == 0) remove_at(f, i);
        return at;
    }
    return -1;
}

void fl_free(FreeList* f, int start, int len) {
    int i = 0;
    while (i < f->count && f->ext[i].start < start) i++;
    for (int j = f->count; j > i; j--) f->ext[j] = f->ext[j - 1];
    f->ext[i].start = start;
    f->ext[i].len = len;
    f->count++;
    if (i + 1 < f->count && start + len == f->ext[i + 1].start) {
        f->ext[i].len += f->ext[i + 1].len;
        remove_at(f, i + 1);
    }
    if (i > 0 && f->ext[i - 1].start + f->ext[i - 1].len == start) {
        f->ext[i - 1].len += f->ext[i].len;
        remove_at(f, i);
    }
}
--- hint
`fl_alloc` walks the extents in order and takes from the first one that is long enough: move its `start` on by `len` and shrink its `len`. An extent left with length 0 must be removed by shifting the later ones down.
--- hint
`fl_free` first finds the position `i` of the first extent that starts after `start`, shifts everything from `i` on up by one place, and writes the new extent at `i`.
--- hint
Then merge: with the next extent if `start + len` equals its start, and with the previous one if its start plus its length equals `start`. Merge with the next one first, so the index `i` is still right when you look at the previous one.
--- check test | The example from the task
[] { FreeList f; fl_init(&f, 100); int a = fl_alloc(&f, 10), b = fl_alloc(&f, 20), c = fl_alloc(&f, 30); bool s1 = a == 0 && b == 10 && c == 30 && f.count == 1 && f.ext[0].start == 60 && f.ext[0].len == 40; fl_free(&f, 10, 20); bool s2 = f.count == 2 && f.ext[0].start == 10 && f.ext[0].len == 20 && f.ext[1].start == 60; fl_free(&f, 30, 30); return s1 && s2 && f.count == 1 && f.ext[0].start == 10 && f.ext[0].len == 90; }()
--- check test | An exact fit removes the extent
[] { FreeList f; fl_init(&f, 50); int a = fl_alloc(&f, 50); return a == 0 && f.count == 0 && fl_alloc(&f, 1) == -1; }()
--- check test | First fit skips extents that are too short
[] { FreeList f; fl_init(&f, 100); fl_alloc(&f, 10); fl_alloc(&f, 5); fl_alloc(&f, 30); fl_free(&f, 0, 10); int a = fl_alloc(&f, 20); int b = fl_alloc(&f, 10); return a == 45 && b == 0 && f.count == 1 && f.ext[0].start == 65 && f.ext[0].len == 35; }()
--- check test | Zero, negative and too-long requests return -1 and change nothing
[] { FreeList f; fl_init(&f, 40); bool ok = fl_alloc(&f, 0) == -1 && fl_alloc(&f, -5) == -1 && fl_alloc(&f, 41) == -1; return ok && f.count == 1 && f.ext[0].start == 0 && f.ext[0].len == 40; }()
--- check test | An empty list, and freeing into it
[] { FreeList f; fl_init(&f, 0); bool a = f.count == 0 && fl_alloc(&f, 1) == -1; fl_free(&f, 7, 3); return a && f.count == 1 && f.ext[0].start == 7 && f.ext[0].len == 3; }()
--- check test | Freeing in the middle merges on both sides
[] { FreeList f; fl_init(&f, 30); fl_alloc(&f, 10); fl_alloc(&f, 10); fl_alloc(&f, 10); fl_free(&f, 0, 10); fl_free(&f, 20, 10); bool two = f.count == 2; fl_free(&f, 10, 10); return two && f.count == 1 && f.ext[0].start == 0 && f.ext[0].len == 30; }()
--- check test | A freed run that touches nothing stays separate, in order
[] { FreeList f; fl_init(&f, 100); for (int i = 0; i < 10; i++) fl_alloc(&f, 10); fl_free(&f, 70, 10); fl_free(&f, 10, 10); fl_free(&f, 40, 10); return f.count == 3 && f.ext[0].start == 10 && f.ext[1].start == 40 && f.ext[2].start == 70 && f.ext[2].len == 10; }()
--- check test | Freeing next to the start and the end of the space
[] { FreeList f; fl_init(&f, 60); fl_alloc(&f, 60); fl_free(&f, 50, 10); fl_free(&f, 0, 10); bool a = f.count == 2 && f.ext[0].start == 0 && f.ext[1].start == 50; fl_free(&f, 40, 10); return a && f.count == 2 && f.ext[1].start == 40 && f.ext[1].len == 20; }()

+++ practice | Debug: best fit that picks the worst
--- task
A best-fit allocator takes the **smallest** free block that is big enough, to leave large blocks for large requests. The starter uses the lesson's header layout, and `find_best(h, total, need)` should walk the heap of `total` bytes at `h` and return the header of the smallest free block whose `size` is at least `need`, or `nullptr` if there is none. When several are equally small, it should return the first of them.

It returns the **largest** fitting block instead. Find the bug and fix it. No `main`.
--- starter
#include <cstdint>

struct Header {
    uint32_t size;
    uint32_t used;
};

Header* find_best(unsigned char* h, uint32_t total, uint32_t need) {
    Header* best = nullptr;
    uint32_t off = 0;
    while (off < total) {
        Header* b = (Header*)(h + off);
        if (!b->used && b->size >= need && (best == nullptr || b->size > best->size)) best = b;
        off += 8 + b->size;
    }
    return best;
}
--- solution
#include <cstdint>

struct Header {
    uint32_t size;
    uint32_t used;
};

Header* find_best(unsigned char* h, uint32_t total, uint32_t need) {
    Header* best = nullptr;
    uint32_t off = 0;
    while (off < total) {
        Header* b = (Header*)(h + off);
        if (!b->used && b->size >= need && (best == nullptr || b->size < best->size)) best = b;
        off += 8 + b->size;
    }
    return best;
}
--- hint
Read the condition that decides when a block replaces `best`. Which blocks does it prefer?
--- hint
A block should replace the best so far only when it is strictly **smaller**. Strictly, so the first of equal blocks is kept.
--- check test | Picks the smallest fitting block
[] { alignas(8) static unsigned char h[128]; Header* a = (Header*)h; a->size = 48; a->used = 0; Header* b = (Header*)(h + 56); b->size = 8; b->used = 1; Header* c = (Header*)(h + 72); c->size = 16; c->used = 0; Header* d = (Header*)(h + 96); d->size = 24; d->used = 1; return find_best(h, 128, 16) == c; }()
--- check test | Too-small free blocks are skipped
[] { alignas(8) static unsigned char h[96]; Header* a = (Header*)h; a->size = 8; a->used = 0; Header* b = (Header*)(h + 16); b->size = 16; b->used = 1; Header* c = (Header*)(h + 40); c->size = 48; c->used = 0; return find_best(h, 96, 16) == c; }()
--- check test | Ties go to the first
[] { alignas(8) static unsigned char h[96]; Header* a = (Header*)h; a->size = 16; a->used = 0; Header* b = (Header*)(h + 24); b->size = 8; b->used = 1; Header* c = (Header*)(h + 40); c->size = 16; c->used = 0; Header* d = (Header*)(h + 64); d->size = 24; d->used = 0; return find_best(h, 96, 10) == a; }()
--- check test | Nothing fits: nullptr
[] { alignas(8) static unsigned char h[64]; Header* a = (Header*)h; a->size = 24; a->used = 0; Header* b = (Header*)(h + 32); b->size = 24; b->used = 1; return find_best(h, 64, 32) == nullptr; }()
--- check test | An exact fit wins over a bigger block before it
[] { alignas(8) static unsigned char h[80]; Header* a = (Header*)h; a->size = 32; a->used = 0; Header* b = (Header*)(h + 40); b->size = 8; b->used = 1; Header* c = (Header*)(h + 56); c->size = 16; c->used = 0; return find_best(h, 80, 16) == c; }()

+++ practice | A buddy allocator
--- task
Operating systems hand out pages with a **buddy allocator**. Every block is a power of two in size, here 32 bytes times 2 to the power `k`, where `k` is the block's **order**, from 0 (32 bytes) to 5 (1,024 bytes, the whole region). A block of order `k` at offset `x` splits into two halves of order `k - 1`, at `x` and `x + size / 2`, which are each other's **buddies**. A block's buddy is always at offset `x ^ size` (bitwise XOR), because splitting only ever flips the one bit that is worth `size`.

The starter declares:

```cpp fragment
struct Buddy {
    bool free_at[6][32];     // free_at[k][i]: the order-k block at offset i * (32 << k) is free
    signed char order_of[32]; // for an allocated block at offset 32 * j: its order; -1 if none starts there
};
```

`32 << k` is 32 times 2 to the power `k`. Write three functions, returning offsets from the start of the region. No `main`.

- `void buddy_init(Buddy* b)`: one free block of order 5 at offset 0, nothing allocated.
- `int buddy_alloc(Buddy* b, int n)`: if `n` is less than 1 or more than 1,024, return -1. Otherwise let `k` be the smallest order with `32 << k` at least `n`. Find the smallest order `j` at least `k` that has any free block, and take its free block with the **lowest offset** (return -1 if there is none at all). While `j > k`, split it: the right half becomes a free block of order `j - 1`, and you keep splitting the left half. Record the order of the block you hand out, and return its offset.
- `void buddy_free(Buddy* b, int offset)`: if no allocated block starts at `offset` (including an offset that is out of range or not a multiple of 32), do nothing. Otherwise the block becomes free, and then **merges**: while its order is below 5 and its buddy is free at the same order, remove both and replace them by their parent, one order up, at the lower of the two offsets.

For example, after `buddy_init`, `buddy_alloc(100)` needs order 2 (128 bytes): the 1,024 block splits into two 512s, the left 512 into two 256s, the left 256 into two 128s, and the result is offset 0. A following `buddy_alloc(200)` (order 3) returns 256.
--- starter
struct Buddy {
    bool free_at[6][32];
    signed char order_of[32];
};

void buddy_init(Buddy* b) {
}

int buddy_alloc(Buddy* b, int n) {
    return -1;
}

void buddy_free(Buddy* b, int offset) {
}
--- solution
struct Buddy {
    bool free_at[6][32];
    signed char order_of[32];
};

void buddy_init(Buddy* b) {
    for (int k = 0; k < 6; k++) {
        for (int i = 0; i < 32; i++) b->free_at[k][i] = false;
    }
    for (int j = 0; j < 32; j++) b->order_of[j] = -1;
    b->free_at[5][0] = true;
}

int buddy_alloc(Buddy* b, int n) {
    if (n < 1 || n > 1024) return -1;
    int k = 0;
    while ((32 << k) < n) k++;
    for (int j = k; j <= 5; j++) {
        int blocks = 1024 / (32 << j);
        for (int i = 0; i < blocks; i++) {
            if (!b->free_at[j][i]) continue;
            b->free_at[j][i] = false;
            int offset = i * (32 << j);
            while (j > k) {
                j--;
                int half = 32 << j;
                b->free_at[j][(offset + half) / half] = true;
            }
            b->order_of[offset / 32] = (signed char)k;
            return offset;
        }
    }
    return -1;
}

void buddy_free(Buddy* b, int offset) {
    if (offset < 0 || offset >= 1024 || offset % 32 != 0) return;
    int k = b->order_of[offset / 32];
    if (k < 0) return;
    b->order_of[offset / 32] = -1;
    while (k < 5) {
        int size = 32 << k;
        int buddy = offset ^ size;
        if (!b->free_at[k][buddy / size]) break;
        b->free_at[k][buddy / size] = false;
        if (buddy < offset) offset = buddy;
        k++;
    }
    b->free_at[k][offset / (32 << k)] = true;
}
--- hint
The order-`k` block at offset `x` is entry `x / (32 << k)` of `free_at[k]`. There are `1024 / (32 << k)` blocks of order `k`.
--- hint
In `buddy_alloc`, once you have taken the lowest free block at order `j`, split down one order at a time: the half size is `32 << (j - 1)`, and the right half at `offset + half` becomes free. The block you keep always starts at the same `offset`.
--- hint
In `buddy_free`, look up the order in `order_of[offset / 32]` and clear it. Then loop: the buddy is at `offset ^ size`; if it is free at this order, clear it, move `offset` to the smaller of the two, and go up one order. When the loop stops, mark the (possibly merged) block free.
--- check test | The example: 100 bytes at 0, then 200 bytes at 256
[] { static Buddy b; buddy_init(&b); int a = buddy_alloc(&b, 100); int c = buddy_alloc(&b, 200); return a == 0 && c == 256 && b.free_at[2][1] && b.free_at[4][1] && !b.free_at[5][0]; }()
--- check test | Sizes to orders: 1 and 32 need 32 bytes, 33 needs 64
[] { static Buddy b; buddy_init(&b); int x = buddy_alloc(&b, 1); int y = buddy_alloc(&b, 32); int z = buddy_alloc(&b, 33); return x == 0 && y == 32 && z == 64 && b.order_of[0] == 0 && b.order_of[1] == 0 && b.order_of[2] == 1; }()
--- check test | Out-of-range sizes return -1
[] { static Buddy b; buddy_init(&b); return buddy_alloc(&b, 0) == -1 && buddy_alloc(&b, -3) == -1 && buddy_alloc(&b, 1025) == -1 && b.free_at[5][0]; }()
--- check test | The whole region, and then nothing is left
[] { static Buddy b; buddy_init(&b); int a = buddy_alloc(&b, 1024); return a == 0 && buddy_alloc(&b, 1) == -1; }()
--- check test | Freeing merges buddies all the way back up
[] { static Buddy b; buddy_init(&b); int a = buddy_alloc(&b, 100); int c = buddy_alloc(&b, 200); int d = buddy_alloc(&b, 30); buddy_free(&b, a); buddy_free(&b, c); buddy_free(&b, d); return b.free_at[5][0] && buddy_alloc(&b, 1024) == 0; }()
--- check test | A block does not merge with a neighbour that is not its buddy
[] { static Buddy b; buddy_init(&b); int p[4]; for (int i = 0; i < 4; i++) p[i] = buddy_alloc(&b, 256); buddy_free(&b, p[1]); buddy_free(&b, p[2]); bool apart = b.free_at[3][1] && b.free_at[3][2] && !b.free_at[4][0] && !b.free_at[4][1]; buddy_free(&b, p[0]); return apart && p[1] == 256 && p[2] == 512 && b.free_at[4][0] && b.free_at[3][2]; }()
--- check test | Freeing an offset that holds no block does nothing; a double free is ignored
[] { static Buddy b; buddy_init(&b); int a = buddy_alloc(&b, 64); int c = buddy_alloc(&b, 64); buddy_free(&b, 32); buddy_free(&b, 5); buddy_free(&b, 2048); buddy_free(&b, -32); bool same = !b.free_at[1][0] && !b.free_at[1][1]; buddy_free(&b, a); buddy_free(&b, a); return same && c == 64 && b.free_at[1][0] && !b.free_at[2][0] && buddy_alloc(&b, 64) == 0; }()
--- check test | The lowest free block is reused first
[] { static Buddy b; buddy_init(&b); int p[8]; for (int i = 0; i < 8; i++) p[i] = buddy_alloc(&b, 128); buddy_free(&b, p[5]); buddy_free(&b, p[2]); return buddy_alloc(&b, 100) == 256 && buddy_alloc(&b, 100) == 640 && buddy_alloc(&b, 1) == -1; }()

+++ question | What is left after a split
--- ask
In the lesson's allocator, a free block with a payload of 120 bytes is split for a request of 40 bytes. What is the payload size of the new free block?
--- answer
72
--- why
The used block keeps 40 bytes. Of the remaining 80, the new free block's header takes 8, leaving 120 − 40 − 8 = 72. Both before and after, the bytes add up: 8 + 120 = 8 + 40 + 8 + 72.

+++ question | Why two neighbours are enough
--- ask
Why does `heap_free` only need to check the blocks immediately before and after the freed one?
--- choice
Because first fit always hands out the block with the lowest address.
--- choice correct
Because before the free, no two free blocks touched: a free neighbour cannot have another free block beyond it, so one merge on each side restores the invariant.
--- choice
Because blocks are kept sorted by size.
--- why
