Invariant 3 does the work. Freeing one block can only create touching free pairs with its two neighbours, and each neighbour, if free, is bordered on its far side by a used block or the end of the heap.

+++ question | The line that makes blocks overlap
--- ask
While splitting a free block `h` of size S at offset `off` for a request `need`, which line makes two blocks overlap?
--- choice
`Header* rest = (Header*)(heap + off + 8 + need);`
--- choice correct
`rest->size = h->size - need;`
--- choice
`h->size = need;`
--- why
The remainder must lose 8 bytes to its own header: its size is S − need − 8. With S − need, the walk from `rest` steps 8 bytes too far, and its last 8 bytes lie over the header of the next block.

=== sys-07 | Bit manipulation: masks, fields, endianness and memcpy
--- teach
Last lesson's buddy allocator found a block's buddy with a single XOR, and the allocators rounded sizes up with a division. Systems code works on individual bits like this all the time: the registers that control hardware, the headers of network and space packets, file formats, flags, and allocators. Computer Organization showed you binary and two's complement. This lesson is the C toolkit for them: operators, masks, fields, byte order, and the one correct way to look at the bytes of an object.

### Unsigned, fixed-width types

For bit work, use **unsigned** types of a known width, from `<cstdint>`: `uint8_t`, `uint16_t`, `uint32_t` and `uint64_t`, which are exactly 8, 16, 32 and 64 bits on every machine. Two reasons:

- An unsigned type has no sign bit to worry about, and its arithmetic is defined to wrap round modulo 2 to the power of its width. Signed overflow is undefined behaviour.
- Plain `int` and `long` change size from machine to machine. `long` is 64 bits on a Linux laptop but 32 bits in ORBIT's browser build and on Windows.

Write bit patterns in **hexadecimal**, where each digit is exactly 4 bits: `0xF0` is `1111 0000`. C++ also accepts binary literals such as `0b1111'0000`, where the `'` is only a separator for the reader.

### The six operators

| operator | name | result bit is 1 when | `0b1100` op `0b1010` |
|---|---|---|---|
| `a & b` | AND | both bits are 1 | `0b1000` |
| `a \| b` | OR | either bit is 1 | `0b1110` |
| `a ^ b` | XOR | the bits differ | `0b0110` |
| `~a` | NOT | the bit of `a` is 0 | (flips every bit) |
| `a << n` | left shift | bits move up `n` places, zeros come in at the bottom | |
| `a >> n` | right shift | bits move down `n` places, bits fall off the bottom | |

Shifting left by `n` multiplies by 2ⁿ (while nothing falls off the top), and shifting an unsigned value right by `n` divides by 2ⁿ, rounding down.

Two rules keep shifts defined:

- **Never shift by the width or more, or by a negative amount.** `x << 32` on a `uint32_t` is undefined behaviour, not 0. On x86 the hardware uses only the low 5 bits of the count, so it often behaves like `x << 0`.
- **Shift unsigned values.** The literal `1` is a signed `int`, so `1 << 31` shifts a 1 into the sign bit. Write `1u << 31`, where the `u` makes the literal unsigned.

There is one more trap, from the C rule called [[integer promotion|promotion]]: before any arithmetic, a value smaller than `int`, such as a `uint8_t`, is converted to `int`. So `~x` for a `uint8_t x` is an `int` with 24 extra 1 bits on top. Cast the result back, `(uint8_t)~x`, when you store it.

### One bit at a time

A **mask** is a value with 1s exactly at the bits you care about. The mask for bit `k` alone is `1u << k`. With it, four operations cover every single-bit job:

```cpp fragment
flags = flags | (1u << k);       // set bit k
flags = flags & ~(1u << k);      // clear bit k
flags = flags ^ (1u << k);       // toggle bit k
bool on = (flags >> k) & 1u;     // test bit k
```

Each works because of what AND, OR and XOR do with a 0 or a 1: OR with 0 and XOR with 0 leave a bit alone, and AND with 1 leaves it alone. The mask has 0s everywhere except bit `k`, and `~mask` has 1s everywhere except bit `k`, so every other bit is untouched.

### Fields: several bits with one meaning

Hardware and protocols pack several small values into one word. Here is a made-up 16-bit status word from a reaction wheel:

| bits | 15 to 12 | 11 to 4 | 3 to 1 | 0 |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| meaning | mode (4 bits) | temperature (8 bits) | fault code (3 bits) | powered (1 bit) |

A **field** that starts at bit `pos` and is `width` bits wide is read by shifting it down to bit 0, then masking off everything above it. The mask of `width` ones is `(1u << width) - 1`: for width 3 that is `0b1000 - 1 = 0b0111`.

```cpp
#include <cstdint>
#include <cstdio>

int main() {
    uint16_t status = 0x5A37;                          // 0101 1010 0011 0111
    unsigned mode = (status >> 12) & 0xFu;             // 0101 = 5
    unsigned temp = (status >> 4) & 0xFFu;             // 1010 0011 = 163
    unsigned fault = (status >> 1) & 0x7u;             // 011 = 3
    unsigned powered = status & 1u;                    // 1
    printf("%u %u %u %u\n", mode, temp, fault, powered);   // 5 163 3 1
    return 0;
}
```

To **write** a field, first clear its bits in the word, then OR in the new value, shifted into place and masked so it cannot spill into its neighbours:

```cpp fragment
status = (uint16_t)((status & ~(0x7u << 1)) | ((code & 0x7u) << 1));   // fault code := code
```

**Why the other fields survive.** The mask `0x7u << 1` has 1s exactly at bits 1 to 3, so AND with its complement clears those bits and keeps every other bit. The new value is masked to 3 bits before shifting, so after the shift it has 1s only in bits 1 to 3. OR puts those in and changes nothing elsewhere, because it ORs 0s into every other bit.

One width needs care: for a 32-bit field in a 32-bit word, `(1u << 32) - 1` shifts by the full width, which is undefined. The mask of all 32 bits is `0xFFFFFFFFu`, and code that accepts any width must treat 32 as its own case.

### Three classic tricks, and why they work

**`x & (x - 1)` clears the lowest 1 bit.** Write x in binary as some bits, then a 1, then k zeros. Subtracting 1 borrows through the k zeros: they become 1s, and the lowest 1 becomes 0. The bits above are untouched. AND-ing the two, the top part is kept, and everything from the lowest 1 down becomes 0. Two uses follow:

- **x is a power of two** exactly when `x != 0 && (x & (x - 1)) == 0`: a power of two has a single 1 bit, and clearing it leaves nothing.
- **Counting 1 bits** by clearing the lowest one until nothing is left runs once per 1 bit, not once per bit of the word. This is Brian Kernighan's method. Modern CPUs also have a single instruction for it, reached through `std::popcount` in C++20.

**`x & (a - 1)` is `x % a`, and `x & ~(a - 1)` rounds down to a multiple of `a`, when `a` is a power of two.** If a = 2ᵐ, then a − 1 is m ones. The low m bits of x are its remainder on dividing by 2ᵐ, and the bits above are a multiple of 2ᵐ. So keeping the low bits gives the remainder, and clearing them gives the multiple below.

**Rounding up to a multiple of a power of two** is then `(n + a - 1) & ~(a - 1)`: add a − 1 and round down, the same argument you proved with division in the stack and heap lesson. It is how allocators align sizes, in one addition and one AND.

### Byte order

A `uint32_t` takes 4 bytes. Which comes first in memory? For the value `0x0A0B0C0D`:

| | address +0 | +1 | +2 | +3 |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| **little-endian** | `0D` | `0C` | `0B` | `0A` |
| **big-endian** | `0A` | `0B` | `0C` | `0D` |

**Little-endian** machines store the least significant byte first: x86, almost all ARM and RISC-V systems, and WebAssembly. **Big-endian** stores the most significant byte first, the way we write numbers. It is the order of internet protocols, where it is called **network byte order**, of many file formats, and of the packets spacecraft send to the ground. The names come from [[Gulliver's Travels|endian-name]].

So bytes that arrive from a network, a file or a radio link must be put together by their **meaning**, never by copying them into an integer and hoping the host has the same byte order. Build the value arithmetically: each byte, shifted to the place its position is worth, OR-ed together. Here is a 16-bit little-endian value read from two bytes:

```cpp fragment
uint16_t read_le16(const unsigned char* p) {
    return (uint16_t)(p[0] | (p[1] << 8));   // p[0] is worth 1, p[1] is worth 256
}
```

**Why this works on every machine.** The value of a little-endian 16-bit number is p[0] + 256 × p[1], by definition of the format. Shifting left by 8 multiplies by 256, and the two terms occupy different bits, so OR gives the same result as +. None of it depends on how the host stores its own integers, because it works on values in registers, not on memory layout.

The bytes must be **unsigned**. On most machines a plain `char` is signed, so the byte `0xF0` held in a `char` is −16. Converted to a wider type it is **sign-extended** to `0xFFFFFFF0`, and those extra 1 bits wreck the OR. Use `unsigned char` or `uint8_t` for raw bytes, always.

### Looking at an object's bytes: memcpy

Sometimes you need the bits of a value of one type as another type: the bit pattern of a `float` as a `uint32_t`, to inspect its sign, exponent and fraction, as in the floating point lesson of Computer Organization. The tempting way is a pointer cast:

```cpp fragment
float f = 1.0f;
uint32_t bits = *(uint32_t*)&f;   // wrong: undefined behaviour
```

This breaks the **[[strict aliasing|aliasing]]** rule: an object may only be read through a pointer to its own type (or to a character type). The optimiser relies on that rule, and real compilers do produce wrong code from casts like this. Reading one member of a `union` after writing another is allowed in C, but not in C++.

The correct way, in both languages, is to copy the bytes with `memcpy`. Compilers recognise it and turn it into a single register move, so it costs nothing:

```cpp
#include <cstdint>
#include <cstdio>
#include <cstring>

int main() {
    float f = -2.0f;
    uint32_t bits;
    memcpy(&bits, &f, sizeof bits);
    printf("0x%08X\n", (unsigned)bits);   // 0xC0000000: sign 1, exponent 128, fraction 0

    uint32_t probe = 1;
    unsigned char first;
    memcpy(&first, &probe, 1);
    printf("%s-endian\n", first == 1 ? "little" : "big");
    return 0;
}
```

C++20 adds `std::bit_cast<uint32_t>(f)` in `<bit>`, which does the same in one expression.

### What it costs

Every operator here is one machine instruction, O(1). A field read is a shift and an AND; a field write is a few more. Packing eight flags into one byte uses one-eighth of the memory of eight `bool`s, which matters for bitsets of millions of entries and for radio links where every bit costs power.

### Where it is used

- **Hardware registers.** Devices are controlled by writing bit patterns to special addresses: set bit 3 of a control register to switch a transmitter on.
- **Protocols.** The CCSDS space packet header, used by most spacecraft, packs a version, a type, a flag, an 11-bit application id, sequence flags, a 14-bit counter and a length into 6 big-endian bytes. IP and TCP headers are built the same way.
- **Files and compression.** PNG, ZIP and every audio format read and write fields bit by bit.
- **Everywhere else:** Unix file permissions (`rwx` is three bits), bitsets and bitboards in chess engines, hash functions, and the alignment arithmetic of allocators.

**Watch out:** operator precedence. `&`, `^` and `|` bind **more loosely** than `==`, so `flags & MASK == 0` means `flags & (MASK == 0)`, which is almost never what you meant, and it compiles without complaint. Always put parentheses around a bitwise expression that you compare: `(flags & MASK) == 0`.

::: context promotion Why small types turn into int
C was designed on machines where arithmetic on anything narrower than a machine word was no faster, so it converts `char`, `short` and their unsigned forms to `int` before any arithmetic: this is integer promotion. A `uint8_t` holding 0x0F becomes the `int` 15, and `~` of that is the `int` −16, whose bit pattern is 0xFFFFFFF0. Compare it with a `uint8_t` and you are comparing −16 with 0 to 255: never equal. Casting back to `uint8_t` keeps only the low 8 bits, 0xF0.
:::

::: context endian-name Big-endians and little-endians
In Jonathan Swift's 1726 novel Gulliver's Travels, the people of Lilliput fight a long war over which end of a boiled egg to crack first, the big end or the little end. In 1980 the computer scientist Danny Cohen borrowed the names in a paper about byte order in network protocols, "On Holy Wars and a Plea for Peace", arguing that the choice matters far less than everyone agreeing on one. The internet agreed on big-endian.
:::

::: context aliasing The strict aliasing rule
The C and C++ standards let the compiler assume that pointers of unrelated types never point at the same memory: a `float*` and a `uint32_t*` cannot alias. That lets it keep values in registers instead of reloading them after every store, which is a large speed-up. A program that reads a `float` through a `uint32_t*` breaks the assumption, and the compiler may reorder or delete its loads and stores. Character types are the exception: any object may be read as `unsigned char` bytes, which is exactly what `memcpy` does.
:::
--- task
Write four functions for 32-bit words and big-endian bytes. No `main`.

- `uint32_t get_field(uint32_t word, int pos, int width)`: return the field of `width` bits that starts at bit `pos` (bit 0 is the lowest), shifted down to bit 0. The arguments always satisfy 1 ≤ `width` ≤ 32 and `pos + width` ≤ 32.
- `uint32_t set_field(uint32_t word, int pos, int width, uint32_t value)`: return `word` with that field replaced by the low `width` bits of `value`. Bits of `value` above `width` are ignored, and every other bit of `word` is kept.
- `uint32_t read_be32(const unsigned char* p)`: return the 32-bit value stored big-endian in the 4 bytes at `p`, so `p[0]` is the most significant byte.
- `void write_be32(unsigned char* p, uint32_t v)`: store `v` big-endian into the 4 bytes at `p`.

Watch the full width: a 32-bit field must work, and shifting a 32-bit value by 32 is undefined. The last two must give the same result on any machine, whatever its own byte order: use shifts, not `memcpy`.

For example, `get_field(0xABCD1234, 8, 12)` is `0xD12`, and `read_be32` of the bytes `DE AD BE EF` is `0xDEADBEEF`.
--- starter
#include <cstdint>

uint32_t get_field(uint32_t word, int pos, int width) {
    return 0;
}

uint32_t set_field(uint32_t word, int pos, int width, uint32_t value) {
    return word;
}

uint32_t read_be32(const unsigned char* p) {
    return 0;
}

void write_be32(unsigned char* p, uint32_t v) {
}
--- solution
#include <cstdint>

static uint32_t low_mask(int width) {
    return width >= 32 ? 0xFFFFFFFFu : (1u << width) - 1u;
}

uint32_t get_field(uint32_t word, int pos, int width) {
    return (word >> pos) & low_mask(width);
}

uint32_t set_field(uint32_t word, int pos, int width, uint32_t value) {
    uint32_t mask = low_mask(width) << pos;
    return (word & ~mask) | ((value << pos) & mask);
}

uint32_t read_be32(const unsigned char* p) {
    return ((uint32_t)p[0] << 24) | ((uint32_t)p[1] << 16) | ((uint32_t)p[2] << 8) | (uint32_t)p[3];
}

void write_be32(unsigned char* p, uint32_t v) {
    p[0] = (unsigned char)(v >> 24);
    p[1] = (unsigned char)(v >> 16);
    p[2] = (unsigned char)(v >> 8);
    p[3] = (unsigned char)v;
}
--- hint
Write a helper that returns a mask of `width` ones: `(1u << width) - 1u`, except for width 32, where it must return `0xFFFFFFFFu` without shifting by 32.
--- hint
`get_field` shifts the word down by `pos` and ANDs with the mask. `set_field` moves the mask up to the field (`mask << pos`), clears those bits in the word, and ORs in `value << pos` ANDed with the same shifted mask, so extra high bits of `value` cannot leak out.
--- hint
`read_be32` puts `p[0]` in the top 8 bits: convert each byte to `uint32_t` before shifting it by 24, 16, 8 and 0, then OR the four together. `write_be32` does the reverse: byte `i` is `v` shifted down by 24, 16, 8 and 0, cut to `unsigned char`.
--- check test | get_field of 0xABCD1234, bits 8 to 19, is 0xD12
[] { return get_field(0xABCD1234u, 8, 12) == 0xD12u; }()
--- check test | get_field of single bits, including bit 31
[] { return get_field(0x80000001u, 0, 1) == 1u && get_field(0x80000001u, 31, 1) == 1u && get_field(0x80000001u, 30, 1) == 0u; }()
--- check test | get_field of the whole 32-bit word
[] { return get_field(0xDEADBEEFu, 0, 32) == 0xDEADBEEFu && get_field(0xFFFFFFFFu, 0, 32) == 0xFFFFFFFFu; }()
--- check test | get_field of the top 4 bits and the low byte
[] { return get_field(0xF1234567u, 28, 4) == 0xFu && get_field(0xF1234567u, 0, 8) == 0x67u; }()
--- check test | set_field replaces a middle field and keeps the rest
[] { return set_field(0xABCD1234u, 8, 12, 0x777u) == 0xABC77734u; }()
--- check test | set_field ignores extra high bits of the value
[] { return set_field(0x00000000u, 4, 4, 0xFFu) == 0x000000F0u && set_field(0xFFFFFFFFu, 4, 4, 0x10u) == 0xFFFFFF0Fu; }()
--- check test | set_field of the whole word, and of bit 31
[] { return set_field(0x12345678u, 0, 32, 0xCAFEF00Du) == 0xCAFEF00Du && set_field(0u, 31, 1, 1u) == 0x80000000u && set_field(0xFFFFFFFFu, 31, 1, 0u) == 0x7FFFFFFFu; }()
--- check test | get_field after set_field gives the value back
[] { uint32_t w = 0x5A5A5A5Au; for (int pos = 0; pos < 32; pos++) for (int width = 1; pos + width <= 32; width++) { uint32_t v = 0x9E3779B9u; uint32_t mask = width == 32 ? 0xFFFFFFFFu : (1u << width) - 1u; uint32_t x = set_field(w, pos, width, v); if (get_field(x, pos, width) != (v & mask)) return false; if ((x & ~(mask << pos)) != (w & ~(mask << pos))) return false; } return true; }()
--- check test | read_be32 of DE AD BE EF
[] { unsigned char b[4] = {0xDE, 0xAD, 0xBE, 0xEF}; return read_be32(b) == 0xDEADBEEFu; }()
--- check test | read_be32 of small and top-bit values
[] { unsigned char a[4] = {0, 0, 0, 7}; unsigned char c[4] = {0x80, 0, 0, 0}; return read_be32(a) == 7u && read_be32(c) == 0x80000000u; }()
--- check test | write_be32 stores the most significant byte first
[] { unsigned char b[6] = {9, 9, 9, 9, 9, 9}; write_be32(b + 1, 0x0A0B0C0Du); return b[0] == 9 && b[1] == 0x0A && b[2] == 0x0B && b[3] == 0x0C && b[4] == 0x0D && b[5] == 9; }()
--- check test | write then read gives the value back
[] { unsigned char b[4]; uint32_t vs[5] = {0u, 1u, 0xFFFFFFFFu, 0x80000000u, 0x12345678u}; for (int i = 0; i < 5; i++) { write_be32(b, vs[i]); if (read_be32(b) != vs[i]) return false; } return true; }()
--- check source absent | Byte order by shifts, not memcpy
\bmemcpy\s*\(

+++ practice | A command register
--- task
An 8-bit command register switches eight heaters, one per bit. Write two functions. No `main`.

- `uint8_t apply(uint8_t reg, char op, int bit)`: return the register after one command. `op` is `'s'` to set the bit (switch that heater on), `'c'` to clear it, and `'t'` to toggle it. If `op` is anything else, or `bit` is not from 0 to 7, return `reg` unchanged.
- `bool is_on(uint8_t reg, int bit)`: whether that bit is 1. A `bit` outside 0 to 7 gives `false`.

For example, `apply(0x05, 's', 1)` is `0x07`, `apply(0x05, 'c', 0)` is `0x04`, and `apply(0x05, 't', 2)` is `0x01`.
--- starter
#include <cstdint>

uint8_t apply(uint8_t reg, char op, int bit) {
    return reg;
}

bool is_on(uint8_t reg, int bit) {
    return false;
}
--- solution
#include <cstdint>

uint8_t apply(uint8_t reg, char op, int bit) {
    if (bit < 0 || bit > 7) return reg;
    uint8_t mask = (uint8_t)(1u << bit);
    if (op == 's') return (uint8_t)(reg | mask);
    if (op == 'c') return (uint8_t)(reg & ~mask);
    if (op == 't') return (uint8_t)(reg ^ mask);
    return reg;
}

bool is_on(uint8_t reg, int bit) {
    if (bit < 0 || bit > 7) return false;
    return (reg >> bit) & 1u;
}
--- hint
Check the bit's range first, so you never shift by a negative amount or by more than the width. The mask is `1u << bit`.
--- hint
Set is OR with the mask, clear is AND with its complement, toggle is XOR. The results are `int`s because of promotion, so cast back to `uint8_t`.
--- check test | The three examples
[] { return apply(0x05, 's', 1) == 0x07 && apply(0x05, 'c', 0) == 0x04 && apply(0x05, 't', 2) == 0x01; }()
--- check test | Setting a set bit, or clearing a clear one, changes nothing
[] { return apply(0x05, 's', 0) == 0x05 && apply(0x05, 'c', 1) == 0x05; }()
--- check test | Bit 7 works
[] { return apply(0x00, 's', 7) == 0x80 && apply(0xFF, 'c', 7) == 0x7F && apply(0x80, 't', 7) == 0x00; }()
--- check test | Unknown commands and bits out of range change nothing
[] { return apply(0x3C, 'x', 2) == 0x3C && apply(0x3C, 's', 8) == 0x3C && apply(0x3C, 'c', -1) == 0x3C && apply(0x3C, 't', 100) == 0x3C; }()
--- check test | is_on reads single bits
[] { return is_on(0xA0, 7) && !is_on(0xA0, 6) && is_on(0xA0, 5) && !is_on(0xA0, 0); }()
--- check test | is_on out of range is false
[] { return !is_on(0xFF, 8) && !is_on(0xFF, -1); }()

+++ practice | Counting and finding bits
--- task
Write three functions. No `main`.

- `int popcount32(uint32_t x)`: the number of 1 bits in `x`. Use Kernighan's method from the lesson, so the loop runs once per 1 bit.
- `bool is_pow2(uint32_t x)`: whether `x` is a power of two (1, 2, 4, …, 2³¹). Zero is not.
- `int lowest_bit(uint32_t x)`: the position of the lowest 1 bit (0 for the lowest bit of the word), or -1 if `x` is 0.

For example, `popcount32(0xF0F0)` is 8, and `lowest_bit(40)` is 3, because 40 is `101000` in binary.
--- starter
#include <cstdint>

int popcount32(uint32_t x) {
    return 0;
}

bool is_pow2(uint32_t x) {
    return false;
}

int lowest_bit(uint32_t x) {
    return -1;
}
--- solution
#include <cstdint>

int popcount32(uint32_t x) {
    int count = 0;
    while (x != 0) {
        x &= x - 1;
        count++;
    }
    return count;
}

bool is_pow2(uint32_t x) {
    return x != 0 && (x & (x - 1)) == 0;
}

int lowest_bit(uint32_t x) {
    if (x == 0) return -1;
    int pos = 0;
    while ((x & 1u) == 0) {
        x >>= 1;
        pos++;
    }
    return pos;
}
--- hint
`x & (x - 1)` is `x` with its lowest 1 bit cleared. Repeat it until `x` is 0, counting the steps.
--- hint
A power of two has exactly one 1 bit, so clearing its lowest 1 bit leaves 0. Put parentheses around the AND before comparing with 0, and exclude 0 itself.
--- hint
For `lowest_bit`, shift `x` right until its lowest bit is 1, counting the shifts. Handle 0 first, or the loop would never end.
--- check test | popcount32 of 0xF0F0 is 8
[] { return popcount32(0xF0F0u) == 8; }()
--- check test | popcount32 of 0, 1 and all ones
[] { return popcount32(0u) == 0 && popcount32(1u) == 1 && popcount32(0xFFFFFFFFu) == 32 && popcount32(0x80000000u) == 1; }()
--- check test | popcount32 agrees with a bit-by-bit count
[] { unsigned s = 7u; for (int i = 0; i < 2000; i++) { s = s * 1664525u + 1013904223u; int slow = 0; for (int b = 0; b < 32; b++) slow += (s >> b) & 1u; if (popcount32(s) != slow) return false; } return true; }()
--- check test | Powers of two, including 1 and 2 to the 31
[] { for (int k = 0; k < 32; k++) if (!is_pow2(1u << k)) return false; return true; }()
--- check test | Not powers of two, including 0
[] { return !is_pow2(0u) && !is_pow2(3u) && !is_pow2(6u) && !is_pow2(0xFFFFFFFFu) && !is_pow2(0x80000001u); }()
--- check test | lowest_bit of 40 is 3
[] { return lowest_bit(40u) == 3; }()
--- check test | lowest_bit of 0, 1 and the top bit
[] { return lowest_bit(0u) == -1 && lowest_bit(1u) == 0 && lowest_bit(0x80000000u) == 31 && lowest_bit(0xFFFFFFFFu) == 0; }()

+++ practice | Inside a float
--- task
A `float` is 32 bits in the IEEE 754 single format, as in the floating point lesson of Computer Organization: 1 sign bit (bit 31), 8 exponent bits (bits 30 to 23) and 23 fraction bits (bits 22 to 0). Write two functions. Get the bits with `memcpy` into a `uint32_t`; never cast a `float*` to another pointer type. No `main`.

- `void float_fields(float f, uint32_t* sign, uint32_t* exponent, uint32_t* fraction)`: write the three fields, each shifted down to bit 0.
- `int float_kind(float f)`: return 0 for zero (exponent 0 and fraction 0, either sign), 1 for a subnormal number (exponent 0, fraction not 0), 2 for a normal number, 3 for infinity (exponent 255 and fraction 0) and 4 for NaN (exponent 255, fraction not 0).

For example, `1.0f` has sign 0, exponent 127 and fraction 0, and `-2.5f` has sign 1, exponent 128 and fraction `0x200000`.
--- starter
#include <cstdint>
#include <cstring>

void float_fields(float f, uint32_t* sign, uint32_t* exponent, uint32_t* fraction) {
}

int float_kind(float f) {
    return 2;
}
--- solution
#include <cstdint>
#include <cstring>

void float_fields(float f, uint32_t* sign, uint32_t* exponent, uint32_t* fraction) {
    uint32_t bits;
    memcpy(&bits, &f, sizeof bits);
    *sign = bits >> 31;
    *exponent = (bits >> 23) & 0xFFu;
    *fraction = bits & 0x7FFFFFu;
}

int float_kind(float f) {
    uint32_t s, e, m;
    float_fields(f, &s, &e, &m);
    if (e == 0) return m == 0 ? 0 : 1;
    if (e == 255) return m == 0 ? 3 : 4;
    return 2;
}
--- hint
`memcpy(&bits, &f, sizeof bits)` copies the float's 4 bytes into a `uint32_t` without breaking any rule.
--- hint
The sign is the top bit, `bits >> 31`. The exponent is 8 bits starting at bit 23: shift by 23, mask with `0xFF`. The fraction is the low 23 bits: mask with `0x7FFFFF`.
--- check test | 1.0f is sign 0, exponent 127, fraction 0
[] { uint32_t s = 9, e = 9, m = 9; float_fields(1.0f, &s, &e, &m); return s == 0 && e == 127 && m == 0; }()
--- check test | -2.5f is sign 1, exponent 128, fraction 0x200000
[] { uint32_t s, e, m; float_fields(-2.5f, &s, &e, &m); return s == 1 && e == 128 && m == 0x200000u; }()
--- check test | 0.1f has the rounded fraction 0x4CCCCD
[] { uint32_t s, e, m; float_fields(0.1f, &s, &e, &m); return s == 0 && e == 123 && m == 0x4CCCCDu; }()
--- check test | Zeros of both signs are kind 0
[] { uint32_t s, e, m; float_fields(-0.0f, &s, &e, &m); return float_kind(0.0f) == 0 && float_kind(-0.0f) == 0 && s == 1; }()
--- check test | The smallest subnormal is kind 1
[] { uint32_t one = 1u; float tiny; memcpy(&tiny, &one, 4); uint32_t s, e, m; float_fields(tiny, &s, &e, &m); return float_kind(tiny) == 1 && e == 0 && m == 1; }()
--- check test | Normal numbers are kind 2
[] { return float_kind(1.0f) == 2 && float_kind(-123456.0f) == 2 && float_kind(1.17549435e-38f) == 2 && float_kind(3.4e38f) == 2; }()
--- check test | Infinities are kind 3, NaN is kind 4
[] { return float_kind(INFINITY) == 3 && float_kind(-INFINITY) == 3 && float_kind(NAN) == 4; }()

+++ practice | Rotations and the next power of two
--- task
Write two functions that must be correct for **every** input. No `main`.

- `uint32_t rotl32(uint32_t x, int k)`: rotate `x` left by `k` bits: bits that fall off the top come back in at the bottom. `k` may be any `int`: rotating by 32 is the same as by 0, by 33 the same as by 1, and a negative `k` rotates right (so -1 is the same as 31).
- `uint32_t next_pow2(uint32_t x)`: the smallest power of two that is at least `x`. `next_pow2(0)` is 1. If the answer would be more than 2³¹, it does not fit in 32 bits: return 0.

Never shift a 32-bit value by 32 or more, or by a negative amount: both are undefined behaviour.

For example, `rotl32(0x80000001, 1)` is `0x00000003`, and `next_pow2(100)` is 128.
--- starter
#include <cstdint>

uint32_t rotl32(uint32_t x, int k) {
    return x;
}

uint32_t next_pow2(uint32_t x) {
    return x;
}
--- solution
#include <cstdint>

uint32_t rotl32(uint32_t x, int k) {
    int r = k % 32;
    if (r < 0) r += 32;
    if (r == 0) return x;
    return (x << r) | (x >> (32 - r));
}

uint32_t next_pow2(uint32_t x) {
    if (x <= 1) return 1;
    if (x > 0x80000000u) return 0;
    uint32_t p = 1;
    while (p < x) p <<= 1;
    return p;
}
--- hint
Reduce `k` to a count from 0 to 31 first. In C++, `%` of a negative number is negative or 0, so add 32 when it is below 0.
--- hint
A rotation by 0 must return `x` itself: the formula `x >> (32 - r)` would shift by 32 there. Handle `r == 0` on its own.
--- hint
For `next_pow2`, deal with 0 and 1, then with anything above `0x80000000`, before doubling `p` from 1 until it reaches `x`. That order means `p` can never overflow.
--- check test | rotl32 of 0x80000001 by 1
[] { return rotl32(0x80000001u, 1) == 0x00000003u; }()
--- check test | rotl32 by 0, 32 and 64 changes nothing
[] { return rotl32(0x12345678u, 0) == 0x12345678u && rotl32(0x12345678u, 32) == 0x12345678u && rotl32(0x12345678u, 64) == 0x12345678u; }()
--- check test | rotl32 by 8, and by 40
[] { return rotl32(0x12345678u, 8) == 0x34567812u && rotl32(0x12345678u, 40) == 0x34567812u; }()
--- check test | Negative counts rotate right
[] { return rotl32(0x12345678u, -8) == 0x78123456u && rotl32(1u, -1) == 0x80000000u && rotl32(0x12345678u, -32) == 0x12345678u; }()
--- check test | Rotating left then right gives x back, for many counts
[] { for (int k = -70; k <= 70; k++) if (rotl32(rotl32(0xC0FFEE11u, k), -k) != 0xC0FFEE11u) return false; return true; }()
--- check test | next_pow2 of 100 is 128, of 0 and 1 is 1
[] { return next_pow2(100u) == 128u && next_pow2(0u) == 1u && next_pow2(1u) == 1u && next_pow2(2u) == 2u && next_pow2(3u) == 4u; }()
--- check test | Powers of two are their own answer
[] { for (int k = 0; k < 32; k++) if (next_pow2(1u << k) != (1u << k)) return false; return true; }()
--- check test | Above 2 to the 31 there is no answer: 0
[] { return next_pow2(0x80000000u) == 0x80000000u && next_pow2(0x80000001u) == 0u && next_pow2(0xFFFFFFFFu) == 0u; }()

+++ practice | Debug: the negative sensor ids
--- task
Sensor packets arrive as raw bytes, and `read_le32(p)` should put together the 32-bit **little-endian** value in the 4 bytes at `p`: `p[0]` is the least significant byte. It works for small ids, but any byte of 128 or more corrupts the result. The bytes `F0 00 00 00` should give `0x000000F0`, and gives `0xFFFFFFF0` instead.

Find the bug and fix it. Keep the parameter type `const char*`: that is how the packet buffer is declared elsewhere. No `main`.
--- starter
#include <cstdint>

uint32_t read_le32(const char* p) {
    return (uint32_t)p[0] | ((uint32_t)p[1] << 8) | ((uint32_t)p[2] << 16) | ((uint32_t)p[3] << 24);
}
--- solution
#include <cstdint>

uint32_t read_le32(const char* p) {
    const unsigned char* b = (const unsigned char*)p;
    return (uint32_t)b[0] | ((uint32_t)b[1] << 8) | ((uint32_t)b[2] << 16) | ((uint32_t)b[3] << 24);
}
--- hint
What is the value of the byte `0xF0` when it is held in a plain `char`, and what happens when that is converted to a 32-bit unsigned value?
--- hint
A plain `char` is signed here, so `0xF0` is −16, and converting it to `uint32_t` sign-extends it to `0xFFFFFFF0`. Read the bytes through a `const unsigned char*` instead.
--- check test | F0 00 00 00 is 0xF0
[] { char b[4] = {(char)0xF0, 0, 0, 0}; return read_le32(b) == 0xF0u; }()
--- check test | Small ids still work
[] { char b[4] = {0x34, 0x12, 0, 0}; return read_le32(b) == 0x1234u; }()
--- check test | Every byte at 128 or more
[] { char b[4] = {(char)0xEF, (char)0xBE, (char)0xAD, (char)0xDE}; return read_le32(b) == 0xDEADBEEFu; }()
--- check test | High bytes in the middle
[] { char b[4] = {0x01, (char)0x80, 0x02, 0x00}; return read_le32(b) == 0x00028001u; }()

+++ practice | Packing 12-bit samples
--- task
An analogue-to-digital converter produces 12-bit samples, from 0 to 4095. Storing each in 16 bits wastes a quarter of the space, so the samples are packed: every **two** samples go into **three** bytes. For samples `a` and `b`:

- byte 0 holds the top 8 bits of `a`;
- byte 1 holds the low 4 bits of `a` in its top half, and the top 4 bits of `b` in its low half;
- byte 2 holds the low 8 bits of `b`.

If there is an odd number of samples, the last one, `a`, takes two bytes: byte 0 as above, and byte 1 with the low 4 bits of `a` in its top half and 0 in its low half.

Write two functions. No `main`.

- `int pack12(const uint16_t* samples, int n, uint8_t* out)`: pack the `n` samples into `out` and return the number of bytes written. Only the low 12 bits of each sample are used.
- `void unpack12(const uint8_t* in, int n, uint16_t* samples)`: the reverse: read the packed bytes and write the `n` samples.

For example, the samples `0xABC` and `0x123` pack to the bytes `AB C1 23`.
--- starter
#include <cstdint>

int pack12(const uint16_t* samples, int n, uint8_t* out) {
    return 0;
}

void unpack12(const uint8_t* in, int n, uint16_t* samples) {
}
--- solution
#include <cstdint>

int pack12(const uint16_t* samples, int n, uint8_t* out) {
    int w = 0;
    for (int i = 0; i < n; i += 2) {
        uint16_t a = samples[i] & 0xFFFu;
        out[w++] = (uint8_t)(a >> 4);
        if (i + 1 < n) {
            uint16_t b = samples[i + 1] & 0xFFFu;
            out[w++] = (uint8_t)(((a & 0xFu) << 4) | (b >> 8));
            out[w++] = (uint8_t)(b & 0xFFu);
        } else {
            out[w++] = (uint8_t)((a & 0xFu) << 4);
        }
    }
    return w;
}

void unpack12(const uint8_t* in, int n, uint16_t* samples) {
    int r = 0;
    for (int i = 0; i < n; i += 2) {
        uint8_t b0 = in[r++];
        uint8_t b1 = in[r++];
        samples[i] = (uint16_t)((b0 << 4) | (b1 >> 4));
        if (i + 1 < n) {
            uint8_t b2 = in[r++];
            samples[i + 1] = (uint16_t)(((b1 & 0xFu) << 8) | b2);
        }
    }
}
--- hint
Work on pairs: a loop with `i += 2`. Mask each sample with `0xFFF` first. Then byte 0 is `a >> 4`, byte 1 is `(a & 0xF) << 4` OR `b >> 8`, and byte 2 is `b & 0xFF`.
--- hint
An odd last sample has no `b`: write byte 0, then `(a & 0xF) << 4` alone, and stop. Keep a separate counter for the bytes written.
--- hint
Unpacking reverses each step: `a` is byte 0 shifted up by 4, OR the top half of byte 1 (`b1 >> 4`); `b` is the low half of byte 1 shifted up by 8, OR byte 2.
--- check test | 0xABC and 0x123 pack to AB C1 23
[] { uint16_t s[2] = {0xABC, 0x123}; uint8_t o[3] = {}; int n = pack12(s, 2, o); return n == 3 && o[0] == 0xAB && o[1] == 0xC1 && o[2] == 0x23; }()
--- check test | An odd count: the last sample takes two bytes
[] { uint16_t s[3] = {0x000, 0xFFF, 0x9A5}; uint8_t o[5] = {}; int n = pack12(s, 3, o); return n == 5 && o[0] == 0x00 && o[1] == 0x0F && o[2] == 0xFF && o[3] == 0x9A && o[4] == 0x50; }()
--- check test | No samples, no bytes
[] { uint16_t s[1] = {1}; uint8_t o[2] = {7, 7}; return pack12(s, 0, o) == 0 && o[0] == 7; }()
--- check test | Only the low 12 bits are used
[] { uint16_t s[2] = {0xFABC, 0x7123}; uint8_t o[3] = {}; pack12(s, 2, o); return o[0] == 0xAB && o[1] == 0xC1 && o[2] == 0x23; }()
--- check test | unpack12 of AB C1 23
[] { uint8_t in[3] = {0xAB, 0xC1, 0x23}; uint16_t s[2] = {}; unpack12(in, 2, s); return s[0] == 0xABC && s[1] == 0x123; }()
--- check test | 1,001 samples survive a round trip
[] { static uint16_t s[1001], t[1001]; static uint8_t buf[1502]; unsigned x = 2024u; for (int i = 0; i < 1001; i++) { x = x * 1103515245u + 12345u; s[i] = (x >> 8) & 0xFFFu; } int n = pack12(s, 1001, buf); unpack12(buf, 1001, t); for (int i = 0; i < 1001; i++) if (t[i] != s[i]) return false; return n == 1502; }()

+++ practice | A space packet header
--- task
Most spacecraft send telemetry in CCSDS space packets. Every packet starts with a 6-byte primary header: 48 bits, big-endian, holding seven fields in this order from the most significant bit:

| field | bits |
|---|---|
| version | 3 |
| type (0 telemetry, 1 command) | 1 |
| secondary header flag | 1 |
| APID, the application id | 11 |
| sequence flags | 2 |
| sequence count | 14 |
| data length | 16 |

So bytes 0 and 1 hold version, type, flag and APID; bytes 2 and 3 hold the sequence flags and count; bytes 4 and 5 hold the data length. The starter declares:

```cpp fragment
struct SpacePacket {
    uint8_t version;
    uint8_t type;
    uint8_t sec_hdr;
    uint16_t apid;
    uint8_t seq_flags;
    uint16_t seq_count;
    uint16_t data_len;
};
```

Write two functions. No `main`.

- `void ccsds_write(const SpacePacket* p, uint8_t* out)`: write the 6 header bytes. Use only as many low bits of each field as the table gives it.
- `bool ccsds_read(const uint8_t* in, SpacePacket* p)`: fill in every field from the 6 bytes, and return `true`. If the version is not 0, the only version in use, return `false` (the fields may still be filled in).

For example, version 0, type 0, flag 1, APID `0x123`, sequence flags 3, count 42 and length 99 give the bytes `09 23 C0 2A 00 63`.
--- starter
#include <cstdint>

struct SpacePacket {
    uint8_t version;
    uint8_t type;
    uint8_t sec_hdr;
    uint16_t apid;
    uint8_t seq_flags;
    uint16_t seq_count;
    uint16_t data_len;
};

void ccsds_write(const SpacePacket* p, uint8_t* out) {
}

bool ccsds_read(const uint8_t* in, SpacePacket* p) {
    return false;
}
--- solution
#include <cstdint>

struct SpacePacket {
    uint8_t version;
    uint8_t type;
    uint8_t sec_hdr;
    uint16_t apid;
    uint8_t seq_flags;
    uint16_t seq_count;
    uint16_t data_len;
};

void ccsds_write(const SpacePacket* p, uint8_t* out) {
    uint16_t id = (uint16_t)(((p->version & 0x7u) << 13) | ((p->type & 0x1u) << 12) | ((p->sec_hdr & 0x1u) << 11) | (p->apid & 0x7FFu));
    uint16_t seq = (uint16_t)(((p->seq_flags & 0x3u) << 14) | (p->seq_count & 0x3FFFu));
    out[0] = (uint8_t)(id >> 8);
    out[1] = (uint8_t)id;
    out[2] = (uint8_t)(seq >> 8);
    out[3] = (uint8_t)seq;
    out[4] = (uint8_t)(p->data_len >> 8);
    out[5] = (uint8_t)p->data_len;
}

bool ccsds_read(const uint8_t* in, SpacePacket* p) {
    uint16_t id = (uint16_t)((in[0] << 8) | in[1]);
    uint16_t seq = (uint16_t)((in[2] << 8) | in[3]);
    p->version = (uint8_t)(id >> 13);
    p->type = (uint8_t)((id >> 12) & 0x1u);
    p->sec_hdr = (uint8_t)((id >> 11) & 0x1u);
    p->apid = (uint16_t)(id & 0x7FFu);
    p->seq_flags = (uint8_t)(seq >> 14);
    p->seq_count = (uint16_t)(seq & 0x3FFFu);
    p->data_len = (uint16_t)((in[4] << 8) | in[5]);
    return p->version == 0;
}
--- hint
Build the first 16-bit word from its four fields: the version goes up by 13, the type by 12, the flag by 11, and the APID sits at the bottom. Mask each field to its width before shifting.
--- hint
The second word is the sequence flags shifted up by 14, OR the count masked to 14 bits (`0x3FFF`). Write each 16-bit word as two bytes, the high byte first.
--- hint
Reading reverses it: put each pair of bytes together into a 16-bit word (the first byte shifted up by 8), then shift and mask each field back out.
--- check test | The example writes 09 23 C0 2A 00 63
[] { SpacePacket p = {0, 0, 1, 0x123, 3, 42, 99}; uint8_t o[6] = {}; ccsds_write(&p, o); return o[0] == 0x09 && o[1] == 0x23 && o[2] == 0xC0 && o[3] == 0x2A && o[4] == 0x00 && o[5] == 0x63; }()
--- check test | The example reads back
[] { uint8_t in[6] = {0x09, 0x23, 0xC0, 0x2A, 0x00, 0x63}; SpacePacket p = {}; bool ok = ccsds_read(in, &p); return ok && p.version == 0 && p.type == 0 && p.sec_hdr == 1 && p.apid == 0x123 && p.seq_flags == 3 && p.seq_count == 42 && p.data_len == 99; }()
--- check test | Every field at its largest value
[] { SpacePacket p = {7, 1, 1, 0x7FF, 3, 0x3FFF, 0xFFFF}; uint8_t o[6] = {}; ccsds_write(&p, o); bool all = true; for (int i = 0; i < 6; i++) all = all && o[i] == 0xFF; SpacePacket q = {}; bool ok = ccsds_read(o, &q); return all && !ok && q.version == 7 && q.apid == 0x7FF && q.seq_count == 0x3FFF && q.data_len == 0xFFFF; }()
--- check test | Too-big fields are cut to their width
[] { SpacePacket p = {0, 1, 0, 0xFFFF, 0, 0xFFFF, 0}; uint8_t o[6] = {}; ccsds_write(&p, o); return o[0] == 0x17 && o[1] == 0xFF && o[2] == 0x3F && o[3] == 0xFF && o[4] == 0 && o[5] == 0; }()
--- check test | A command packet
[] { uint8_t in[6] = {0x18, 0x01, 0x40, 0x00, 0x01, 0x00}; SpacePacket p = {}; bool ok = ccsds_read(in, &p); return ok && p.type == 1 && p.sec_hdr == 1 && p.apid == 1 && p.seq_flags == 1 && p.seq_count == 0 && p.data_len == 256; }()
--- check test | Write then read gives every field back
[] { unsigned x = 99u; for (int i = 0; i < 500; i++) { x = x * 1664525u + 1013904223u; SpacePacket p = {0, (uint8_t)(x & 1u), (uint8_t)((x >> 1) & 1u), (uint16_t)((x >> 2) & 0x7FFu), (uint8_t)((x >> 13) & 3u), (uint16_t)((x >> 15) & 0x3FFFu), (uint16_t)(x ^ (x >> 16))}; uint8_t o[6]; ccsds_write(&p, o); SpacePacket q = {}; if (!ccsds_read(o, &q)) return false; if (q.type != p.type || q.sec_hdr != p.sec_hdr || q.apid != p.apid || q.seq_flags != p.seq_flags || q.seq_count != p.seq_count || q.data_len != p.data_len) return false; } return true; }()

+++ question | What the flag test really tests
--- ask
What does `if (flags & 0x04 == 0)` test?
--- choice
Whether bit 2 of `flags` is 0.
--- choice correct
`flags & (0x04 == 0)`, which is `flags & 0`: always false.
--- choice
Whether `flags` equals 0x04.
--- why
`==` binds more tightly than `&`, so `0x04 == 0` is worked out first, giving 0 (false). Write `(flags & 0x04) == 0`.

+++ question | Clearing the lowest bit
--- ask
What does this print?

```cpp fragment
uint32_t x = 88;          // 1011000 in binary
printf("%u\n", (unsigned)(x & (x - 1)));
```
--- answer
80
--- why
87 is 1010111. AND-ing 1011000 with 1010111 keeps the top bits and clears the lowest 1 bit: 1010000, which is 80.

+++ question | Why memcpy for the bits of a float
--- ask
Why does the lesson read a float's bits with `memcpy` instead of `*(uint32_t*)&f`?
--- choice
`memcpy` is faster, because the cast needs a function call.
--- choice correct
Reading a `float` through a `uint32_t*` breaks the strict aliasing rule, which is undefined behaviour; `memcpy` copies the bytes legally, and compilers turn it into a single move anyway.
--- choice
The cast only works on big-endian machines.
--- why
The optimiser assumes that pointers to unrelated types never point at the same object, and can reorder or drop loads and stores that break the rule. Any object may be copied as bytes, which is what `memcpy` does; in C++20, `std::bit_cast` is the one-expression form.

=== sys-08 | Structs in memory: padding, alignment and arrays of structs
--- teach
Last lesson you packed fields into the bits of one word by hand. The compiler packs the fields of every struct you write, too, and it follows rules you can predict exactly. In the malloc lesson you met **alignment**: every type must start at an address that is a multiple of its alignment, `alignof(T)`. This lesson shows what alignment does to a struct: where each field lands, why there are unused bytes between fields, why `sizeof` of a struct is often more than the sum of its fields, and how to order fields and choose between an array of structs and a struct of arrays so that your data is small and fast.

### Looking inside a struct

`sizeof(T)` gives the size of a type in bytes. The macro `offsetof(T, field)`, from `<cstddef>`, gives how many bytes from the start of a `T` the field begins. Here is a beacon record from a ground station:

```cpp
#include <cstddef>
#include <cstdio>

struct Beacon {
    char kind;       // 1 byte
    double time;     // 8 bytes
    short id;        // 2 bytes
};

int main() {
    printf("kind at %zu\n", offsetof(Beacon, kind));   // kind at 0
    printf("time at %zu\n", offsetof(Beacon, time));   // time at 8
    printf("id at %zu\n", offsetof(Beacon, id));       // id at 16
    printf("size %zu\n", sizeof(Beacon));              // size 24
    return 0;
}
```

The fields hold 1 + 8 + 2 = 11 bytes of data, yet the struct takes 24. Here is where every byte goes:

| bytes | 0 | 1 to 7 | 8 to 15 | 16 to 17 | 18 to 23 |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| holds | `kind` | padding | `time` | `id` | padding |

The unused bytes are called **padding**. The compiler adds them so that every field is aligned: `time` has alignment 8, so it cannot start at byte 1.

### The four rules

On every mainstream machine and compiler (x86, ARM, RISC-V, WebAssembly), a plain struct is laid out by these rules. They come from the [[ABI|abi]], the agreement that lets code from different compilers work together.

1. **Fields are stored in the order they are declared.** C and C++ never reorder them.
2. **Each field starts at the smallest offset that is not before the end of the previous field and is a multiple of the field's alignment.** The first field starts at offset 0.
3. **The struct's own alignment is the largest alignment of any of its fields.**
4. **The struct's size is the end of its last field, rounded up to a multiple of the struct's alignment.** The bytes added at the end are called **tail padding**.

For `Beacon`: `kind` goes at 0 and ends at 1. `time` needs a multiple of 8 that is at least 1: offset 8, ending at 16. `id` needs a multiple of 2 that is at least 16: offset 16, ending at 18. The struct's alignment is 8, the largest of 1, 8 and 2, so the size is 18 rounded up to a multiple of 8: 24.

"Rounded up to a multiple of `a`" is the calculation you proved correct in the stack and heap lesson, and made fast with a mask in the last one. Alignments are always powers of two, so both forms work.

Two more facts complete the rules. A field that is itself a struct has that struct's size and alignment, so it is placed like any other field. A field that is an array of `k` elements of type `T` has size `k * sizeof(T)` and the alignment of `T`: a `char name[6]` takes 6 bytes and can start anywhere.

### Why the size is rounded up: arrays

Rule 4 looks wasteful. Why should `Beacon` end in 6 bytes that hold nothing? The reason is arrays. In an array `Beacon log[100]`, element `i` starts exactly `i * sizeof(Beacon)` bytes after element 0: arrays have no gaps, and pointer arithmetic depends on it. Every field of every element must still be aligned. Here is the argument that rule 4 guarantees it.

Call the struct's alignment A, and suppose the array starts at an address that is a multiple of A (the compiler and `malloc` make sure of that).

- A field with alignment a sits at an offset that is a multiple of a, by rule 2.
- Every alignment is a power of two, and A is the largest of them, so a divides A.
- Element `i` starts at the array's start plus `i * sizeof(Beacon)`. The start is a multiple of A, and the size is a multiple of A by rule 4, so the element's start is a multiple of A, and so a multiple of a.
- The field's address is the element's start plus the field's offset: the sum of two multiples of a, which is a multiple of a. So the field is aligned, in every element.

Without rule 4, a 18-byte `Beacon` would put the second element's `time` at byte 18 + 8 = 26, which is not a multiple of 8. So `sizeof` always includes the tail padding, and `sizeof(Beacon) * 100` is exactly the size of the array.

A consequence for C++: every object has a size of at least 1, so a struct with no fields has `sizeof` 1. Two different objects must have different addresses, and an array of size-0 elements would put them all at one address.

### Choosing the order of the fields

You cannot change the rules, but you choose the order of the fields. Move the 8-byte field first:

```cpp
#include <cstdio>

struct Beacon2 {
    double time;
    short id;
    char kind;
};

int main() {
    printf("%zu\n", sizeof(Beacon2));   // 16: time 0..7, id 8..9, kind 10, 5 bytes of tail padding
    return 0;
}
```

Same data, a third less memory. The rule of thumb is **declare the fields in order of decreasing alignment**. Here is why that order is not just good but the best possible.

Assume, as holds for every C type, that each alignment is a power of two and each field's size is a multiple of its own alignment. Let S be the sum of the field sizes, and A the largest alignment.

**No order can do better than S rounded up to a multiple of A.** Every layout holds all S bytes of data, so its size is at least S, and by rule 4 its size is a multiple of A. The smallest multiple of A that is at least S is S rounded up.

**Decreasing alignment reaches that bound.** Walk through the fields in that order. Suppose the fields placed so far all have alignments of at least a, the alignment of the next field. Each of them has a size that is a multiple of its own alignment, which is a multiple of a (powers of two again). So the offset where they end, the sum of their sizes, is a multiple of a, and the next field fits there with **no padding at all**. By induction there is no padding between any fields, the last field ends at exactly S, and rule 4 rounds S up to a multiple of A. That is the bound, so no order is smaller.

So padding between fields is always avoidable, and the only padding you cannot remove is at the tail. For `Beacon`, S = 11 and A = 8, so 16 is the best possible, and `Beacon2` reaches it.

### Padding bytes are real bytes

The padding bytes exist in memory, and nobody promises what they hold. They contain whatever was there before: old stack data, old heap data. Three bugs follow from that.

- **Comparing structs with `memcmp`.** `memcmp(&a, &b, sizeof a)` compares every byte, padding included. Two `Beacon`s with equal fields can compare unequal because their padding differs. Compare field by field.
- **Writing structs to a file or a network as raw bytes.** The padding goes out too. If it held a password that was on the stack a moment ago, you have sent the password: a real [[information leak|leak]] that has hit operating system kernels. Setting the whole struct to zero with `memset` first stops the leak, but not the next problem.
- **Reading raw struct bytes on another machine.** The receiver may have different padding, a different byte order, or a different size for the same type. In ORBIT's browser build, a pointer and a `long` take 4 bytes; on a PC they take 8. A file of raw structs written by one is garbage to the other. Write each field on its own, in a byte order you chose, as the bit manipulation lesson showed.

Compilers also offer **packed** structs (`__attribute__((packed))` in GCC and Clang, `#pragma pack(1)`), which leave out all padding. That is sometimes used to describe a wire format exactly, but the fields are then misaligned: reading them is slower on some machines and crashes on others, and taking the address of a packed field gives a pointer the rest of the program will misuse. Prefer writing the bytes yourself.

### Arrays of structs, and structs of arrays

A spacecraft simulation tracks a million debris objects:

```cpp fragment
struct Debris {            // an array of these is an "array of structs"
    double x, y, z;        // position, 24 bytes
    float radius;          // 4 bytes
    int id;                // 4 bytes
};                         // 32 bytes, no padding
Debris field[1000000];
```

To find the largest radius, a loop reads `field[i].radius` for every `i`. The CPU never fetches single bytes from main memory: it fetches whole [[cache lines|cache-line]] of 64 bytes. One line holds two `Debris`, so the loop pulls in all 32 million bytes to use 4 million of them. Seven of every eight bytes it waits for are wasted.

The alternative keeps one array per field, a **struct of arrays**:

```cpp fragment
struct DebrisField {       // a "struct of arrays"
    double x[1000000], y[1000000], z[1000000];
    float radius[1000000];
    int id[1000000];
};
```

Now the radii are side by side: one 64-byte line holds 16 of them, and the loop touches 4 million bytes, every one of them useful. The loop does the same arithmetic, but it waits for memory an eighth as often, and the compiler can also use [[SIMD|simd]] instructions that work on several neighbouring radii at once.

**The cost, counted.** A loop that reads one field of size f from each of n records laid out with a stride of s bytes (the distance from one record to the next) touches about n·s/64 cache lines when s ≤ 64, because it walks through every byte of the array. With a struct of arrays the stride is f itself, so about n·f/64 lines. The ratio is s/f: 32/4 = 8 for the radius. When a loop uses **every** field of each record, the two layouts touch the same bytes, and the array of structs can even win, because the fields of one record sit in the same line.

So the choice follows the access pattern:

| layout | good when | examples |
|---|---|---|
| array of structs | code handles one whole record at a time | a packet, a file record, a list of commands |
| struct of arrays | loops scan one or two fields over many records | physics, analytics, telemetry columns |

A middle way, **hot/cold splitting**, keeps the fields that are read constantly in a small struct and moves the rarely used ones to a second struct, so the hot array is dense.

### What it costs

Computing a layout by the rules is one pass over the fields: O(number of fields). Sorting fields by alignment costs O(k log k) for k fields, once, when you write the struct. At run time the layout costs nothing, since every offset is a constant built into the instructions. What layout changes is memory: bytes wasted in padding, multiplied by the number of records, and cache lines fetched, which for a scan over n records is proportional to n times the stride.

### Where it is used

- **Every C interface.** The operating system, graphics drivers and libraries in other languages agree on struct layouts through the ABI rules above. Python's `ctypes` and `struct` modules reproduce them.
- **Game engines** store their objects as structs of arrays, in a design called an [[entity component system|ecs]], so that the physics loop streams through positions only.
- **Databases.** Row stores keep each record together (an array of structs); column stores such as those behind analytics systems keep each column together (a struct of arrays), which is why they scan one column of a billion rows so fast.
- **Flight software** lays out telemetry tables so that the downlink loop reads densely, and checks struct sizes with `static_assert(sizeof(Frame) == 64)` so a change to a field can never silently change a format.

**Watch out:** `sizeof` of a struct is not the sum of its fields, and the layout of the same struct can differ between machines. Code that computes a record size by adding field sizes, or that writes whole structs to a file and reads them back elsewhere, works on the machine where it was written and breaks on the next. Use `sizeof` and `offsetof` for in-memory layout, and write external formats field by field.

::: context abi The rules everyone agrees on
An Application Binary Interface (ABI) is the agreement, below the level of source code, that lets separately compiled code work together: how big each type is, how structs are laid out, which registers hold function arguments. The System V ABI covers Linux and most Unix systems on x86-64 and ARM; Windows has its own. Because they agree, a library compiled by GCC can be called from a program compiled by Clang, or from Python. Change a struct's layout in a shared library and every program built against the old layout breaks.
:::

::: context leak Secrets in the padding
Suppose a function copies a struct to user programs or out over a network with `memcpy`, fields filled in one by one but the padding never set. The padding bytes still hold whatever was on the stack before: pieces of keys, addresses, other users' data. Linux kernel developers have fixed many such leaks, and security tools now search for them. Addresses leaked this way also help attackers defeat address randomisation, which you will meet in the Security course.
:::

::: context cache-line Memory arrives in lines
Main memory is about a hundred times slower than the CPU, so the CPU keeps copies of recently used memory in small fast caches. It moves memory between them in fixed blocks called cache lines, 64 bytes on almost every current processor. Reading one byte brings in its whole line; reading the next 63 bytes is then nearly free. Code that uses every byte of each line it fetches runs many times faster than code that jumps around, even with the same number of instructions.
:::

::: context simd One instruction, many values
SIMD means "single instruction, multiple data": CPU instructions that add, compare or multiply several neighbouring values at once, for example eight floats with one AVX instruction on x86. They need the values side by side in memory, which a struct of arrays provides and an array of structs does not. Compilers use them automatically for simple loops over arrays; this is called auto-vectorisation.
:::

::: context ecs Entities, components, systems
An entity component system is a way to organise a game or simulation. An entity is just a number. Its data lives in component arrays, one per kind of data: positions, velocities, health. A system is a loop that runs over one or two component arrays, such as "add velocity to position". Unity's DOTS and many modern engines use it because every loop streams through dense memory.
:::
--- task
Write two functions that compute struct layouts by the rules of this lesson. No `main`.

A struct is described by `n` fields (n ≥ 1), in declaration order: field `i` has size `sizes[i]` bytes and alignment `aligns[i]`. Every alignment is a power of two, and every size is a positive multiple of its field's alignment.

- `int struct_layout(const int* sizes, const int* aligns, int n, int* offsets)`: write the offset of each field into `offsets[0]` to `offsets[n - 1]`, and return the size of the struct, tail padding included.
- `void best_order(const int* sizes, const int* aligns, int n, int* order)`: write into `order` the field indices in the order that gives the smallest struct: decreasing alignment. Fields with equal alignment keep their declaration order. So for sizes `{1, 8, 2}` and alignments `{1, 8, 2}`, the order is `{1, 2, 0}`.

For example, `Beacon` from the lesson is sizes `{1, 8, 2}` and alignments `{1, 8, 2}`: `struct_layout` writes offsets `{0, 8, 16}` and returns 24. The checks compare your answers with the real `sizeof` and `offsetof` of structs the compiler lays out.
--- starter
#include <cstddef>

int struct_layout(const int* sizes, const int* aligns, int n, int* offsets) {
    int total = 0;
    for (int i = 0; i < n; i++) {
        offsets[i] = total;
        total += sizes[i];
    }
    return total;
}

void best_order(const int* sizes, const int* aligns, int n, int* order) {
    for (int i = 0; i < n; i++) order[i] = i;
}
--- solution
#include <cstddef>

static int round_up(int x, int a) {
    return (x + a - 1) / a * a;
}

int struct_layout(const int* sizes, const int* aligns, int n, int* offsets) {
    int end = 0;
    int largest = 1;
    for (int i = 0; i < n; i++) {
        int at = round_up(end, aligns[i]);
        offsets[i] = at;
        end = at + sizes[i];
        if (aligns[i] > largest) largest = aligns[i];
    }
    return round_up(end, largest);
}

void best_order(const int* sizes, const int* aligns, int n, int* order) {
    for (int i = 0; i < n; i++) order[i] = i;
    // insertion sort by decreasing alignment; it is stable, so ties keep declaration order
    for (int i = 1; i < n; i++) {
        int f = order[i];
        int j = i - 1;
        while (j >= 0 && aligns[order[j]] < aligns[f]) {
            order[j + 1] = order[j];
            j--;
        }
        order[j + 1] = f;
    }
}
--- hint
Keep two numbers as you walk the fields: where the previous field ended, and the largest alignment so far. Each field starts at the end, rounded up to its own alignment.
--- hint
After the loop, the size is the end of the last field rounded up to the largest alignment. That rounding is rule 4, and it is what the starter forgets, along with the padding before each field.
--- hint
For `best_order`, start from `order[i] = i` and sort it with a stable sort (insertion sort is stable) that moves an index earlier only while the one before it has a strictly smaller alignment.
--- check test | Beacon: offsets 0, 8, 16 and size 24, as the compiler says

--- check test | char, int, char has 3 bytes of padding twice

--- check test | A single field
[] { int sz[1] = {8}; int al[1] = {8}; int off[1] = {99}; return struct_layout(sz, al, 1, off) == 8 && off[0] == 0; }()
--- check test | Only chars: no padding at all

--- check test | best_order keeps ties in declaration order
