--- check test | The best order reaches the bound: the sum rounded up to the largest alignment
[] { unsigned s = 12345u; for (int t = 0; t < 300; t++) { int n = 1 + t % 9; int sz[9], al[9], o[9], off[9]; int sum = 0, big = 1; for (int i = 0; i < n; i++) { s = s * 1103515245u + 12345u; int a = 1 << ((s >> 16) % 4); s = s * 1103515245u + 12345u; sz[i] = a * (1 + (int)((s >> 16) % 3)); al[i] = a; sum += sz[i]; if (a > big) big = a; } best_order(sz, al, n, o); int sz2[9], al2[9]; for (int i = 0; i < n; i++) { sz2[i] = sz[o[i]]; al2[i] = al[o[i]]; } int got = struct_layout(sz2, al2, n, off); if (got != (sum + big - 1) / big * big) return false; } return true; }()

+++ practice | From rows to columns
--- task
A star catalogue arrives as an array of structs:

```cpp fragment
struct Star {
    float mag;      // brightness
    int id;
    double ra;      // right ascension, in degrees
};
```

Write two functions that convert it to a struct of arrays and back. No `main`.

- `void split_stars(const Star* stars, int n, float* mag, int* id, double* ra)`: copy field `mag` of star `i` into `mag[i]`, and the same for `id` and `ra`.
- `void join_stars(const float* mag, const int* id, const double* ra, int n, Star* stars)`: the reverse.

With `n` equal to 0, neither function writes anything. Include the struct definition exactly as shown.
--- starter
struct Star {
    float mag;
    int id;
    double ra;
};

void split_stars(const Star* stars, int n, float* mag, int* id, double* ra) {
}

void join_stars(const float* mag, const int* id, const double* ra, int n, Star* stars) {
}
--- solution
struct Star {
    float mag;
    int id;
    double ra;
};

void split_stars(const Star* stars, int n, float* mag, int* id, double* ra) {
    for (int i = 0; i < n; i++) {
        mag[i] = stars[i].mag;
        id[i] = stars[i].id;
        ra[i] = stars[i].ra;
    }
}

void join_stars(const float* mag, const int* id, const double* ra, int n, Star* stars) {
    for (int i = 0; i < n; i++) {
        stars[i].mag = mag[i];
        stars[i].id = id[i];
        stars[i].ra = ra[i];
    }
}
--- hint
One loop over `i` does it: element `i` of each column is field of record `i`.
--- hint
In `split_stars`, write `mag[i] = stars[i].mag;` and the same for the other two fields. `join_stars` assigns the other way round.
--- check test | Three stars split into three columns
[] { Star s[3] = {{1.5f, 7, 10.25}, {-0.5f, 8, 200.0}, {6.0f, 9, 359.5}}; float m[3]; int d[3]; double r[3]; split_stars(s, 3, m, d, r); return m[0] == 1.5f && m[1] == -0.5f && m[2] == 6.0f && d[0] == 7 && d[2] == 9 && r[1] == 200.0 && r[2] == 359.5; }()
--- check test | Three columns joined into three stars
[] { float m[3] = {2.0f, 3.0f, 4.0f}; int d[3] = {11, 12, 13}; double r[3] = {1.0, 2.0, 3.0}; Star s[3]; join_stars(m, d, r, 3, s); return s[0].mag == 2.0f && s[1].id == 12 && s[2].ra == 3.0 && s[2].mag == 4.0f; }()
--- check test | Split then join gives the stars back
[] { Star s[50], t[50]; for (int i = 0; i < 50; i++) { s[i].mag = i * 0.25f; s[i].id = 1000 + i; s[i].ra = i * 7.5; } float m[50]; int d[50]; double r[50]; split_stars(s, 50, m, d, r); join_stars(m, d, r, 50, t); for (int i = 0; i < 50; i++) if (t[i].mag != s[i].mag || t[i].id != s[i].id || t[i].ra != s[i].ra) return false; return true; }()
--- check test | n = 0 writes nothing
[] { Star s[1] = {{9.0f, 9, 9.0}}; float m[1] = {-1.0f}; int d[1] = {-1}; double r[1] = {-1.0}; split_stars(s, 0, m, d, r); join_stars(m, d, r, 0, s); return m[0] == -1.0f && d[0] == -1 && r[0] == -1.0 && s[0].id == 9; }()

+++ practice | Padding, counted
--- task
Using the layout rules of the lesson, write two functions about the padding in a struct. No `main`.

A struct has `n` fields (n ≥ 1): field `i` has size `sizes[i]` and alignment `aligns[i]`, in declaration order. Alignments are powers of two, and each size is a positive multiple of its alignment.

- `int padding_bytes(const int* sizes, const int* aligns, int n)`: the total number of padding bytes in the struct, between fields and at the tail.
- `int padding_after(const int* sizes, const int* aligns, int n, int k)`: the number of padding bytes right after field `k` (0 ≤ k < n): up to the start of field `k + 1`, or, for the last field, up to the end of the struct.

For example, for `char, int, char` (sizes `{1, 4, 1}`, alignments `{1, 4, 1}`) the struct is 12 bytes: `padding_bytes` is 6, `padding_after` of field 0 is 3, of field 1 is 0, and of field 2 is 3.
--- starter
int padding_bytes(const int* sizes, const int* aligns, int n) {
    return 0;
}

int padding_after(const int* sizes, const int* aligns, int n, int k) {
    return 0;
}
--- solution
static int round_up(int x, int a) {
    return (x + a - 1) / a * a;
}

static int largest_align(const int* aligns, int n) {
    int a = 1;
    for (int i = 0; i < n; i++)
        if (aligns[i] > a) a = aligns[i];
    return a;
}

int padding_after(const int* sizes, const int* aligns, int n, int k) {
    int end = 0;
    for (int i = 0; i <= k; i++) end = round_up(end, aligns[i]) + sizes[i];
    int next = k + 1 < n ? round_up(end, aligns[k + 1]) : round_up(end, largest_align(aligns, n));
    return next - end;
}

int padding_bytes(const int* sizes, const int* aligns, int n) {
    int end = 0;
    int data = 0;
    for (int i = 0; i < n; i++) {
        end = round_up(end, aligns[i]) + sizes[i];
        data += sizes[i];
    }
    return round_up(end, largest_align(aligns, n)) - data;
}
--- hint
Lay the struct out with the rules: each field starts at the previous end rounded up to its alignment. The total padding is the struct's size minus the sum of the field sizes.
--- hint
For `padding_after`, find where field `k` ends. The padding is the distance from there to the start of the next field, which is that end rounded up to the next field's alignment, or, after the last field, rounded up to the largest alignment.
--- check test | char, int, char: 6 in total, 3 after the first and last fields
[] { int s[3] = {1, 4, 1}; int a[3] = {1, 4, 1}; return padding_bytes(s, a, 3) == 6 && padding_after(s, a, 3, 0) == 3 && padding_after(s, a, 3, 1) == 0 && padding_after(s, a, 3, 2) == 3; }()
--- check test | Agrees with the compiler for char, double, short
[] { struct S { char a; double b; short c; }; int s[3] = {1, 8, 2}; int a[3] = {1, (int)alignof(double), 2}; int pad = (int)sizeof(S) - 11; int after_a = (int)offsetof(S, b) - 1; int after_c = (int)sizeof(S) - ((int)offsetof(S, c) + 2); return padding_bytes(s, a, 3) == pad && padding_after(s, a, 3, 0) == after_a && padding_after(s, a, 3, 2) == after_c; }()
--- check test | A sorted struct has no padding between fields
[] { int s[4] = {8, 4, 2, 1}; int a[4] = {8, 4, 2, 1}; return padding_after(s, a, 4, 0) == 0 && padding_after(s, a, 4, 1) == 0 && padding_after(s, a, 4, 2) == 0 && padding_after(s, a, 4, 3) == 1 && padding_bytes(s, a, 4) == 1; }()
--- check test | One field, and only chars: no padding
[] { int s1[1] = {4}; int a1[1] = {4}; int s2[3] = {1, 1, 1}; int a2[3] = {1, 1, 1}; return padding_bytes(s1, a1, 1) == 0 && padding_after(s1, a1, 1, 0) == 0 && padding_bytes(s2, a2, 3) == 0; }()
--- check test | Arrays of shorts and a 16-byte alignment
[] { int s[3] = {6, 16, 2}; int a[3] = {2, 16, 2}; return padding_after(s, a, 3, 0) == 10 && padding_after(s, a, 3, 2) == 14 && padding_bytes(s, a, 3) == 24; }()
--- check test | Padding after each field adds up to the total
[] { unsigned x = 99u; for (int t = 0; t < 200; t++) { int n = 1 + t % 7; int s[7], a[7]; for (int i = 0; i < n; i++) { x = x * 1664525u + 1013904223u; a[i] = 1 << ((x >> 20) % 4); x = x * 1664525u + 1013904223u; s[i] = a[i] * (1 + (int)((x >> 20) % 3)); } int sum = 0; for (int k = 0; k < n; k++) sum += padding_after(s, a, n, k); if (sum != padding_bytes(s, a, n)) return false; } return true; }()

+++ practice | A record without its padding
--- task
A temperature reading is kept in memory as this struct, which has padding:

```cpp fragment
struct Reading {
    uint8_t sensor;
    uint32_t time;
    int16_t value;
};
```

To send it to the ground it is packed into exactly 7 bytes, with no padding, in field order, each field **little-endian**: byte 0 is `sensor`, bytes 1 to 4 are `time` (lowest byte first), and bytes 5 and 6 are `value` (lowest byte first, as its two's complement bit pattern).

Write two functions. No `main`. Include `<cstdint>` and the struct exactly as shown.

- `int pack_reading(const Reading* r, unsigned char* out)`: write the 7 bytes into `out` and return 7.
- `void unpack_reading(const unsigned char* in, Reading* r)`: read 7 bytes back into `*r`.

The result must be the same on every machine: build and take apart each value with shifts, never by copying the struct's bytes. For example, sensor 3, time `0x01020304` and value -2 pack to `03 04 03 02 01 FE FF`.
--- starter
#include <cstdint>
#include <cstring>

struct Reading {
    uint8_t sensor;
    uint32_t time;
    int16_t value;
};

int pack_reading(const Reading* r, unsigned char* out) {
    memcpy(out, r, 7);
    return 7;
}

void unpack_reading(const unsigned char* in, Reading* r) {
    memcpy(r, in, 7);
}
--- solution
#include <cstdint>

struct Reading {
    uint8_t sensor;
    uint32_t time;
    int16_t value;
};

int pack_reading(const Reading* r, unsigned char* out) {
    uint16_t v = (uint16_t)r->value;
    out[0] = r->sensor;
    out[1] = (unsigned char)(r->time);
    out[2] = (unsigned char)(r->time >> 8);
    out[3] = (unsigned char)(r->time >> 16);
    out[4] = (unsigned char)(r->time >> 24);
    out[5] = (unsigned char)(v);
    out[6] = (unsigned char)(v >> 8);
    return 7;
}

void unpack_reading(const unsigned char* in, Reading* r) {
    r->sensor = in[0];
    r->time = (uint32_t)in[1] | ((uint32_t)in[2] << 8) | ((uint32_t)in[3] << 16) | ((uint32_t)in[4] << 24);
    r->value = (int16_t)(uint16_t)(in[5] | (in[6] << 8));
}
--- hint
The struct is 12 bytes in memory, with padding after `sensor` and at the end, so copying its bytes puts padding into the packet. Write each field yourself.
--- hint
For `time`, byte `k` of the packet (k from 0 to 3) is `time >> (8 * k)` cut to `unsigned char`. For `value`, convert it to `uint16_t` first, so you shift its bit pattern and not a negative number.
--- hint
To unpack, OR the bytes together shifted back into place, converting each to `uint32_t` before shifting it by 24. For `value`, rebuild the `uint16_t` pattern and convert it to `int16_t`.
--- check test | The example: 03 04 03 02 01 FE FF
[] { Reading r = {3, 0x01020304u, -2}; unsigned char b[8] = {0, 0, 0, 0, 0, 0, 0, 0xAA}; int n = pack_reading(&r, b); unsigned char want[7] = {0x03, 0x04, 0x03, 0x02, 0x01, 0xFE, 0xFF}; for (int i = 0; i < 7; i++) if (b[i] != want[i]) return false; return n == 7 && b[7] == 0xAA; }()
--- check test | Unpack the example
[] { unsigned char b[7] = {0x03, 0x04, 0x03, 0x02, 0x01, 0xFE, 0xFF}; Reading r; unpack_reading(b, &r); return r.sensor == 3 && r.time == 0x01020304u && r.value == -2; }()
--- check test | Extremes: 255, the largest time, and -32768
[] { Reading r = {255, 0xFFFFFFFFu, -32768}; unsigned char b[7]; pack_reading(&r, b); Reading q; unpack_reading(b, &q); return b[0] == 0xFF && b[4] == 0xFF && b[5] == 0x00 && b[6] == 0x80 && q.sensor == 255 && q.time == 0xFFFFFFFFu && q.value == -32768; }()
--- check test | Positive value 32767 and time 0
[] { Reading r = {0, 0u, 32767}; unsigned char b[7]; pack_reading(&r, b); return b[1] == 0 && b[2] == 0 && b[5] == 0xFF && b[6] == 0x7F; }()
--- check test | Pack then unpack gives the reading back, 500 times
[] { unsigned s = 5u; for (int i = 0; i < 500; i++) { s = s * 1664525u + 1013904223u; Reading r = {(uint8_t)(s >> 24), s * 2654435761u, (int16_t)(uint16_t)(s >> 8)}; unsigned char b[7]; pack_reading(&r, b); Reading q; unpack_reading(b, &q); if (q.sensor != r.sensor || q.time != r.time || q.value != r.value) return false; } return true; }()
--- check source absent | Fields by shifts, not by copying the struct
\bmemcpy\s*\(

+++ practice | Arrays inside, and the empty struct
--- task
Extend the layout rules to fields that are arrays. No `main`.

A struct has `n` fields in declaration order, where n may be 0. Field `i` is an array of `count[i]` elements (count ≥ 1; a plain field has count 1), and each element has size `elem_size[i]` and alignment `elem_align[i]`. As in the lesson, alignments are powers of two and each element's size is a positive multiple of its alignment.

Write `int layout_arrays(const int* elem_size, const int* elem_align, const int* count, int n, int* offsets)`: write each field's offset into `offsets`, and return the struct's size. A struct with no fields has size 1, as in C++, and writes no offsets.

For example, `struct { char tag; short samples[3]; int crc; }` is sizes `{1, 2, 4}`, alignments `{1, 2, 4}`, counts `{1, 3, 1}`: offsets `{0, 2, 8}`, size 12.
--- starter
int layout_arrays(const int* elem_size, const int* elem_align, const int* count, int n, int* offsets) {
    int end = 0;
    for (int i = 0; i < n; i++) {
        offsets[i] = end;
        end += elem_size[i];
    }
    return end;
}
--- solution
static int round_up(int x, int a) {
    return (x + a - 1) / a * a;
}

int layout_arrays(const int* elem_size, const int* elem_align, const int* count, int n, int* offsets) {
    if (n == 0) return 1;
    int end = 0;
    int largest = 1;
    for (int i = 0; i < n; i++) {
        int at = round_up(end, elem_align[i]);
        offsets[i] = at;
        end = at + elem_size[i] * count[i];
        if (elem_align[i] > largest) largest = elem_align[i];
    }
    return round_up(end, largest);
}
--- hint
An array field is placed like any other field. Its alignment is its element's alignment, and its size is the element size times the count.
--- hint
Handle `n == 0` first: return 1. Then lay out as in the lesson, and round the end up to the largest alignment.
--- check test | The example: offsets 0, 2, 8 and size 12
[] { int s[3] = {1, 2, 4}; int a[3] = {1, 2, 4}; int c[3] = {1, 3, 1}; int off[3]; int total = layout_arrays(s, a, c, 3, off); return total == 12 && off[0] == 0 && off[1] == 2 && off[2] == 8; }()
--- check test | The empty struct has size 1, as the compiler says
[] { struct E {}; int off[1] = {77}; return layout_arrays(nullptr, nullptr, nullptr, 0, off) == (int)sizeof(E) && off[0] == 77; }()
--- check test | A name of 5 chars before a double
[] { struct S { char name[5]; double v; char flag; }; int s[3] = {1, 8, 1}; int a[3] = {1, (int)alignof(double), 1}; int c[3] = {5, 1, 1}; int off[3]; int total = layout_arrays(s, a, c, 3, off); return total == (int)sizeof(S) && off[1] == (int)offsetof(S, v) && off[2] == (int)offsetof(S, flag); }()
--- check test | Arrays of ints and shorts, as the compiler lays them out
[] { struct S { short a[3]; int b[2]; char c[7]; short d; }; int s[4] = {2, 4, 1, 2}; int a[4] = {2, 4, 1, 2}; int c[4] = {3, 2, 7, 1}; int off[4]; int total = layout_arrays(s, a, c, 4, off); return total == (int)sizeof(S) && off[0] == (int)offsetof(S, a) && off[1] == (int)offsetof(S, b) && off[2] == (int)offsetof(S, c) && off[3] == (int)offsetof(S, d); }()
--- check test | A single array of chars has no padding
[] { int s[1] = {1}; int a[1] = {1}; int c[1] = {13}; int off[1]; return layout_arrays(s, a, c, 1, off) == 13 && off[0] == 0; }()
--- check test | A nested struct used as array elements
[] { struct P { double x; char k; }; struct S { char tag; P pts[2]; short n; }; int s[3] = {1, (int)sizeof(P), 2}; int a[3] = {1, (int)alignof(P), 2}; int c[3] = {1, 2, 1}; int off[3]; int total = layout_arrays(s, a, c, 3, off); return total == (int)sizeof(S) && off[1] == (int)offsetof(S, pts) && off[2] == (int)offsetof(S, n); }()

+++ practice | Debug: equal readings that compare unequal
--- task
A ground station drops repeated telemetry by comparing each new reading with the last one. This program is meant to report whether two readings have equal fields, but it sometimes says two readings with exactly the same fields are different. Find the bug and fix it.

`bool same_reading(const Reading* a, const Reading* b)` must return `true` exactly when all three fields are equal. Keep the struct as it is. No `main`.
--- starter
#include <cstdint>
#include <cstring>

struct Reading {
    uint8_t sensor;
    uint32_t time;
    int16_t value;
};

bool same_reading(const Reading* a, const Reading* b) {
    return memcmp(a, b, sizeof(Reading)) == 0;
}
--- solution
#include <cstdint>
#include <cstring>

struct Reading {
    uint8_t sensor;
    uint32_t time;
    int16_t value;
};

bool same_reading(const Reading* a, const Reading* b) {
    return a->sensor == b->sensor && a->time == b->time && a->value == b->value;
}
--- hint
How big is `Reading`, and how many of those bytes belong to fields? What is in the rest?
--- hint
`memcmp` compares the padding bytes too, and they hold whatever was in memory before. Compare the three fields one by one.
--- check test | Equal fields, different garbage in the padding
[] { Reading a, b; memset(&a, 0xAA, sizeof a); memset(&b, 0x55, sizeof b); a.sensor = 4; a.time = 1000u; a.value = -7; b.sensor = 4; b.time = 1000u; b.value = -7; return same_reading(&a, &b); }()
--- check test | Different sensors are different
[] { Reading a, b; memset(&a, 0, sizeof a); memset(&b, 0, sizeof b); a.sensor = 1; b.sensor = 2; a.time = b.time = 5u; a.value = b.value = 9; return !same_reading(&a, &b); }()
--- check test | Different times and different values are different
[] { Reading a = {1, 5u, 9}; Reading b = {1, 6u, 9}; Reading c = {1, 5u, 10}; return !same_reading(&a, &b) && !same_reading(&a, &c); }()
--- check test | A reading equals itself
[] { Reading a = {200, 0xFFFFFFFFu, -32768}; return same_reading(&a, &a); }()

+++ practice | How many cache lines a loop touches
--- task
A loop reads one field of each of `n` records. Record `i` starts at byte address `base + i * stride`, and the field occupies the `size` bytes starting `offset` bytes into the record. Memory is fetched in cache lines of `line` bytes: line number `k` holds addresses `k * line` to `k * line + line - 1`.

Write `long long lines_touched(long long base, long long stride, long long offset, long long size, long long n, long long line)`: the number of **different** cache lines that contain at least one byte the loop reads. All arguments are positive except `base` and `offset`, which may be 0, and `offset + size ≤ stride`, so records never overlap. `n` can be up to 10 million. No `main`.

For example, the radii of 4 `Debris` records from the lesson (base 0, stride 32, offset 24, size 4, line 64) sit at bytes 24, 56, 88 and 120, which are in lines 0, 0, 1 and 1: the answer is 2. The same 4 radii stored side by side (stride 4, offset 0, size 4) are bytes 0 to 15: 1 line.
--- starter
long long lines_touched(long long base, long long stride, long long offset, long long size, long long n, long long line) {
    return n;
}
--- solution
long long lines_touched(long long base, long long stride, long long offset, long long size, long long n, long long line) {
    long long count = 0;
    long long last = -1;   // the highest line counted so far
    for (long long i = 0; i < n; i++) {
        long long first_byte = base + i * stride + offset;
        long long last_byte = first_byte + size - 1;
        long long a = first_byte / line;
        long long b = last_byte / line;
        if (a <= last) a = last + 1;   // lines up to `last` are already counted
        if (b >= a) {
            count += b - a + 1;
            last = b;
        }
    }
    return count;
}
--- hint
The bytes one record's field covers run from `base + i * stride + offset` to that plus `size - 1`. Dividing an address by `line` gives its line number, so the field covers every line from the first byte's line to the last byte's.
--- hint
Records come in increasing address order, so the lines do too. Remember the highest line counted so far, and for each field count only the lines above it. That counts each line once, in one pass.
--- hint
Keep everything in `long long`: 10 million records with a stride of 1,000 bytes pass 2 billion.
--- check test | The example: 4 debris radii touch 2 lines
[] { return lines_touched(0, 32, 24, 4, 4, 64) == 2; }()
--- check test | The same radii side by side touch 1 line
[] { return lines_touched(0, 4, 0, 4, 4, 64) == 1; }()
--- check test | A field that straddles two lines counts both
[] { return lines_touched(60, 64, 0, 8, 1, 64) == 2 && lines_touched(56, 64, 0, 8, 1, 64) == 1; }()
--- check test | A stride bigger than a line: one line per record
[] { return lines_touched(0, 128, 8, 4, 1000, 64) == 1000; }()
--- check test | A million records: 32-byte records against a column of floats
[] { return lines_touched(0, 32, 24, 4, 1000000, 64) == 500000 && lines_touched(0, 4, 0, 4, 1000000, 64) == 62500; }()
--- check test | An unaligned base
[] { return lines_touched(10, 4, 0, 4, 16, 64) == 2 && lines_touched(1000, 12, 4, 8, 100, 64) == 20; }()
--- check test | Ten million records with a large stride fit in long long
[] { return lines_touched(0, 1000, 0, 8, 10000000, 64) == 10000000; }()

+++ question | Why the tail padding
--- ask
`struct S { double d; char c; };` has 9 bytes of data, but `sizeof(S)` is 16. Why does the compiler add 7 bytes at the end?
--- choice
Because every struct must be a multiple of 8 bytes long.
--- choice correct
So that in an array of `S`, where element `i` starts `i * sizeof(S)` bytes in, every element's `d` is still at a multiple of 8.
--- choice
So that `c` has room to grow if the struct is changed later.
--- choice
Because `malloc` cannot return blocks whose size is not a multiple of 8.
--- why
Arrays have no gaps, so the size must be a multiple of the struct's alignment (here 8, from `double`) for every element to start aligned. A struct of three `char`s has alignment 1 and size 3: there is no rule about multiples of 8.

+++ question | The smallest order
--- ask
A struct has the fields `char a; double b; char c; int d;` in some order. What is the smallest `sizeof` any order of these four fields can give, on a machine where `double` has size and alignment 8 and `int` 4?
--- answer
16
--- why
The data is 1 + 8 + 1 + 4 = 14 bytes, and the size must be a multiple of the largest alignment, 8: at least 16. Decreasing alignment, `double b; int d; char a; char c;`, puts the fields at 0, 8, 12 and 13 with no gaps, ends at 14, and rounds up to 16.
=== sys-09 | Function pointers: callbacks, dispatch tables and a hand-built qsort
--- teach
Last lesson was about where data sits in memory. Code sits in memory too: every function is a run of machine instructions starting at some address. C lets you store that address in a variable, pass it to another function, and call through it. That one feature gives C most of what other languages get from interfaces, virtual methods and lambdas. This lesson covers **function pointers**, the **callbacks** and **dispatch tables** built from them, and the most famous user of both, the library sort `qsort`, which you will rebuild yourself.

### A function has an address

A **function pointer** is a variable that holds the address of a function. Its type says which functions it may point at: their parameter types and return type.

```cpp
#include <cstdio>

double to_fahrenheit(double c) { return c * 9.0 / 5.0 + 32.0; }
double to_kelvin(double c) { return c + 273.15; }

int main() {
    double (*convert)(double) = to_fahrenheit;   // convert points at to_fahrenheit
    printf("%.1f\n", convert(100.0));            // 212.0
    convert = to_kelvin;                         // now it points at to_kelvin
    printf("%.2f\n", convert(100.0));            // 373.15
    return 0;
}
```

Read `double (*convert)(double)` from the name outwards, the [[inside-out rule|inside-out]]: `convert` is a pointer (`*convert`), to a function taking a `double` (`(double)`), that returns a `double`. The parentheses around `*convert` matter: without them, `double *convert(double)` declares a function that returns a `double*`.

Three more facts:

- A function's name, used as a value, turns into its address, the way an array's name turns into a pointer to its first element. `convert = to_kelvin` and `convert = &to_kelvin` mean the same.
- Calling through the pointer is written like a normal call. `(*convert)(100.0)` also works and means the same.
- The types must match exactly. Calling a function through a pointer of a different function type is undefined behaviour, even if it happens to work.

The syntax gets hard to read quickly, so give the type a name, with `using` (C++) or `typedef` (C):

```cpp fragment
using Convert = double (*)(double);      // C++: Convert is "pointer to function (double) -> double"
typedef double (*ConvertC)(double);      // C: the same, older syntax
Convert table_of_units[2] = {to_fahrenheit, to_kelvin};
```

### Callbacks: handing behaviour to a function

A **callback** is a function you pass to another function, for it to call back. The function receiving it does the general work; the callback supplies the one step that varies.

```cpp fragment
void apply_all(double* values, int n, double (*f)(double)) {
    for (int i = 0; i < n; i++) values[i] = f(values[i]);
}
// apply_all(temps, 24, to_kelvin);   converts every reading in place
```

`apply_all` does not know or care what `f` does. The same loop converts units, clamps outliers or applies a calibration, depending on the callback.

### Context: the void* that travels with the callback

Often a callback needs some data of its own: a threshold, a counter, a place to put results. In C++ you would write a lambda that captures it. A C function pointer is just an address with no room for data, and only a lambda that captures nothing converts to one.

The C answer is a **context pointer**: the function that takes a callback also takes a `void*`, and passes it, untouched, to every call of the callback. The caller points it at whatever the callback needs, and the callback converts it back to the right type.

```cpp
#include <cstdio>

struct Stats {
    int count;
    double sum;
    double largest;
};

void each_reading(const double* v, int n, void (*visit)(double, void*), void* ctx) {
    for (int i = 0; i < n; i++) visit(v[i], ctx);   // ctx is passed along, never looked at
}

void add_to_stats(double x, void* ctx) {
    Stats* s = (Stats*)ctx;                          // we know what the caller passed
    if (s->count == 0 || x > s->largest) s->largest = x;
    s->count++;
    s->sum += x;
}

int main() {
    double temps[4] = {21.5, 19.0, 23.25, 20.0};
    Stats s = {0, 0.0, 0.0};
    each_reading(temps, 4, add_to_stats, &s);
    printf("%d %.2f %.2f\n", s.count, s.sum / s.count, s.largest);   // 4 20.94 23.25
    return 0;
}
```

`each_reading` never looks inside `ctx`, so it works for any kind of context. The price is safety: the cast in `add_to_stats` is a promise the compiler cannot check. Pass the wrong pointer and the callback reads garbage. This pairing of a function pointer and a `void*` is the C form of a [[closure|closure]], and you will see it in every C library that takes callbacks.

### Dispatch tables

A **dispatch table** is an array of function pointers, indexed by a number that says which one to run. Flight software receives commands as numeric codes; a table turns each code into its handler in one step:

```cpp
#include <cstdio>

int add(int a, int b) { return a + b; }
int sub(int a, int b) { return a - b; }
int mul(int a, int b) { return a * b; }

using BinOp = int (*)(int, int);
const BinOp ops[3] = {add, sub, mul};   // opcode 0, 1, 2

int execute(int opcode, int a, int b, int* out) {
    if (opcode < 0 || opcode >= 3) return -1;   // never index with an unchecked number
    *out = ops[opcode](a, b);
    return 0;
}

int main() {
    int r = 0;
    execute(2, 6, 7, &r);
    printf("%d\n", r);                         // 42
    printf("%d\n", execute(9, 1, 1, &r));      // -1: no such opcode
    return 0;
}
```

The range check is not optional. `ops[9]` reads whatever 8 bytes follow the table, and calling that as a function jumps to an address an attacker may control: it is a classic way to take over a program.

Why a table instead of a `switch`? For a fixed set of cases, a `switch` is just as fast, and the compiler often turns it into a table itself. A table wins when the set changes while the program runs: a driver fills in its own functions, a plugin registers a handler, a test swaps one in. The operating system's own tables work like this: the Linux kernel describes every kind of file with a struct of function pointers (open, read, write, and so on), and the [[interrupt vector table|vectors]] of a microcontroller is an array of function addresses the hardware itself indexes. C++ virtual functions are built the same way, with a hidden table per class called a [[vtable|vtable]].

### qsort: a sort for any type

The C library's sort, in `<cstdlib>`, is

```cpp fragment
void qsort(void* base, size_t count, size_t size, int (*compar)(const void*, const void*));
```

It sorts `count` elements of `size` bytes each, starting at `base`. It knows nothing about their type. To decide the order it calls `compar` with the addresses of two elements, and the comparator returns a negative number if the first should come first, zero if they are equal in the order, and a positive number if the second should come first.

```cpp
#include <cstdio>
#include <cstdlib>

int by_value(const void* a, const void* b) {
    int x = *(const int*)a;
    int y = *(const int*)b;
    return (x > y) - (x < y);   // 1, 0 or -1, and it cannot overflow
}

int main() {
    int burns[6] = {40, -3, 17, 0, 17, 5};
    qsort(burns, 6, sizeof burns[0], by_value);
    for (int i = 0; i < 6; i++) printf("%d ", burns[i]);   // -3 0 5 17 17 40
    printf("\n");
    return 0;
}
```

The comparator receives **addresses of elements**. When the elements are themselves pointers, such as an array of `const char*` names, each argument is the address of a `const char*`, so it is really a `const char* const*`. The comparator must follow it once to reach the pointer, and only then pass the texts to `strcmp`. Passing the arguments straight to `strcmp` compares the bytes of the pointers themselves: a classic bug that compiles without a warning.

**The comparator's contract.** A sort is only correct if the comparator describes a consistent order. For all elements a, b and c:

1. cmp(a, a) is 0;
2. cmp(a, b) and cmp(b, a) have opposite signs (or are both 0);
3. if a comes before b and b before c, then a comes before c (**transitivity**), and the same for "equal".

A sort's correctness proof uses these facts at every step. For example, insertion sort stops shifting as soon as it finds an element not greater than the one it holds, and it is transitivity that makes that element not greater than everything before it. Break the contract and the sort may return garbage, loop, or worse: in 2024 researchers found that glibc's `qsort` could [[write outside the array|qsort-bug]] when given a comparator that is not transitive. Comparing floating-point values with `<` breaks it, because a NaN is neither less than, greater than, nor equal to anything.

### Building a generic sort

How can one function sort ints, doubles and structs? It never looks at the elements. It only needs three things, and all three work on raw bytes:

- **Find element i:** `(char*)base + i * size`. Converting to `char*` makes pointer arithmetic count in bytes, so stepping by `size` bytes reaches the next element. This is the stride from the pointer arithmetic lesson.
- **Compare two elements:** call the comparator with their addresses.
- **Move an element:** `memcpy` its `size` bytes.

Here is insertion sort, rewritten for any type:

```cpp
#include <cstdio>
#include <cstring>

void insertion_sort_any(void* base, size_t n, size_t size, int (*cmp)(const void*, const void*)) {
    char* a = (char*)base;                  // bytes, so we can step by size
    char held[256];                         // this sketch handles elements up to 256 bytes
    for (size_t i = 1; i < n; i++) {
        memcpy(held, a + i * size, size);   // take element i out
        size_t j = i;
        while (j > 0 && cmp(a + (j - 1) * size, held) > 0) {
            memcpy(a + j * size, a + (j - 1) * size, size);   // shift one element right
            j--;
        }
        memcpy(a + j * size, held, size);   // drop it into the gap
    }
}

struct Pass { int orbit; char station; };

int by_orbit(const void* a, const void* b) {
    int x = ((const Pass*)a)->orbit;
    int y = ((const Pass*)b)->orbit;
    return (x > y) - (x < y);
}

int main() {
    Pass p[5] = {{3, 'A'}, {1, 'B'}, {3, 'C'}, {2, 'D'}, {1, 'E'}};
    insertion_sort_any(p, 5, sizeof(Pass), by_orbit);
    for (int i = 0; i < 5; i++) printf("%d%c ", p[i].orbit, p[i].station);   // 1B 1E 2D 3A 3C
    printf("\n");
    return 0;
}
```

The proof of correctness is the insertion sort proof from DSA, word for word: only the way elements are reached has changed. It is **stable**: the loop shifts only while the earlier element is strictly greater, so equal orbits keep their order (B before E, A before C). And it costs what insertion sort costs: Θ(n²) comparisons in the worst case, and Θ(n²) moves of `size` bytes each. The library `qsort` uses an O(n log n) method instead; glibc's version long used a merge sort whenever it could get the extra memory.

### What it costs

A call through a function pointer is an **indirect call**: the CPU must load the address before it can jump. That costs a few nanoseconds more than a direct call, but the bigger cost is that the compiler cannot see which function will run, so it cannot **inline** it (copy its body into the loop and optimise the two together). This is why C++'s `std::sort`, which takes the comparison as a template parameter the compiler can inline, is often two or three times faster than `qsort` on the same data. The complexity is the same; only the constant changes.

A dispatch table lookup is O(1): one index and one indirect call, whatever the number of entries.

### Where it is used

- **The C library:** `qsort` and `bsearch` take comparators; `atexit` takes functions to run when the program ends; `signal` takes a handler for events such as Ctrl-C.
- **Operating systems:** device drivers fill in tables of function pointers; system calls are dispatched through a table indexed by the system call number.
- **Event loops and GUIs:** "when this button is pressed, call this function, with this context".
- **Flight software:** command dispatchers map command codes to handlers, and schedulers keep tables of periodic tasks, each a function pointer and a rate.

**Watch out:** the comparator `return *(const int*)a - *(const int*)b;`. It looks neat, and it overflows: for a = 2,000,000,000 and b = −2,000,000,000 the true difference does not fit in an `int`, and the result wraps to a negative number, so the sort puts the bigger number first. Signed overflow is undefined behaviour, the subject of the next lesson. Return `(x > y) - (x < y)`, or compare with `if`s.

::: context inside-out Reading C declarations
C declarations are written the way the variable is used. `double (*convert)(double)` says: if you write `(*convert)(1.0)`, you get a `double`. To read one, start at the name, go right while you can, then left, and step out of each pair of parentheses. `int (*ops[3])(int, int)` is: ops is an array of 3, of pointers, to functions taking two ints, returning int. When a declaration takes more than a few seconds to read, give the type a name with `using`.
:::

::: context closure Code plus the data it needs
A closure is a function bundled with the variables it uses from where it was created. A C++ lambda `[limit](int v) { return v > limit; }` is one: an object holding `limit`, with a call operator. C has no closures, so C code passes the two halves separately: a function pointer, and a `void*` to the data. Libraries such as POSIX threads (`pthread_create`) and the GTK toolkit are built entirely on this pairing.
:::

::: context vectors The table the hardware reads
When a microcontroller is reset, or when a timer, a serial port or a fault interrupts it, the hardware looks up the address of the handler in a table at a fixed place in memory: the interrupt vector table. On ARM Cortex-M chips, which run many small satellites, entry 1 of that table is the reset handler, the first code that ever runs, and later entries handle each interrupt. In C it is written as an array of function pointers placed at that address.
:::

::: context vtable How virtual functions work
For each class with virtual functions, a C++ compiler builds a table of function pointers, the vtable, and puts a hidden pointer to it at the start of each object. Calling a virtual method loads the object's vtable pointer, loads the right entry, and calls through it: two loads and an indirect call. It is the dispatch table of this lesson, generated for you.
:::

::: context qsort-bug When a bad comparator becomes a security hole
In January 2024 the security company Qualys reported that glibc's qsort, in its fallback path, trusted the comparator's answers so fully that a non-transitive comparator could make it write past the end of the array. Programs that sorted attacker-controlled data with a careless comparator were exposed. The glibc developers changed the sort, but the lesson stands: a comparator must describe a real order.
:::
--- task
Write your own `qsort`, with the same interface, that is fast and stable. No `main`.

`void my_qsort(void* base, size_t n, size_t size, int (*cmp)(const void*, const void*))` sorts the `n` elements of `size` bytes each at `base`, so that `cmp` never returns a positive number for an element and the one after it.

- It must make O(n log n) comparisons on every input: use **merge sort**, as in DSA. The checks count the calls to `cmp`: at most n·⌈log₂ n⌉ of them.
- It must be **stable**: elements that compare equal keep their original order.
- It must work for any element size. Take a scratch buffer of `n * size` bytes from `malloc` for the merging, and free it before returning.
- With `n` of 0 or 1 it does nothing, and it may be given a null `base` when `n` is 0.

Do not call the library `qsort` or `std::sort`.
--- starter
#include <cstddef>
#include <cstdlib>
#include <cstring>

void my_qsort(void* base, size_t n, size_t size, int (*cmp)(const void*, const void*)) {
    // not written yet
}
--- solution
#include <cstddef>
#include <cstdlib>
#include <cstring>

using Cmp = int (*)(const void*, const void*);

// Sorts the n elements at a, using tmp (room for n elements) for merging.
static void merge_sort(char* a, char* tmp, size_t n, size_t size, Cmp cmp) {
    if (n < 2) return;
    size_t mid = n / 2;
    merge_sort(a, tmp, mid, size, cmp);
    merge_sort(a + mid * size, tmp, n - mid, size, cmp);
    size_t i = 0, j = mid, k = 0;
    while (i < mid && j < n) {
        // take from the right only when it is strictly smaller: that keeps the sort stable
        if (cmp(a + j * size, a + i * size) < 0) {
            memcpy(tmp + k * size, a + j * size, size);
            j++;
        } else {
            memcpy(tmp + k * size, a + i * size, size);
            i++;
        }
        k++;
    }
    // the unused left elements go to the end; unused right elements are already in place
    memcpy(tmp + k * size, a + i * size, (mid - i) * size);
    k += mid - i;
    memcpy(a, tmp, k * size);
}

static void swap_bytes(char* p, char* q, size_t size) {
    for (size_t b = 0; b < size; b++) {
        char t = p[b];
        p[b] = q[b];
        q[b] = t;
    }
}

void my_qsort(void* base, size_t n, size_t size, int (*cmp)(const void*, const void*)) {
    if (n < 2) return;
    char* a = (char*)base;
    char* tmp = (char*)malloc(n * size);
    if (tmp == nullptr) {
        // no memory for merging: fall back to a slow but correct, stable in-place sort
        for (size_t i = 1; i < n; i++)
            for (size_t j = i; j > 0 && cmp(a + (j - 1) * size, a + j * size) > 0; j--)
                swap_bytes(a + (j - 1) * size, a + j * size, size);
        return;
    }
    merge_sort(a, tmp, n, size, cmp);
    free(tmp);
}
--- hint
Work on `char*` so that element `i` is at `a + i * size`. A recursive helper sorts the left half and the right half, then merges them into the scratch buffer with `memcpy`, and copies the result back.
--- hint
For stability, when the two front elements compare equal, take the one from the left half: take from the right only when `cmp(right, left) < 0`.
--- hint
Allocate the scratch buffer once in `my_qsort`, pass it down to every call, and free it at the end. When one half runs out, copy what is left of the other; if it is the right half, it is already where it belongs.
--- check test | Six ints, with a repeat
[] { int a[6] = {40, -3, 17, 0, 17, 5}; auto c = [](const void* x, const void* y) -> int { int p = *(const int*)x, q = *(const int*)y; return (p > q) - (p < q); }; my_qsort(a, 6, sizeof a[0], c); int w[6] = {-3, 0, 5, 17, 17, 40}; for (int i = 0; i < 6; i++) if (a[i] != w[i]) return false; return true; }()
--- check test | Zero and one elements, and a null base with n = 0

--- check test | Doubles in decreasing order, with the comparator reversed

--- check test | Names: an array of const char*

--- check test | 40-byte records sorted by key, and stable
[] { struct R { int key; int seq; char pad[32]; }; R a[200]; unsigned s = 3u; for (int i = 0; i < 200; i++) { s = s * 1103515245u + 12345u; a[i].key = (int)((s >> 16) % 10); a[i].seq = i; for (int k = 0; k < 32; k++) a[i].pad[k] = (char)(i + k); } auto c = [](const void* x, const void* y) -> int { int p = ((const R*)x)->key, q = ((const R*)y)->key; return (p > q) - (p < q); }; my_qsort(a, 200, sizeof(R), c); for (int i = 0; i < 200; i++) { if (a[i].pad[5] != (char)(a[i].seq + 5)) return false; if (i && (a[i - 1].key > a[i].key || (a[i - 1].key == a[i].key && a[i - 1].seq > a[i].seq))) return false; } return true; }()

--- check source absent | Does not call the library sorts

+++ practice | Counting with a predicate
--- task
Write three functions. No `main`.

- `int count_if(const int* values, int n, bool (*pred)(int))`: how many of the `n` values make `pred` return `true`. With `n` equal to 0 the answer is 0.
- `bool is_negative(int v)`: whether `v` is below zero.
- `bool is_even(int v)`: whether `v` is even. Remember that negative numbers can be even too: -4 is even, -3 is not.

For example, with the values `{3, -4, 0, -7, 8}`, `count_if(values, 5, is_negative)` is 2 and `count_if(values, 5, is_even)` is 3.
--- starter
int count_if(const int* values, int n, bool (*pred)(int)) {
    return 0;
}

bool is_negative(int v) {
    return false;
}

bool is_even(int v) {
    return false;
}
--- solution
int count_if(const int* values, int n, bool (*pred)(int)) {
    int count = 0;
    for (int i = 0; i < n; i++)
        if (pred(values[i])) count++;
    return count;
}

bool is_negative(int v) {
    return v < 0;
}

bool is_even(int v) {
    return v % 2 == 0;
}
--- hint
Inside `count_if`, call `pred(values[i])` for each value, like any function, and count the `true`s.
--- hint
For `is_even`, `v % 2` is 0 for every even number, negative ones included; for odd negative numbers it is -1, not 1, so test for 0 rather than for 1.
--- check test | The example: 2 negative, 3 even
[] { int v[5] = {3, -4, 0, -7, 8}; return count_if(v, 5, is_negative) == 2 && count_if(v, 5, is_even) == 3; }()
--- check test | Negative odd and even numbers
[] { return is_even(-4) && !is_even(-3) && is_even(0) && !is_even(7) && is_negative(-1) && !is_negative(0); }()
--- check test | An empty array counts 0
[] { int v[1] = {-5}; return count_if(v, 0, is_negative) == 0; }()
--- check test | Works with any predicate, such as one from the checks
[] { int v[6] = {10, 200, 3000, 5, 999, 1000}; auto big = [](int x) -> bool { return x >= 1000; }; return count_if(v, 6, big) == 2; }()
--- check test | Extremes
[] { int v[3] = {-2147483647 - 1, 2147483647, 0}; return count_if(v, 3, is_even) == 2 && count_if(v, 3, is_negative) == 1; }()

+++ practice | A callback with a context
--- task
Write two functions. No `main`.

- `int filter_into(const int* in, int n, int* out, bool (*keep)(int, void*), void* ctx)`: copy every value of `in` for which `keep(value, ctx)` is `true` into `out`, in their original order, and return how many were copied. Pass `ctx` to every call of `keep`, unchanged.
- `bool in_range(int v, void* ctx)`: `ctx` points at a `Range`, defined below. Return whether `lo ≤ v ≤ hi`.

```cpp fragment
struct Range {
    int lo;
    int hi;
};
```

Include the struct exactly as shown. For example, filtering `{5, 12, -3, 8, 30}` with `in_range` and the range 0 to 10 copies `{5, 8}` and returns 2.
--- starter
struct Range {
    int lo;
    int hi;
};

int filter_into(const int* in, int n, int* out, bool (*keep)(int, void*), void* ctx) {
    return 0;
}

bool in_range(int v, void* ctx) {
    return false;
}
--- solution
struct Range {
    int lo;
    int hi;
};

int filter_into(const int* in, int n, int* out, bool (*keep)(int, void*), void* ctx) {
    int count = 0;
    for (int i = 0; i < n; i++)
        if (keep(in[i], ctx)) out[count++] = in[i];
    return count;
}

bool in_range(int v, void* ctx) {
    const Range* r = (const Range*)ctx;
    return r->lo <= v && v <= r->hi;
}
--- hint
`filter_into` never looks inside `ctx`: it hands it to `keep` with every value. Keep a count of how many you have copied; it is also the next free place in `out`.
--- hint
In `in_range`, convert `ctx` back to `const Range*`, then compare `v` with both ends, including them.
--- check test | The example: 5 and 8 are kept
[] { int in[5] = {5, 12, -3, 8, 30}; int out[5] = {0}; Range r = {0, 10}; int n = filter_into(in, 5, out, in_range, &r); return n == 2 && out[0] == 5 && out[1] == 8; }()
--- check test | Both ends of the range are included
[] { int in[4] = {-1, 0, 10, 11}; int out[4]; Range r = {0, 10}; int n = filter_into(in, 4, out, in_range, &r); return n == 2 && out[0] == 0 && out[1] == 10; }()
--- check test | The context reaches every call unchanged
[] { static void* seen[4]; static int k; k = 0; int in[4] = {1, 2, 3, 4}; int out[4]; int token = 0; auto spy = [](int v, void* c) -> bool { seen[k++] = c; return v % 2 == 0; }; int n = filter_into(in, 4, out, spy, &token); return n == 2 && out[0] == 2 && out[1] == 4 && k == 4 && seen[0] == &token && seen[3] == &token; }()
--- check test | Nothing kept, and an empty input
[] { int in[3] = {100, 200, 300}; int out[3] = {7, 7, 7}; Range r = {0, 10}; return filter_into(in, 3, out, in_range, &r) == 0 && filter_into(in, 0, out, in_range, &r) == 0 && out[0] == 7; }()
--- check test | A range of one value, and negative ranges
[] { int in[6] = {-5, -4, -3, 7, 7, -4}; int out[6]; Range a = {7, 7}; Range b = {-4, -3}; int na = filter_into(in, 6, out, in_range, &a); int ok = na == 2 && out[0] == 7; int nb = filter_into(in, 6, out, in_range, &b); return ok && nb == 3 && out[0] == -4 && out[1] == -3 && out[2] == -4; }()

+++ practice | Commands through a table
--- task
A rover on a grid obeys a script of commands separated by single spaces. Write it with a dispatch table. No `main`.

```cpp fragment
struct Rover {
    int x;
    int y;
    int heading;   // 0 north (y + 1), 1 east (x + 1), 2 south (y - 1), 3 west (x - 1)
};
```

The commands are:

- `F` followed by a number: move forward that many cells in the heading's direction, for example `F12`;
- `B` followed by a number: move backward that many cells;
- `L`: turn left (north becomes west); `R`: turn right (north becomes east).

Write `int run_script(Rover* r, const char* script)`. It runs the commands in order and returns how many it ran. If it meets a command letter it does not know, it stops there and returns -1, leaving the rover as the earlier commands left it. An empty script runs 0 commands. Numbers are 1 to 999 with no sign, and `L` and `R` never have one.

Use a table: an array of structs, each holding a command letter and a pointer to its handler, `void handler(Rover*, int amount)`, where the amount is 0 for `L` and `R`. `run_script` finds the letter in the table and calls through the pointer. Include the struct exactly as shown.

For example, starting at (0, 0) facing north, `F3 R F2 L B1` runs 5 commands and ends at (2, 2), facing north.
--- starter
struct Rover {
    int x;
    int y;
    int heading;
};

int run_script(Rover* r, const char* script) {
    return 0;
}
--- solution
struct Rover {
    int x;
    int y;
    int heading;
};

static const int DX[4] = {0, 1, 0, -1};
static const int DY[4] = {1, 0, -1, 0};

static void forward(Rover* r, int amount) {
    r->x += DX[r->heading] * amount;
    r->y += DY[r->heading] * amount;
}

static void backward(Rover* r, int amount) {
    forward(r, -amount);
}

static void turn_left(Rover* r, int amount) {
    r->heading = (r->heading + 3) % 4;
}

static void turn_right(Rover* r, int amount) {
    r->heading = (r->heading + 1) % 4;
}

struct Command {
    char letter;
    void (*run)(Rover*, int);
};

static const Command COMMANDS[4] = {{'F', forward}, {'B', backward}, {'L', turn_left}, {'R', turn_right}};

int run_script(Rover* r, const char* script) {
    int done = 0;
    const char* p = script;
    while (*p != '\0') {
        char letter = *p++;
        int amount = 0;
        while (*p >= '0' && *p <= '9') amount = amount * 10 + (*p++ - '0');
        const Command* found = nullptr;
        for (int i = 0; i < 4; i++)
            if (COMMANDS[i].letter == letter) found = &COMMANDS[i];
        if (found == nullptr) return -1;
        found->run(r, amount);
        done++;
        if (*p == ' ') p++;
    }
    return done;
}
--- hint
Write four small handlers with the same type, `void (Rover*, int)`, and a `Command` struct with a `char` and a function pointer. An array of four `Command`s is the table.
--- hint
Walk the script with a pointer: read the letter, then any digits into a number (as in the strings lesson), then skip one space. Look the letter up in the table; if it is not there, return -1 before changing anything else.
--- hint
Two small arrays, `DX` and `DY`, indexed by heading, make forward one line. Turning left is `(heading + 3) % 4`, which avoids a negative remainder.
--- check test | The example ends at (2, 2) facing north
[] { Rover r = {0, 0, 0}; int n = run_script(&r, "F3 R F2 L B1"); return n == 5 && r.x == 2 && r.y == 2 && r.heading == 0; }()
--- check test | Turning all the way round, both ways
[] { Rover r = {0, 0, 0}; int n = run_script(&r, "L L L L R R R"); return n == 7 && r.heading == 3 && r.x == 0 && r.y == 0; }()
--- check test | Moving south and west gives negative positions
[] { Rover r = {0, 0, 2}; int n = run_script(&r, "F10 R F999"); return n == 3 && r.y == -10 && r.x == -999 && r.heading == 3; }()
--- check test | An unknown command stops the script with -1
[] { Rover r = {5, 5, 1}; int n = run_script(&r, "F2 X F3"); return n == -1 && r.x == 7 && r.y == 5; }()
--- check test | An empty script runs nothing
[] { Rover r = {1, 2, 3}; return run_script(&r, "") == 0 && r.x == 1 && r.y == 2 && r.heading == 3; }()
--- check test | One long script of 300 commands
[] { static char s[2000]; int k = 0; for (int i = 0; i < 100; i++) { s[k++] = 'F'; s[k++] = '1'; s[k++] = '2'; s[k++] = ' '; s[k++] = 'R'; s[k++] = ' '; s[k++] = 'B'; s[k++] = '5'; s[k++] = ' '; } s[k - 1] = '\0'; Rover r = {0, 0, 0}; int n = run_script(&r, s); return n == 300 && r.x == 0 && r.y == 0 && r.heading == 0; }()
--- check source | Uses a function pointer
\(\s*\*\s*\w*\s*\)\s*\(

+++ practice | Comparators that never overflow
--- task
Write three comparators for the library `qsort`, each following the contract from the lesson. No `main`.

- `int cmp_ll_desc(const void* a, const void* b)`: for an array of `long long`, sorting it from largest to smallest. It must work for every value, including the largest and smallest `long long`.
- `int cmp_name(const void* a, const void* b)`: for an array of `const char*` texts, sorting them in `strcmp` order.
- `int cmp_nan_last(const void* a, const void* b)`: for an array of `double`, sorting numbers from smallest to largest with every NaN after all the numbers. Two NaNs compare equal. Test for a NaN with `x != x`, which is true only for a NaN.

For example, sorting `{3, -9223372036854775807 - 1, 9223372036854775807}` with `cmp_ll_desc` gives `{9223372036854775807, 3, -9223372036854775808}`.
--- starter
#include <cstdlib>
#include <cstring>

int cmp_ll_desc(const void* a, const void* b) {
    return (int)(*(const long long*)b - *(const long long*)a);
}

int cmp_name(const void* a, const void* b) {
    return strcmp((const char*)a, (const char*)b);
}

int cmp_nan_last(const void* a, const void* b) {
    double x = *(const double*)a;
    double y = *(const double*)b;
    return (x > y) - (x < y);
}
--- solution
#include <cstdlib>
#include <cstring>

int cmp_ll_desc(const void* a, const void* b) {
    long long x = *(const long long*)a;
    long long y = *(const long long*)b;
    return (x < y) - (x > y);
}

int cmp_name(const void* a, const void* b) {
    const char* x = *(const char* const*)a;
    const char* y = *(const char* const*)b;
    return strcmp(x, y);
}

int cmp_nan_last(const void* a, const void* b) {
    double x = *(const double*)a;
    double y = *(const double*)b;
    bool xn = x != x;
    bool yn = y != y;
    if (xn || yn) return (int)xn - (int)yn;   // NaN after numbers; two NaNs are equal
    return (x > y) - (x < y);
}
--- hint
Never subtract to compare. `(x < y) - (x > y)` gives 1 when x should come after y in a decreasing order, -1 when before, and 0 when equal, for every value.
--- hint
`qsort` hands `cmp_name` the addresses of two array elements, and each element is a `const char*`. Follow each address once, as `*(const char* const*)a`, before calling `strcmp`.
--- hint
In `cmp_nan_last`, deal with NaNs first: if either is a NaN, the NaN goes after, and two NaNs are equal. Only when both are numbers compare them with `<` and `>`.
--- check test | The example: extremes sorted from largest to smallest
[] { long long a[3] = {3, -9223372036854775807LL - 1, 9223372036854775807LL}; qsort(a, 3, sizeof a[0], cmp_ll_desc); return a[0] == 9223372036854775807LL && a[1] == 3 && a[2] == -9223372036854775807LL - 1; }()
--- check test | cmp_ll_desc gives the right signs for far-apart values
[] { long long big = 9000000000000000000LL, small = -9000000000000000000LL; return cmp_ll_desc(&big, &small) < 0 && cmp_ll_desc(&small, &big) > 0 && cmp_ll_desc(&big, &big) == 0; }()
--- check test | Names in strcmp order
[] { const char* a[5] = {"thruster", "antenna", "battery", "antenna2", "Zeta"}; qsort(a, 5, sizeof a[0], cmp_name); return strcmp(a[0], "Zeta") == 0 && strcmp(a[1], "antenna") == 0 && strcmp(a[2], "antenna2") == 0 && strcmp(a[3], "battery") == 0 && strcmp(a[4], "thruster") == 0; }()
--- check test | NaNs go last and numbers are sorted
[] { double nan = std::nan(""); double a[7] = {2.0, nan, -1.5, nan, 0.0, 100.0, -1.5}; qsort(a, 7, sizeof a[0], cmp_nan_last); return a[0] == -1.5 && a[1] == -1.5 && a[2] == 0.0 && a[3] == 2.0 && a[4] == 100.0 && a[5] != a[5] && a[6] != a[6]; }()
--- check test | cmp_nan_last keeps the contract for NaN pairs
[] { double nan = std::nan(""); double one = 1.0; return cmp_nan_last(&nan, &nan) == 0 && cmp_nan_last(&nan, &one) > 0 && cmp_nan_last(&one, &nan) < 0; }()
--- check test | 1000 random long longs sorted from largest to smallest
[] { static long long a[1000]; unsigned long long s = 7; for (int i = 0; i < 1000; i++) { s = s * 6364136223846793005ULL + 1442695040888963407ULL; a[i] = (long long)s; } qsort(a, 1000, sizeof a[0], cmp_ll_desc); for (int i = 1; i < 1000; i++) if (a[i - 1] < a[i]) return false; return true; }()

+++ practice | Debug: the maximum that looks at the wrong bytes
--- task
This generic function should return the address of the largest of `n` elements of `size` bytes each, using the comparator `cmp` (the same kind `qsort` takes). If several elements are equally largest it returns the first of them, and with `n` equal to 0 it returns a null pointer. It works on arrays of `char`, but on arrays of `int` or of structs it returns wrong answers. Find the bug and fix it.

The function is `const void* find_max(const void* base, size_t n, size_t size, int (*cmp)(const void*, const void*))`. No `main`.
--- starter
#include <cstddef>

const void* find_max(const void* base, size_t n, size_t size, int (*cmp)(const void*, const void*)) {
    if (n == 0) return nullptr;
    const char* best = (const char*)base;
    for (size_t i = 1; i < n; i++) {
        const char* p = (const char*)base + i;
        if (cmp(p, best) > 0) best = p;
    }
    return best;
}
--- solution
#include <cstddef>

const void* find_max(const void* base, size_t n, size_t size, int (*cmp)(const void*, const void*)) {
    if (n == 0) return nullptr;
    const char* best = (const char*)base;
    for (size_t i = 1; i < n; i++) {
        const char* p = (const char*)base + i * size;
        if (cmp(p, best) > 0) best = p;
    }
    return best;
}
--- hint
`base` has been converted to `const char*`. How many bytes does adding `i` move it, and how many bytes long is one element?
--- hint
Element `i` starts `i * size` bytes after `base`. With `size` equal to 1, as for `char`, the two are the same, which is why the bug hides there.
--- check test | Largest of five ints
[] { int a[5] = {4, 90, -2, 17, 3}; auto c = [](const void* x, const void* y) -> int { int p = *(const int*)x, q = *(const int*)y; return (p > q) - (p < q); }; return find_max(a, 5, sizeof(int), c) == &a[1]; }()
--- check test | Ties give the first of the largest
[] { int a[4] = {8, 1, 8, 8}; auto c = [](const void* x, const void* y) -> int { int p = *(const int*)x, q = *(const int*)y; return (p > q) - (p < q); }; return find_max(a, 4, sizeof(int), c) == &a[0]; }()
--- check test | A struct array, by one field
[] { struct T { double temp; int id; }; T a[4] = {{20.5, 1}, {-3.0, 2}, {31.25, 3}, {31.0, 4}}; auto c = [](const void* x, const void* y) -> int { double p = ((const T*)x)->temp, q = ((const T*)y)->temp; return (p > q) - (p < q); }; const T* m = (const T*)find_max(a, 4, sizeof(T), c); return m == &a[2] && m->id == 3; }()
--- check test | Zero elements give a null pointer, one element gives itself
[] { int a[1] = {5}; auto c = [](const void* x, const void* y) -> int { int p = *(const int*)x, q = *(const int*)y; return (p > q) - (p < q); }; return find_max(a, 0, sizeof(int), c) == nullptr && find_max(a, 1, sizeof(int), c) == &a[0]; }()

+++ practice | Timers that call back
--- task
A flight computer keeps timers: "at time 500, call this function with this context". Write a small timer queue. No `main`.

```cpp fragment
using TimerFn = void (*)(void* ctx);

struct Timer {
    long long due;   // when to fire
    TimerFn fn;
    void* ctx;
    int seq;         // the order it was added in
};

struct TimerQueue {
    Timer items[64];
    int count;
    int next_seq;
};
```

Include these definitions exactly as shown, and write:

- `void tq_init(TimerQueue* q)`: an empty queue.
- `bool tq_add(TimerQueue* q, long long due, TimerFn fn, void* ctx)`: add a timer and return `true`, or return `false` and change nothing if the queue already holds 64.
- `int tq_run_due(TimerQueue* q, long long now)`: remove every timer with `due ≤ now` and call each one's `fn(ctx)`, in order of `due` time, and timers with equal `due` in the order they were added. Return how many were called.

A callback may call `tq_add` on the same queue. A timer added while `tq_run_due` is running is never called by that same run, even if it is already due: it waits for the next call. So take the due timers out of the queue before calling any of them.
--- starter
using TimerFn = void (*)(void* ctx);

struct Timer {
    long long due;
    TimerFn fn;
    void* ctx;
    int seq;
};

struct TimerQueue {
    Timer items[64];
    int count;
    int next_seq;
};

void tq_init(TimerQueue* q) {
    q->count = 0;
    q->next_seq = 0;
}

bool tq_add(TimerQueue* q, long long due, TimerFn fn, void* ctx) {
    return false;
}

int tq_run_due(TimerQueue* q, long long now) {
    return 0;
}
--- solution
using TimerFn = void (*)(void* ctx);

struct Timer {
    long long due;
    TimerFn fn;
    void* ctx;
    int seq;
};

struct TimerQueue {
    Timer items[64];
    int count;
    int next_seq;
};

void tq_init(TimerQueue* q) {
    q->count = 0;
    q->next_seq = 0;
}

bool tq_add(TimerQueue* q, long long due, TimerFn fn, void* ctx) {
    if (q->count == 64) return false;
    q->items[q->count++] = {due, fn, ctx, q->next_seq++};
    return true;
}

static bool before(const Timer* a, const Timer* b) {
    return a->due < b->due || (a->due == b->due && a->seq < b->seq);
}

int tq_run_due(TimerQueue* q, long long now) {
    // take the due timers out first, so callbacks can add new ones safely
    Timer due[64];
    int n = 0;
    int kept = 0;
    for (int i = 0; i < q->count; i++) {
        if (q->items[i].due <= now) due[n++] = q->items[i];
        else q->items[kept++] = q->items[i];
    }
    q->count = kept;
    // insertion sort by (due, seq)
    for (int i = 1; i < n; i++) {
        Timer t = due[i];
        int j = i - 1;
        while (j >= 0 && before(&t, &due[j])) {
            due[j + 1] = due[j];
            j--;
        }
        due[j + 1] = t;
    }
    for (int i = 0; i < n; i++) due[i].fn(due[i].ctx);
    return n;
}
--- hint
Give each timer a sequence number from `next_seq` when it is added. Then "order of due time, ties in the order added" is one comparison: smaller `due`, or equal `due` and smaller `seq`.
--- hint
In `tq_run_due`, first copy the due timers into a local array and compact the rest in the queue (keep the others, in order, at the front). Only then sort the local array and call the functions.
--- hint
Because the queue no longer holds the due timers when the callbacks run, a callback's `tq_add` just appends to the queue, and that new timer is not in your local array, so this run cannot call it.
--- check test | Three timers fire in time order, and later ones wait
[] { static int log[8]; static int k; k = 0; auto rec = [](void* c) { log[k++] = *(int*)c; }; static int a = 1, b = 2, c = 3, d = 4; TimerQueue q; tq_init(&q); tq_add(&q, 300, rec, &c); tq_add(&q, 100, rec, &a); tq_add(&q, 900, rec, &d); tq_add(&q, 200, rec, &b); int n = tq_run_due(&q, 500); return n == 3 && k == 3 && log[0] == 1 && log[1] == 2 && log[2] == 3 && q.count == 1; }()
--- check test | Equal due times fire in the order added
[] { static int log[8]; static int k; k = 0; auto rec = [](void* c) { log[k++] = *(int*)c; }; static int v[4] = {10, 20, 30, 40}; TimerQueue q; tq_init(&q); tq_add(&q, 50, rec, &v[2]); tq_add(&q, 50, rec, &v[0]); tq_add(&q, 40, rec, &v[3]); tq_add(&q, 50, rec, &v[1]); int n = tq_run_due(&q, 50); return n == 4 && log[0] == 40 && log[1] == 30 && log[2] == 10 && log[3] == 20 && q.count == 0; }()
--- check test | Nothing due: nothing called
[] { static int k; k = 0; auto rec = [](void*) { k++; }; TimerQueue q; tq_init(&q); tq_add(&q, 1000, rec, nullptr); return tq_run_due(&q, 999) == 0 && k == 0 && q.count == 1 && tq_run_due(&q, 1000) == 1 && k == 1; }()
--- check test | The 65th timer is refused
[] { auto rec = [](void*) {}; TimerQueue q; tq_init(&q); for (int i = 0; i < 64; i++) if (!tq_add(&q, i, rec, nullptr)) return false; return !tq_add(&q, 5, rec, nullptr) && q.count == 64 && tq_run_due(&q, 1000) == 64 && q.count == 0 && tq_add(&q, 5, rec, nullptr); }()
--- check test | A timer added by a callback waits for the next run
[] { static TimerQueue q; static int k; k = 0; auto second = [](void*) { k += 100; }; static TimerFn second_fn; second_fn = second; auto first = [](void*) { k += 1; tq_add(&q, 0, second_fn, nullptr); }; tq_init(&q); tq_add(&q, 10, first, nullptr); int n1 = tq_run_due(&q, 10); int k1 = k; int n2 = tq_run_due(&q, 10); return n1 == 1 && k1 == 1 && n2 == 1 && k == 101 && q.count == 0; }()
--- check test | Each callback gets its own context
[] { auto bump = [](void* c) { (*(int*)c)++; }; int x = 0, y = 0; TimerQueue q; tq_init(&q); tq_add(&q, 1, bump, &x); tq_add(&q, 2, bump, &y); tq_add(&q, 3, bump, &x); tq_run_due(&q, 5); return x == 2 && y == 1; }()

+++ question | Why a void* context
--- ask
Why do C functions that take a callback, like `each_reading` in the lesson, usually take a `void* ctx` as well?
--- choice
Because a function pointer cannot point at a function with more than one parameter.
--- choice correct
Because a function pointer is only an address, with no room for data, so the data the callback needs has to travel beside it, and `void*` lets it be any type.
--- choice
Because `void*` makes the call faster than passing a typed pointer.
--- choice
Because the callback must free the context when it has finished.
--- why
A C function pointer carries no state. The context pointer is the other half of a closure: the caller packs whatever the callback needs behind it, and the callback converts it back. The cost is that the compiler cannot check the conversion.

+++ question | The comparator that subtracts
--- ask
A programmer sorts an array of `int` with the comparator `return *(const int*)a - *(const int*)b;`. On which input can it put the elements in the wrong order?
--- choice
Any array that has two equal elements.
--- choice
Any array with negative numbers.
--- choice correct
An array with values far apart, such as 2,000,000,000 and -2,000,000,000, whose difference does not fit in an `int`.
--- choice
None: it is exactly equivalent to `(x > y) - (x < y)`.
--- why
Equal elements give 0 and small negatives are fine. The problem is overflow: 2,000,000,000 − (−2,000,000,000) is 4,000,000,000, beyond the largest `int`, so the result wraps round to a negative number and claims the larger value is smaller. Signed overflow is also undefined behaviour.
=== sys-10 | Undefined behaviour: what the compiler may assume
--- teach
Last lesson ended with a comparator that subtracts, `return x - y;`, which fails when the difference does not fit in an `int`. You have met this kind of rule many times in the course already: reading past the end of an array, using a dangling pointer, shifting by 32, reading a `float` through a `uint32_t*`. Each time the course said "undefined behaviour" and moved on. This lesson stops and looks at it squarely: what the words mean, why C and C++ have it, what a modern compiler does with it, and how to write arithmetic and memory code that never has it.

### Three ways the standard can leave something open

The C and C++ standards are contracts between you and the compiler. Mostly they say exactly what a program does. In three kinds of places they leave something open, and the three are very different.

- **Implementation-defined behaviour:** the compiler chooses, and must document its choice. `sizeof(long)` is 8 on a Linux laptop and 4 in ORBIT's browser build; whether a plain `char` is signed is up to the compiler. Your program is still well defined on each machine; it is just not portable.
- **Unspecified behaviour:** the compiler chooses among a few allowed results, and need not say which or be consistent. In `f(a(), b())`, whether `a()` or `b()` runs first is unspecified. Both orders are allowed; nothing else is.
- **Undefined behaviour (UB):** the standard places **no requirements at all** on what happens. Not "an unknown number", not "a crash": nothing. The program may do what you hoped, crash, print garbage, or behave differently when compiled with other flags. Programmers joke that it may make [[demons fly out of your nose|demons]].

In everyday words: the standard promises what your program does only as long as you keep your side of the contract. Undefined behaviour is breaking your side. After that, there is no promise left, not even about the lines that ran before.

### A catalogue

These are the cases systems programmers meet most often. Most of them you have already seen.

| undefined behaviour | example | the defined alternative |
|---|---|---|
| signed integer overflow | `INT_MAX + 1`, `-INT_MIN`, `abs(INT_MIN)` | check first, or use unsigned types, which wrap |
| division by zero, and `INT_MIN / -1` | `x / 0`, `x % 0` | test the divisor first |
| shifting by the width or more, or by a negative amount | `x << 32` for a 32-bit `x` | reduce or test the count |
| reading or writing outside an object | `a[n]` for `int a[n]`; a pointer more than one past the end | pass lengths and check indexes |
| reading an uninitialised variable | `int x; printf("%d", x);` | initialise everything |
| null, dangling and freed pointers | `*p` after `free(p)`; `free(p)` twice | set pointers to null after freeing |
| breaking strict aliasing | reading a `float` through a `uint32_t*` | `memcpy` |
| writing to a string literal | `char* s = (char*)"abc"; s[0] = 'x';` | copy it into an array first |
| a non-`void` function that ends without `return` | forgetting the last `return` | turn on warnings |
| converting a floating-point value that does not fit to an integer | `(int)3e10` | test the range first |

**Unsigned arithmetic is not on the list.** The standard defines it as wrapping modulo 2ⁿ, where n is the width: `0u - 1u` is 4294967295 for a 32-bit `unsigned`, always. Only **signed** overflow is undefined. Integer conversions are defined too: converting an out-of-range integer to an unsigned type wraps, and since C++20, converting it to a signed type wraps as well.

### What the compiler may assume

Why would a language leave so much undefined? Because the rule "the compiler may assume undefined behaviour never happens" lets it generate much faster code. An optimiser proves facts about your program and uses them, and every rule you promise to keep is a fact it gets for free.

Here is the kind of reasoning it does. Take

```cpp fragment
bool grows(int x) {
    return x + 1 > x;
}
```

- If x is less than `INT_MAX`, then x + 1 is a real number one bigger than x, so the comparison is true.
- If x equals `INT_MAX`, then x + 1 overflows. That is undefined behaviour, so the standard does not care what this call returns.
- So returning `true` is correct for **every** call the standard cares about. The compiler replaces the whole function with `return true;`.

That is the entire argument, and it is airtight: **the compiler only has to be correct for executions that have no undefined behaviour.** Every surprising optimisation below follows the same three steps.

**The overflow check that disappears.** People write overflow checks that do the overflow first and look at the result afterwards:

```cpp fragment
int sum = a + b;
if (b > 0 && sum < a) { /* overflow! */ }
```

By the argument above, with b > 0 the compiler knows `a + b` is bigger than a in every execution it cares about, so `sum < a` is false and the check is deleted. Here is what clang 18 did with a check of this kind, called with a = `INT_MAX` and b = 1:

| build | the check reports |
|---|---|
| `-O0`, no optimisation (a debug build) | overflow |
| `-O1` and `-O2` (ORBIT, and every release build) | no overflow: the check was deleted |

The tests passed in the debug build, and the check vanished in the release build.

**The null check that disappears.** In 2009 a Linux kernel driver read a field through a pointer and only then tested the pointer for null. Reading through a null pointer is undefined, so the compiler reasoned that the pointer could not be null at the test, and deleted the test. Attackers used the missing check to take over machines: the [[tun driver bug|tun]].

**The loop that runs off the end.**

```cpp fragment
int table[4];
bool in_table(int v) {
    for (int i = 0; i <= 4; i++)   // <= reads table[4], one past the end
        if (table[i] == v) return true;
    return false;
}
```

Reading `table[4]` is undefined, so an execution reaches the fifth pass only if it returned `true` in the first four. GCC has been seen to compile this whole function to `return true;`. A bug on one line changed what the lines before it do; this is sometimes called time travel.

**The benefit.** The same assumption makes ordinary code fast. In `for (int i = 0; i < n; i++) sum += a[i];` the compiler may assume `i` never overflows, so it knows the loop runs exactly n times, can keep `i` in a 64-bit register without re-checking it, and can process several elements per instruction. Languages that define overflow give up some of that speed, or pay for checks.

### Writing arithmetic that never overflows

The fix is always the same idea: **test whether the operation would overflow, using only operations that cannot overflow, before doing it.**

For addition of two `int`s, a + b overflows exactly when

- b > 0 and a > `INT_MAX` − b, or
- b < 0 and a < `INT_MIN` − b.

**Why these tests are safe and exact.** If b > 0, then `INT_MAX` − b lies between −1 and `INT_MAX` − 1, so computing it cannot overflow. And a + b > `INT_MAX` holds exactly when a > `INT_MAX` − b, by subtracting b from both sides of an inequality between real numbers. The case b < 0 is the mirror image: `INT_MIN` − b lies between `INT_MIN` + 1 and −1, and a + b < `INT_MIN` exactly when a < `INT_MIN` − b. When b = 0 nothing can overflow. So the test is computed without overflow and says "overflow" exactly when the real sum is out of range.

Subtraction, multiplication and division need tests of their own, built the same way; you will write them in the task. Division is the odd one: `a / b` has two bad cases, b = 0 and `INT_MIN / -1`, whose true answer, 2³¹, is one more than `INT_MAX`. The same pair makes `%` undefined.

Other ways out, and when to use them:

- **A wider type.** Two `int`s multiplied as `long long` cannot overflow, so compute wide and compare with the limits. It stops working at the widest type you have.
- **Unsigned arithmetic** when wrapping is what you want: hashes, checksums, sequence counters, bit patterns.
- **Compiler builtins.** GCC and Clang have `__builtin_add_overflow(a, b, &out)`, which uses the CPU's overflow flag; C23 standardises the same as `ckd_add`. In real code, use them. Here you will write the tests yourself, because the reasoning is what carries over to every language.

### Uninitialised values

A local variable that is never given a value holds whatever bytes were on the stack. Reading it is undefined behaviour, and the compiler really does exploit it: it may treat the value as different things in two places where it is read. Initialise every variable where it is declared, `int count = 0;`, and structs and arrays with `= {}`, which sets every member to zero.

### Finding undefined behaviour

UB rarely shows up in tests, so use tools that look for it.

- **Warnings:** `-Wall -Wextra` catch a missing `return`, unused variables and many suspicious comparisons. Read them.
- **Sanitizers:** build with `-fsanitize=undefined` or `-fsanitize=address` and the program checks itself at run time, stopping with a report at the first overflow, bad shift or out-of-bounds access. [[They are how UB is found in practice|sanitizers]].
- **Constant evaluation:** C++ forbids undefined behaviour in a `constexpr` calculation evaluated at compile time, so the compiler reports it as an error. Writing tests as `static_assert`s catches some UB for free.
- **Changing the language:** `-fwrapv` makes signed overflow wrap, and `-fno-strict-aliasing` turns off the aliasing rule. The Linux kernel builds with `-fno-strict-aliasing`, and with `-fno-strict-overflow`, which in current GCC means the same as `-fwrapv`. Your code then depends on those flags, so it is a decision for a whole project, not a fix for one bug.

### What it costs

An overflow test is one or two comparisons and a branch: a nanosecond or less, and the branch is almost always predicted correctly because overflow is rare. Checking every array index costs about the same per access. Programs that never have undefined behaviour are not measurably slower in most code; the real cost is the care it takes to write them.

### Where it matters

- **Security.** Most serious security holes in C and C++ programs, in browsers, kernels and phones, are undefined behaviour: out-of-bounds writes, use-after-free, and integer overflows that make a size too small. It is why companies are moving new systems code to memory-safe languages.
- **Flight and safety software.** Coding standards such as MISRA C and JPL's rules forbid the constructs in the catalogue, and require static analysis to prove their absence.
- **Counters that run for a long time.** In 2015 the US aviation authority ordered Boeing 787 operators to restart the aircraft's power units regularly, because a counter would [[overflow after 248 days|b787]] and shut them down.

**Watch out:** "it works, I tested it" proves nothing about undefined behaviour. Code with UB can pass every test at `-O0`, fail at `-O1`, and change again with the next compiler version. The only defence is to know the catalogue, write the checks before the operation, and run the sanitizers.

::: context demons Nasal demons
In 1992, on the Usenet group comp.std.c, a programmer explained that undefined behaviour allows the compiler to do anything at all, "to make demons fly out of your nose". The phrase stuck. It is a joke, but a precise one: the standard does not only allow a wrong answer or a crash, it allows any behaviour whatsoever, and real compilers produce results that feel almost as strange.
:::

::: context tun The null check the compiler removed
In 2009 Linux's tun network driver contained `struct sock *sk = tun->sk;` followed by `if (!tun) return POLLERR;`. Because `tun->sk` had already been read, the compiler could assume `tun` was not null and deleted the check. On systems where memory at address 0 could be mapped by a user program, an attacker placed data there and gained full control of the kernel. The kernel then started building with `-fno-delete-null-pointer-checks`.
:::

::: context sanitizers Programs that check themselves
Sanitizers are compiler options that add checks around every risky operation. UndefinedBehaviorSanitizer reports signed overflow, bad shifts, misaligned pointers and more. AddressSanitizer surrounds every heap and stack object with poisoned bytes and reports any access to them, finding out-of-bounds reads, use-after-free and double frees. They slow a program down about two times, so they are used in testing and fuzzing, where they have found tens of thousands of bugs in Chrome, Linux and other large programs.
:::

::: context b787 The 248-day counter
The Boeing 787's generator control units are widely reported to have counted time in hundredths of a second in a signed 32-bit integer. That counter reaches its largest value, 2,147,483,647, after 2³¹ / 100 seconds, which is about 248.6 days. The resulting overflow would make all four units go into a failsafe mode at once, cutting electrical power. The fix before a software update was simple: power the units off and on before 248 days.
:::
--- task
Write checked 32-bit arithmetic that never has undefined behaviour. Each function returns `true` and stores the result in `*out` when the true mathematical result fits in an `int`, and returns `false` **without changing `*out`** when it does not. No `main`.

- `bool add_ok(int a, int b, int* out)`: a + b.
- `bool sub_ok(int a, int b, int* out)`: a − b.
- `bool mul_ok(int a, int b, int* out)`: a × b.
- `bool div_ok(int a, int b, int* out)`: a / b, rounded towards zero as C does. Division by zero is also `false`.

The whole point is doing it within `int`: do not use a wider type (`long`, `long long`, `int64_t`, `double`) or a compiler builtin. `INT_MAX` and `INT_MIN` come from `<climits>`.
