For example, `add_ok(2147483000, 647, &r)` is `true` with `r` = 2147483647, `add_ok(2147483647, 1, &r)` is `false`, `mul_ok(-65536, 32768, &r)` is `true` with `r` = −2147483648, and `div_ok(-2147483648, -1, &r)` is `false`.
--- starter
#include <climits>

bool add_ok(int a, int b, int* out) {
    return false;
}

bool sub_ok(int a, int b, int* out) {
    return false;
}

bool mul_ok(int a, int b, int* out) {
    return false;
}

bool div_ok(int a, int b, int* out) {
    return false;
}
--- solution
#include <climits>

bool add_ok(int a, int b, int* out) {
    if (b > 0 && a > INT_MAX - b) return false;
    if (b < 0 && a < INT_MIN - b) return false;
    *out = a + b;
    return true;
}

bool sub_ok(int a, int b, int* out) {
    // a - b > INT_MAX  exactly when  a > INT_MAX + b  (b < 0, so INT_MAX + b is safe)
    if (b < 0 && a > INT_MAX + b) return false;
    // a - b < INT_MIN  exactly when  a < INT_MIN + b  (b > 0, so INT_MIN + b is safe)
    if (b > 0 && a < INT_MIN + b) return false;
    *out = a - b;
    return true;
}

bool mul_ok(int a, int b, int* out) {
    if (a > 0) {
        if (b > 0) {
            if (a > INT_MAX / b) return false;   // positive times positive
        } else {
            if (b < INT_MIN / a) return false;   // positive times zero or negative
        }
    } else {
        if (b > 0) {
            if (a < INT_MIN / b) return false;   // zero or negative times positive
        } else {
            if (a != 0 && b < INT_MAX / a) return false;   // both zero or negative
        }
    }
    *out = a * b;
    return true;
}

bool div_ok(int a, int b, int* out) {
    if (b == 0) return false;
    if (a == INT_MIN && b == -1) return false;
    *out = a / b;
    return true;
}
--- hint
`add_ok` follows the lesson's two tests exactly. For `sub_ok`, work out the same way which sign of `b` can push `a - b` above `INT_MAX` and which below `INT_MIN`, and move `b` to the other side of the inequality so that the test itself cannot overflow.
--- hint
For `mul_ok`, split on the signs of `a` and `b`, and compare with a limit divided by one of them. For example, with both positive, a × b > `INT_MAX` exactly when a > `INT_MAX / b` (whole-number division rounds down, which is exactly right here). Be careful which limit and which sign each case needs, and never divide by 0.
--- hint
When both are negative the product is positive, and it is too big when `b < INT_MAX / a` (dividing a positive limit by a negative `a` gives a negative bound, and flips the inequality). `div_ok` has two bad cases: `b == 0`, and `a == INT_MIN` with `b == -1`.
--- check test | add_ok: the largest sum fits, one more does not
[] { int r = 5; bool a = add_ok(2147483000, 647, &r) && r == 2147483647; int s = 5; bool b = !add_ok(2147483647, 1, &s) && s == 5; return a && b; }()
--- check test | add_ok: negative overflow, and mixed signs never overflow
[] { int r = 9; bool a = !add_ok(-2147483647 - 1, -1, &r) && r == 9; int s = 0; bool b = add_ok(-2147483647 - 1, 2147483647, &s) && s == -1; int t = 0; bool c = add_ok(-5, 3, &t) && t == -2; return a && b && c; }()
--- check test | sub_ok at both ends
[] { int r = 7; bool a = !sub_ok(-2147483647 - 1, 1, &r) && r == 7; bool b = !sub_ok(0, -2147483647 - 1, &r) && r == 7; int s = 0; bool c = sub_ok(-1, -2147483647 - 1, &s) && s == 2147483647; bool d = !sub_ok(2147483647, -1, &r) && r == 7; return a && b && c && d; }()
--- check test | mul_ok: the example and the limits
[] { int r = 0; bool a = mul_ok(-65536, 32768, &r) && r == -2147483647 - 1; int s = 3; bool b = !mul_ok(65536, 32768, &s) && s == 3; bool c = !mul_ok(-2147483647 - 1, -1, &s) && s == 3; int t = 0; bool d = mul_ok(46340, 46340, &t) && t == 2147395600; bool e = !mul_ok(46341, 46341, &s); return a && b && c && d && e; }()
--- check test | mul_ok with zero, one and minus one
[] { int r = 1; bool a = mul_ok(0, -2147483647 - 1, &r) && r == 0; r = 1; bool b = mul_ok(-2147483647 - 1, 0, &r) && r == 0; r = 0; bool c = mul_ok(-2147483647 - 1, 1, &r) && r == -2147483647 - 1; r = 0; bool d = mul_ok(-1, 2147483647, &r) && r == -2147483647; return a && b && c && d; }()
--- check test | div_ok: zero and INT_MIN / -1 fail, everything else works
[] { int r = 4; bool a = !div_ok(10, 0, &r) && r == 4; bool b = !div_ok(-2147483647 - 1, -1, &r) && r == 4; int s = 0; bool c = div_ok(-7, 2, &s) && s == -3; bool d = div_ok(-2147483647 - 1, 1, &s) && s == -2147483647 - 1; bool e = div_ok(2147483647, -1, &s) && s == -2147483647; return a && b && c && d && e; }()
--- check test | All four agree with the compiler's overflow builtins on 20000 random pairs
[] { unsigned x = 2024u; int edges[10] = {0, 1, -1, 2, -2, 46341, -46341, 65536, 2147483647, -2147483647 - 1}; for (int t = 0; t < 20000; t++) { x = x * 1664525u + 1013904223u; int a = t % 3 == 0 ? edges[x % 10] : (int)x; x = x * 1664525u + 1013904223u; int b = t % 5 == 0 ? edges[x % 10] : (t % 2 ? (int)x : (int)(x >> (x % 31))); int got = 12345, want = 0; bool ok = !__builtin_add_overflow(a, b, &want); if (add_ok(a, b, &got) != ok || (ok ? got != want : got != 12345)) return false; got = 12345; ok = !__builtin_sub_overflow(a, b, &want); if (sub_ok(a, b, &got) != ok || (ok ? got != want : got != 12345)) return false; got = 12345; ok = !__builtin_mul_overflow(a, b, &want); if (mul_ok(a, b, &got) != ok || (ok ? got != want : got != 12345)) return false; got = 12345; ok = b != 0 && !(a == -2147483647 - 1 && b == -1); if (div_ok(a, b, &got) != ok || (ok ? got != a / b : got != 12345)) return false; } return true; }()
--- check source absent | No wider types and no builtins
\blong\b|int64_t|__int128|__builtin|\bdouble\b|\bfloat\b

+++ practice | A midpoint that never overflows
--- task
Binary search computes the middle of a range, and the obvious `(lo + hi) / 2` overflows when both are large. Write two functions that work for every pair of `int`s. No `main`.

- `int midpoint(int lo, int hi)`: for `lo ≤ hi`, return the middle rounded down: the largest whole number that is at most (lo + hi) / 2 as a real number. For example `midpoint(3, 8)` is 5, `midpoint(-8, -3)` is -6, and `midpoint(-2147483648, 2147483647)` is -1.
- `unsigned distance(int a, int b)`: the absolute difference |a − b|, as an `unsigned`. For example `distance(-2147483648, 2147483647)` is 4294967295.

Hint for the whole problem: `lo + (hi - lo) / 2` is only safe when `hi - lo` fits in an `int`, which is not always. Unsigned arithmetic wraps, and that is defined.
--- starter
int midpoint(int lo, int hi) {
    return (lo + hi) / 2;
}

unsigned distance(int a, int b) {
    return a > b ? a - b : b - a;
}
--- solution
unsigned distance(int a, int b) {
    // unsigned subtraction wraps modulo 2^32; the true difference fits, so it is exact
    return a > b ? (unsigned)a - (unsigned)b : (unsigned)b - (unsigned)a;
}

int midpoint(int lo, int hi) {
    unsigned half = ((unsigned)hi - (unsigned)lo) / 2;
    // lo + half lies between lo and hi, so it fits in an int; add it without signed overflow
    return (int)((unsigned)lo + half);
}
--- hint
Converting both numbers to `unsigned` and subtracting gives the true difference whenever it is between 0 and 4294967295, because unsigned arithmetic is exact modulo 2³², and the true difference lies in that range.
--- hint
For `midpoint`, half of `hi - lo` (computed in unsigned) is at most 2147483647. Adding it to `lo` lands between `lo` and `hi`. Do the addition in unsigned too, and convert back to `int`: since C++20 that conversion is defined, and here the value fits anyway.
--- check test | The examples
[] { return midpoint(3, 8) == 5 && midpoint(-8, -3) == -6 && midpoint(-2147483647 - 1, 2147483647) == -1 && distance(-2147483647 - 1, 2147483647) == 4294967295u; }()
--- check test | Both ends at the largest and the smallest int
[] { return midpoint(2147483646, 2147483647) == 2147483646 && midpoint(2147483647, 2147483647) == 2147483647 && midpoint(-2147483647 - 1, -2147483647 - 1) == -2147483647 - 1 && midpoint(-2147483647 - 1, -2147483647) == -2147483647 - 1; }()
--- check test | Rounds down for negative sums
[] { return midpoint(-3, 0) == -2 && midpoint(-1, 0) == -1 && midpoint(-5, 2) == -2 && midpoint(0, 1) == 0; }()
--- check test | distance is symmetric and exact at the ends
[] { return distance(5, 5) == 0u && distance(-3, 4) == 7u && distance(4, -3) == 7u && distance(2147483647, -2147483647 - 1) == 4294967295u && distance(-2147483647 - 1, 0) == 2147483648u; }()
--- check test | 5000 random pairs agree with 64-bit arithmetic
[] { unsigned x = 77u; for (int t = 0; t < 5000; t++) { x = x * 1664525u + 1013904223u; int a = (int)x; x = x * 1664525u + 1013904223u; int b = (int)x; int lo = a < b ? a : b, hi = a < b ? b : a; long long s = (long long)lo + hi; long long m = s >= 0 ? s / 2 : -((-s + 1) / 2); if (midpoint(lo, hi) != (int)m) return false; long long d = (long long)a - b; if (d < 0) d = -d; if (distance(a, b) != (unsigned)d) return false; } return true; }()

+++ practice | Saturating arithmetic
--- task
Sensor fusion code often wants arithmetic that **saturates**: a result too big becomes the largest value, and one too small becomes the smallest, instead of an error. Write two functions. No `main`.

- `int sat_add(int a, int b)`: a + b, clamped to the range `INT_MIN` to `INT_MAX`.
- `int sat_sub(int a, int b)`: a − b, clamped the same way.

Neither may ever overflow, and, as in the lesson's task, use only `int` (no wider type, no builtins). For example, `sat_add(2000000000, 2000000000)` is 2147483647 and `sat_sub(-2000000000, 2000000000)` is -2147483648.
--- starter
#include <climits>

int sat_add(int a, int b) {
    return a + b;
}

int sat_sub(int a, int b) {
    return a - b;
}
--- solution
#include <climits>

int sat_add(int a, int b) {
    if (b > 0 && a > INT_MAX - b) return INT_MAX;
    if (b < 0 && a < INT_MIN - b) return INT_MIN;
    return a + b;
}

int sat_sub(int a, int b) {
    if (b < 0 && a > INT_MAX + b) return INT_MAX;
    if (b > 0 && a < INT_MIN + b) return INT_MIN;
    return a - b;
}
--- hint
Use the lesson's tests for when a sum would overflow, but return the limit it would cross instead of reporting an error.
--- hint
Do not compute `-b` to turn subtraction into addition: when `b` is `INT_MIN`, `-b` itself overflows. Write the two tests for subtraction directly: with `b` negative the result can only be too big, and with `b` positive only too small.
--- check test | The examples
[] { return sat_add(2000000000, 2000000000) == 2147483647 && sat_sub(-2000000000, 2000000000) == -2147483647 - 1; }()
--- check test | Results that fit are exact
[] { return sat_add(5, -9) == -4 && sat_add(-2147483647 - 1, 2147483647) == -1 && sat_sub(10, 3) == 7 && sat_sub(-1, -2147483647 - 1) == 2147483647; }()
--- check test | Saturates at the smallest int
[] { return sat_add(-2147483647 - 1, -1) == -2147483647 - 1 && sat_add(-2000000000, -2000000000) == -2147483647 - 1; }()
--- check test | Subtracting INT_MIN
[] { return sat_sub(0, -2147483647 - 1) == 2147483647 && sat_sub(-2147483647 - 1, -2147483647 - 1) == 0 && sat_sub(-2, -2147483647 - 1) == 2147483646; }()
--- check test | Agrees with 64-bit arithmetic, clamped, on 5000 pairs
[] { unsigned x = 3u; for (int t = 0; t < 5000; t++) { x = x * 1664525u + 1013904223u; int a = (int)x; x = x * 1664525u + 1013904223u; int b = (int)(x >> (t % 32)); if (t % 2) b = ~b; long long s = (long long)a + b, d = (long long)a - b; long long cs = s > 2147483647LL ? 2147483647LL : s < -2147483648LL ? -2147483648LL : s; long long cd = d > 2147483647LL ? 2147483647LL : d < -2147483648LL ? -2147483648LL : d; if (sat_add(a, b) != cs || sat_sub(a, b) != cd) return false; } return true; }()
--- check source absent | Only int arithmetic
\blong\b|int64_t|__int128|__builtin|\bdouble\b

+++ practice | Parsing a number without overflow
--- task
Write `bool parse_int(const char* s, int* out)`, which reads a whole text as a decimal `int`. No `main`.

The text is valid when it is an optional sign (`+` or `-`), then one or more digits, and nothing else: no spaces, no other characters. If it is valid and its value fits in an `int`, store the value in `*out` and return `true`. Otherwise return `false` and leave `*out` unchanged.

It must work for every `int`, including `"-2147483648"`, and must never overflow, even on a text like `"99999999999999999999"`. Use only `int` arithmetic (no wider type, no library conversion functions such as `atoi` or `strtol`). For example `"+42"` gives 42, `"-0"` gives 0, and `"2147483648"`, `""`, `"-"` and `"12a"` give `false`.
--- starter
#include <cstdio>

bool parse_int(const char* s, int* out) {
    int value = 0;
    for (int i = 0; s[i] != '\0'; i++) value = value * 10 + (s[i] - '0');
    *out = value;
    return true;
}
--- solution
#include <climits>
#include <cstdio>

bool parse_int(const char* s, int* out) {
    bool negative = false;
    if (*s == '+' || *s == '-') {
        negative = *s == '-';
        s++;
    }
    if (*s == '\0') return false;
    // build the value as a negative number: INT_MIN has no positive partner, but every int has a negative one
    int value = 0;
    for (; *s != '\0'; s++) {
        if (*s < '0' || *s > '9') return false;
        int digit = *s - '0';
        if (value < INT_MIN / 10) return false;          // value * 10 would go below INT_MIN
        if (value * 10 < INT_MIN + digit) return false;  // value * 10 - digit would
        value = value * 10 - digit;
    }
    if (!negative) {
        if (value == INT_MIN) return false;              // 2147483648 does not fit
        value = -value;
    }
    *out = value;
    return true;
}
--- hint
Deal with the sign first, then reject an empty rest. Every other character must be a digit.
--- hint
Before each step `value = value * 10 + digit`, test that it cannot overflow: compare `value` with `INT_MAX / 10`, and then with the remaining room for the digit. Tests like these must themselves never overflow.
--- hint
The negative range is one bigger than the positive range, so `-2147483648` cannot be built as a positive number and then negated. Build every number as a negative value, `value * 10 - digit`, checking against `INT_MIN`, and negate at the end only for a positive text, where `INT_MIN` itself is out of range.
--- check test | Plain, signed and zero values
[] { int r = 0; bool a = parse_int("42", &r) && r == 42; bool b = parse_int("+42", &r) && r == 42; bool c = parse_int("-17", &r) && r == -17; bool d = parse_int("-0", &r) && r == 0; bool e = parse_int("007", &r) && r == 7; return a && b && c && d && e; }()
--- check test | The largest and smallest int
[] { int r = 0; bool a = parse_int("2147483647", &r) && r == 2147483647; bool b = parse_int("-2147483648", &r) && r == -2147483647 - 1; return a && b; }()
--- check test | One past either end is refused, and *out is kept
[] { int r = 5; return !parse_int("2147483648", &r) && !parse_int("-2147483649", &r) && !parse_int("+2147483648", &r) && r == 5; }()
--- check test | Very long numbers are refused without overflowing
[] { int r = 5; return !parse_int("99999999999999999999", &r) && !parse_int("-100000000000000000000000", &r) && !parse_int("21474836470", &r) && r == 5; }()
--- check test | Empty texts, lone signs and junk are refused
[] { int r = 5; return !parse_int("", &r) && !parse_int("-", &r) && !parse_int("+", &r) && !parse_int("12a", &r) && !parse_int(" 12", &r) && !parse_int("1 2", &r) && !parse_int("--1", &r) && !parse_int("0x10", &r) && r == 5; }()
--- check test | Many values round-trip through snprintf
[] { char buf[32]; unsigned x = 1u; for (int t = 0; t < 3000; t++) { x = x * 1664525u + 1013904223u; int v = (int)(x >> (t % 32)); if (t % 2) v = ~v; snprintf(buf, sizeof buf, "%d", v); int r = 0; if (!parse_int(buf, &r) || r != v) return false; } return true; }()
--- check source absent | Only int arithmetic, no library conversions
\blong\b|int64_t|__int128|__builtin|\bdouble\b|\batoi\b|\bstrtol|\bsscanf\b|\bstoi\b

+++ practice | Absolute values and remainders
--- task
Two innocent-looking operations have hidden undefined cases. Write both so that they never have undefined behaviour. No `main`.

- `bool abs_ok(int x, int* out)`: store |x| and return `true`, or return `false` if it does not fit in an `int`.
- `bool mod_ok(int a, int b, int* out)`: store the **mathematical** remainder of a divided by b, which is always from 0 to |b| − 1 (so `mod_ok(-7, 3, &r)` gives 2, where C's `-7 % 3` is -1), and return `true`. Return `false` only if b is 0.

On `false`, leave `*out` unchanged. Watch every case: `INT_MIN % -1` is undefined in C, although the mathematical remainder, 0, exists and must be returned.
--- starter
#include <climits>
#include <cstdlib>

bool abs_ok(int x, int* out) {
    *out = abs(x);
    return true;
}

bool mod_ok(int a, int b, int* out) {
    if (b == 0) return false;
    *out = a % b;
    return true;
}
--- solution
#include <climits>

bool abs_ok(int x, int* out) {
    if (x == INT_MIN) return false;
    *out = x < 0 ? -x : x;
    return true;
}

bool mod_ok(int a, int b, int* out) {
    if (b == 0) return false;
    if (b == -1) {           // every number divides by -1 exactly; also avoids INT_MIN % -1
        *out = 0;
        return true;
    }
    int r = a % b;           // has the sign of a, and |r| < |b|
    if (r < 0) {
        // add |b| without computing -b: r + b when b > 0, r - b when b < 0.
        // Both land in 1 .. |b| - 1, so neither can overflow.
        if (b > 0) r += b;
        else r -= b;
    }
    *out = r;
    return true;
}
--- hint
`abs` of `INT_MIN` would be 2147483648, one more than `INT_MAX`: that is the one `false` case.
--- hint
C's `%` gives a result with the sign of `a`. When it is negative, add |b| to bring it into the range 0 to |b| − 1. With `b` negative, adding |b| is the same as subtracting `b`, and never needs `-b` to be computed.
--- hint
Handle `b == -1` before using `%` at all: every remainder by −1 is 0, and that avoids `INT_MIN % -1`.
--- check test | abs_ok works everywhere but INT_MIN
[] { int r = 3; bool a = abs_ok(-5, &r) && r == 5; bool b = abs_ok(2147483647, &r) && r == 2147483647; bool c = abs_ok(-2147483647, &r) && r == 2147483647; int s = 3; bool d = !abs_ok(-2147483647 - 1, &s) && s == 3; bool e = abs_ok(0, &s) && s == 0; return a && b && c && d && e; }()
--- check test | Mathematical remainders of negative numbers
[] { int r = 0; bool a = mod_ok(-7, 3, &r) && r == 2; bool b = mod_ok(7, 3, &r) && r == 1; bool c = mod_ok(-7, -3, &r) && r == 2; bool d = mod_ok(7, -3, &r) && r == 1; bool e = mod_ok(-6, 3, &r) && r == 0; return a && b && c && d && e; }()
--- check test | Division by zero is false and keeps *out
[] { int r = 8; return !mod_ok(5, 0, &r) && !mod_ok(-2147483647 - 1, 0, &r) && r == 8; }()
--- check test | INT_MIN with -1, and with INT_MIN itself
[] { int r = 8; bool a = mod_ok(-2147483647 - 1, -1, &r) && r == 0; r = 8; bool b = mod_ok(-2147483647 - 1, -2147483647 - 1, &r) && r == 0; r = 8; bool c = mod_ok(-1, -2147483647 - 1, &r) && r == 2147483647; r = 8; bool d = mod_ok(2147483647, -2147483647 - 1, &r) && r == 2147483647; return a && b && c && d; }()
--- check test | Agrees with 64-bit arithmetic on 5000 pairs
[] { unsigned x = 9u; for (int t = 0; t < 5000; t++) { x = x * 1664525u + 1013904223u; int a = (int)x; x = x * 1664525u + 1013904223u; int b = (int)(x >> (t % 32)); if (t % 3 == 0) b = -(b & 0x7FFFFFFF); if (b == 0) b = 7; long long A = a, B = b < 0 ? -(long long)b : b; long long m = ((A % B) + B) % B; int r = 0; if (!mod_ok(a, b, &r) || r != m) return false; } return true; }()

+++ practice | Debug: the check that vanished
--- task
This function is meant to add two `int`s and report overflow. It returns `false` (and leaves `*out` alone) when the true sum does not fit in an `int`, and otherwise stores the sum and returns `true`. Its author tested it in a debug build, `-O0`, and every test passed. In ORBIT, and in any optimised build, `safe_add(2147483647, 1, &r)` returns `true`. Explain to yourself why, then fix it so it is correct in every build.

`bool safe_add(int a, int b, int* out)`. No `main`.
--- starter
bool safe_add(int a, int b, int* out) {
    int sum = a + b;
    if (b > 0 ? sum < a : sum > a) return false;   // did it wrap round?
    *out = sum;
    return true;
}
--- solution
#include <climits>

bool safe_add(int a, int b, int* out) {
    if (b > 0 && a > INT_MAX - b) return false;
    if (b < 0 && a < INT_MIN - b) return false;
    *out = a + b;
    return true;
}
--- hint
The sum is computed before it is checked. If it overflows, that is undefined behaviour, so the compiler may assume it did not. Under that assumption, can `sum < a` ever be true when `b > 0`?
--- hint
The compiler proves the test is always false and deletes it. Test whether the addition would overflow before doing it, with comparisons that cannot overflow themselves.
--- check test | INT_MAX + 1 is reported, and *out is kept
[] { int r = 3; return !safe_add(2147483647, 1, &r) && r == 3; }()
--- check test | INT_MIN - 1 is reported
[] { int r = 3; return !safe_add(-2147483647 - 1, -1, &r) && r == 3; }()
--- check test | Big sums near the limits
[] { int r = 3; bool a = !safe_add(2000000000, 2000000000, &r); int s = 0; bool b = safe_add(2000000000, 147483647, &s) && s == 2147483647; bool c = !safe_add(-2000000000, -147483649, &r) && r == 3; return a && b && c; }()
--- check test | Ordinary sums work, including b = 0
[] { int r = 0; bool a = safe_add(5, -9, &r) && r == -4; bool b = safe_add(2147483647, 0, &r) && r == 2147483647; bool c = safe_add(-2147483647 - 1, 2147483647, &r) && r == -1; return a && b && c; }()

+++ practice | Sequence numbers that wrap
--- task
Every telemetry packet carries a 16-bit sequence number that goes up by one per packet and wraps from 65535 back to 0. On the ground, packets may be lost, repeated, or arrive late. Write two functions. No `main`.

- `int seq_diff(uint16_t newer, uint16_t older)`: how far `newer` is ahead of `older`, taking wrap-around into account. It is the number d from -32768 to 32767 such that `older + d` equals `newer` modulo 65536. For example `seq_diff(5, 3)` is 2, `seq_diff(2, 65534)` is 4, and `seq_diff(3, 5)` is -2.
- `long long count_lost(const uint16_t* seq, int n)`: the total number of lost packets in a stream of `n` received sequence numbers. Keep the latest number seen, starting with `seq[0]`. For each later number, compute d = `seq_diff(number, latest)`. If d > 0, the d − 1 numbers skipped over were lost, and this number becomes the latest. If d ≤ 0, it is a repeat or a late packet: ignore it. With `n` of 0 or 1, nothing is lost.

For example, the stream `65533, 65535, 0, 0, 65534, 3` loses 65534 (skipped between 65533 and 65535), nothing at 0, ignores the repeated 0 and the late 65534, and then loses 1 and 2: 3 in total. Unsigned arithmetic wraps, and that is defined: use it.
--- starter
#include <cstdint>

int seq_diff(uint16_t newer, uint16_t older) {
    return newer - older;
}

long long count_lost(const uint16_t* seq, int n) {
    long long lost = 0;
    for (int i = 1; i < n; i++) lost += seq[i] - seq[i - 1] - 1;
    return lost;
}
--- solution
#include <cstdint>

int seq_diff(uint16_t newer, uint16_t older) {
    int d = (uint16_t)(newer - older);   // 0 .. 65535: the distance forward, modulo 65536
    if (d >= 32768) d -= 65536;          // more than half way round means it is behind
    return d;
}

long long count_lost(const uint16_t* seq, int n) {
    if (n < 2) return 0;
    long long lost = 0;
    uint16_t latest = seq[0];
    for (int i = 1; i < n; i++) {
        int d = seq_diff(seq[i], latest);
        if (d > 0) {
            lost += d - 1;
            latest = seq[i];
        }
    }
    return lost;
}
--- hint
`newer - older` is computed in `int` after promotion, so it can be negative. Cutting it back to `uint16_t` gives the forward distance modulo 65536, a number from 0 to 65535.
--- hint
A forward distance of 32768 or more really means `newer` is behind: subtract 65536. That maps 0 to 65535 onto -32768 to 32767.
--- hint
In `count_lost`, compare each number with the latest one accepted, not with the previous one received, so repeats and late packets do not count as gaps.
--- check test | seq_diff examples, including wrap-around both ways
[] { return seq_diff(5, 3) == 2 && seq_diff(2, 65534) == 4 && seq_diff(3, 5) == -2 && seq_diff(65534, 2) == -4 && seq_diff(7, 7) == 0; }()
--- check test | seq_diff at half way round
[] { return seq_diff(32767, 0) == 32767 && seq_diff(32768, 0) == -32768 && seq_diff(0, 32768) == -32768 && seq_diff(0, 32769) == 32767; }()
--- check test | The example stream loses 3
[] { uint16_t s[6] = {65533, 65535, 0, 0, 65534, 3}; return count_lost(s, 6) == 3; }()
--- check test | Empty and single-packet streams lose nothing
[] { uint16_t s[1] = {100}; return count_lost(s, 0) == 0 && count_lost(s, 1) == 0; }()
--- check test | A perfect stream through several wraps loses nothing
[] { static uint16_t s[200000]; for (int i = 0; i < 200000; i++) s[i] = (uint16_t)(i + 60000); return count_lost(s, 200000) == 0; }()
--- check test | Every other packet lost, over 3 wraps
[] { static uint16_t s[100000]; for (int i = 0; i < 100000; i++) s[i] = (uint16_t)(2 * i); return count_lost(s, 100000) == 99999; }()
--- check test | Repeats and late packets are ignored
[] { uint16_t s[8] = {10, 12, 11, 12, 10, 13, 13, 20}; return count_lost(s, 8) == 7; }()

+++ question | Why the compiler may delete the check
--- ask
A compiler turns `bool grows(int x) { return x + 1 > x; }` into `return true;`. Which reason is correct?
--- choice
Because the compiler has a bug: `grows(INT_MAX)` should return `false`.
--- choice correct
Because for every `x` below `INT_MAX` the answer is `true`, and for `INT_MAX` the addition overflows, which is undefined, so any answer is allowed there.
--- choice
Because ints wrap round, and a wrapped value is always bigger.
--- choice
Because the compiler evaluates `x + 1` in 64 bits, where it never overflows.
--- why
A compiler only has to be correct for executions without undefined behaviour. `true` is right for every such call, so it is a correct translation. A debug build may still compute the wrapped value and return `false`, which is why the result changes with the optimisation level.

+++ question | Unsigned below zero
--- ask
What does this print, on any machine where `unsigned` is 32 bits?

```cpp fragment
unsigned u = 0;
u = u - 1;
printf("%u\n", u);
```
--- answer
4294967295
--- why
Unsigned arithmetic is defined to wrap modulo 2³², so 0 − 1 is 2³² − 1 = 4294967295. This is not undefined behaviour: only signed overflow is.
=== sys-11 | Linking: translation units, declarations, static and extern
--- teach
Last lesson's null check was deleted because the compiler could see the whole function at once. It cannot see your whole program at once. Every program in this course so far has been one file, but real ones are thousands: the Linux kernel has tens of thousands of `.c` files, and flight software is split into hundreds of modules written by different teams. Each file is compiled on its own, and a separate program, the **linker**, joins the results. This lesson explains how that works: what the compiler sees, what it leaves for the linker, the rules the linker follows, and the keywords `extern` and `static` that control what each file shares.

### From source files to a program

Building a C or C++ program happens in four stages:

1. **Preprocessing.** The preprocessor handles the lines that start with `#`. `#include "engine.h"` pastes the whole text of `engine.h` in its place; `#define` makes text substitutions; `#if` keeps or drops lines.
2. **Compiling.** The compiler turns one preprocessed file into machine code.
3. **Assembling.** The machine code is stored in an **[[object file|elf]]** (`engine.o`), together with a table of the names it defines and the names it needs from elsewhere.
4. **Linking.** The linker combines all the object files, and the libraries, into one executable.

On a command line it looks like this, with `-c` meaning "compile only, do not link":

```text
$ g++ -c engine.cpp            # engine.cpp -> engine.o
$ g++ -c main.cpp              # main.cpp   -> main.o
$ g++ engine.o main.o -o probe # link the two into the program "probe"
```

One preprocessed source file, with everything it includes pasted in, is called a **translation unit**. The compiler sees exactly one translation unit at a time and knows nothing about the others. That single fact explains the rest of this lesson.

### Declarations and definitions

If `main.cpp` calls `thrust`, and `thrust` is written in `engine.cpp`, how can the compiler of `main.cpp` check the call? You tell it, with a **declaration**: a statement that a name exists and what its type is. A **definition** is a declaration that also creates the thing: a function with its body, or a variable with its storage.

```cpp fragment
int thrust(int level);                  // declaration: thrust exists, takes an int, returns an int
int thrust(int level) { return 10 * level; }   // definition: here is its body

extern int total_impulse;               // declaration: an int of this name exists somewhere
int total_impulse = 0;                  // definition: the storage is here
```

`extern` in front of a variable says "declared here, defined elsewhere". Without `extern`, `int total_impulse;` at file scope is a definition, even with no initial value. A function declaration needs no `extern`, because the missing body already says it is not a definition. And one surprise: `extern int limit = 5;` is a definition, because it gives a value, and giving a value creates storage.

The two must agree, and the rule that ties them together is the **one definition rule** (ODR):

- A function or variable that is used must be **defined exactly once in the whole program**, though it may be declared any number of times.
- A type (a `struct`) or an `inline` function may be defined once in **each** translation unit that uses it, and all those definitions must be identical, token for token.

You can see declarations at work in one file. A declaration lets code use a name before its definition appears:

```cpp
#include <cstdio>

extern int burn_count;   // declaration: defined at the bottom
int record_burn();       // declaration: defined at the bottom

int main() {
    record_burn();
    record_burn();
    printf("%d\n", burn_count);   // 2
    return 0;
}

int burn_count = 0;               // definition
int record_burn() { return ++burn_count; }
```

### Symbols: what the compiler leaves for the linker

Here is a two-file program. `engine.cpp` defines things; `main.cpp` declares them and uses them.

```cpp fragment
// engine.cpp
#include <cstdio>

static int burns = 0;        // private to engine.cpp (explained below)
int total_impulse = 0;       // shared with other files

int thrust(int level) {
    burns++;
    total_impulse += level;
    return level * 10;
}

void report() {
    printf("%d burns, impulse %d\n", burns, total_impulse);
}
```

```cpp fragment
// main.cpp
int thrust(int level);       // declarations: defined in another file
void report();
extern int total_impulse;

int main() {
    thrust(3);
    thrust(4);
    report();
    return total_impulse == 7 ? 0 : 1;
}
```

Each object file carries a **symbol table**, which the tool `nm` prints. These are the real tables clang made:

```text
$ nm engine.o                        $ nm main.o
0000000000000030 T _Z6reportv                         U _Z6reportv
0000000000000000 T _Z6thrusti                         U _Z6thrusti
0000000000000004 b _ZL5burns        0000000000000000 T main
                 U printf                             U total_impulse
0000000000000000 B total_impulse
```

The letter in the middle says what each **symbol** (a name the linker can see) is: `T` is code defined here, `B` is a variable defined here, `U` is **undefined**: used here, and the linker must find it elsewhere. A capital letter means the symbol is visible to other files; a lower-case letter, like the `b` of `burns`, means it is private to this file. The odd names such as `_Z6thrusti` are explained below.

The linker then does two jobs:

1. **Symbol resolution.** It matches every undefined symbol to **exactly one** definition with external visibility, in some object file or library.
2. **Relocation.** The compiler did not know where `thrust` would end up, so it left a hole in each instruction that calls it, with a note saying "put the address of `thrust` here". Once the linker has placed every function and variable at an address, it fills in all the holes.

If resolution fails, you get the two classic link errors, straight from the linker, after the compiler has already accepted every file:

```text
main.cpp:(.text+0x15): undefined reference to `thrust(int)'
engine.cpp:(.text+0x0): multiple definition of `thrust(int)'; engine.o: first defined here
```

The first means no file defined it: you forgot to link `engine.o`, misspelled the name, or declared a different type than you defined. The second means two files defined it.

**Why a program that links is wired correctly.** Every use of an external name in a translation unit becomes an undefined symbol plus a relocation note. Resolution ties each undefined symbol to one definition, and relocation writes that definition's address into every note. So after linking, every use reaches the one definition of its name. What the linker cannot check is **types**: in C, the symbol for `total_impulse` is just the name, so if `main.c` declared it `extern double total_impulse;` the program would link and then read an `int`'s bytes as a `double`. C++ puts the parameter types of functions into the symbol name, which catches mismatched function declarations, but not variables of the wrong type. The cure is headers, below.

### Linkage: who can see a name

A name's **linkage** says which translation units can refer to it:

- **External linkage:** the name is visible to every translation unit. Functions and file-scope variables have it by default.
- **Internal linkage:** the name is private to its own translation unit. Two files can each have their own `static int burns;`, and they are two different variables that never clash. The linker never matches a use in another file to it.
- **No linkage:** local variables, visible only in their block.

`static` at file scope gives **internal linkage**. Use it for every helper function and variable that other files do not need: it keeps names from clashing across a large program, it tells the reader the thing is private, and it lets the compiler inline or delete the function freely, since no other file can call it. In C++, putting definitions inside an unnamed `namespace { ... }` does the same.

Confusingly, `static` inside a function means something else entirely: **static storage duration**. The variable lives for the whole run of the program, is initialised once, and keeps its value between calls:

```cpp fragment
int next_ticket() {
    static int last = 0;   // created once, keeps its value between calls
    return ++last;         // 1, then 2, then 3, ...
}
```

One more C++ rule, different from C: a `const` variable at file scope, such as `const int max_burns = 12;`, has **internal linkage** by default, so it can live in a header without clashing. Write `extern const int max_burns = 12;` to give it external linkage. The rule is about the variable itself being `const`. In `const char* station = "Kiruna";` the characters are const but the pointer variable is not (you may point it elsewhere later), so it has external linkage like any other variable.

### Headers

Repeating declarations by hand in every file that needs them is how mismatches creep in. So each module has a **header**, `engine.h`, holding its declarations. Every user includes it, and so does `engine.cpp` itself, so the compiler checks that the declarations and the definitions agree.

```cpp fragment
// engine.h
#ifndef ENGINE_H
#define ENGINE_H

struct Burn {                 // a type: may be defined in every file that includes this
    int level;
    double seconds;
};

extern int total_impulse;     // declarations only
int thrust(int level);
void report();

#endif
```

A header holds declarations, type definitions, macros, `const` values and `inline` functions: everything the one definition rule allows in many translation units. It never holds the definition of an ordinary function or variable, because every `.cpp` file that includes it would then define it, and linking two of them gives "multiple definition".

The `#ifndef ENGINE_H` / `#define ENGINE_H` / `#endif` lines are an **include guard**. If a translation unit includes `engine.h` twice, perhaps once directly and once through another header, the second copy sees `ENGINE_H` already defined and is skipped, so `struct Burn` is not defined twice in one translation unit, which would be a compile error. Most compilers also accept `#pragma once` as the first line, meaning the same.

### Name mangling and extern "C"

C++ allows two functions called `thrust`, one taking an `int` and one a `double`. The linker only matches names, so the compiler **mangles** each name into a unique symbol that includes its parameter types. On Linux and macOS the scheme is the [[Itanium C++ ABI|itanium]]: `_Z`, then the length of the name, the name, and a letter per parameter type. So `thrust(int)` is `_Z6thrusti` and `thrust(double)` is `_Z6thrustd`. `static int burns` became `_ZL5burns`: the `L` marks internal linkage.

C does not mangle: its symbols are the plain names. To call a C library from C++, or to let C (or Python's `ctypes`) call your C++ function, turn mangling off with **`extern "C"`**:

```cpp fragment
extern "C" int crc16(const unsigned char* data, int n);   // symbol "crc16", not "_Z5crc16PKhi"
```

### Libraries

A **static library** (`libnav.a`) is an archive of object files. The linker does not take all of it. It scans its inputs **in command-line order**, keeping a list of symbols still undefined, and from an archive it pulls in only the members that define something on that list. So order matters: `g++ main.o -lm` works, but `g++ -lm main.o` can fail, because when the linker reached the maths library nothing was undefined yet, and the traditional Unix linker, GNU `ld`, never goes back. (Newer linkers such as LLVM's `lld` do remember earlier archives, but portable build files still list each library after the code that uses it.) The flag `-lm` means "the library `libm`".

A **shared library** (`libnav.so` on Linux, `.dll` on Windows) is linked when the program starts, by the **[[dynamic linker|ld-so]]**. One copy in memory serves every program using it, and it can be updated without relinking them. A program can even load one while it runs and look a function up by name: `dlopen` opens the library, and `dlsym` returns the function's address, which you convert to a function pointer and call. That is how plugins work.

### What it costs

Separate compilation makes rebuilding cheap: change one `.cpp` file and only it is recompiled, then everything relinked. Change a header, and **every** translation unit that includes it must be recompiled. Headers are also parsed again in every translation unit: a header of H lines included by N files costs about N × H lines of compiling. Large C++ projects spend most of their build time re-reading the same standard headers, which is why precompiled headers and C++20 modules exist. Linking itself is roughly linear in the number of symbols and relocations, since linkers look symbols up in hash tables.

Because the compiler sees one translation unit at a time, it cannot inline a function defined in another file. **Link-time optimisation** (`-flto`) keeps the compiler's internal form in the object files and optimises across files at link time.

### Where it is used

- **Every build system** (make, CMake, Bazel) exists to track which translation units depend on which headers, so it recompiles exactly those.
- **Operating systems** load shared libraries into every process: the C library itself is one, `libc.so.6` on Linux.
- **Flight software frameworks** such as [[NASA's cFS|cfs]] build each application as a separately linked module with a small public header, and keep everything else `static`, so teams cannot depend on each other's internals.
- **Python** calls C through `ctypes` and extension modules by the plain `extern "C"` names.

**Watch out:** a variable **defined** in a header. `int total_impulse = 0;` in `engine.h` compiles in every file, and the moment two `.cpp` files include the header, the link fails with "multiple definition". Put `extern int total_impulse;` in the header, and the one definition in `engine.cpp`. The same goes for ordinary functions with bodies: in a header, mark them `inline` or `static`, or move the body to a `.cpp` file.

::: context elf What is inside an object file
On Linux an object file is in ELF, the Executable and Linkable Format; macOS uses Mach-O and Windows uses COFF, with the same ideas. It holds sections: `.text` for machine code, `.data` for variables with initial values, `.bss` for variables that start at zero (only their size is stored), and `.rodata` for constants such as string literals. Beside them sit the symbol table and the relocation entries. The tool `objdump -d` shows the code, and `readelf -s` the symbols.
:::

::: context itanium How C++ names are mangled
Most compilers outside Microsoft's follow the mangling rules of the Itanium C++ ABI, first written for Intel's Itanium processor around 2000 and now used by GCC and Clang on every platform they support. Builtin types have one-letter codes: i for int, j for unsigned int, x for long long, d for double, f for float, c for char, b for bool, v for void. A pointer adds P in front, and a const adds K, so `const char*` is PKc. The tool `c++filt` turns a mangled name back into a readable one: `c++filt _Z6thrusti` prints `thrust(int)`.
:::

::: context ld-so The program that runs first
When you start a dynamically linked program on Linux, the kernel first loads a small program named in the executable, the dynamic linker `ld-linux.so`. It finds each shared library the program needs, maps it into memory, and fills in the addresses of the library functions the program calls, often lazily, the first time each is called. Setting the variable `LD_DEBUG=bindings` makes it print every symbol it resolves.
:::

::: context cfs NASA's core Flight System
The core Flight System (cFS) is NASA's open-source framework for flight software, used on missions from small cubesats to the Lunar Gateway. Each application, such as housekeeping, telemetry output or a science instrument, is its own module with a published header of messages and functions, and is loaded and linked into the running system separately. Teams can develop and test applications independently because nothing outside the public header is visible.
:::
--- task
Write the symbol resolution step of a linker. No `main`.

```cpp fragment
struct Symbol {
    const char* name;
    char kind;   // 'D' defined here, external linkage; 'S' defined here, static; 'U' used here, defined elsewhere
};

struct Object {
    const char* file;
    const Symbol* syms;
    int count;
};
```

Include these definitions exactly as shown, and write `int link_objects(const Object* objs, int n, const char** bad)`, which checks whether the `n` object files link into a program:

- **Multiple definition:** a name with kind `'D'` in two or more places. Return 1 and set `*bad` to that name. If there are several, report the one whose **second** `'D'` comes first, scanning the files in order and each file's symbols in order.
- **Undefined reference:** otherwise, a `'U'` whose name has no `'D'` anywhere. An `'S'` symbol in another file does **not** satisfy it, because static names are private. Return 2 and set `*bad` to the first such `'U'` in scanning order.
- **No main:** otherwise, if no file has `main` with kind `'D'`, return 2 and set `*bad` to `"main"`.
- Otherwise return 0 and set `*bad` to `nullptr`.

`'S'` symbols never clash with anything, not even with a `'D'` of the same name in another file. Names are compared as texts with `strcmp`. A file never has both a `'U'` and an `'S'` for the same name.
--- starter
#include <cstring>

struct Symbol {
    const char* name;
    char kind;
};

struct Object {
    const char* file;
    const Symbol* syms;
    int count;
};

int link_objects(const Object* objs, int n, const char** bad) {
    *bad = nullptr;
    return 0;
}
--- solution
#include <cstring>

struct Symbol {
    const char* name;
    char kind;
};

struct Object {
    const char* file;
    const Symbol* syms;
    int count;
};

// Is `name` defined with external linkage before position (file f, symbol s)?
// With f == n it looks through every file.
static bool defined_before(const Object* objs, int n, int f, int s, const char* name) {
    for (int i = 0; i < n && i <= f; i++) {
        int end = i < f ? objs[i].count : s;
        for (int j = 0; j < end; j++)
            if (objs[i].syms[j].kind == 'D' && strcmp(objs[i].syms[j].name, name) == 0) return true;
    }
    return false;
}

int link_objects(const Object* objs, int n, const char** bad) {
    // 1. multiple definitions: a 'D' whose name already had a 'D' earlier
    for (int f = 0; f < n; f++)
        for (int s = 0; s < objs[f].count; s++) {
            const Symbol* sym = &objs[f].syms[s];
            if (sym->kind == 'D' && defined_before(objs, n, f, s, sym->name)) {
                *bad = sym->name;
                return 1;
            }
        }
    // 2. undefined references: a 'U' with no 'D' anywhere
    for (int f = 0; f < n; f++)
        for (int s = 0; s < objs[f].count; s++) {
            const Symbol* sym = &objs[f].syms[s];
            if (sym->kind == 'U' && !defined_before(objs, n, n, 0, sym->name)) {
                *bad = sym->name;
                return 2;
            }
        }
    // 3. the program must define main
    if (!defined_before(objs, n, n, 0, "main")) {
        *bad = "main";
        return 2;
    }
    *bad = nullptr;
    return 0;
}
--- hint
Write one helper that answers "is this name defined, with kind `'D'`, in any file?", and a version of it that only looks at the symbols before a given position. Both are two nested loops with `strcmp`.
--- hint
Do the three checks in order. For multiple definitions, walk every symbol in scanning order; the first `'D'` whose name already had a `'D'` earlier is the one to report. Then walk again for `'U'`s with no `'D'` anywhere, ignoring `'S'` symbols completely.
--- hint
The `main` check comes last, and only if the first two found nothing. Remember to set `*bad` on every path, to `nullptr` on success.
--- check test | The lesson's two files link
[] { Symbol e[5] = {{"_Z6reportv", 'D'}, {"_Z6thrusti", 'D'}, {"_ZL5burns", 'S'}, {"printf", 'U'}, {"total_impulse", 'D'}}; Symbol m[4] = {{"_Z6reportv", 'U'}, {"_Z6thrusti", 'U'}, {"main", 'D'}, {"total_impulse", 'U'}}; Symbol c[1] = {{"printf", 'D'}}; Object o[3] = {{"engine.o", e, 5}, {"main.o", m, 4}, {"libc", c, 1}}; const char* bad = "x"; return link_objects(o, 3, &bad) == 0 && bad == nullptr; }()
--- check test | Forgetting engine.o: undefined reference to thrust
[] { Symbol m[4] = {{"_Z6reportv", 'U'}, {"_Z6thrusti", 'U'}, {"main", 'D'}, {"total_impulse", 'U'}}; Object o[1] = {{"main.o", m, 4}}; const char* bad = nullptr; return link_objects(o, 1, &bad) == 2 && bad != nullptr && strcmp(bad, "_Z6reportv") == 0; }()
--- check test | Linking engine.o twice: multiple definition, the first one met
[] { Symbol e[3] = {{"_Z6reportv", 'D'}, {"_Z6thrusti", 'D'}, {"_ZL5burns", 'S'}}; Symbol m[2] = {{"main", 'D'}, {"_Z6thrusti", 'U'}}; Object o[3] = {{"engine.o", e, 3}, {"main.o", m, 2}, {"engine2.o", e, 3}}; const char* bad = nullptr; return link_objects(o, 3, &bad) == 1 && strcmp(bad, "_Z6reportv") == 0; }()
--- check test | Static names never clash, even with an external one
[] { Symbol a[2] = {{"count", 'S'}, {"main", 'D'}}; Symbol b[2] = {{"count", 'S'}, {"helper", 'D'}}; Symbol c[1] = {{"count", 'D'}}; Object o[3] = {{"a.o", a, 2}, {"b.o", b, 2}, {"c.o", c, 1}}; const char* bad = "x"; return link_objects(o, 3, &bad) == 0 && bad == nullptr; }()
--- check test | A static symbol does not satisfy another file's use
[] { Symbol a[2] = {{"main", 'D'}, {"calibrate", 'U'}}; Symbol b[1] = {{"calibrate", 'S'}}; Object o[2] = {{"main.o", a, 2}, {"cal.o", b, 1}}; const char* bad = nullptr; return link_objects(o, 2, &bad) == 2 && strcmp(bad, "calibrate") == 0; }()
--- check test | No main is an undefined reference to main
[] { Symbol a[2] = {{"thrust", 'D'}, {"report", 'D'}}; Object o[1] = {{"engine.o", a, 2}}; const char* bad = nullptr; bool first = link_objects(o, 1, &bad) == 2 && strcmp(bad, "main") == 0; Symbol s[1] = {{"main", 'S'}}; Object p[1] = {{"odd.o", s, 1}}; bad = nullptr; return first && link_objects(p, 1, &bad) == 2 && strcmp(bad, "main") == 0; }()
--- check test | Multiple definitions are reported before undefined references
[] { Symbol a[3] = {{"main", 'D'}, {"missing", 'U'}, {"dup", 'D'}}; Symbol b[1] = {{"dup", 'D'}}; Object o[2] = {{"a.o", a, 3}, {"b.o", b, 1}}; const char* bad = nullptr; return link_objects(o, 2, &bad) == 1 && strcmp(bad, "dup") == 0; }()
--- check test | Of two clashes, the one whose second definition comes first
[] { Symbol a[2] = {{"alpha", 'D'}, {"beta", 'D'}}; Symbol b[2] = {{"beta", 'D'}, {"main", 'D'}}; Symbol c[1] = {{"alpha", 'D'}}; Object o[3] = {{"a.o", a, 2}, {"b.o", b, 2}, {"c.o", c, 1}}; const char* bad = nullptr; return link_objects(o, 3, &bad) == 1 && strcmp(bad, "beta") == 0; }()
--- check test | Names compared as text, not as pointers
[] { static char n1[8] = "thrust"; static char n2[8] = "thrust"; static char n3[8] = "main"; Symbol a[2] = {{n3, 'D'}, {n1, 'U'}}; Symbol b[1] = {{n2, 'D'}}; Object o[2] = {{"a.o", a, 2}, {"b.o", b, 1}}; const char* bad = "x"; return link_objects(o, 2, &bad) == 0 && bad == nullptr; }()
--- check test | Zero object files: no main
[] { const char* bad = nullptr; return link_objects(nullptr, 0, &bad) == 2 && strcmp(bad, "main") == 0; }()

+++ practice | Ticket numbers with private state
--- task
A ground station hands out ticket numbers. Write three functions that share one counter, which no other file of the program can see. No `main`.

- `int next_id()`: return the next ticket number. The first call returns 1, then 2, 3, and so on.
- `void reset_ids(int start)`: make the next call of `next_id` return `start`.
- `int ids_issued()`: how many tickets `next_id` has returned since the program started or since the last `reset_ids`, whichever is later.

Keep the state in variables at file scope with internal linkage, so no other file could reach them by name.
--- starter
int next_id() {
    int last = 0;
    return ++last;
}

void reset_ids(int start) {
}

int ids_issued() {
    return 0;
}
--- solution
static int next_value = 1;   // internal linkage: private to this file
static int issued = 0;

int next_id() {
    issued++;
    return next_value++;
}

void reset_ids(int start) {
    next_value = start;
    issued = 0;
}

int ids_issued() {
    return issued;
}
--- hint
A local variable is created afresh on every call, so the starter always returns 1. The counter must outlive the calls and be shared by three functions, so it belongs at file scope.
--- hint
`static` at file scope gives the variable internal linkage: it lives for the whole program, and only this file can name it. Keep two of them: the next number to hand out, and how many were handed out since the last reset.
--- check test | Counts 1, 2, 3 from the start, then resets to 100
[] { int a = next_id(), b = next_id(), c = next_id(); int issued = ids_issued(); reset_ids(100); int d = next_id(), e = next_id(); return a == 1 && b == 2 && c == 3 && issued == 3 && d == 100 && e == 101 && ids_issued() == 2; }()
--- check test | A reset with no calls after it has issued 0
[] { reset_ids(7); return ids_issued() == 0 && next_id() == 7 && ids_issued() == 1; }()
--- check test | Resetting to zero and to negative numbers
[] { reset_ids(-2); int a = next_id(), b = next_id(), c = next_id(); return a == -2 && b == -1 && c == 0; }()
--- check test | A thousand tickets in a row
[] { reset_ids(1); int last = 0; for (int i = 0; i < 1000; i++) last = next_id(); return last == 1000 && ids_issued() == 1000; }()
--- check source | The counter has internal linkage
^\s*static\s+(int|unsigned|long)

+++ practice | Mangled names
--- task
Write `int mangle(const char* name, const char* const* params, int nparams, char* out, int cap)`, which builds the Itanium C++ symbol name of a function, as clang and GCC do. No `main`.

The symbol is `_Z`, then the length of `name` in decimal, then `name`, then one code per parameter, in order. A function with no parameters gets the single code `v`. The parameter types are texts from this list:

| type | code |
|---|---|
| `int`, `unsigned`, `long long` | `i`, `j`, `x` |
| `double`, `float` | `d`, `f` |
| `char`, `bool` | `c`, `b` |

Any of these, or `void`, may be followed by `*` to make a pointer, which puts `P` in front of the code (`void*` is `Pv`). A pointer may also start with `const `, which puts `PK` in front (`const char*` is `PKc`). Pointers to pointers do not occur, and no pointer type appears twice in one list (real mangling abbreviates repeats).

Write the symbol into `out`, followed by a terminating zero, and return its length. If a type is not in this list, or the symbol and its zero do not fit in `cap` bytes, return -1.

For example, `thrust(int)` is `_Z6thrusti`, `log_event(const char*, int)` is `_Z9log_eventPKci`, and `reset()` is `_Z5resetv`.
--- starter
#include <cstdio>
#include <cstring>

int mangle(const char* name, const char* const* params, int nparams, char* out, int cap) {
    return snprintf(out, cap, "%s", name);
}
--- solution
#include <cstdio>
#include <cstring>

// The one-letter code of a builtin type, or 0 if it is not one we know.
static char code_of(const char* t, int len) {
    static const char* const names[8] = {"int", "unsigned", "long long", "double", "float", "char", "bool", "void"};
    static const char codes[8] = {'i', 'j', 'x', 'd', 'f', 'c', 'b', 'v'};
    for (int i = 0; i < 8; i++)
        if ((int)strlen(names[i]) == len && strncmp(names[i], t, len) == 0) return codes[i];
    return 0;
}

int mangle(const char* name, const char* const* params, int nparams, char* out, int cap) {
    char buf[256];
    int k = snprintf(buf, sizeof buf, "_Z%d%s", (int)strlen(name), name);
    if (nparams == 0) buf[k++] = 'v';
    for (int p = 0; p < nparams; p++) {
        const char* t = params[p];
        int len = (int)strlen(t);
        bool pointer = len > 0 && t[len - 1] == '*';
        bool is_const = strncmp(t, "const ", 6) == 0;
        if (is_const && !pointer) return -1;
        if (pointer) len--;
        if (is_const) {
            t += 6;
            len -= 6;
        }
        char c = code_of(t, len);
        if (c == 0 || (c == 'v' && !pointer)) return -1;
        if (k + 3 >= (int)sizeof buf) return -1;
        if (pointer) buf[k++] = 'P';
        if (is_const) buf[k++] = 'K';
        buf[k++] = c;
    }
    if (k + 1 > cap) return -1;
    memcpy(out, buf, k);
    out[k] = '\0';
    return k;
}
--- hint
Build the start with `snprintf(buf, sizeof buf, "_Z%d%s", length, name)`, then append one code per parameter. Only at the end, check that the whole symbol and its zero fit in `cap`, and copy it to `out`.
--- hint
For each type, peel off the decorations first: a `*` at the end means pointer, and `const ` at the start (only allowed on a pointer) means const. What is left must be exactly one of the listed names; compare its length as well as its letters, so `in` or `integer` do not match `int`.
--- hint
The order of the prefixes is `P` then `K`, then the code: `const double*` is `PKd`. Plain `void` as a parameter is not in the list; only `void*` is.
--- check test | The three examples
[] { char o[64]; const char* p1[1] = {"int"}; const char* p2[2] = {"const char*", "int"}; bool a = mangle("thrust", p1, 1, o, 64) == 10 && strcmp(o, "_Z6thrusti") == 0; bool b = mangle("log_event", p2, 2, o, 64) == 16 && strcmp(o, "_Z9log_eventPKci") == 0; bool c = mangle("reset", nullptr, 0, o, 64) == 9 && strcmp(o, "_Z5resetv") == 0; return a && b && c; }()
--- check test | Every builtin code, as clang mangles set_mode and burn
[] { char o[64]; const char* p1[3] = {"unsigned", "bool", "char"}; const char* p2[2] = {"long long", "long long"}; bool a = mangle("set_mode", p1, 3, o, 64) > 0 && strcmp(o, "_Z8set_modejbc") == 0; bool b = mangle("burn", p2, 2, o, 64) > 0 && strcmp(o, "_Z4burnxx") == 0; return a && b; }()
--- check test | Pointers, const pointers and void*
[] { char o[64]; const char* p1[3] = {"float*", "long long", "double"}; const char* p2[2] = {"void*", "const double*"}; const char* p3[3] = {"const unsigned*", "int", "int"}; const char* p4[2] = {"char*", "unsigned*"}; bool a = mangle("aim", p1, 3, o, 64) > 0 && strcmp(o, "_Z3aimPfxd") == 0; bool b = mangle("x", p2, 2, o, 64) > 0 && strcmp(o, "_Z1xPvPKd") == 0; bool c = mangle("pack", p3, 3, o, 64) > 0 && strcmp(o, "_Z4packPKjii") == 0; bool d = mangle("beacon", p4, 2, o, 64) > 0 && strcmp(o, "_Z6beaconPcPj") == 0; return a && b && c && d; }()
--- check test | A name of 12 letters gets a two-digit length

--- check test | Unknown types are refused

--- check test | Exactly enough room, and one byte too few

+++ practice | Looking symbols up by name
--- task
A shared library exports its functions in a table sorted by name, and the dynamic linker finds a function by name at run time, like `dlsym`. Write that lookup. No `main`.

```cpp fragment
using Fn = int (*)(int);

struct Export {
    const char* name;
    Fn fn;
};
```

Include these definitions exactly as shown, and write:

- `Fn find_symbol(const Export* table, int n, const char* name)`: the function exported under `name`, or `nullptr` if there is none. The `n` entries are sorted by name in `strcmp` order, with no repeats. Use **binary search**: the checks look up 200,000 names in a table of 200,000 entries, which a linear scan cannot finish in time.
- `int call_by_name(const Export* table, int n, const char* name, int arg, int* result)`: find the function; if it exists, call it with `arg`, store its return value in `*result` and return 0; otherwise return -1 and leave `*result` unchanged.
--- starter
#include <cstdio>
#include <cstring>

using Fn = int (*)(int);

struct Export {
    const char* name;
    Fn fn;
};

Fn find_symbol(const Export* table, int n, const char* name) {
    for (int i = 0; i < n; i++)
        if (strcmp(table[i].name, name) == 0) return nullptr;
    return nullptr;
}

int call_by_name(const Export* table, int n, const char* name, int arg, int* result) {
    return -1;
}
--- solution
#include <cstdio>
#include <cstring>

using Fn = int (*)(int);

struct Export {
    const char* name;
    Fn fn;
};

Fn find_symbol(const Export* table, int n, const char* name) {
    int lo = 0, hi = n - 1;
    while (lo <= hi) {
        int mid = lo + (hi - lo) / 2;
        int c = strcmp(name, table[mid].name);
        if (c == 0) return table[mid].fn;
        if (c < 0) hi = mid - 1;
        else lo = mid + 1;
    }
    return nullptr;
}

int call_by_name(const Export* table, int n, const char* name, int arg, int* result) {
    Fn f = find_symbol(table, n, name);
    if (f == nullptr) return -1;
    *result = f(arg);
    return 0;
}
--- hint
Binary search, as in DSA, with `strcmp` in place of `<`: compare `name` with the middle entry's name; 0 means found, negative means look left, positive means look right.
--- hint
`call_by_name` is three steps: look the function up, return -1 if it is missing, otherwise call it through the pointer and store the result.
--- check test | Finds and calls functions by name
[] { static Export t[3] = {{"double_it", [](int x) { return 2 * x; }}, {"negate", [](int x) { return -x; }}, {"square", [](int x) { return x * x; }}}; int r = 0; bool a = call_by_name(t, 3, "square", 7, &r) == 0 && r == 49; bool b = call_by_name(t, 3, "double_it", 21, &r) == 0 && r == 42; bool c = call_by_name(t, 3, "negate", 5, &r) == 0 && r == -5; return a && b && c && find_symbol(t, 3, "negate") == t[1].fn; }()
--- check test | Missing names give nullptr and -1
[] { static Export t[3] = {{"b", [](int x) { return x; }}, {"d", [](int x) { return x; }}, {"f", [](int x) { return x; }}}; int r = 99; return find_symbol(t, 3, "a") == nullptr && find_symbol(t, 3, "c") == nullptr && find_symbol(t, 3, "g") == nullptr && find_symbol(t, 3, "dd") == nullptr && call_by_name(t, 3, "e", 1, &r) == -1 && r == 99; }()
--- check test | An empty table and a table of one
[] { static Export t[1] = {{"only", [](int x) { return x + 1; }}}; int r = 0; return find_symbol(t, 0, "only") == nullptr && call_by_name(t, 1, "only", 9, &r) == 0 && r == 10; }()
--- check test | The first and last entries of a table of 1000
[] { static char names[1000][8]; static Export t[1000]; for (int i = 0; i < 1000; i++) { snprintf(names[i], 8, "f%04d", i); t[i].name = names[i]; t[i].fn = (i % 2) ? (Fn)[](int x) { return x + 1; } : (Fn)[](int x) { return x - 1; }; } int r = 0; bool a = call_by_name(t, 1000, "f0000", 10, &r) == 0 && r == 9; bool b = call_by_name(t, 1000, "f0999", 10, &r) == 0 && r == 11; return a && b && find_symbol(t, 1000, "f1000") == nullptr; }()
--- check test | 200000 lookups in a table of 200000
[] { static char names[200000][8]; static Export t[200000]; for (int i = 0; i < 200000; i++) { snprintf(names[i], 8, "s%06d", i); t[i].name = names[i]; t[i].fn = [](int x) { return x * 3; }; } char key[8]; long long total = 0; for (int i = 0; i < 200000; i++) { snprintf(key, 8, "s%06d", (i * 7919) % 200000); Fn f = find_symbol(t, 200000, key); if (f == nullptr) return false; total += f(1); } snprintf(key, 8, "s%06d", 200000); return total == 600000 && find_symbol(t, 200000, key) == nullptr; }()

+++ practice | Declaration or definition?
--- task
Write a function that reads one line of C++ at file scope and says whether it is a declaration or a definition, and what linkage the name has. No `main`.

`int classify(const char* line, char* linkage)` returns 1 for a definition and 0 for a declaration, and stores `'E'` (external linkage) or `'I'` (internal linkage) in `*linkage`.

Every line has this form, with single spaces between words: optionally `static ` or `extern `, then optionally `const `, then a type and a name, and then one of: `;` (a variable), ` = value;` (a variable with a value), `(parameters);` (a function declaration) or `(parameters) { body }` (a function with a body). A line is a function exactly when it contains `(`.

Apply the lesson's rules:

- A function is a definition when it has a body. Its linkage is internal with `static`, external otherwise.
- A variable with `extern` is a declaration, unless it has a value, which makes it a definition. Any other variable is a definition.
- A variable has internal linkage with `static`, or with `const` and no `extern` (the C++ rule). Otherwise it has external linkage. But a `const` in front of a pointer type, as in `const char* name`, makes the characters const, not the variable, so it does not count: the variable's type is a pointer exactly when a `*` comes before the `=` (or before the `;` when there is no value).

For example, `extern int burns;` is a declaration with external linkage (0, `'E'`), and `const int max_burns = 12;` is a definition with internal linkage (1, `'I'`).
--- starter
#include <cstring>

int classify(const char* line, char* linkage) {
    *linkage = 'E';
    return strchr(line, ';') != nullptr ? 0 : 1;
}
--- solution
#include <cstring>

static bool starts_with(const char* s, const char* prefix) {
    return strncmp(s, prefix, strlen(prefix)) == 0;
}

int classify(const char* line, char* linkage) {
    bool is_static = starts_with(line, "static ");
    bool is_extern = starts_with(line, "extern ");
    const char* rest = line;
    if (is_static || is_extern) rest += 7;
    bool is_const = starts_with(rest, "const ");

    if (strchr(line, '(') != nullptr) {
        *linkage = is_static ? 'I' : 'E';
        return strchr(line, '{') != nullptr ? 1 : 0;
    }
    const char* eq = strchr(line, '=');
    bool has_value = eq != nullptr;
    size_t decl_len = has_value ? (size_t)(eq - line) : strlen(line);
    bool pointer = memchr(line, '*', decl_len) != nullptr;   // const char* p: the chars are const, not p
    bool const_variable = is_const && !pointer;
    *linkage = (is_static || (const_variable && !is_extern)) ? 'I' : 'E';
    if (is_extern && !has_value) return 0;
    return 1;
}
--- hint
Pick the line apart with a few tests: does it start with `static ` or `extern `? After that word, does it start with `const `? Does it contain `(`, `{` or `=`? `strncmp` and `strchr` answer each in one call.
--- hint
Decide functions first: body or not, and `static` or not. For variables, the only declaration is `extern` without a value.
--- hint
For a variable's linkage, internal comes from `static`, or from `const` when there is no `extern` and no `*` before the value. So `extern const int limit = 5;` is an external definition, and so is `const char* mode;`.
--- check test | The two examples
[] { char k = '?'; bool a = classify("extern int burns;", &k) == 0 && k == 'E'; bool b = classify("const int max_burns = 12;", &k) == 1 && k == 'I'; return a && b; }()
--- check test | Plain variables are external definitions, with or without a value
[] { char k = '?'; bool a = classify("int count;", &k) == 1 && k == 'E'; bool b = classify("double gain = 1.5;", &k) == 1 && k == 'E'; return a && b; }()
--- check test | extern with a value is a definition, and extern const stays external
[] { char k = '?'; bool a = classify("extern int limit = 5;", &k) == 1 && k == 'E'; bool b = classify("extern const int limit = 5;", &k) == 1 && k == 'E'; bool c = classify("extern const double g;", &k) == 0 && k == 'E'; return a && b && c; }()
--- check test | static variables are internal, const or not
[] { char k = '?'; bool a = classify("static int calls = 0;", &k) == 1 && k == 'I'; bool b = classify("static const double g = 9.81;", &k) == 1 && k == 'I'; bool c = classify("static char buffer;", &k) == 1 && k == 'I'; return a && b && c; }()
--- check test | Functions: body or no body, static or not
[] { char k = '?'; bool a = classify("int thrust(int level);", &k) == 0 && k == 'E'; bool b = classify("int thrust(int level) { return level; }", &k) == 1 && k == 'E'; bool c = classify("static int helper(int x);", &k) == 0 && k == 'I'; bool d = classify("static void tick() { }", &k) == 1 && k == 'I'; bool e = classify("extern void report();", &k) == 0 && k == 'E'; return a && b && c && d && e; }()
--- check test | A const function return type does not make a function internal
[] { char k = '?'; bool a = classify("const char* name_of(int id);", &k) == 0 && k == 'E'; bool b = classify("const char* name_of(int id) { return 0; }", &k) == 1 && k == 'E'; return a && b; }()
--- check test | A pointer to const chars is not a const variable
[] { char k = '?'; bool a = classify("const char* mode = \"static\";", &k) == 1 && k == 'E'; bool b = classify("const char* station;", &k) == 1 && k == 'E'; bool c = classify("static const char* tag = \"x\";", &k) == 1 && k == 'I'; bool d = classify("const double* table;", &k) == 1 && k == 'E'; return a && b && c && d; }()

+++ practice | Debug: the header pasted twice
--- task
This function simulates the preprocessor's `#include`. Each file is a list of lines. A line `#include NAME` is replaced by the lines of file `NAME`, themselves expanded the same way. A file whose first line is `#pragma once` is pasted at most once in a translation unit: later includes of it are skipped. The `#pragma once` line itself is never output, and neither is any `#include` line. An include of a name not in the list is dropped.

```cpp fragment
struct SourceFile {
    const char* name;
    const char* const* lines;
    int count;
};
```

`int preprocess(const SourceFile* files, int nfiles, const char* main_name, const char** out, int cap)` expands the file `main_name` and stores pointers to the output lines, in order, in `out`, returning how many there are. Lines beyond `cap` are dropped. It pastes a header marked `#pragma once` again every time it is included, and two headers marked `#pragma once` that include each other make it crash. Find the bug and fix it. No `main`.
--- starter
#include <cstring>

struct SourceFile {
    const char* name;
    const char* const* lines;
    int count;
};

static int find_file(const SourceFile* files, int nfiles, const char* name) {
    for (int i = 0; i < nfiles; i++)
        if (strcmp(files[i].name, name) == 0) return i;
    return -1;
}

static int expand(const SourceFile* files, int nfiles, int f, bool* seen, const char** out, int count, int cap) {
    const SourceFile* sf = &files[f];
    int start = 0;
    if (sf->count > 0 && strcmp(sf->lines[0], "#pragma once") == 0) {
        if (seen[f]) return count;
        start = 1;
    }
    for (int i = start; i < sf->count; i++) {
        const char* line = sf->lines[i];
        if (strncmp(line, "#include ", 9) == 0) {
            int g = find_file(files, nfiles, line + 9);
            if (g >= 0) count = expand(files, nfiles, g, seen, out, count, cap);
        } else if (count < cap) {
            out[count++] = line;
        }
    }
    return count;
}

int preprocess(const SourceFile* files, int nfiles, const char* main_name, const char** out, int cap) {
    bool seen[64] = {};
    int m = find_file(files, nfiles, main_name);
    if (m < 0) return 0;
    return expand(files, nfiles, m, seen, out, 0, cap);
}
--- solution
#include <cstring>

struct SourceFile {
    const char* name;
    const char* const* lines;
    int count;
};

static int find_file(const SourceFile* files, int nfiles, const char* name) {
    for (int i = 0; i < nfiles; i++)
        if (strcmp(files[i].name, name) == 0) return i;
    return -1;
}

static int expand(const SourceFile* files, int nfiles, int f, bool* seen, const char** out, int count, int cap) {
    const SourceFile* sf = &files[f];
    int start = 0;
    if (sf->count > 0 && strcmp(sf->lines[0], "#pragma once") == 0) {
        if (seen[f]) return count;
        seen[f] = true;   // mark it before expanding, so includes inside it cannot paste it again
        start = 1;
    }
    for (int i = start; i < sf->count; i++) {
        const char* line = sf->lines[i];
        if (strncmp(line, "#include ", 9) == 0) {
            int g = find_file(files, nfiles, line + 9);
            if (g >= 0) count = expand(files, nfiles, g, seen, out, count, cap);
        } else if (count < cap) {
            out[count++] = line;
        }
    }
    return count;
}

int preprocess(const SourceFile* files, int nfiles, const char* main_name, const char** out, int cap) {
    bool seen[64] = {};
    int m = find_file(files, nfiles, main_name);
    if (m < 0) return 0;
    return expand(files, nfiles, m, seen, out, 0, cap);
}
--- hint
The code reads `seen[f]` to decide whether to skip a file. Where is it ever set to `true`?
--- hint
Mark the file as seen as soon as you decide to paste it, before expanding its lines. Marking it only afterwards would still let a file that includes itself, through another header, recurse forever.
--- check test | A header included twice through two paths is pasted once
[] { static const char* m[3] = {"#include nav.h", "#include engine.h", "int main() {}"}; static const char* n[3] = {"#pragma once", "#include types.h", "void nav();"}; static const char* e[3] = {"#pragma once", "#include types.h", "void engine();"}; static const char* t[2] = {"#pragma once", "struct Burn {};"}; SourceFile f[4] = {{"main.cpp", m, 3}, {"nav.h", n, 3}, {"engine.h", e, 3}, {"types.h", t, 2}}; const char* out[16]; int k = preprocess(f, 4, "main.cpp", out, 16); return k == 4 && strcmp(out[0], "struct Burn {};") == 0 && strcmp(out[1], "void nav();") == 0 && strcmp(out[2], "void engine();") == 0 && strcmp(out[3], "int main() {}") == 0; }()
--- check test | A file without pragma once is pasted every time
[] { static const char* m[3] = {"#include table.inc", "#include table.inc", "end"}; static const char* t[1] = {"row"}; SourceFile f[2] = {{"main.cpp", m, 3}, {"table.inc", t, 1}}; const char* out[8]; int k = preprocess(f, 2, "main.cpp", out, 8); return k == 3 && strcmp(out[0], "row") == 0 && strcmp(out[1], "row") == 0 && strcmp(out[2], "end") == 0; }()
--- check test | Two headers that include each other stop
[] { static const char* m[2] = {"#include a.h", "done"}; static const char* a[3] = {"#pragma once", "#include b.h", "A"}; static const char* b[3] = {"#pragma once", "#include a.h", "B"}; SourceFile f[3] = {{"main.cpp", m, 2}, {"a.h", a, 3}, {"b.h", b, 3}}; const char* out[8]; int k = preprocess(f, 3, "main.cpp", out, 8); return k == 3 && strcmp(out[0], "B") == 0 && strcmp(out[1], "A") == 0 && strcmp(out[2], "done") == 0; }()
--- check test | Unknown includes are dropped, and the output is cut at cap
[] { static const char* m[4] = {"one", "#include missing.h", "two", "three"}; SourceFile f[1] = {{"main.cpp", m, 4}}; const char* out[8]; int k = preprocess(f, 1, "main.cpp", out, 8); int j = preprocess(f, 1, "main.cpp", out, 2); return k == 3 && j == 2 && strcmp(out[0], "one") == 0 && strcmp(out[1], "two") == 0; }()
--- check test | One header included from 10 places appears once
[] { static const char* m[11]; for (int i = 0; i < 10; i++) m[i] = "#include common.h"; m[10] = "main"; static const char* c[2] = {"#pragma once", "common"}; SourceFile f[2] = {{"main.cpp", m, 11}, {"common.h", c, 2}}; const char* out[32]; int k = preprocess(f, 2, "main.cpp", out, 32); return k == 2 && strcmp(out[0], "common") == 0 && strcmp(out[1], "main") == 0; }()

+++ practice | Libraries in the right order
--- task
Simulate how a Unix linker takes object files and static libraries from its command line, and show why their order matters. No `main`.

```cpp fragment
struct Member {
    const char* name;
    const char* const* defines;
    int ndef;
    const char* const* uses;
    int nuse;
};

struct Input {
    bool is_archive;          // false: one object file; true: a static library
    const Member* members;    // an object file has exactly one member
    int count;
};
```

Include these definitions exactly as shown, and write `int link_inputs(const Input* inputs, int n, const char** missing)`. The linker keeps a set of defined names and a list of undefined names, in the order they became undefined. **Including** a member means: add each of its `defines` to the defined set (removing it from the undefined list), then add each of its `uses` that is not defined yet, and not already in the list, to the end of the undefined list.

Process the inputs from left to right:

- An object file is always included.
- For an archive, scan its members in order, and include every member not yet included that defines at least one name currently in the undefined list. Repeat the scan of **this** archive until a full scan includes nothing, then move on. The linker never goes back to an earlier input.

At the end, if the undefined list is empty, return the number of archive members included. Otherwise return -1 and set `*missing` to the first name still in the undefined list. Assume no name is defined twice.

For example, with `main.o` using `sqrt`, and the maths library defining it: `main.o libm.a` includes 1 member and returns 1, but `libm.a main.o` returns -1 with `*missing` equal to `"sqrt"`.
--- starter
#include <cstring>

struct Member {
    const char* name;
    const char* const* defines;
    int ndef;
    const char* const* uses;
    int nuse;
};

struct Input {
    bool is_archive;
    const Member* members;
    int count;
};

int link_inputs(const Input* inputs, int n, const char** missing) {
    return 0;
}
--- solution
#include <cstring>

struct Member {
    const char* name;
    const char* const* defines;
    int ndef;
    const char* const* uses;
    int nuse;
};

struct Input {
    bool is_archive;
    const Member* members;
    int count;
};

struct Names {
    const char* items[512];
    int count;
};

static int find(const Names* s, const char* name) {
    for (int i = 0; i < s->count; i++)
        if (strcmp(s->items[i], name) == 0) return i;
    return -1;
}

static void drop(Names* s, int i) {
    for (int j = i + 1; j < s->count; j++) s->items[j - 1] = s->items[j];   // keep the order
    s->count--;
}

static void include(const Member* m, Names* defined, Names* undefined) {
    for (int i = 0; i < m->ndef; i++) {
        if (find(defined, m->defines[i]) < 0) defined->items[defined->count++] = m->defines[i];
        int u = find(undefined, m->defines[i]);
        if (u >= 0) drop(undefined, u);
    }
    for (int i = 0; i < m->nuse; i++)
        if (find(defined, m->uses[i]) < 0 && find(undefined, m->uses[i]) < 0)
            undefined->items[undefined->count++] = m->uses[i];
}

static bool wanted(const Member* m, const Names* undefined) {
    for (int i = 0; i < m->ndef; i++)
        if (find(undefined, m->defines[i]) >= 0) return true;
    return false;
}

int link_inputs(const Input* inputs, int n, const char** missing) {
    static Names defined, undefined;
    defined.count = 0;
    undefined.count = 0;
    int pulled = 0;
    for (int k = 0; k < n; k++) {
        const Input* in = &inputs[k];
        if (!in->is_archive) {
            include(&in->members[0], &defined, &undefined);
            continue;
        }
        bool taken[256] = {};
        bool progress = true;
        while (progress) {
            progress = false;
            for (int i = 0; i < in->count; i++) {
                if (!taken[i] && wanted(&in->members[i], &undefined)) {
                    taken[i] = true;
                    include(&in->members[i], &defined, &undefined);
                    pulled++;
                    progress = true;
                }
            }
        }
    }
    if (undefined.count > 0) {
        *missing = undefined.items[0];
        return -1;
    }
    return pulled;
}
--- hint
Keep two lists of names: defined, and undefined in the order they became undefined. Write one helper, "include this member", that updates both lists exactly as the task describes. Removing from the undefined list must keep the order of the rest.
--- hint
For an archive, remember which of its members are already included. Loop over its members, including any member that defines something now undefined; if a scan included anything, scan again, because a member included late may need a member that came earlier in the same archive.
--- hint
At most 512 names and 256 members per archive appear in the checks, so fixed arrays are enough. Count only archive members in the return value; object files are always included and do not count.
--- check test | main.o then libm.a works; libm.a then main.o fails on sqrt
[] { static const char* md[1] = {"main"}; static const char* mu[1] = {"sqrt"}; static const char* sd[1] = {"sqrt"}; static Member mainm[1] = {{"main.o", md, 1, mu, 1}}; static Member libm[1] = {{"sqrt.o", sd, 1, nullptr, 0}}; Input good[2] = {{false, mainm, 1}, {true, libm, 1}}; Input bad[2] = {{true, libm, 1}, {false, mainm, 1}}; const char* miss = nullptr; int a = link_inputs(good, 2, &miss); int b = link_inputs(bad, 2, &miss); return a == 1 && b == -1 && miss != nullptr && strcmp(miss, "sqrt") == 0; }()
