+++ question | Two meanings of static
--- ask
Two files of one program each contain, at file scope, the line `static int calls = 0;`. What happens?
--- choice
The link fails with "multiple definition of calls".
--- choice correct
Each file gets its own private variable called `calls`; they never clash, and neither file can reach the other's.
--- choice
The linker merges them into one shared variable, since they have the same name and type.
--- choice
It compiles only if one of the two lines is changed to `extern int calls;`.
--- why
`static` at file scope gives internal linkage: the name is not visible to the linker as a shared symbol, so the two variables are separate. Without `static`, both lines would be definitions of one external name, and the link would fail with "multiple definition".

+++ question | A definition in a header
--- ask
`config.h` contains `int max_retries = 3;` and has an include guard. Two files, `radio.cpp` and `logger.cpp`, both include it, and each compiles without error. What happens when they are linked together?
--- choice
Nothing goes wrong: the include guard makes sure the variable is only defined once.
--- choice correct
The link fails with "multiple definition of max_retries", because each translation unit defines it once.
--- choice
Each file silently gets its own copy of `max_retries`.
--- choice
The compiler of the second file reports that `max_retries` is already defined.
--- why
An include guard only stops a header from being pasted twice into **one** translation unit. Each `.cpp` file is compiled separately, so each gets one definition, and the linker then sees two definitions of an external name. Put `extern int max_retries;` in the header and the definition in one `.cpp` file.
=== sys-12 | Files and streams: fopen, fread, fwrite, binary records and buffering
--- teach
Last lesson's linker read object files and wrote an executable: files on disk, full of binary records. Every program you have written in this course so far forgot everything when it ended. Files are how programs remember: logs, configuration, telemetry recordings, databases. This lesson covers the C library's way of reading and writing them, through **streams**: text and binary data, fixed-size records you can jump between, and the buffering that makes it all fast.

In ORBIT, files live in memory for the length of one run: a program can create a file and read it back, but nothing is there when it starts.

### Opening and closing: FILE*

`fopen`, from `<cstdio>`, opens a file and returns a `FILE*`: a pointer to a **stream**, a struct the library keeps with the file's position, its buffer and its error state. You never look inside it; you pass it to the other functions.

```cpp fragment
FILE* f = fopen("orbit_log.txt", "w");
if (f == nullptr) { /* could not open: handle it */ }
// ... use f ...
fclose(f);
```

The second argument is the **mode**:

| mode | meaning | if the file exists | if it does not |
|---|---|---|---|
| `"r"` | read | read from the start | fail |
| `"w"` | write | **empty it first** | create it |
| `"a"` | append | every write goes to the end | create it |
| `"r+"` | read and write | start at the beginning, keep the contents | fail |
| `"w+"` | read and write | empty it first | create it |

Add `b` for a **binary** file: `"rb"`, `"wb"`, `"r+b"`. On Linux and macOS it changes nothing. On Windows, text mode turns every `\n` you write into the two bytes `\r\n` and back again when reading, which corrupts binary data. Always use `b` for anything that is not text, so your code works everywhere.

`fopen` returns a null pointer when it fails: the file does not exist, the name is not allowed, there are too many files open. Check it every time; the next lesson shows how to find out why it failed. `fclose` writes out anything still waiting in the buffer and releases the stream. As with `malloc` and `free`, every successful `fopen` needs exactly one `fclose`: an unclosed stream **leaks** a limited resource, and can lose data that was never written out.

### Text: fprintf, fgets and getc

Writing text works like `printf`, with the stream in front: `fprintf(f, ...)`. `fputs(text, f)` writes a string, and `fputc(c, f)` one character.

Reading has two common tools. `fgets(buf, size, f)` reads one line into `buf`: it stops after a `\n` (which it keeps), or when `size - 1` characters have been read, or at the end of the file, and always adds the terminating zero. It returns a null pointer when it could read nothing at all.

```cpp
#include <cstdio>

int main() {
    FILE* f = fopen("files_demo_passes.txt", "w");   // create it, or empty it
    if (f == nullptr) return 1;
    fprintf(f, "pass %d over %s\n", 1, "Kiruna");
    fprintf(f, "pass %d over %s\n", 2, "Svalbard");
    fclose(f);

    f = fopen("files_demo_passes.txt", "r");
    if (f == nullptr) return 1;
    char line[64];
    while (fgets(line, sizeof line, f) != nullptr)   // null at the end of the file
        printf("read: %s", line);                    // the line keeps its \n
    fclose(f);
    return 0;
}
```

It prints `read: pass 1 over Kiruna` and `read: pass 2 over Svalbard`. A line longer than the buffer comes back in pieces: the first piece has no `\n` at its end, which is how you can tell.

`getc(f)` reads one byte and returns it as an `int` from 0 to 255, or the special value `EOF` (which is −1) when there is nothing more to read. The result must be kept in an `int`, not a `char`, because `EOF` has to be different from all 256 possible bytes:

```cpp fragment
int c;                                // int, so it can hold EOF as well as every byte
long digits = 0;
while ((c = getc(f)) != EOF) {
    if (c >= '0' && c <= '9') digits++;
}
```

### The end of the file, done right

Every read function tells you when it ran out: `fgets` returns null, `getc` returns `EOF`, and `fread` (below) returns fewer items than you asked for. **Test that return value.** The function `feof(f)` answers a different question: after a read has already failed, was it because the end was reached (rather than an error, which `ferror(f)` reports)?

Why not loop `while (!feof(f))`? Because `feof` only becomes true **after** a read has tried to go past the end. After the last line is read successfully, `feof` is still false, so the loop runs once more; that read fails, and the body processes whatever was left in the buffer from before. The last item gets handled twice.

**Why the loop `while (fgets(line, size, f) != nullptr) { use(line); }` is exactly right.** Its invariant is: each pass uses a piece that was really just read, and every piece before it has been used once. `fgets` returns non-null exactly when it stored at least one new character, so the body never sees stale data. When the file is exhausted it returns null and the loop ends, having used every piece once, in order.

### Binary data: fread and fwrite

For binary data, `fwrite(ptr, size, count, f)` writes `count` items of `size` bytes each, taken from `ptr`, and returns how many **whole items** it wrote. `fread(ptr, size, count, f)` reads up to `count` items and returns how many whole items it read. Note that they count items, not bytes.

A return value smaller than `count` is a **short** read or write. For `fwrite` it means an error, such as a full disk. For `fread` it usually means the end of the file came first. If a file holds 10 bytes and you read items of 4 bytes, you get 2 items, and the last 2 bytes are not a whole item: that is often a sign of a **[[torn record|torn]]**, a file whose last write was cut off, for example by a crash.

### Records you can jump to

A file of **fixed-size records** is a simple, fast format: flight recorders, databases and many instrument files use it. If each record is R bytes, record k starts at byte k × R, so you can go straight to it with `fseek`:

- `fseek(f, offset, SEEK_SET)` moves to `offset` bytes from the start; `SEEK_CUR` counts from the current position and `SEEK_END` from the end. It returns 0 on success.
- `ftell(f)` returns the current position, as a `long`.
- So `fseek(f, 0, SEEK_END)` followed by `ftell(f)` gives the file's size in bytes.

A record's bytes must not depend on the machine, so, as the structs lesson warned, never `fwrite` a whole struct: its padding, its byte order and the sizes of its types can differ between the machine that writes and the one that reads. Encode each field into a byte buffer in a byte order you chose, and write the buffer. Here is a ground-station pass record of 6 bytes, big-endian: a 16-bit station number and a 32-bit start time.

```cpp
#include <cstdint>
#include <cstdio>

const int PASS_BYTES = 6;

void encode_pass(unsigned char* b, uint16_t station, uint32_t start) {
    b[0] = (unsigned char)(station >> 8);
    b[1] = (unsigned char)station;
    for (int i = 0; i < 4; i++) b[2 + i] = (unsigned char)(start >> (24 - 8 * i));
}

int main() {
    FILE* f = fopen("files_demo_passes.bin", "wb");
    if (f == nullptr) return 1;
    unsigned char b[PASS_BYTES];
    for (int k = 0; k < 3; k++) {
        encode_pass(b, (uint16_t)(700 + k), 1000u * (k + 1));
        if (fwrite(b, PASS_BYTES, 1, f) != 1) return 1;   // one item of 6 bytes
    }
    fclose(f);

    f = fopen("files_demo_passes.bin", "rb");
    if (f == nullptr) return 1;
    fseek(f, 0, SEEK_END);
    printf("%ld bytes\n", ftell(f));                     // 18 bytes
    fseek(f, 2 * PASS_BYTES, SEEK_SET);                  // straight to record 2
    if (fread(b, PASS_BYTES, 1, f) == 1)
        printf("station %d\n", (b[0] << 8) | b[1]);      // station 702
    fclose(f);
    return 0;
}
```

One rule for streams opened for both reading and writing, such as `"r+b"`: between a write and a following read, or a read and a following write, you must call `fseek` (or `fflush` after a write). The stream has a single buffer and position, and it has to be told you are changing direction.

### Buffering

Reading or writing a file really happens in the operating system, through a **[[system call|syscall]]**, which costs far more than an ordinary function call: roughly a microsecond, against a nanosecond. If every `fputc` made a system call, writing a million bytes would take a million of them.

So a stream keeps a **buffer**, a block of memory inside the `FILE`, [[typically 4 KiB|buffer-size]]. `fwrite` and `fprintf` copy your bytes into the buffer, and only when it is full does the stream hand the whole block to the operating system in one system call. Reading works the same way in reverse: one system call fills the buffer, and `getc` then takes bytes from memory. Streams use one of three modes, which `setvbuf` can change:

- **fully buffered:** written out when the buffer fills. Files use this.
- **line buffered:** also written out at every `\n`. A terminal uses this, so each line appears as soon as it is printed.
- **unbuffered:** written out at once. `stderr` uses this, so error messages are never stuck in a buffer.

`fflush(f)` writes the buffer out now, and `fclose` does it too. Anything still in the buffer when a program crashes is lost, which is why a log line printed just before a crash is often missing. And even after `fflush`, the data may only have reached the operating system's own memory, not the disk; guaranteeing that it survives a power cut needs a further call (`fsync`), which the Operating Systems course covers.

**Why buffering never changes what ends up in the file.** The invariant is: the bytes already handed to the operating system, followed by the bytes in the buffer, are exactly the bytes written so far, in order. Copying into the buffer keeps it true, and so does emptying the buffer into the operating system from the front. When the stream is flushed or closed the buffer is empty, so the operating system has received everything, in the order you wrote it. Only the number of system calls changes.

### What it costs

- **System calls:** writing n bytes through a buffer of B bytes makes about n / B system calls instead of up to n. With B = 4096, a million single-byte writes need 245.
- **Sequential reading and writing** costs O(n) for n bytes, and runs at the speed of the storage.
- **Seeking** within a file is O(1) for the library. On a solid-state drive, reading a record after a seek costs tens of microseconds; on a spinning hard disk the head has to move, which costs several milliseconds. That difference is why databases lay records out so that related ones sit together.

### Where it is used

- **Logs and telemetry recorders:** spacecraft store housekeeping data in fixed-size records in flash memory, and downlink them by record number when a ground pass allows.
- **Databases** keep their data in pages of a fixed size, 4 KiB in SQLite by default, and read page k at offset k times the page size.
- **File formats** such as WAV audio and BMP images begin with a header of fixed fields in a fixed byte order, followed by the data, and are read with exactly these functions.

**Watch out:** keeping `getc`'s result in a `char`. `char c = getc(f);` squeezes 257 possible results into 256 values. Where `char` is signed, the byte 0xFF becomes −1, equal to `EOF`, and the loop stops early in the middle of a binary file. Where `char` is unsigned, `EOF` becomes 255, never equal to −1, and the loop never ends. Use `int`.

::: context torn Torn writes
If power fails or the program crashes while a record is being written, the file may end with part of a record: some bytes new, some missing. That is a torn write. A reader that assumes every record is complete then misreads the end, or all records after it if they are not aligned. Robust formats detect it: the file length is checked against the record size, and each record carries a checksum. Crash consistency is a lesson of its own in the Operating Systems course.
:::

::: context syscall Asking the kernel
A system call is how a program asks the operating system kernel to do something only it may do: read a file, send a packet, start a process. The CPU switches from user mode to kernel mode, the kernel checks the request and does the work, and control comes back. The switch and the checks cost hundreds of nanoseconds or more, so programs batch their requests. On Linux the calls behind a stream are `open`, `read`, `write`, `lseek` and `close`; the Operating Systems course starts with them.
:::

::: context buffer-size How big a buffer
The C library chooses a stream's buffer size when the stream is first used, usually from the file system's preferred block size, often 4096 bytes. The constant `BUFSIZ` gives a default. Programs that stream large files can pass a bigger buffer with `setvbuf`, which must be called before the first read or write. Past 64 KiB or so, bigger buffers rarely help, because the cost of each system call is already spread over many bytes.
:::
--- task
A telemetry recorder stores samples in a binary file of fixed-size records. Write three functions. No `main`.

```cpp fragment
struct Sample {
    uint32_t time;
    int16_t value;
    uint8_t sensor;
};
```

On disk each sample is a record of exactly **7 bytes**, little-endian: bytes 0 to 3 are `time` (lowest byte first), bytes 4 and 5 are `value` (its two's complement bit pattern, lowest byte first), and byte 6 is `sensor`. Include `<cstdint>`, `<cstdio>` and the struct exactly as shown.

- `int save_samples(const char* path, const Sample* s, int n)`: create the file (replacing any old one) holding the `n` samples in order. Return 0, or -1 if the file cannot be opened or a write comes up short. With `n` equal to 0, the file is created empty.
- `int load_samples(const char* path, Sample* out, int cap)`: read the samples from the start of the file into `out`, at most `cap` of them, and return how many were read. Return -1 if the file cannot be opened, or if its size is not a whole number of records (a torn record).
- `int read_sample_at(const char* path, int k, Sample* out)`: read only record `k` (counting from 0), by seeking to it, into `*out`, and return 0. Return -1 if the file cannot be opened or record `k` is not wholly in the file, including for negative `k`.

Close every file you open, on every path.
--- starter
#include <cstdint>
#include <cstdio>

struct Sample {
    uint32_t time;
    int16_t value;
    uint8_t sensor;
};

int save_samples(const char* path, const Sample* s, int n) {
    FILE* f = fopen(path, "wb");
    if (f == nullptr) return -1;
    fwrite(s, sizeof(Sample), n, f);
    fclose(f);
    return 0;
}

int load_samples(const char* path, Sample* out, int cap) {
    return -1;
}

int read_sample_at(const char* path, int k, Sample* out) {
    return -1;
}
--- solution
#include <cstdint>
#include <cstdio>

struct Sample {
    uint32_t time;
    int16_t value;
    uint8_t sensor;
};

const int RECORD = 7;

static void encode(const Sample* s, unsigned char* b) {
    for (int i = 0; i < 4; i++) b[i] = (unsigned char)(s->time >> (8 * i));
    uint16_t v = (uint16_t)s->value;
    b[4] = (unsigned char)v;
    b[5] = (unsigned char)(v >> 8);
    b[6] = s->sensor;
}

static void decode(const unsigned char* b, Sample* s) {
    s->time = (uint32_t)b[0] | ((uint32_t)b[1] << 8) | ((uint32_t)b[2] << 16) | ((uint32_t)b[3] << 24);
    s->value = (int16_t)(uint16_t)(b[4] | (b[5] << 8));
    s->sensor = b[6];
}

// The file's size in bytes, leaving the position at the start; -1 on failure.
static long file_size(FILE* f) {
    if (fseek(f, 0, SEEK_END) != 0) return -1;
    long size = ftell(f);
    if (fseek(f, 0, SEEK_SET) != 0) return -1;
    return size;
}

int save_samples(const char* path, const Sample* s, int n) {
    FILE* f = fopen(path, "wb");
    if (f == nullptr) return -1;
    unsigned char b[RECORD];
    for (int i = 0; i < n; i++) {
        encode(&s[i], b);
        if (fwrite(b, RECORD, 1, f) != 1) {
            fclose(f);
            return -1;
        }
    }
    if (fclose(f) != 0) return -1;
    return 0;
}

int load_samples(const char* path, Sample* out, int cap) {
    FILE* f = fopen(path, "rb");
    if (f == nullptr) return -1;
    long size = file_size(f);
    if (size < 0 || size % RECORD != 0) {
        fclose(f);
        return -1;
    }
    int count = 0;
    unsigned char b[RECORD];
    while (count < cap && fread(b, RECORD, 1, f) == 1) {
        decode(b, &out[count]);
        count++;
    }
    fclose(f);
    return count;
}

int read_sample_at(const char* path, int k, Sample* out) {
    if (k < 0) return -1;
    FILE* f = fopen(path, "rb");
    if (f == nullptr) return -1;
    unsigned char b[RECORD];
    bool ok = fseek(f, (long)k * RECORD, SEEK_SET) == 0 && fread(b, RECORD, 1, f) == 1;
    fclose(f);
    if (!ok) return -1;
    decode(b, out);
    return 0;
}
--- hint
Write two helpers first: `encode` turns a `Sample` into 7 bytes with shifts, and `decode` turns 7 bytes back into a `Sample`, exactly as in the bit manipulation lesson. Never `fwrite` the struct itself: it is 8 bytes in memory, with padding.
--- hint
`save_samples` writes one 7-byte item per sample and checks that `fwrite` returns 1 each time. `load_samples` first finds the file's size with `fseek` to the end and `ftell`, rejects a size that is not a multiple of 7, seeks back to the start, and reads records while `fread` returns 1 and there is room.
--- hint
`read_sample_at` seeks to `k * 7` and reads one record; if `fread` does not return 1, the record is not wholly there. Close the file before every `return` after a successful `fopen`.
--- check test | save_samples writes 7-byte little-endian records
[] { Sample s[2] = {{0x01020304u, -2, 9}, {1000u, 300, 255}}; if (save_samples("sys12_task_a.bin", s, 2) != 0) return false; FILE* f = fopen("sys12_task_a.bin", "rb"); unsigned char b[32]; int n = (int)fread(b, 1, 32, f); fclose(f); unsigned char w[14] = {0x04, 0x03, 0x02, 0x01, 0xFE, 0xFF, 9, 0xE8, 0x03, 0, 0, 0x2C, 0x01, 255}; if (n != 14) return false; for (int i = 0; i < 14; i++) if (b[i] != w[i]) return false; return true; }()
--- check test | Save then load gives the samples back

+++ practice | Lines in a log
--- task
Write `int count_lines(const char* path, int* longest)`. No `main`.

It opens the text file `path`, and returns the number of lines in it, storing in `*longest` the length of the longest line, not counting its `\n`. A line is a run of characters ending in `\n`, and the last line counts even if it does not end in `\n`. An empty file has 0 lines and a longest line of 0. If the file cannot be opened, return -1 and leave `*longest` unchanged.

Lines can be of any length, far longer than any buffer you might choose, so read the file one character at a time with `getc`. For example, a file holding `"GO\nNO GO\nHOLD"` has 3 lines, and the longest is 5.
--- starter
#include <cstdio>

int count_lines(const char* path, int* longest) {
    FILE* f = fopen(path, "r");
    if (f == nullptr) return -1;
    char buf[16];
    int lines = 0;
    while (fgets(buf, sizeof buf, f) != nullptr) lines++;
    fclose(f);
    *longest = 0;
    return lines;
}
--- solution
#include <cstdio>

int count_lines(const char* path, int* longest) {
    FILE* f = fopen(path, "r");
    if (f == nullptr) return -1;
    int lines = 0;
    int best = 0;
    int current = 0;   // length of the line being read
    int c;
    while ((c = getc(f)) != EOF) {
        if (c == '\n') {
            lines++;
            if (current > best) best = current;
            current = 0;
        } else {
            current++;
        }
    }
    if (current > 0) {   // a last line with no \n
        lines++;
        if (current > best) best = current;
    }
    fclose(f);
    *longest = best;
    return lines;
}
--- hint
Keep the length of the current line. Each `\n` ends a line: count it, compare its length with the longest so far, and start again at 0. Keep `getc`'s result in an `int`.
--- hint
When the loop ends, characters counted since the last `\n` form a last line without a newline: count it too. A file that ends right after a `\n` has no such extra line.
--- check test | The example: 3 lines, the longest 5
[] { FILE* f = fopen("sys12_p1_a.txt", "w"); fputs("GO\nNO GO\nHOLD", f); fclose(f); int L = -1; return count_lines("sys12_p1_a.txt", &L) == 3 && L == 5; }()
--- check test | A file ending in a newline has no extra line
[] { FILE* f = fopen("sys12_p1_b.txt", "w"); fputs("alpha\nbeta\n", f); fclose(f); int L = -1; return count_lines("sys12_p1_b.txt", &L) == 2 && L == 5; }()
--- check test | An empty file, and a file of empty lines
[] { FILE* f = fopen("sys12_p1_c.txt", "w"); fclose(f); FILE* g = fopen("sys12_p1_d.txt", "w"); fputs("\n\n\n", g); fclose(g); int L1 = -1, L2 = -1; return count_lines("sys12_p1_c.txt", &L1) == 0 && L1 == 0 && count_lines("sys12_p1_d.txt", &L2) == 3 && L2 == 0; }()
--- check test | A line of 100000 characters
[] { FILE* f = fopen("sys12_p1_e.txt", "w"); fputs("short\n", f); for (int i = 0; i < 100000; i++) fputc('x', f); fputs("\nend", f); fclose(f); int L = -1; return count_lines("sys12_p1_e.txt", &L) == 3 && L == 100000; }()
--- check test | A missing file gives -1 and keeps *longest
[] { int L = 42; return count_lines("sys12_p1_missing.txt", &L) == -1 && L == 42; }()

+++ practice | Copying a file in blocks
--- task
Write `long long copy_file(const char* from, const char* to)`, which copies the file `from` to a new file `to` (replacing any old one), byte for byte, and returns the number of bytes copied. No `main`.

Copy in blocks with `fread` and `fwrite`, using a buffer of 4096 bytes, and open both files in binary mode, because the file may hold any bytes at all, zeros included. Return -1 if either file cannot be opened or a write comes up short. Close every file you opened, on every path.
--- starter
#include <cstdio>

long long copy_file(const char* from, const char* to) {
    FILE* in = fopen(from, "r");
    FILE* out = fopen(to, "w");
    char line[256];
    long long total = 0;
    while (fgets(line, sizeof line, in) != nullptr) {
        fputs(line, out);
        for (int i = 0; line[i] != '\0'; i++) total++;
    }
    fclose(in);
    fclose(out);
    return total;
}
--- solution
#include <cstdio>

long long copy_file(const char* from, const char* to) {
    FILE* in = fopen(from, "rb");
    if (in == nullptr) return -1;
    FILE* out = fopen(to, "wb");
    if (out == nullptr) {
        fclose(in);
        return -1;
    }
    unsigned char buf[4096];
    long long total = 0;
    size_t got;
    while ((got = fread(buf, 1, sizeof buf, in)) > 0) {
        if (fwrite(buf, 1, got, out) != got) {
            fclose(in);
            fclose(out);
            return -1;
        }
        total += (long long)got;
    }
    fclose(in);
    if (fclose(out) != 0) return -1;
    return total;
}
--- hint
Read with items of 1 byte: `fread(buf, 1, sizeof buf, in)` returns how many bytes it read, which is less than 4096 for the last block and 0 at the end. Write exactly that many.
--- hint
Text functions such as `fgets` and `fputs` stop at a zero byte, so they cannot copy binary data. If the second `fopen` fails, close the first file before returning -1.
--- check test | Copies a small text file
[] { FILE* f = fopen("sys12_p2_a.txt", "wb"); fputs("telemetry\nok\n", f); fclose(f); long long n = copy_file("sys12_p2_a.txt", "sys12_p2_a_copy.txt"); FILE* g = fopen("sys12_p2_a_copy.txt", "rb"); char b[32] = {0}; size_t r = fread(b, 1, 31, g); fclose(g); return n == 13 && r == 13 && std::string(b) == "telemetry\nok\n"; }()
--- check test | Copies 100000 bytes holding every byte value, zeros included
[] { static unsigned char data[100000], back[100001]; for (int i = 0; i < 100000; i++) data[i] = (unsigned char)(i * 7 + i / 256); FILE* f = fopen("sys12_p2_b.bin", "wb"); fwrite(data, 1, 100000, f); fclose(f); long long n = copy_file("sys12_p2_b.bin", "sys12_p2_b_copy.bin"); FILE* g = fopen("sys12_p2_b_copy.bin", "rb"); size_t r = fread(back, 1, 100001, g); fclose(g); if (n != 100000 || r != 100000) return false; for (int i = 0; i < 100000; i++) if (back[i] != data[i]) return false; return true; }()
--- check test | An empty file copies 0 bytes and creates the copy
[] { FILE* f = fopen("sys12_p2_c.bin", "wb"); fclose(f); long long n = copy_file("sys12_p2_c.bin", "sys12_p2_c_copy.bin"); FILE* g = fopen("sys12_p2_c_copy.bin", "rb"); bool exists = g != nullptr; if (g) fclose(g); return n == 0 && exists; }()
--- check test | Exactly 8192 bytes: a whole number of blocks

+++ practice | An append-only log with a header
--- task
An event log is a binary file with a header, then records:

- bytes 0 to 3: the four characters `O`, `B`, `L`, `G`, a **magic number** that marks the file as a log;
- bytes 4 to 7: the number of records, as a 32-bit unsigned value, little-endian;
- then one record per event: a 32-bit signed value, little-endian (4 bytes, the two's complement bit pattern).

Write three functions. No `main`.

- `int log_append(const char* path, int32_t value)`: if the file does not exist, first create it with a header and a count of 0. Then add the record at the end of the file and increase the count in the header by one. Return 0, or -1 if the file exists but does not start with the magic number, or if any read, write or seek fails.
- `int log_count(const char* path)`: the count from the header, or -1 if the file cannot be opened or is not a log.
- `int log_get(const char* path, int i, int32_t* out)`: record `i` (counting from 0) into `*out`, returning 0; or -1 if the file cannot be opened, is not a log, or `i` is not below the count.

Open an existing log with `"r+b"` to change it, and remember the rule from the lesson: seek between a read and a write on the same stream. Close every file you open.
--- starter
#include <cstdint>
#include <cstdio>

int log_append(const char* path, int32_t value) {
    FILE* f = fopen(path, "ab");
    if (f == nullptr) return -1;
    fwrite(&value, 4, 1, f);
    fclose(f);
    return 0;
}

int log_count(const char* path) {
    return -1;
}

int log_get(const char* path, int i, int32_t* out) {
    return -1;
}
--- solution
#include <cstdint>
#include <cstdio>
#include <cstring>

static void put32(unsigned char* b, uint32_t v) {
    for (int i = 0; i < 4; i++) b[i] = (unsigned char)(v >> (8 * i));
}

static uint32_t get32(const unsigned char* b) {
    return (uint32_t)b[0] | ((uint32_t)b[1] << 8) | ((uint32_t)b[2] << 16) | ((uint32_t)b[3] << 24);
}

// Reads the header of an open log; returns false if it is missing or wrong.
static bool read_header(FILE* f, uint32_t* count) {
    unsigned char h[8];
    if (fseek(f, 0, SEEK_SET) != 0 || fread(h, 8, 1, f) != 1) return false;
    if (memcmp(h, "OBLG", 4) != 0) return false;
    *count = get32(h + 4);
    return true;
}

int log_append(const char* path, int32_t value) {
    FILE* f = fopen(path, "r+b");
    if (f == nullptr) {
        f = fopen(path, "w+b");   // no log yet: create one with an empty header
        if (f == nullptr) return -1;
        unsigned char h[8] = {'O', 'B', 'L', 'G', 0, 0, 0, 0};
        if (fwrite(h, 8, 1, f) != 1) {
            fclose(f);
            return -1;
        }
    }
    uint32_t count = 0;
    unsigned char b[4];
    put32(b, (uint32_t)value);
    bool ok = read_header(f, &count) && fseek(f, 8 + 4L * count, SEEK_SET) == 0 && fwrite(b, 4, 1, f) == 1;
    if (ok) {
        put32(b, count + 1);
        ok = fseek(f, 4, SEEK_SET) == 0 && fwrite(b, 4, 1, f) == 1;
    }
    if (fclose(f) != 0) ok = false;
    return ok ? 0 : -1;
}

int log_count(const char* path) {
    FILE* f = fopen(path, "rb");
    if (f == nullptr) return -1;
    uint32_t count = 0;
    bool ok = read_header(f, &count);
    fclose(f);
    return ok ? (int)count : -1;
}

int log_get(const char* path, int i, int32_t* out) {
    FILE* f = fopen(path, "rb");
    if (f == nullptr) return -1;
    uint32_t count = 0;
    unsigned char b[4];
    bool ok = read_header(f, &count) && i >= 0 && (uint32_t)i < count && fseek(f, 8 + 4L * i, SEEK_SET) == 0 && fread(b, 4, 1, f) == 1;
    fclose(f);
    if (!ok) return -1;
    *out = (int32_t)get32(b);
    return 0;
}
--- hint
Write little helpers to put and get a 32-bit little-endian value in 4 bytes, and one that seeks to 0, reads the 8-byte header and checks the magic with `memcmp`. All three functions use it.
--- hint
In `log_append`, try `fopen(path, "r+b")` first. If that fails, the file does not exist: open it with `"w+b"` and write a header with count 0. Then read the header, seek to 8 + 4 × count, write the record, seek to 4, and write the new count.
--- hint
Record `i` is at byte 8 + 4 × i. Using the count in the header, rather than the file's size, as the place to append means a record torn by a crash is simply overwritten by the next append.
--- check test | Three appends, then count and read them back
[] { log_append("sys12_p3_a.log", 5); log_append("sys12_p3_a.log", -7); log_append("sys12_p3_a.log", 2147483647); int32_t a = 0, b = 0, c = 0; bool ok = log_get("sys12_p3_a.log", 0, &a) == 0 && log_get("sys12_p3_a.log", 1, &b) == 0 && log_get("sys12_p3_a.log", 2, &c) == 0; return log_count("sys12_p3_a.log") == 3 && ok && a == 5 && b == -7 && c == 2147483647; }()
--- check test | The bytes on disk follow the format exactly
[] { log_append("sys12_p3_b.log", -2); log_append("sys12_p3_b.log", 258); FILE* f = fopen("sys12_p3_b.log", "rb"); unsigned char h[32]; size_t n = fread(h, 1, 32, f); fclose(f); unsigned char w[16] = {'O', 'B', 'L', 'G', 2, 0, 0, 0, 0xFE, 0xFF, 0xFF, 0xFF, 2, 1, 0, 0}; if (n != 16) return false; for (int i = 0; i < 16; i++) if (h[i] != w[i]) return false; return true; }()
--- check test | Out-of-range indexes and missing files give -1
[] { log_append("sys12_p3_c.log", 1); int32_t v = 99; return log_get("sys12_p3_c.log", 1, &v) == -1 && log_get("sys12_p3_c.log", -1, &v) == -1 && v == 99 && log_count("sys12_p3_missing.log") == -1 && log_get("sys12_p3_missing.log", 0, &v) == -1; }()
--- check test | A file that is not a log is refused and left alone
[] { FILE* f = fopen("sys12_p3_d.log", "wb"); fputs("HELLO WORLD", f); fclose(f); int r = log_append("sys12_p3_d.log", 3); FILE* g = fopen("sys12_p3_d.log", "rb"); char b[32] = {0}; size_t n = fread(b, 1, 31, g); fclose(g); return r == -1 && log_count("sys12_p3_d.log") == -1 && n == 11 && std::string(b) == "HELLO WORLD"; }()
--- check test | A thousand appends
[] { for (int i = 0; i < 1000; i++) if (log_append("sys12_p3_e.log", i * i - 500) != 0) return false; int32_t v = 0; bool ok = log_get("sys12_p3_e.log", 999, &v) == 0 && v == 999 * 999 - 500; bool ok2 = log_get("sys12_p3_e.log", 0, &v) == 0 && v == -500; return ok && ok2 && log_count("sys12_p3_e.log") == 1000; }()

+++ practice | A settings file
--- task
A ground-station program reads its settings from a text file of lines like `rate = 20`. Write `int read_setting(const char* path, const char* key, char* value, int cap)`. No `main`.

The rules:

- Each line is at most 255 characters. A line that is empty, holds only spaces, or whose first character that is not a space is `#`, is ignored.
- Every other line has the form *key* `=` *value*. Spaces and tabs around the key and around the value are not part of them, but spaces inside the value are (`name = Deep Space 1` has the value `Deep Space 1`). The value may be empty.
- The key must match exactly: `rate` does not match `rate_max`. If the key appears on several lines, the first one wins.
- The last line may end without `\n`, and lines may end with `\r\n`; the `\r` is not part of the value.

Copy the value into `value`, with a terminating zero, and return its length. Return -1 if the file cannot be opened or the key is not found, and -2 if the value and its zero do not fit in `cap` bytes (then `value` may hold anything).
--- starter
#include <cstdio>
#include <cstring>

int read_setting(const char* path, const char* key, char* value, int cap) {
    FILE* f = fopen(path, "r");
    if (f == nullptr) return -1;
    char line[256];
    while (fgets(line, sizeof line, f) != nullptr) {
        if (strncmp(line, key, strlen(key)) == 0) {
            char* eq = strchr(line, '=');
            strcpy(value, eq + 1);
            fclose(f);
            return (int)strlen(value);
        }
    }
    fclose(f);
    return -1;
}
--- solution
#include <cstdio>
#include <cstring>

static bool is_space(char c) {
    return c == ' ' || c == '\t' || c == '\r' || c == '\n';
}

// Trims spaces from both ends of s[0..len), returning the new start and setting *len.
static const char* trim(const char* s, int* len) {
    int a = 0, b = *len;
    while (a < b && is_space(s[a])) a++;
    while (b > a && is_space(s[b - 1])) b--;
    *len = b - a;
    return s + a;
}

int read_setting(const char* path, const char* key, char* value, int cap) {
    FILE* f = fopen(path, "r");
    if (f == nullptr) return -1;
    char line[258];   // 255 characters, \r\n, and the zero
    int result = -1;
    while (fgets(line, sizeof line, f) != nullptr) {
        int len = (int)strlen(line);
        const char* s = trim(line, &len);
        if (len == 0 || s[0] == '#') continue;
        const char* eq = (const char*)memchr(s, '=', len);
        if (eq == nullptr) continue;
        int klen = (int)(eq - s);
        const char* k = trim(s, &klen);
        if (klen != (int)strlen(key) || strncmp(k, key, klen) != 0) continue;
        int vlen = (int)(s + len - (eq + 1));
        const char* v = trim(eq + 1, &vlen);
        if (vlen + 1 > cap) {
            result = -2;
        } else {
            memcpy(value, v, vlen);
            value[vlen] = '\0';
            result = vlen;
        }
        break;
    }
    fclose(f);
    return result;
}
--- hint
Read line by line with `fgets` into a buffer big enough for 255 characters, a `\r\n` and the zero. Write one helper that trims spaces, tabs, `\r` and `\n` from both ends of a piece of text given by a pointer and a length.
--- hint
For each line: trim it, skip it if it is empty or starts with `#`, find the `=`, trim the part before it and compare it with the key, checking the lengths are equal as well as the letters.
--- hint
On the first matching key, trim the part after the `=`, check that its length plus 1 fits in `cap`, copy it with `memcpy` and add the zero. Stop reading at the first match, and close the file on every path.
--- check test | Reads keys, ignoring comments and blank lines
[] { FILE* f = fopen("sys12_p4_a.cfg", "w"); fputs("# ground station\n\nrate = 20\nname = Deep Space 1\n   # indented comment\nmode=safe\n", f); fclose(f); char v[64]; bool a = read_setting("sys12_p4_a.cfg", "rate", v, 64) == 2 && std::string(v) == "20"; bool b = read_setting("sys12_p4_a.cfg", "name", v, 64) == 12 && std::string(v) == "Deep Space 1"; bool c = read_setting("sys12_p4_a.cfg", "mode", v, 64) == 4 && std::string(v) == "safe"; return a && b && c; }()
--- check test | A key must match exactly, and the first one wins
[] { FILE* f = fopen("sys12_p4_b.cfg", "w"); fputs("rate_max = 50\nrate = 20\nrate = 99\n", f); fclose(f); char v[64]; bool a = read_setting("sys12_p4_b.cfg", "rate", v, 64) == 2 && std::string(v) == "20"; bool b = read_setting("sys12_p4_b.cfg", "rat", v, 64) == -1; bool c = read_setting("sys12_p4_b.cfg", "rate_max", v, 64) == 2 && std::string(v) == "50"; return a && b && c; }()
--- check test | Tabs, carriage returns, and a last line with no newline
[] { FILE* f = fopen("sys12_p4_c.cfg", "w"); fputs("\tgain\t=\t1.5 \r\nband = S\r\nlast=done", f); fclose(f); char v[64]; bool a = read_setting("sys12_p4_c.cfg", "gain", v, 64) == 3 && std::string(v) == "1.5"; bool b = read_setting("sys12_p4_c.cfg", "band", v, 64) == 1 && std::string(v) == "S"; bool c = read_setting("sys12_p4_c.cfg", "last", v, 64) == 4 && std::string(v) == "done"; return a && b && c; }()
--- check test | An empty value, and a value that does not fit
[] { FILE* f = fopen("sys12_p4_d.cfg", "w"); fputs("note =\nstation = Kiruna\n", f); fclose(f); char v[8]; bool a = read_setting("sys12_p4_d.cfg", "note", v, 8) == 0 && v[0] == '\0'; bool b = read_setting("sys12_p4_d.cfg", "station", v, 6) == -2; bool c = read_setting("sys12_p4_d.cfg", "station", v, 7) == 6 && std::string(v) == "Kiruna"; return a && b && c; }()
--- check test | Missing keys, missing files and a commented-out key
[] { FILE* f = fopen("sys12_p4_e.cfg", "w"); fputs("# rate = 5\nalpha = 1\n", f); fclose(f); char v[16]; return read_setting("sys12_p4_e.cfg", "rate", v, 16) == -1 && read_setting("sys12_p4_e.cfg", "beta", v, 16) == -1 && read_setting("sys12_p4_missing.cfg", "alpha", v, 16) == -1; }()
--- check test | A line of 255 characters
[] { FILE* f = fopen("sys12_p4_f.cfg", "w"); fputs("k = ", f); for (int i = 0; i < 251; i++) fputc('a' + i % 26, f); fputs("\nnext = 1\n", f); fclose(f); static char v[300]; bool a = read_setting("sys12_p4_f.cfg", "k", v, 300) == 251 && v[250] == 'a' + 250 % 26; bool b = read_setting("sys12_p4_f.cfg", "next", v, 300) == 1; return a && b; }()

+++ practice | Debug: the last record counted twice
--- task
This function adds up a file of 32-bit integers, written in this machine's own byte order, and returns the total. With the file holding 1, 2 and 3 it should return 6, but it returns 9. An empty file should give 0, and a missing file -1. Find the bug and fix it.

`long long sum_file(const char* path)`. No `main`.
--- starter
#include <cstdint>
#include <cstdio>

long long sum_file(const char* path) {
    FILE* f = fopen(path, "rb");
    if (f == nullptr) return -1;
    long long total = 0;
    int32_t x = 0;
    while (!feof(f)) {
        fread(&x, sizeof x, 1, f);
        total += x;
    }
    fclose(f);
    return total;
}
--- solution
#include <cstdint>
#include <cstdio>

long long sum_file(const char* path) {
    FILE* f = fopen(path, "rb");
    if (f == nullptr) return -1;
    long long total = 0;
    int32_t x = 0;
    while (fread(&x, sizeof x, 1, f) == 1) total += x;
    fclose(f);
    return total;
}
--- hint
When does `feof` become true: before the read that finds the end, or after it? Follow the loop by hand for a file of one number.
--- hint
After the last number is read, `feof` is still false, so the loop runs again; that `fread` reads nothing and leaves the old value in `x`, which is added a second time. Test what `fread` returns instead.
--- check test | 1, 2 and 3 add up to 6
[] { int32_t v[3] = {1, 2, 3}; FILE* f = fopen("sys12_p5_a.bin", "wb"); fwrite(v, sizeof v[0], 3, f); fclose(f); return sum_file("sys12_p5_a.bin") == 6; }()
--- check test | One number
[] { int32_t v = -40; FILE* f = fopen("sys12_p5_b.bin", "wb"); fwrite(&v, sizeof v, 1, f); fclose(f); return sum_file("sys12_p5_b.bin") == -40; }()
--- check test | An empty file gives 0, a missing one -1
[] { FILE* f = fopen("sys12_p5_c.bin", "wb"); fclose(f); return sum_file("sys12_p5_c.bin") == 0 && sum_file("sys12_p5_missing.bin") == -1; }()
--- check test | 10000 large numbers, beyond the range of int
[] { static int32_t v[10000]; for (int i = 0; i < 10000; i++) v[i] = 2000000000 - i; FILE* f = fopen("sys12_p5_d.bin", "wb"); fwrite(v, sizeof v[0], 10000, f); fclose(f); return sum_file("sys12_p5_d.bin") == 20000000000000LL - 49995000LL; }()

+++ practice | A buffered writer
--- task
Build the buffering of a stream yourself, over a simulated operating system that counts its system calls. The starter provides the "operating system": a `Sink`, and `sink_write`, which stands for one system call. Leave that code as it is.

Write three functions. No `main`.

- `void writer_init(Writer* w, Sink* sink, unsigned char* buf, int cap)`: a writer that sends its output to `sink`, using the `cap` bytes at `buf` as its buffer. It starts empty.
- `void writer_put(Writer* w, const unsigned char* p, int n)`: write `n` bytes. If they fit in the space left in the buffer, copy them in. Otherwise, first flush the buffer if it holds anything (one `sink_write` of its contents); then, if `n` is at least `cap`, send the bytes straight to the sink in one `sink_write`, and if not, copy them into the now empty buffer. With `n` equal to 0 nothing happens.
- `void writer_flush(Writer* w)`: if the buffer holds anything, send it in one `sink_write` and empty it. Otherwise do nothing: never call `sink_write` with 0 bytes.

The sink must always receive the bytes in exactly the order they were written. For example, with `cap` 8: putting 3 bytes, then 3, then 3, makes one system call (of the first 6 bytes, when the third put does not fit); a flush then makes a second (of the last 3).
--- starter
#include <cstring>

// ---- The simulated operating system. Each sink_write is one system call. Do not change it.
struct Sink {
    unsigned char data[4096];   // the first 4096 bytes received, for the checks
    long long size;             // bytes received in total
    int calls;                  // system calls made
    unsigned hash;              // a hash of every byte received, in order
};

void sink_init(Sink* s) {
    s->size = 0;
    s->calls = 0;
    s->hash = 2166136261u;
}

void sink_write(Sink* s, const unsigned char* p, int n) {
    s->calls++;
    for (int i = 0; i < n; i++) {
        if (s->size < 4096) s->data[s->size] = p[i];
        s->size++;
        s->hash = (s->hash ^ p[i]) * 16777619u;
    }
}
// ---- End of the operating system.

struct Writer {
    Sink* sink;
    unsigned char* buf;
    int cap;
    int used;
};

void writer_init(Writer* w, Sink* sink, unsigned char* buf, int cap) {
    w->sink = sink;
    w->buf = buf;
    w->cap = cap;
    w->used = 0;
}

void writer_put(Writer* w, const unsigned char* p, int n) {
    sink_write(w->sink, p, n);
}

void writer_flush(Writer* w) {
}
--- solution
#include <cstring>

// ---- The simulated operating system. Each sink_write is one system call. Do not change it.
struct Sink {
    unsigned char data[4096];   // the first 4096 bytes received, for the checks
    long long size;             // bytes received in total
    int calls;                  // system calls made
    unsigned hash;              // a hash of every byte received, in order
};

void sink_init(Sink* s) {
    s->size = 0;
    s->calls = 0;
    s->hash = 2166136261u;
}

void sink_write(Sink* s, const unsigned char* p, int n) {
    s->calls++;
    for (int i = 0; i < n; i++) {
        if (s->size < 4096) s->data[s->size] = p[i];
        s->size++;
        s->hash = (s->hash ^ p[i]) * 16777619u;
    }
}
// ---- End of the operating system.

struct Writer {
    Sink* sink;
    unsigned char* buf;
    int cap;
    int used;
};

void writer_init(Writer* w, Sink* sink, unsigned char* buf, int cap) {
    w->sink = sink;
    w->buf = buf;
    w->cap = cap;
    w->used = 0;
}

void writer_flush(Writer* w) {
    if (w->used == 0) return;
    sink_write(w->sink, w->buf, w->used);
    w->used = 0;
}

void writer_put(Writer* w, const unsigned char* p, int n) {
    if (n <= 0) return;
    if (n <= w->cap - w->used) {
        memcpy(w->buf + w->used, p, n);
        w->used += n;
        return;
    }
    writer_flush(w);               // the buffered bytes go first, to keep the order
    if (n >= w->cap) {
        sink_write(w->sink, p, n); // too big to buffer: send it directly
    } else {
        memcpy(w->buf, p, n);
        w->used = n;
    }
}
--- hint
Keep `used`, the number of bytes in the buffer. A put that fits, `n <= cap - used`, is one `memcpy` to `buf + used`.
--- hint
When it does not fit, flush first, so the bytes already buffered reach the sink before the new ones. After that the buffer is empty: a big write (at least `cap` bytes) goes straight to the sink, and a small one is copied into the buffer.
--- hint
`writer_flush` must check `used` first, so an empty buffer never costs a system call.
--- check test | The example: 3, 3, 3 with cap 8 makes one call, and the flush a second
[] { static Sink s; sink_init(&s); unsigned char buf[8]; Writer w; writer_init(&w, &s, buf, 8); unsigned char a[3] = {1, 2, 3}, b[3] = {4, 5, 6}, c[3] = {7, 8, 9}; writer_put(&w, a, 3); writer_put(&w, b, 3); int before = s.calls; writer_put(&w, c, 3); int middle = s.calls; writer_flush(&w); if (before != 0 || middle != 1 || s.calls != 2 || s.size != 9) return false; for (int i = 0; i < 9; i++) if (s.data[i] != i + 1) return false; return true; }()
--- check test | A big write goes straight through, after the buffered bytes
[] { static Sink s; sink_init(&s); unsigned char buf[8]; Writer w; writer_init(&w, &s, buf, 8); unsigned char a[2] = {'a', 'b'}; unsigned char big[20]; for (int i = 0; i < 20; i++) big[i] = (unsigned char)('A' + i); writer_put(&w, a, 2); writer_put(&w, big, 20); int calls = s.calls; writer_flush(&w); return calls == 2 && s.calls == 2 && s.size == 22 && s.data[0] == 'a' && s.data[1] == 'b' && s.data[2] == 'A' && s.data[21] == 'T'; }()
--- check test | Filling the buffer exactly does not flush it yet
[] { static Sink s; sink_init(&s); unsigned char buf[4]; Writer w; writer_init(&w, &s, buf, 4); unsigned char a[4] = {9, 9, 9, 9}; writer_put(&w, a, 4); int calls = s.calls; writer_put(&w, a, 1); return calls == 0 && s.calls == 1 && s.size == 4; }()
--- check test | Flushing an empty writer makes no call, and writing 0 bytes does nothing
[] { static Sink s; sink_init(&s); unsigned char buf[16]; Writer w; writer_init(&w, &s, buf, 16); writer_flush(&w); unsigned char a[1] = {5}; writer_put(&w, a, 0); writer_flush(&w); return s.calls == 0 && s.size == 0; }()
--- check test | A million single bytes through 4096 bytes of buffer: 245 calls
[] { static Sink s, t; sink_init(&s); sink_init(&t); static unsigned char buf[4096]; Writer w; writer_init(&w, &s, buf, 4096); for (int i = 0; i < 1000000; i++) { unsigned char b = (unsigned char)(i * 31); writer_put(&w, &b, 1); unsigned char c = b; sink_write(&t, &c, 1); } writer_flush(&w); return s.calls == 245 && s.size == 1000000 && s.hash == t.hash; }()
--- check test | Random sizes keep every byte in order
[] { static Sink s, t; sink_init(&s); sink_init(&t); static unsigned char buf[100]; static unsigned char src[500]; Writer w; writer_init(&w, &s, buf, 100); unsigned x = 5u; for (int k = 0; k < 2000; k++) { x = x * 1664525u + 1013904223u; int n = (int)((x >> 8) % 250); for (int i = 0; i < n; i++) src[i] = (unsigned char)(x >> (i % 24)); writer_put(&w, src, n); if (n > 0) sink_write(&t, src, n); } writer_flush(&w); return s.size == t.size && s.hash == t.hash && s.calls < t.calls; }()

+++ question | The missing log line
--- ask
A program writes `fprintf(log, "starting burn\n");` to a log file opened with `fopen`, and a moment later crashes. Afterwards, the line is not in the file. What is the most likely reason?
--- choice
`fprintf` only writes to the screen unless the file was opened in binary mode.
--- choice correct
The line was still in the stream's buffer in the program's memory, waiting for the buffer to fill or a flush, and the crash lost it.
--- choice
The operating system deletes files written by programs that crash.
--- choice
The `\n` was translated into `\r\n`, which hid the line.
--- why
Files are fully buffered: `fprintf` copies into a buffer, and the operating system only receives the data when the buffer fills, on `fflush`, or on `fclose`. A program that must not lose a line calls `fflush` after writing it, or writes errors to the unbuffered `stderr`.

+++ question | How many system calls
--- ask
A program writes 10,000 bytes, one byte at a time, through a stream with a 4096-byte buffer, and then closes it. The buffer is only written out when it is full, and at the close. How many system calls write data?
--- answer
3
three
--- why
The buffer fills after 4096 bytes and again after 8192, which makes two calls; the remaining 1808 bytes go out when the stream is closed. So 3 system calls instead of 10,000.
=== sys-13 | Error handling without exceptions: return codes, errno and cleanup paths
--- teach
Last lesson, almost every function you wrote returned -1 when something went wrong, and had to close its files on every path. That was error handling without exceptions, done by hand. C has no exceptions at all, and ORBIT's C++ is compiled with them switched off, as is much of the C++ in games, embedded systems and flight software. This lesson turns the habit into a discipline: how a function reports failure, how the C library reports it through `errno`, how to release everything on every failure path, and how to test the paths that almost never run.

### Errors are values

Without exceptions, a failure has to travel back through ordinary return values. Three conventions cover nearly all C code:

- **A status code as the return value**, with results written through out-parameters: `int load_config(const char* path, Config* out)` returns 0 on success and a nonzero code saying what went wrong. The Linux kernel returns 0 or a negative error number, such as `-ENOMEM`.
- **A special value of the result**, called a **sentinel**: `malloc` and `fopen` return a null pointer, many POSIX functions return -1, `getc` returns `EOF`.
- **The error number itself**: some interfaces, such as POSIX threads, return 0 or a positive error number directly.

Give your codes names, so both the reader and the compiler know what they mean:

```cpp fragment
enum Status {
    OK = 0,
    ERR_ARG = -1,     // the caller passed something invalid
    ERR_NOMEM = -2,   // an allocation failed
    ERR_IO = -3,      // a file could not be opened, read or written
};
```

Whatever the convention, two rules make it work. **Every call that can fail is checked**, immediately, by its caller. And **on failure, outputs are left untouched**: a function builds its results in local variables and writes them to the out-parameters only once nothing can fail any more. A caller who sees an error code then knows its data is exactly as it was.

### errno: why the library failed

A library function such as `fopen` can only say "null" when it fails, but callers want to know why: no such file, no permission, out of memory. The C library records the reason in **`errno`**, from `<cerrno>`: an `int` that library functions set to a code when they fail. The codes are macros: `ENOENT` (no such file or directory), `EACCES` (permission denied), `ENOMEM` (out of memory), `ERANGE` (a result out of range), `EINVAL` (an invalid argument), and many more. `strerror(code)` gives a text for a code.

```cpp
#include <cerrno>
#include <cstdio>
#include <cstring>

int main() {
    FILE* f = fopen("no_such_folder/plan.txt", "r");
    if (f == nullptr) {
        int err = errno;   // save it at once: later calls may change it
        printf("%s\n", err == ENOENT ? "ENOENT" : "something else");   // ENOENT
        printf("%s\n", strerror(err));   // a text such as: No such file or directory
        return 0;
    }
    fclose(f);
    return 0;
}
```

The rules of `errno` are strict, and each one is broken in real code all the time:

1. **Check the return value first, and only then look at `errno`.** Functions set `errno` when they fail, but a successful call may leave an old value there, or even change it on the way. `errno` is only meaningful right after a call that reported failure.
2. **Save it at once.** Any later library call, even a `printf` to report the error, may change it.
3. **When the return value cannot show failure by itself, set `errno = 0` before the call.** The next section has the classic case.

Each thread of a program has its own `errno`, so threads cannot overwrite each other's.

### Converting text to a number, with every error caught

`atoi("12x")` returns 12 and `atoi("abc")` returns 0: it cannot report errors at all, and a number too big for an `int` is undefined behaviour. The `strto` family can. `strtol`, `strtoll` and `strtod` take the text and the address of a `char*`, and they report three things:

- They set that `char*` to point just past the last character they used. If it still points at the start of the text, **there were no digits at all**.
- If the value does not fit, they return the largest or smallest value of the type and **set `errno` to `ERANGE`**. Since that largest value is also a perfectly good answer for the text of that number, the only way to tell is `errno`, so you set it to 0 first.
- Anything after the number is left for you: if the end pointer is not at the terminating zero, **there are extra characters**.

```cpp
#include <cerrno>
#include <cstdio>
#include <cstdlib>

int main() {
    const char* texts[4] = {"-273.15", "hot", "12.5kg", "1e999"};
    for (int i = 0; i < 4; i++) {
        char* end = nullptr;
        errno = 0;
        double v = strtod(texts[i], &end);
        printf("%-8s value %g, used %d chars, rest \"%s\", %s\n", texts[i], v, (int)(end - texts[i]), end,
               errno == ERANGE ? "ERANGE" : "ok");
    }
    return 0;
}
```

It prints:

```text
-273.15  value -273.15, used 7 chars, rest "", ok
hot      value 0, used 0 chars, rest "hot", ok
12.5kg   value 12.5, used 4 chars, rest "kg", ok
1e999    value inf, used 5 chars, rest "", ERANGE
```

So a complete conversion checks, in order: did the end pointer move; is `errno` equal to `ERANGE`; is the end pointer at the terminating zero. Only when all three pass is the value real. They also skip spaces at the start of the text, which you may or may not want.

### Cleanup paths

The hard part of error handling is not detecting failure, it is **undoing**. A function that opens two files and allocates a buffer can fail at any of those steps, and each failure must release exactly what was acquired before it, no more and no less. Returning early copies the release code into every failure branch, and one day someone adds a resource and forgets one copy.

C programmers, the Linux kernel above all, solve it with **`goto`** and a ladder of labels at the end of the function, in the reverse order of acquisition:

```cpp
#include <cstdio>
#include <cstdlib>

// Copies the file `from` to `to` with every letter in upper case.
// Returns 0, -1 if a file cannot be opened, -2 if memory runs out, -3 on a write error.
int copy_upper(const char* from, const char* to) {
    int status = 0;
    FILE* in = nullptr;
    FILE* out = nullptr;
    char* buf = nullptr;
    size_t got = 0;

    in = fopen(from, "rb");
    if (in == nullptr) { status = -1; goto done; }
    out = fopen(to, "wb");
    if (out == nullptr) { status = -1; goto close_in; }
    buf = (char*)malloc(4096);
    if (buf == nullptr) { status = -2; goto close_out; }

    while ((got = fread(buf, 1, 4096, in)) > 0) {
        for (size_t i = 0; i < got; i++)
            if (buf[i] >= 'a' && buf[i] <= 'z') buf[i] = (char)(buf[i] - 'a' + 'A');
        if (fwrite(buf, 1, got, out) != got) { status = -3; break; }
    }

    free(buf);
close_out:
    if (fclose(out) != 0 && status == 0) status = -3;   // a failed close can lose data
close_in:
    fclose(in);
done:
    return status;
}

int main() {
    FILE* f = fopen("errors_demo_in.txt", "wb");
    fputs("burn at t+42s\n", f);
    fclose(f);
    printf("%d\n", copy_upper("errors_demo_in.txt", "errors_demo_out.txt"));   // 0
    printf("%d\n", copy_upper("errors_demo_missing.txt", "errors_demo_x.txt")); // -1
    f = fopen("errors_demo_out.txt", "rb");
    char line[32] = {};
    fgets(line, sizeof line, f);
    fclose(f);
    printf("%s", line);   // BURN AT T+42S
    return 0;
}
```

**Why every resource is released exactly once.** Number the resources in the order they are acquired: 1 is `in`, 2 is `out`, 3 is `buf`. The labels are laid out so that execution arriving at the label for resource k releases resources k, k − 1, …, 1, falling through from each release to the next. Now check each way out of the function:

- A failure while acquiring resource k + 1 means resources 1 to k are held, and it jumps to the label that releases k. So exactly 1 to k are released.
- The normal path holds all three and runs through every release.

So on every path, each resource that was acquired is released once, and nothing that was not acquired is touched. The order is the reverse of acquisition, which matters when a later resource depends on an earlier one.

Two variations are common. You can keep a single `cleanup:` label that releases every resource that is not null, after initialising all of them to null at the top; `free(nullptr)` does nothing, but `fclose(nullptr)` is not allowed, so test first. And in C++, a `goto` may not jump forward over the declaration of a variable with an initial value, which is why `copy_upper` declares everything at the top. C++ code with exceptions switched on uses destructors (**[[RAII|raii]]**) for all of this; C and exception-free code use exactly these patterns.

### Bugs are not errors

Some failures are the caller's fault, a broken promise inside the program: a null pointer passed where the documentation says "never null", an index past the end. Others are facts of the world: the file is missing, the disk is full, the input is malformed. Treat them differently.

- **Errors from the world** are reported with codes and handled, always. They will happen in the field.
- **Bugs** are caught with `assert(condition)` from `<cassert>`, which stops the program with a message if the condition is false. Assertions document assumptions and catch bugs in testing, but they are compiled out when `NDEBUG` is defined, as it is in many release builds. So never put an input check, or an expression with a side effect, inside `assert`.

### Testing the paths that never run

Error paths run rarely, so they are the least tested code in most programs, and a study of failures in large distributed systems found that most catastrophic outages came from [[mishandled errors|failure-study]]. The cure is **fault injection**: make the failures happen on purpose. The tracker from the malloc lesson can make the k-th allocation fail; a test runs the function once for every k, and checks after each run that it returned the right code and that no block is still live. [[SQLite|sqlite]] runs its whole test suite this way, failing every allocation in turn. The checks in this lesson do the same.

### What it costs

A status check is a comparison and a branch, and since failure is rare the branch is almost always predicted correctly: close to free. Exceptions, where they are allowed, cost nothing on the path that does not throw but make each throw slow, make the program bigger, and make timing hard to predict, which is why hard real-time code forbids them. The real cost of return codes is discipline: every call site must check. C++17's `[[nodiscard]]` on a function makes ignoring its return value a compiler warning.

### Where it is used

- **Operating system interfaces:** every system call returns -1 or a negative code and sets an error number; the kernel's own functions use `goto` cleanup ladders by the thousand.
- **Libraries:** SQLite returns `SQLITE_OK` or one of dozens of codes; Windows returns `HRESULT` values.
- **Flight software:** NASA's cFE functions return `CFE_SUCCESS` or an error code, and rule 7 of JPL's Power of Ten requires checking the return value of every function that has one.
- **Newer languages** kept the idea and made it safer: Go returns an error value beside each result, and Rust's `Result` type cannot be used without handling the error case.

**Watch out:** reading `errno` without a failed call. Code such as `f = fopen(...); if (errno == ENOENT) ...` looks at `errno` even when `fopen` succeeded, and then sees a stale code left behind by some earlier call, which may be ENOENT from a file that was never meant to exist. Test the return value first; read `errno` only when it says the call failed, and copy it before doing anything else.

::: context raii Destructors that clean up
RAII, "resource acquisition is initialisation", is the C++ way of tying a resource to an object: the constructor acquires it and the destructor releases it, so leaving a scope for any reason, a return, a break or an exception, releases everything automatically, in reverse order. `std::unique_ptr` and `std::ifstream` work this way. It removes whole classes of leak, and C code imitates it by hand with the cleanup ladder of this lesson.
:::

::: context failure-study Most outages start in an error handler
A 2014 study at the OSDI conference, "Simple Testing Can Prevent Most Critical Failures", examined 198 real failures in systems such as Cassandra, HBase and Hadoop. It found that 92% of the catastrophic ones were caused by incorrect handling of errors the software had already detected: empty error handlers, handlers that aborted the whole cluster, and TODO comments where handling should be. Many would have been caught by a test that simply ran each error path once.
:::

::: context sqlite How SQLite tests its failure paths
SQLite, the most widely deployed database in the world, has test code hundreds of times larger than the library itself. Its out-of-memory tests run a test case with an allocator that fails on the first allocation, then again failing on the second, and so on until the test completes; after each run they check that the result was a clean error and that nothing leaked. It does the same for failed reads and writes. This is why SQLite is trusted in aircraft, phones and browsers.
:::
--- task
A telemetry session owns three resources: a copy of its name, an array for its samples, and an open log file. Write the functions that open, use and close one, releasing everything correctly on every failure. The starter begins with the tracker from the malloc lesson: use `t_malloc` and `t_free` for all memory, and leave the tracker's code as it is. No `main`.

```cpp fragment
enum { OK = 0, ERR_ARG = -1, ERR_NOMEM = -2, ERR_IO = -3, ERR_FULL = -4 };

struct Session {
    char* name;        // a copy of the name
    double* samples;   // room for capacity samples
    int capacity;
    int count;
    FILE* log;         // the log file, open for writing
};
```

Include these exactly as shown, and write:

- `int session_open(Session* s, const char* name, int capacity, const char* log_path)`:
  - `ERR_ARG` if `name` or `log_path` is null, `name` is empty, or `capacity` is not from 1 to 100000. Nothing is allocated.
  - Otherwise acquire, in this order: a copy of `name` (with `t_malloc`), room for `capacity` doubles (with `t_malloc`), and the log (with `fopen(log_path, "w")`). If an allocation fails, return `ERR_NOMEM`; if the log cannot be opened, return `ERR_IO`. Either way, release everything acquired so far, and leave `*s` exactly as it was.
  - On success, fill in every field of `*s` (`count` is 0) and return `OK`.
- `int session_record(Session* s, double v)`: if the session is full, return `ERR_FULL`. Otherwise store `v` as the next sample, write the line `index value` to the log, with the value printed by `%.2f` (so the first sample 3.5 writes `0 3.50`), and return `OK`, or `ERR_IO` if writing to the log fails.
- `void session_close(Session* s)`: close the log and free both blocks, then set the pointers to null and the numbers to 0. Calling it again on a closed session does nothing.
--- starter
#include <cstdio>
#include <cstdlib>
#include <cstring>

// ---- The tracker. The checks watch your blocks through it: use t_malloc,
// ---- t_realloc and t_free exactly like malloc, realloc and free. Do not change it.
static void* live[1024];
static int live_count = 0;   // blocks allocated and not yet freed
static int bad_frees = 0;    // frees of pointers that were not live blocks
static int alloc_calls = 0;  // successful allocations and reallocations
static int fail_after = -1;  // -1: never fail; k: k more succeed, then one fails
static size_t last_size = 0; // bytes asked for by the last successful allocation

static bool should_fail() {
    if (fail_after == 0) {
        fail_after = -1;
        return true;
    }
    if (fail_after > 0) fail_after--;
    return false;
}

static int find_live(void* p) {
    for (int i = 0; i < live_count; i++) {
        if (live[i] == p) return i;
    }
    return -1;
}

void* t_malloc(size_t n) {
    if (should_fail() || live_count == 1024) return nullptr;
    void* p = malloc(n == 0 ? 1 : n);
    if (p != nullptr) {
        live[live_count++] = p;
        alloc_calls++;
        last_size = n;
    }
    return p;
}

void t_free(void* p) {
    if (p == nullptr) return;
    int i = find_live(p);
    if (i < 0) {
        bad_frees++;
        return;
    }
    live[i] = live[--live_count];
    free(p);
}

void* t_realloc(void* p, size_t n) {
    if (p == nullptr) return t_malloc(n);
    int i = find_live(p);
    if (i < 0) {
        bad_frees++;
        return nullptr;
    }
    if (should_fail()) return nullptr;
    void* q = realloc(p, n == 0 ? 1 : n);
    if (q != nullptr) {
        live[i] = q;
        alloc_calls++;
        last_size = n;
    }
    return q;
}
// ---- End of the tracker.

enum { OK = 0, ERR_ARG = -1, ERR_NOMEM = -2, ERR_IO = -3, ERR_FULL = -4 };

struct Session {
    char* name;
    double* samples;
    int capacity;
    int count;
    FILE* log;
};

int session_open(Session* s, const char* name, int capacity, const char* log_path) {
    s->name = (char*)t_malloc(strlen(name) + 1);
    strcpy(s->name, name);
    s->samples = (double*)t_malloc(capacity * sizeof(double));
    s->log = fopen(log_path, "w");
    s->capacity = capacity;
    s->count = 0;
    return OK;
}

int session_record(Session* s, double v) {
    return ERR_FULL;
}

void session_close(Session* s) {
}
--- solution
#include <cstdio>
#include <cstdlib>
#include <cstring>

// ---- The tracker. The checks watch your blocks through it: use t_malloc,
// ---- t_realloc and t_free exactly like malloc, realloc and free. Do not change it.
static void* live[1024];
static int live_count = 0;   // blocks allocated and not yet freed
static int bad_frees = 0;    // frees of pointers that were not live blocks
static int alloc_calls = 0;  // successful allocations and reallocations
static int fail_after = -1;  // -1: never fail; k: k more succeed, then one fails
static size_t last_size = 0; // bytes asked for by the last successful allocation

static bool should_fail() {
    if (fail_after == 0) {
        fail_after = -1;
        return true;
    }
    if (fail_after > 0) fail_after--;
    return false;
}

static int find_live(void* p) {
    for (int i = 0; i < live_count; i++) {
        if (live[i] == p) return i;
    }
    return -1;
}

void* t_malloc(size_t n) {
    if (should_fail() || live_count == 1024) return nullptr;
    void* p = malloc(n == 0 ? 1 : n);
    if (p != nullptr) {
        live[live_count++] = p;
        alloc_calls++;
        last_size = n;
    }
    return p;
}

void t_free(void* p) {
    if (p == nullptr) return;
    int i = find_live(p);
    if (i < 0) {
        bad_frees++;
        return;
    }
    live[i] = live[--live_count];
    free(p);
}

void* t_realloc(void* p, size_t n) {
    if (p == nullptr) return t_malloc(n);
    int i = find_live(p);
    if (i < 0) {
        bad_frees++;
        return nullptr;
    }
    if (should_fail()) return nullptr;
    void* q = realloc(p, n == 0 ? 1 : n);
    if (q != nullptr) {
        live[i] = q;
        alloc_calls++;
        last_size = n;
    }
    return q;
}
// ---- End of the tracker.

enum { OK = 0, ERR_ARG = -1, ERR_NOMEM = -2, ERR_IO = -3, ERR_FULL = -4 };

struct Session {
    char* name;
    double* samples;
    int capacity;
    int count;
    FILE* log;
};

int session_open(Session* s, const char* name, int capacity, const char* log_path) {
    if (name == nullptr || log_path == nullptr || name[0] == '\0') return ERR_ARG;
    if (capacity < 1 || capacity > 100000) return ERR_ARG;

    int status = OK;
    char* copy = nullptr;
    double* samples = nullptr;
    FILE* log = nullptr;
    size_t len = strlen(name);

    copy = (char*)t_malloc(len + 1);
    if (copy == nullptr) { status = ERR_NOMEM; goto done; }
    memcpy(copy, name, len + 1);
    samples = (double*)t_malloc((size_t)capacity * sizeof *samples);
    if (samples == nullptr) { status = ERR_NOMEM; goto free_copy; }
    log = fopen(log_path, "w");
    if (log == nullptr) { status = ERR_IO; goto free_samples; }

    // nothing can fail from here on: only now write to *s
    s->name = copy;
    s->samples = samples;
    s->capacity = capacity;
    s->count = 0;
    s->log = log;
    return OK;

free_samples:
    t_free(samples);
free_copy:
    t_free(copy);
done:
    return status;
}

int session_record(Session* s, double v) {
    if (s->count >= s->capacity) return ERR_FULL;
    if (fprintf(s->log, "%d %.2f\n", s->count, v) < 0) return ERR_IO;
    s->samples[s->count] = v;
    s->count++;
    return OK;
}

void session_close(Session* s) {
    if (s->log != nullptr) fclose(s->log);
    t_free(s->samples);
    t_free(s->name);
    s->log = nullptr;
    s->samples = nullptr;
    s->name = nullptr;
    s->capacity = 0;
    s->count = 0;
}
--- hint
Check every argument before acquiring anything. Then acquire into local variables, not into `*s`, so that a failure part way leaves `*s` untouched; copy the locals into `*s` only after the last step has succeeded.
--- hint
Use the cleanup ladder from the lesson: after each acquisition, on failure, set the status and `goto` the label that releases everything acquired so far. The labels come in reverse order: free the samples, then free the name copy, then return. Declare all the locals at the top, before the first `goto`.
--- hint
`session_close` must be safe to call twice: close the log only if it is not null (`fclose(nullptr)` is not allowed), free both blocks (`t_free(nullptr)` does nothing), and then set everything to null and 0.
--- check test | Opening a session holds two blocks and a log
[] { Session s; int r = session_open(&s, "sat-7", 10, "sys13_task_a.log"); bool ok = r == OK && s.name != nullptr && strcmp(s.name, "sat-7") == 0 && s.capacity == 10 && s.count == 0 && s.log != nullptr && live_count == 2; session_close(&s); return ok && live_count == 0 && bad_frees == 0; }()
--- check test | The name is copied, not borrowed
[] { char name[8] = "probe"; Session s; session_open(&s, name, 4, "sys13_task_b.log"); name[0] = 'X'; bool ok = strcmp(s.name, "probe") == 0 && s.name != name; session_close(&s); return ok; }()
--- check test | Recording fills the samples and the log, then reports full
[] { Session s; session_open(&s, "log", 3, "sys13_task_c.log"); int a = session_record(&s, 3.5), b = session_record(&s, -1.25), c = session_record(&s, 100.0), d = session_record(&s, 7.0); bool ok = a == OK && b == OK && c == OK && d == ERR_FULL && s.count == 3 && s.samples[1] == -1.25; session_close(&s); FILE* f = fopen("sys13_task_c.log", "r"); char buf[128] = {}; size_t n = fread(buf, 1, 127, f); fclose(f); return ok && n > 0 && std::string(buf) == "0 3.50\n1 -1.25\n2 100.00\n"; }()
--- check test | Bad arguments allocate nothing and leave *s alone
[] { int before = alloc_calls; Session s; s.name = (char*)"keep"; s.capacity = 55; bool a = session_open(&s, "", 5, "sys13_task_d.log") == ERR_ARG; bool b = session_open(&s, nullptr, 5, "sys13_task_d.log") == ERR_ARG; bool c = session_open(&s, "x", 0, "sys13_task_d.log") == ERR_ARG; bool d = session_open(&s, "x", 100001, "sys13_task_d.log") == ERR_ARG; bool e = session_open(&s, "x", 5, nullptr) == ERR_ARG; return a && b && c && d && e && alloc_calls == before && strcmp(s.name, "keep") == 0 && s.capacity == 55 && live_count == 0; }()
--- check test | The first allocation fails: ERR_NOMEM, nothing held, *s untouched
[] { Session s; s.name = (char*)"keep"; s.capacity = 55; fail_after = 0; int r = session_open(&s, "sat", 8, "sys13_task_e.log"); fail_after = -1; return r == ERR_NOMEM && live_count == 0 && bad_frees == 0 && strcmp(s.name, "keep") == 0 && s.capacity == 55; }()
--- check test | The second allocation fails: the name copy is freed
[] { Session s; s.name = (char*)"keep"; s.capacity = 55; fail_after = 1; int r = session_open(&s, "sat", 8, "sys13_task_f.log"); fail_after = -1; return r == ERR_NOMEM && live_count == 0 && bad_frees == 0 && strcmp(s.name, "keep") == 0 && s.capacity == 55; }()
--- check test | The log cannot be opened: ERR_IO, both blocks freed
[] { Session s; s.name = (char*)"keep"; s.capacity = 55; int r = session_open(&s, "sat", 8, "sys13_no_such_folder/x.log"); return r == ERR_IO && live_count == 0 && bad_frees == 0 && strcmp(s.name, "keep") == 0 && s.capacity == 55; }()
--- check test | Closing twice is harmless, and the session is emptied
[] { Session s; session_open(&s, "twice", 2, "sys13_task_g.log"); session_close(&s); session_close(&s); return s.name == nullptr && s.samples == nullptr && s.log == nullptr && s.capacity == 0 && s.count == 0 && live_count == 0 && bad_frees == 0; }()
--- check test | Every allocation failure in turn, 50 sessions
[] { for (int k = 0; k < 50; k++) { Session s; fail_after = k % 3; int r = session_open(&s, "loop", 1 + k, "sys13_task_h.log"); fail_after = -1; if (k % 3 < 2 && r != ERR_NOMEM) return false; if (k % 3 == 2) { if (r != OK) return false; session_close(&s); } if (live_count != 0 || bad_frees != 0) return false; } return true; }()

+++ practice | A number or a reason
--- task
Write `int parse_ll(const char* s, long long* out)`, which reads a whole text as a decimal `long long` and says exactly what is wrong when it cannot. Use `strtoll` and `errno` as the lesson describes. No `main`.

```cpp fragment
enum { PARSE_OK = 0, PARSE_EMPTY = 1, PARSE_JUNK = 2, PARSE_RANGE = 3 };
```

Include the enum exactly as shown. Decide the result in this order:

1. If the first character is a space or a tab, return `PARSE_JUNK`. (`strtoll` would skip it, but this format does not allow it.)
2. If there are no digits at all (for example `""`, `"-"` or `"abc"`), return `PARSE_EMPTY`.
3. If the number does not fit in a `long long`, return `PARSE_RANGE`, whatever follows it.
4. If anything follows the number, return `PARSE_JUNK`.
5. Otherwise store the value in `*out` and return `PARSE_OK`.

In every case but the last, leave `*out` unchanged. A sign, `+` or `-`, may come before the digits.
--- starter
#include <cerrno>
#include <cstdlib>

enum { PARSE_OK = 0, PARSE_EMPTY = 1, PARSE_JUNK = 2, PARSE_RANGE = 3 };

int parse_ll(const char* s, long long* out) {
    *out = atoll(s);
    return PARSE_OK;
}
--- solution
#include <cerrno>
#include <cstdlib>

enum { PARSE_OK = 0, PARSE_EMPTY = 1, PARSE_JUNK = 2, PARSE_RANGE = 3 };

int parse_ll(const char* s, long long* out) {
    if (s[0] == ' ' || s[0] == '\t') return PARSE_JUNK;
    char* end = nullptr;
    errno = 0;
    long long value = strtoll(s, &end, 10);
    if (end == s) return PARSE_EMPTY;
    if (errno == ERANGE) return PARSE_RANGE;
    if (*end != '\0') return PARSE_JUNK;
    *out = value;
    return PARSE_OK;
}
--- hint
Reject a leading space or tab yourself first. Then set `errno` to 0, call `strtoll(s, &end, 10)`, and look at `end` and `errno` in the order the task gives.
--- hint
No digits means `end` did not move from `s`. Out of range means `errno` is `ERANGE`. Extra characters mean `*end` is not the terminating zero. Write to `*out` only at the very end.
--- check test | Plain and signed numbers
[] { long long v = 0; bool a = parse_ll("42", &v) == PARSE_OK && v == 42; bool b = parse_ll("-17", &v) == PARSE_OK && v == -17; bool c = parse_ll("+8", &v) == PARSE_OK && v == 8; return a && b && c; }()
--- check test | The largest and smallest long long
[] { long long v = 0; bool a = parse_ll("9223372036854775807", &v) == PARSE_OK && v == 9223372036854775807LL; bool b = parse_ll("-9223372036854775808", &v) == PARSE_OK && v == -9223372036854775807LL - 1; return a && b; }()
--- check test | Out of range, even with junk after it
[] { long long v = 5; return parse_ll("9223372036854775808", &v) == PARSE_RANGE && parse_ll("-9223372036854775809", &v) == PARSE_RANGE && parse_ll("99999999999999999999x", &v) == PARSE_RANGE && v == 5; }()
--- check test | No digits at all
[] { long long v = 5; return parse_ll("", &v) == PARSE_EMPTY && parse_ll("-", &v) == PARSE_EMPTY && parse_ll("abc", &v) == PARSE_EMPTY && parse_ll("+x1", &v) == PARSE_EMPTY && v == 5; }()
--- check test | Junk after the number, and leading spaces
[] { long long v = 5; return parse_ll("12x", &v) == PARSE_JUNK && parse_ll("12 ", &v) == PARSE_JUNK && parse_ll(" 12", &v) == PARSE_JUNK && parse_ll("\t12", &v) == PARSE_JUNK && parse_ll("1.5", &v) == PARSE_JUNK && v == 5; }()
--- check test | A stale ERANGE from an earlier call does not leak into a good result
[] { long long v = 0; errno = ERANGE; return parse_ll("123", &v) == PARSE_OK && v == 123; }()
--- check source | Uses strtoll and errno
strtoll[\s\S]*errno|errno[\s\S]*strtoll

+++ practice | Why the files would not open
--- task
Write two functions that report failures by returning the `errno` value of the call that failed, the way POSIX thread functions do. No `main`.

- `int file_size(const char* path, long long* size)`: open the file in binary mode, find its size in bytes, close it, store the size in `*size`, and return 0. If `fopen` fails, return the `errno` it left (for a missing file, `ENOENT`), and leave `*size` unchanged.
- `int open_all(const char* const* paths, int n, FILE** files)`: open all `n` files for reading in binary mode, storing the streams in `files[0]` to `files[n - 1]`, and return 0. If any `fopen` fails, close the files already opened, in reverse order, set every one of the `n` entries of `files` to null, and return the `errno` of the failed `fopen`.

Save `errno` right after the failing call: closing files afterwards may change it.
--- starter
#include <cerrno>
#include <cstdio>

int file_size(const char* path, long long* size) {
    FILE* f = fopen(path, "rb");
    fseek(f, 0, SEEK_END);
    *size = ftell(f);
    fclose(f);
    return 0;
}

int open_all(const char* const* paths, int n, FILE** files) {
    for (int i = 0; i < n; i++) files[i] = fopen(paths[i], "rb");
    return 0;
}
--- solution
#include <cerrno>
#include <cstdio>

int file_size(const char* path, long long* size) {
    FILE* f = fopen(path, "rb");
    if (f == nullptr) return errno;
    int err = 0;
    long end = -1;
