    if (fseek(f, 0, SEEK_END) != 0 || (end = ftell(f)) < 0) err = errno;
    fclose(f);
    if (err != 0) return err;
    *size = end;
    return 0;
}

int open_all(const char* const* paths, int n, FILE** files) {
    for (int i = 0; i < n; i++) files[i] = nullptr;
    for (int i = 0; i < n; i++) {
        files[i] = fopen(paths[i], "rb");
        if (files[i] == nullptr) {
            int err = errno;                 // save it before fclose can change it
            for (int j = i - 1; j >= 0; j--) {
                fclose(files[j]);
                files[j] = nullptr;
            }
            return err;
        }
    }
    return 0;
}
--- hint
In `file_size`, return `errno` straight away if `fopen` returns null. Otherwise seek to the end, read the position with `ftell`, close the file, and only then store the size.
--- hint
In `open_all`, when opening file `i` fails, copy `errno` into a local variable first, then close files `i - 1` down to 0 and set each entry to null. Setting every entry to null at the very start makes the "all null on failure" promise easy to keep.
--- check test | The size of a 1000-byte file
[] { FILE* f = fopen("sys13_p2_a.bin", "wb"); for (int i = 0; i < 1000; i++) fputc(i % 7, f); fclose(f); long long n = -1; return file_size("sys13_p2_a.bin", &n) == 0 && n == 1000; }()
--- check test | An empty file has size 0
[] { FILE* f = fopen("sys13_p2_b.bin", "wb"); fclose(f); long long n = -1; return file_size("sys13_p2_b.bin", &n) == 0 && n == 0; }()
--- check test | A missing file returns ENOENT and keeps *size
[] { long long n = 77; return file_size("sys13_p2_missing.bin", &n) == ENOENT && n == 77 && file_size("sys13_no_dir/x.bin", &n) == ENOENT && n == 77; }()
--- check test | open_all opens every file
[] { const char* names[3] = {"sys13_p2_c1.txt", "sys13_p2_c2.txt", "sys13_p2_c3.txt"}; for (int i = 0; i < 3; i++) { FILE* f = fopen(names[i], "w"); fputc('a' + i, f); fclose(f); } FILE* fs[3]; int r = open_all(names, 3, fs); bool ok = r == 0 && fs[0] && fs[1] && fs[2] && getc(fs[2]) == 'c'; if (r == 0) for (int i = 0; i < 3; i++) fclose(fs[i]); return ok; }()
--- check test | One missing file: ENOENT, and every entry null
[] { const char* names[4] = {"sys13_p2_c1.txt", "sys13_p2_c2.txt", "sys13_p2_nope.txt", "sys13_p2_c3.txt"}; FILE* f = fopen("sys13_p2_c1.txt", "w"); fclose(f); f = fopen("sys13_p2_c2.txt", "w"); fclose(f); f = fopen("sys13_p2_c3.txt", "w"); fclose(f); FILE* fs[4] = {(FILE*)1, (FILE*)1, (FILE*)1, (FILE*)1}; int r = open_all(names, 4, fs); return r == ENOENT && fs[0] == nullptr && fs[1] == nullptr && fs[2] == nullptr && fs[3] == nullptr; }()
--- check test | Zero files is success
[] { FILE* fs[1]; return open_all(nullptr, 0, fs) == 0; }()

+++ practice | Undo in reverse
--- task
A spacecraft configuration change is a sequence of steps, each with an undo. If any step fails, the steps already done must be undone, newest first, so the spacecraft is back where it started. Write the runner. No `main`.

```cpp fragment
struct Step {
    int (*run)(void* ctx);    // returns 0 on success, or an error code
    void (*undo)(void* ctx);  // may be null: nothing to undo
    void* ctx;
};
```

Include the struct exactly as shown, and write `int run_steps(const Step* steps, int n, int* failed_at)`. Run the steps in order, calling `run(ctx)` with each step's own `ctx`. If step `k` returns a nonzero code, call `undo(ctx)` for steps `k - 1` down to 0 (skipping null undos, and never undoing step `k` itself, which did not happen), set `*failed_at` to `k`, and return that code. If every step succeeds, set `*failed_at` to -1 and return 0.
--- starter
struct Step {
    int (*run)(void* ctx);
    void (*undo)(void* ctx);
    void* ctx;
};

int run_steps(const Step* steps, int n, int* failed_at) {
    for (int i = 0; i < n; i++) steps[i].run(steps[i].ctx);
    *failed_at = -1;
    return 0;
}
--- solution
struct Step {
    int (*run)(void* ctx);
    void (*undo)(void* ctx);
    void* ctx;
};

int run_steps(const Step* steps, int n, int* failed_at) {
    for (int k = 0; k < n; k++) {
        int code = steps[k].run(steps[k].ctx);
        if (code != 0) {
            for (int j = k - 1; j >= 0; j--)
                if (steps[j].undo != nullptr) steps[j].undo(steps[j].ctx);
            *failed_at = k;
            return code;
        }
    }
    *failed_at = -1;
    return 0;
}
--- hint
This is the cleanup ladder written as a loop: a step that fails at position `k` means steps 0 to `k - 1` are done, and they must be undone in reverse.
--- hint
Keep the failing code before you start undoing, count `j` down from `k - 1` to 0, and test `undo` for null before calling through it.
--- check test | All steps succeed: each runs once, nothing undone
[] { static char log[32]; static int k; k = 0; auto run = [](void* c) -> int { log[k++] = *(char*)c; return 0; }; auto undo = [](void* c) { log[k++] = (char)(*(char*)c - 32); }; static char a = 'a', b = 'b', c = 'c'; Step s[3] = {{run, undo, &a}, {run, undo, &b}, {run, undo, &c}}; int at = 99; int r = run_steps(s, 3, &at); log[k] = 0; return r == 0 && at == -1 && std::string(log) == "abc"; }()
--- check test | Step 2 fails: steps 1 and 0 are undone, newest first
[] { static char log[32]; static int k; k = 0; auto run = [](void* c) -> int { log[k++] = *(char*)c; return 0; }; auto fail = [](void* c) -> int { log[k++] = *(char*)c; return -5; }; auto undo = [](void* c) { log[k++] = (char)(*(char*)c - 32); }; static char a = 'a', b = 'b', c = 'c', d = 'd'; Step s[4] = {{run, undo, &a}, {run, undo, &b}, {fail, undo, &c}, {run, undo, &d}}; int at = 99; int r = run_steps(s, 4, &at); log[k] = 0; return r == -5 && at == 2 && std::string(log) == "abcBA"; }()
--- check test | The first step fails: nothing to undo
[] { static int undos; undos = 0; auto fail = [](void*) -> int { return 7; }; auto undo = [](void*) { undos++; }; Step s[2] = {{fail, undo, nullptr}, {fail, undo, nullptr}}; int at = 99; return run_steps(s, 2, &at) == 7 && at == 0 && undos == 0; }()
--- check test | Null undos are skipped
[] { static int undos; undos = 0; auto ok = [](void*) -> int { return 0; }; auto fail = [](void*) -> int { return 1; }; auto undo = [](void*) { undos++; }; Step s[4] = {{ok, undo, nullptr}, {ok, nullptr, nullptr}, {ok, undo, nullptr}, {fail, undo, nullptr}}; int at = 99; return run_steps(s, 4, &at) == 1 && at == 3 && undos == 2; }()
--- check test | Undo gets each step's own context
[] { auto inc = [](void* c) -> int { (*(int*)c)++; return 0; }; auto dec = [](void* c) { (*(int*)c)--; }; auto fail = [](void*) -> int { return 2; }; int x = 10, y = 20; Step s[3] = {{inc, dec, &x}, {inc, dec, &y}, {fail, nullptr, nullptr}}; int at = 99; int r = run_steps(s, 3, &at); return r == 2 && x == 10 && y == 20; }()
--- check test | Zero steps succeed
[] { int at = 99; return run_steps(nullptr, 0, &at) == 0 && at == -1; }()

+++ practice | Parsing a list of numbers
--- task
Write `int parse_list(const char* s, int* out, int cap, int* count)`, which reads a comma-separated list of integers, such as `"12, -7,300"`, into `out`. No `main`.

```cpp fragment
enum { LIST_OK = 0, LIST_EMPTY_ITEM = 1, LIST_BAD_NUMBER = 2, LIST_FULL = 3 };
```

Include the enum exactly as shown. The rules:

- Items are separated by commas. Spaces around an item are allowed and ignored.
- An empty text, or one of only spaces, is an empty list: return `LIST_OK` with `*count` 0.
- An item that is empty or only spaces, as in `"1,,2"` or `"1,2,"`, gives `LIST_EMPTY_ITEM`.
- An item that is not a whole decimal integer (an optional sign and digits), or does not fit in an `int`, gives `LIST_BAD_NUMBER`.
- More than `cap` items gives `LIST_FULL`, when the item at position `cap` is reached.
- On success store the items in `out`, set `*count` to how many there are, and return `LIST_OK`. On any error set `*count` to the position (from 0) of the item that caused it; `out` may then hold anything.

For example, `"12, -7,300"` gives 3 items, and `"4, x, 5"` gives `LIST_BAD_NUMBER` with `*count` 1. Use `strtoll` for the numbers, and check the range of `int` yourself.
--- starter
#include <cerrno>
#include <climits>
#include <cstdlib>

enum { LIST_OK = 0, LIST_EMPTY_ITEM = 1, LIST_BAD_NUMBER = 2, LIST_FULL = 3 };

int parse_list(const char* s, int* out, int cap, int* count) {
    int n = 0;
    char* end = nullptr;
    while (*s != '\0') {
        out[n++] = (int)strtol(s, &end, 10);
        s = *end == ',' ? end + 1 : end;
    }
    *count = n;
    return LIST_OK;
}
--- solution
#include <cerrno>
#include <climits>
#include <cstdlib>

enum { LIST_OK = 0, LIST_EMPTY_ITEM = 1, LIST_BAD_NUMBER = 2, LIST_FULL = 3 };

static const char* skip_spaces(const char* p) {
    while (*p == ' ') p++;
    return p;
}

int parse_list(const char* s, int* out, int cap, int* count) {
    const char* p = skip_spaces(s);
    if (*p == '\0') {
        *count = 0;
        return LIST_OK;
    }
    int n = 0;
    while (true) {
        p = skip_spaces(p);
        if (*p == ',' || *p == '\0') {
            *count = n;
            return LIST_EMPTY_ITEM;
        }
        if (n == cap) {
            *count = n;
            return LIST_FULL;
        }
        char* end = nullptr;
        errno = 0;
        long long v = strtoll(p, &end, 10);
        if (end == p || errno == ERANGE || v < INT_MIN || v > INT_MAX) {
            *count = n;
            return LIST_BAD_NUMBER;
        }
        p = skip_spaces(end);
        if (*p != ',' && *p != '\0') {   // junk inside the item, such as "4x"
            *count = n;
            return LIST_BAD_NUMBER;
        }
        out[n++] = (int)v;
        if (*p == '\0') break;
        p++;   // past the comma: another item must follow
    }
    *count = n;
    return LIST_OK;
}
--- hint
Handle the empty list first: skip spaces, and if you reach the end, it is 0 items. After that, every item must be there, so an empty item is an error.
--- hint
For each item: skip spaces; a comma or the end here means an empty item. Check for `LIST_FULL` before converting. Convert with `strtoll` (setting `errno` to 0 first), and reject no digits, `ERANGE`, or a value outside `INT_MIN` to `INT_MAX`.
--- hint
After the number, skip spaces; the next character must be a comma or the end, or the item had junk in it. A comma means another item must follow; the end means you are done.
--- check test | The example, and one item
[] { int o[8]; int c = -1; bool a = parse_list("12, -7,300", o, 8, &c) == LIST_OK && c == 3 && o[0] == 12 && o[1] == -7 && o[2] == 300; bool b = parse_list(" 42 ", o, 8, &c) == LIST_OK && c == 1 && o[0] == 42; return a && b; }()
--- check test | Empty lists
[] { int o[1]; int c = -1; bool a = parse_list("", o, 1, &c) == LIST_OK && c == 0; c = -1; bool b = parse_list("   ", o, 1, &c) == LIST_OK && c == 0; return a && b; }()
--- check test | Empty items anywhere
[] { int o[8]; int c = -1; bool a = parse_list("1,,2", o, 8, &c) == LIST_EMPTY_ITEM && c == 1; bool b = parse_list("1,2,", o, 8, &c) == LIST_EMPTY_ITEM && c == 2; bool d = parse_list(",5", o, 8, &c) == LIST_EMPTY_ITEM && c == 0; bool e = parse_list("3, ,4", o, 8, &c) == LIST_EMPTY_ITEM && c == 1; return a && b && d && e; }()
--- check test | Bad numbers, including ones too big for an int
[] { int o[8]; int c = -1; bool a = parse_list("4, x, 5", o, 8, &c) == LIST_BAD_NUMBER && c == 1; bool b = parse_list("4x", o, 8, &c) == LIST_BAD_NUMBER && c == 0; bool d = parse_list("1, 2147483648", o, 8, &c) == LIST_BAD_NUMBER && c == 1; bool e = parse_list("-2147483649", o, 8, &c) == LIST_BAD_NUMBER && c == 0; bool f = parse_list("1, 2 3", o, 8, &c) == LIST_BAD_NUMBER && c == 1; bool g = parse_list("99999999999999999999", o, 8, &c) == LIST_BAD_NUMBER && c == 0; return a && b && d && e && f && g; }()
--- check test | The limits of int are accepted
[] { int o[4]; int c = -1; return parse_list("2147483647,-2147483648", o, 4, &c) == LIST_OK && c == 2 && o[0] == 2147483647 && o[1] == -2147483647 - 1; }()
--- check test | One item too many gives LIST_FULL at position cap
[] { int o[3] = {0, 0, 0}; int c = -1; bool a = parse_list("1,2,3,4", o, 3, &c) == LIST_FULL && c == 3; bool b = parse_list("1,2,3", o, 3, &c) == LIST_OK && c == 3 && o[2] == 3; bool d = parse_list("9", o, 0, &c) == LIST_FULL && c == 0; return a && b && d; }()

+++ practice | Debug: the leak on the error path
--- task
This function allocates the three buffers of a sensor pipeline. On success it fills in `*out` and returns 0. If any allocation fails, it must free the ones already allocated and return -1, leaving `*out` alone. The tests found that when the third allocation fails, one block is never freed. Find the bug and fix it. The starter begins with the tracker; leave its code as it is. No `main`.

`int make_pipeline(Pipeline* out, int n)`, where `Pipeline` holds `int* raw`, `int* filtered` and `char* status`: `raw` and `filtered` have room for `n` ints, and `status` for `n` chars.
--- starter
#include <cstdlib>
#include <cstring>

// ---- The tracker. The checks watch your blocks through it: use t_malloc,
// ---- t_realloc and t_free exactly like malloc, realloc and free. Do not change it.
static void* live[1024];
static int live_count = 0;   // blocks allocated and not yet freed
static int bad_frees = 0;    // frees of pointers that were not live blocks
static int alloc_calls = 0;  // successful allocations and reallocations
static int fail_after = -1;  // -1: never fail; k: k more succeed, then one fails
static size_t last_size = 0; // bytes asked for by the last successful allocation

static bool should_fail() {
    if (fail_after == 0) {
        fail_after = -1;
        return true;
    }
    if (fail_after > 0) fail_after--;
    return false;
}

static int find_live(void* p) {
    for (int i = 0; i < live_count; i++) {
        if (live[i] == p) return i;
    }
    return -1;
}

void* t_malloc(size_t n) {
    if (should_fail() || live_count == 1024) return nullptr;
    void* p = malloc(n == 0 ? 1 : n);
    if (p != nullptr) {
        live[live_count++] = p;
        alloc_calls++;
        last_size = n;
    }
    return p;
}

void t_free(void* p) {
    if (p == nullptr) return;
    int i = find_live(p);
    if (i < 0) {
        bad_frees++;
        return;
    }
    live[i] = live[--live_count];
    free(p);
}

void* t_realloc(void* p, size_t n) {
    if (p == nullptr) return t_malloc(n);
    int i = find_live(p);
    if (i < 0) {
        bad_frees++;
        return nullptr;
    }
    if (should_fail()) return nullptr;
    void* q = realloc(p, n == 0 ? 1 : n);
    if (q != nullptr) {
        live[i] = q;
        alloc_calls++;
        last_size = n;
    }
    return q;
}
// ---- End of the tracker.

struct Pipeline {
    int* raw;
    int* filtered;
    char* status;
};

int make_pipeline(Pipeline* out, int n) {
    int* raw = nullptr;
    int* filtered = nullptr;
    char* status = nullptr;

    raw = (int*)t_malloc(n * sizeof *raw);
    if (raw == nullptr) goto fail;
    filtered = (int*)t_malloc(n * sizeof *filtered);
    if (filtered == nullptr) goto free_raw;
    status = (char*)t_malloc(n);
    if (status == nullptr) goto free_raw;

    out->raw = raw;
    out->filtered = filtered;
    out->status = status;
    return 0;

free_filtered:
    t_free(filtered);
free_raw:
    t_free(raw);
fail:
    return -1;
}
--- solution
#include <cstdlib>
#include <cstring>

// ---- The tracker. The checks watch your blocks through it: use t_malloc,
// ---- t_realloc and t_free exactly like malloc, realloc and free. Do not change it.
static void* live[1024];
static int live_count = 0;   // blocks allocated and not yet freed
static int bad_frees = 0;    // frees of pointers that were not live blocks
static int alloc_calls = 0;  // successful allocations and reallocations
static int fail_after = -1;  // -1: never fail; k: k more succeed, then one fails
static size_t last_size = 0; // bytes asked for by the last successful allocation

static bool should_fail() {
    if (fail_after == 0) {
        fail_after = -1;
        return true;
    }
    if (fail_after > 0) fail_after--;
    return false;
}

static int find_live(void* p) {
    for (int i = 0; i < live_count; i++) {
        if (live[i] == p) return i;
    }
    return -1;
}

void* t_malloc(size_t n) {
    if (should_fail() || live_count == 1024) return nullptr;
    void* p = malloc(n == 0 ? 1 : n);
    if (p != nullptr) {
        live[live_count++] = p;
        alloc_calls++;
        last_size = n;
    }
    return p;
}

void t_free(void* p) {
    if (p == nullptr) return;
    int i = find_live(p);
    if (i < 0) {
        bad_frees++;
        return;
    }
    live[i] = live[--live_count];
    free(p);
}

void* t_realloc(void* p, size_t n) {
    if (p == nullptr) return t_malloc(n);
    int i = find_live(p);
    if (i < 0) {
        bad_frees++;
        return nullptr;
    }
    if (should_fail()) return nullptr;
    void* q = realloc(p, n == 0 ? 1 : n);
    if (q != nullptr) {
        live[i] = q;
        alloc_calls++;
        last_size = n;
    }
    return q;
}
// ---- End of the tracker.

struct Pipeline {
    int* raw;
    int* filtered;
    char* status;
};

int make_pipeline(Pipeline* out, int n) {
    int* raw = nullptr;
    int* filtered = nullptr;
    char* status = nullptr;

    raw = (int*)t_malloc(n * sizeof *raw);
    if (raw == nullptr) goto fail;
    filtered = (int*)t_malloc(n * sizeof *filtered);
    if (filtered == nullptr) goto free_raw;
    status = (char*)t_malloc(n);
    if (status == nullptr) goto free_filtered;

    out->raw = raw;
    out->filtered = filtered;
    out->status = status;
    return 0;

free_filtered:
    t_free(filtered);
free_raw:
    t_free(raw);
fail:
    return -1;
}
--- hint
When the third allocation fails, which blocks are held at that moment, and which label releases exactly those?
--- hint
The ladder's rule is: a failure while acquiring resource k + 1 jumps to the label that releases resource k. The third failure jumps one label too far down, past `free_filtered`.
--- check test | Success holds three blocks
[] { Pipeline p = {nullptr, nullptr, nullptr}; int r = make_pipeline(&p, 16); bool ok = r == 0 && p.raw && p.filtered && p.status && live_count == 3; t_free(p.raw); t_free(p.filtered); t_free(p.status); return ok && live_count == 0; }()
--- check test | The first allocation fails: nothing held
[] { Pipeline p = {nullptr, nullptr, nullptr}; fail_after = 0; int r = make_pipeline(&p, 16); fail_after = -1; return r == -1 && live_count == 0 && bad_frees == 0 && p.raw == nullptr; }()
--- check test | The second allocation fails: nothing held
[] { Pipeline p = {nullptr, nullptr, nullptr}; fail_after = 1; int r = make_pipeline(&p, 16); fail_after = -1; return r == -1 && live_count == 0 && bad_frees == 0 && p.raw == nullptr; }()
--- check test | The third allocation fails: nothing held
[] { Pipeline p = {nullptr, nullptr, nullptr}; fail_after = 2; int r = make_pipeline(&p, 16); fail_after = -1; return r == -1 && live_count == 0 && bad_frees == 0 && p.raw == nullptr; }()

+++ practice | Decoding a telemetry frame
--- task
A radio receiver hands the ground software raw frames of bytes. Decode one, checking everything before trusting any of it. No `main`.

A frame is laid out as:

| bytes | meaning |
|---|---|
| 0 and 1 | the sync marker, `0xEB` then `0x90` |
| 2 | L, the payload length, at most 32 |
| 3 | the frame type |
| 4 to 3 + L | the payload, L bytes |
| 4 + L and 5 + L | a checksum, big-endian: the sum of bytes 2 to 3 + L, modulo 65536 |

So a whole frame is exactly 6 + L bytes.

```cpp fragment
enum { FRAME_OK = 0, FRAME_SHORT = -1, FRAME_SYNC = -2, FRAME_LENGTH = -3, FRAME_CHECKSUM = -4 };

struct Frame {
    uint8_t type;
    uint8_t len;
    uint8_t payload[32];
};
```

Include these exactly as shown, with `<cstdint>`, and write `int decode_frame(const uint8_t* bytes, int n, Frame* out)`, deciding in this order:

1. `n < 6`: `FRAME_SHORT`.
2. The sync marker is wrong: `FRAME_SYNC`.
3. L is more than 32: `FRAME_LENGTH`.
4. `n < 6 + L`: `FRAME_SHORT`; `n > 6 + L`: `FRAME_LENGTH`.
5. The checksum does not match: `FRAME_CHECKSUM`.
6. Otherwise fill in `*out` (type, len and the L payload bytes) and return `FRAME_OK`.

Never read a byte at or beyond position `n`, and leave `*out` unchanged on every error.
--- starter
#include <cstdint>
#include <cstring>

enum { FRAME_OK = 0, FRAME_SHORT = -1, FRAME_SYNC = -2, FRAME_LENGTH = -3, FRAME_CHECKSUM = -4 };

struct Frame {
    uint8_t type;
    uint8_t len;
    uint8_t payload[32];
};

int decode_frame(const uint8_t* bytes, int n, Frame* out) {
    out->type = bytes[3];
    out->len = bytes[2];
    memcpy(out->payload, bytes + 4, bytes[2]);
    return FRAME_OK;
}
--- solution
#include <cstdint>
#include <cstring>

enum { FRAME_OK = 0, FRAME_SHORT = -1, FRAME_SYNC = -2, FRAME_LENGTH = -3, FRAME_CHECKSUM = -4 };

struct Frame {
    uint8_t type;
    uint8_t len;
    uint8_t payload[32];
};

int decode_frame(const uint8_t* bytes, int n, Frame* out) {
    if (n < 6) return FRAME_SHORT;
    if (bytes[0] != 0xEB || bytes[1] != 0x90) return FRAME_SYNC;
    int len = bytes[2];
    if (len > 32) return FRAME_LENGTH;
    if (n < 6 + len) return FRAME_SHORT;
    if (n > 6 + len) return FRAME_LENGTH;
    uint16_t sum = 0;
    for (int i = 2; i < 4 + len; i++) sum = (uint16_t)(sum + bytes[i]);
    uint16_t sent = (uint16_t)((bytes[4 + len] << 8) | bytes[5 + len]);
    if (sum != sent) return FRAME_CHECKSUM;
    out->type = bytes[3];
    out->len = (uint8_t)len;
    memcpy(out->payload, bytes + 4, len);
    return FRAME_OK;
}
--- hint
Follow the order in the task exactly. Every check guards the reads that come after it: you may look at bytes 0 to 5 only after knowing `n` is at least 6, and at the payload and checksum only after knowing the frame is exactly 6 + L bytes.
--- hint
Add bytes 2 to 3 + L into a `uint16_t`, which wraps modulo 65536 by itself. The sent checksum is `(bytes[4 + L] << 8) | bytes[5 + L]`.
--- hint
Write to `*out` only after every check has passed.
--- check test | A good frame with a 3-byte payload
[] { uint8_t b[9] = {0xEB, 0x90, 3, 7, 10, 20, 30, 0, 0}; uint16_t s = 3 + 7 + 10 + 20 + 30; b[7] = (uint8_t)(s >> 8); b[8] = (uint8_t)s; Frame f; int r = decode_frame(b, 9, &f); return r == FRAME_OK && f.type == 7 && f.len == 3 && f.payload[0] == 10 && f.payload[2] == 30; }()
--- check test | An empty payload
[] { uint8_t b[6] = {0xEB, 0x90, 0, 5, 0, 5}; Frame f; return decode_frame(b, 6, &f) == FRAME_OK && f.type == 5 && f.len == 0; }()
--- check test | Too short for a header, and a wrong sync
[] { uint8_t b[6] = {0xEB, 0x90, 0, 5, 0, 5}; uint8_t c[6] = {0xEB, 0x91, 0, 5, 0, 5}; Frame f; f.type = 99; return decode_frame(b, 5, &f) == FRAME_SHORT && decode_frame(b, 0, &f) == FRAME_SHORT && decode_frame(c, 6, &f) == FRAME_SYNC && f.type == 99; }()
--- check test | A length over 32, a frame cut short, and extra bytes
[] { uint8_t b[40] = {0xEB, 0x90, 33, 1}; uint8_t c[8] = {0xEB, 0x90, 4, 1, 0, 0, 0, 0}; uint8_t d[7] = {0xEB, 0x90, 0, 5, 0, 5, 0}; Frame f; f.len = 99; return decode_frame(b, 40, &f) == FRAME_LENGTH && decode_frame(c, 8, &f) == FRAME_SHORT && decode_frame(d, 7, &f) == FRAME_LENGTH && f.len == 99; }()
--- check test | A wrong checksum is caught, and *out kept
[] { uint8_t b[8] = {0xEB, 0x90, 2, 1, 50, 60, 0, 0}; uint16_t s = 2 + 1 + 50 + 60; b[6] = (uint8_t)(s >> 8); b[7] = (uint8_t)(s + 1); Frame f; f.type = 99; return decode_frame(b, 8, &f) == FRAME_CHECKSUM && f.type == 99; }()
--- check test | A full 32-byte payload whose checksum passes 255
[] { uint8_t b[38]; b[0] = 0xEB; b[1] = 0x90; b[2] = 32; b[3] = 200; unsigned s = 32 + 200; for (int i = 0; i < 32; i++) { b[4 + i] = (uint8_t)(250 - i); s += b[4 + i]; } b[36] = (uint8_t)(s >> 8); b[37] = (uint8_t)s; Frame f; int r = decode_frame(b, 38, &f); return r == FRAME_OK && f.len == 32 && f.payload[31] == 219 && f.type == 200; }()

+++ question | The stale errno
--- ask
What is wrong with this code?

```cpp fragment
FILE* f = fopen("plan.txt", "r");
if (errno == ENOENT) {
    printf("no plan\n");
    return;
}
```
--- choice
Nothing: `fopen` sets `errno` to 0 when it succeeds.
--- choice correct
It reads `errno` without checking that `fopen` failed; after a successful `fopen`, `errno` may still hold an old code from an earlier call.
--- choice
`ENOENT` is not a code `fopen` can produce.
--- choice
`errno` can only be read after calling `perror`.
--- why
Library functions set `errno` when they fail and are allowed to leave it alone, or change it, when they succeed. It means something only right after a call that reported failure: test `f == nullptr` first, then read `errno`.

+++ question | Counting the releases
--- ask
A function acquires four resources in order, with a cleanup ladder of labels at the end as in the lesson. The third acquisition fails. How many resources does the function release before returning?
--- answer
2
two
--- why
When the third acquisition fails, only the first two are held. The failure jumps to the label that releases resource 2, and execution falls through to the release of resource 1: two releases, in reverse order.
=== sys-14 | Data structures in C: a generic dynamic array and a hash table of strings
--- teach
Last lesson finished the toolkit: you can report every failure and release every resource on every path. Together with `void*`, `memcpy`, function pointers and `realloc`, that is everything C offers for building data structures. C has no templates, no `std::vector` and no `std::unordered_map`, so every C program of any size contains its own versions of the two structures in this lesson: a **dynamic array that can hold any type**, and a **hash table keyed by strings**. You built both in Python in DSA; here you will see what they cost when every byte and every pointer is yours to manage.

### A dynamic array of anything

A C++ `std::vector<T>` knows its element type at compile time. A C dynamic array cannot, so it remembers the **size of one element** instead, and treats its storage as raw bytes:

```cpp
#include <cstdio>
#include <cstdlib>
#include <cstring>

struct Vec {
    void* data;         // len elements, each elem_size bytes
    size_t len;
    size_t cap;
    size_t elem_size;
};

void vec_init(Vec* v, size_t elem_size) {
    v->data = nullptr;
    v->len = 0;
    v->cap = 0;
    v->elem_size = elem_size;
}

void* vec_at(const Vec* v, size_t i) {
    return (char*)v->data + i * v->elem_size;   // element i, found by its byte offset
}

bool vec_push(Vec* v, const void* elem) {
    if (v->len == v->cap) {
        size_t new_cap = v->cap == 0 ? 4 : v->cap * 2;
        void* bigger = realloc(v->data, new_cap * v->elem_size);
        if (bigger == nullptr) return false;       // the old block is still there, unchanged
        v->data = bigger;
        v->cap = new_cap;
    }
    memcpy(vec_at(v, v->len), elem, v->elem_size); // copy the element's bytes in
    v->len++;
    return true;
}

void vec_free(Vec* v) {
    free(v->data);
    vec_init(v, v->elem_size);
}

struct Fix {
    double lat, lon;
};

int main() {
    Vec ids;
    vec_init(&ids, sizeof(int));
    for (int i = 0; i < 10; i++) {
        int id = 100 + i;
        vec_push(&ids, &id);
    }
    printf("%zu %zu %d\n", ids.len, ids.cap, *(int*)vec_at(&ids, 9));   // 10 16 109

    Vec track;
    vec_init(&track, sizeof(Fix));
    Fix f = {67.86, 20.96};
    vec_push(&track, &f);
    printf("%.2f\n", ((Fix*)vec_at(&track, 0))->lon);                  // 20.96

    vec_free(&ids);
    vec_free(&track);
    return 0;
}
```

Every idea in it comes from earlier lessons: element `i` is `i * elem_size` bytes from the start (pointer arithmetic on `char*`); elements go in and out by `memcpy`, never by assignment, since the code does not know their type; growth uses `realloc` into a second variable so a failure loses nothing (the malloc lesson); and a real version would also check that `new_cap * elem_size` does not overflow.

**What a push costs.** Doubling makes each push O(1) **amortised**. Here is the accounting argument. Charge every push 3 units of work. One pays for writing the new element. The other two are saved with it. When the array doubles from capacity c to 2c, it copies c elements, and the c/2 elements pushed since the last doubling (all those above c/2) have saved 2 units each, which is exactly c: enough to pay for the copy. So the savings never go negative, and n pushes cost at most 3n units in total, O(1) each. A single push can still cost O(n), when it triggers a copy.

**The price of void*.** The compiler no longer checks types. `*(double*)vec_at(&ids, 0)` compiles and reads an `int`'s bytes as a `double`; a `Vec` made with the wrong `elem_size` corrupts memory. And any pointer returned by `vec_at` becomes **dangling** after the next push that grows the array, because `realloc` may move the whole block. Keep indexes, not pointers, across pushes. The C++ `std::vector` has the same rule, for the same reason.

### A hash table of strings

A **hash table** stores key-value pairs and finds a key in expected O(1) time. As in DSA, it has an array of m **buckets**; a **hash function** turns a key into a number, and the number, reduced to the range 0 to m − 1, picks the bucket. Keys that land in the same bucket **collide**. This lesson uses **separate chaining**: each bucket is the head of a singly linked list of the nodes whose keys landed there.

```cpp fragment
struct Node {
    char* key;         // the table's own copy of the key
    int value;
    Node* next;        // the next node in the same bucket
};
```

**The hash function.** A good one spreads keys evenly and is cheap. A standard choice for short strings is **[[FNV-1a|fnv]]**: start from a fixed number, and for each byte, XOR it in and multiply by a fixed prime.

```cpp fragment
uint32_t fnv1a(const char* s) {
    uint32_t h = 2166136261u;
    for (; *s != '\0'; s++) {
        h ^= (unsigned char)*s;
        h *= 16777619u;          // unsigned: wraps modulo 2^32, which is defined
    }
    return h;
}
```

The arithmetic must be unsigned, because signed overflow is undefined behaviour, and a hash overflows on almost every step. The multiplication spreads each byte's effect into the high bits, and the XOR mixes the next byte in, so similar keys such as `temp1` and `temp2` land far apart.

**From hash to bucket.** Keep m a power of two, and the bucket is `h & (m - 1)`: the low bits of the hash, which is `h % m` without a division, as the bit manipulation lesson proved.

**Owning the keys.** The table must **copy** every key it stores: `malloc(strlen(key) + 1)` and `memcpy`, terminating zero included. If it kept the caller's pointer instead, the key would change when the caller reuses its buffer, or dangle when the caller's array goes out of scope. So the table owns its keys and frees them, and its nodes, when an entry is removed or the table is destroyed.

**Removing from a chain.** Deleting a node from a singly linked list needs the pointer that points **to** it, which is the bucket's head for the first node and the previous node's `next` for the others. A pointer to that pointer, a `Node**`, handles both cases the same way. Here it is on a plain list of numbers, removing every negative one:

```cpp fragment
struct Cell { int v; Cell* next; };

void drop_negatives(Cell** head) {
    Cell** link = head;                 // the pointer that points at the current cell
    while (*link != nullptr) {
        Cell* c = *link;
        if (c->v < 0) {
            *link = c->next;            // unhook it: whoever pointed at c now points past it
            free(c);
        } else {
            link = &c->next;            // move on to the next pointer
        }
    }
}
```

### Growing the table

The **load factor** α = n / m is the average number of keys per bucket. As it grows, chains get longer and lookups slower, so the table keeps α at most 3/4: before adding a new key would push it over, it allocates an array of twice as many buckets and moves every node into its bucket in the new array (**rehashing**). Nodes and keys are reused, not copied; only their `next` pointers change. The bucket of each node must be recomputed with the **new** m, because `h & (2m - 1)` looks at one more bit than `h & (m - 1)`.

**Why lookups are correct.** The table keeps one invariant: **every key is in exactly one node, and that node is in the chain of bucket `hash(key) & (m - 1)`**. A lookup walks exactly that chain, so if the key is in the table it is found, and if the walk ends it is not there. Insert keeps the invariant (a new key goes into its own bucket's chain; an existing key's value is updated in place, never duplicated); remove unhooks the node from the only chain it is in; and rehashing places every node in its bucket for the new m, so the invariant holds again when it finishes.

### What it costs

- **Lookup, insert and remove** walk one chain. Assume the hash sends each key to each of the m buckets with equal chance, independently (the **simple uniform hashing** assumption). Then each of the other keys is in the searched bucket with probability 1/m, so the expected chain length is (n − 1)/m < α. The expected cost is O(1 + α), which is **O(1)** while α stays at most 3/4.
- **Growing** costs O(n) when it happens, but, exactly as for the dynamic array, the table doubles, so the total work of all growth over n inserts is O(n): O(1) amortised per insert.
- **The worst case** is O(n) per operation, if all keys land in one bucket. That happens with a bad hash function, or when an attacker chooses keys on purpose, which is a real attack on web servers, called [[hash flooding|flooding]].
- **Memory:** each entry costs a node (key pointer, value and next pointer, plus padding) and a separate key block, and each allocation carries the allocator's own header. For millions of small entries, that overhead is why many tables store entries in one array instead, with [[open addressing|open-addressing]].

### Where it is used

- **Compilers and linkers** keep their symbol tables in hash tables of strings: the linker of lesson 11 looks up every undefined symbol in one.
- **Interpreters:** every Python dictionary, and so every Python object's attributes and every module's names, is a hash table.
- **Operating systems** cache file names in hash tables, so opening `/home/user/log.txt` does not reread every directory from disk.
- **Ground software** looks telemetry parameters up by name, such as `batt.voltage`, thousands of times a second.

**Watch out:** storing the caller's pointer as the key. `node->key = key;` compiles and passes a test that uses string literals, which never change. In real use the caller reads each name into one buffer, inserts it, and reads the next name into the same buffer: every key in the table now points at that buffer, and they all read as the last name. Copy the key into memory the table owns.

::: context fnv Where FNV comes from
FNV is named after Glenn Fowler, Landon Curt Noll and Kiem-Phong Vo, who designed it in 1991. The two constants are an offset basis and a prime chosen so that multiplying by it mixes bits well. It is fast, simple and good enough for hash tables, and is used in DNS servers, compilers and databases, but it is not a cryptographic hash: anyone can find keys that collide, which is why tables exposed to attackers need a keyed hash.
:::

::: context flooding Choosing keys to slow a server down
If an attacker knows a server's hash function, they can compute thousands of keys that all land in one bucket and send them, for example as form fields in a web request. Every insert then walks one long chain, and a single request can take minutes of CPU time. In 2011 this was shown to work against PHP, Java, Python and Ruby web servers at once. The fix is a keyed hash such as SipHash, whose output depends on a secret random seed chosen when the program starts, which Python and Rust now use by default.
:::

::: context open-addressing Tables without chains
Open addressing stores the entries themselves in the bucket array. When a key's bucket is taken, it tries the next bucket, and the next (linear probing), until it finds the key or an empty slot. There are no nodes and no next pointers, so it uses less memory and walks through neighbouring memory, which is cache-friendly. Removal is harder, since emptying a slot can break another key's probe sequence. Python's dictionaries and Google's Swiss tables use open addressing.
:::
--- task
Build a hash table from strings to `int`s, with separate chaining, that owns its keys and grows. The starter begins with the tracker from the malloc lesson, rebuilt to follow many blocks: use `t_malloc` and `t_free` for all memory, and leave the tracker's code as it is. No `main`.

```cpp fragment
struct Node {
    char* key;
    int value;
    Node* next;
};

struct HashMap {
    Node** buckets;   // nbuckets chain heads
    int nbuckets;     // always a power of two
    int size;         // number of keys
};
```

Include these definitions exactly as shown, and write:

- `HashMap* hm_create()`: an empty map with 8 buckets, all null. Return `nullptr` if an allocation fails, with nothing left allocated.
- `bool hm_put(HashMap* m, const char* key, int value)`: if `key` is present, set its value. Otherwise add it, with a copy of the key. Before adding a **new** key, if `(size + 1) * 4 > nbuckets * 3`, first double the number of buckets and move every node to its bucket in the new array. Return `false` if any allocation fails, leaving the map exactly as it was; otherwise `true`.
- `bool hm_get(const HashMap* m, const char* key, int* out)`: store the key's value in `*out` and return `true`, or return `false` if it is absent.
- `bool hm_erase(HashMap* m, const char* key)`: remove the key, freeing its node and its copy, and return `true`; or return `false` if it is absent.
- `void hm_destroy(HashMap* m)`: free every key, node, the bucket array and the map. `nullptr` is allowed and does nothing.

Use FNV-1a from the lesson, and bucket `hash & (nbuckets - 1)`.
--- starter
#include <cstdint>
#include <cstdio>
#include <cstdlib>
#include <cstring>

// ---- The tracker from the malloc lesson, rebuilt to follow many blocks quickly. The checks watch
// ---- your blocks through it: use t_malloc, t_realloc and t_free exactly like malloc, realloc and
// ---- free. Do not change it.
static void* live[1 << 18];  // the live blocks, in no particular order
static int live_count = 0;   // blocks allocated and not yet freed
static int bad_frees = 0;    // frees of pointers that were not live blocks
static int alloc_calls = 0;  // successful allocations and reallocations
static int fail_after = -1;  // -1: never fail; k: k more succeed, then one fails

struct THead {               // hidden in front of every block
    int index;               // where the block is in live[]
    unsigned magic;
    long long pad;
};

static bool should_fail() {
    if (fail_after == 0) {
        fail_after = -1;
        return true;
    }
    if (fail_after > 0) fail_after--;
    return false;
}

static THead* head_of(void* p) {
    return (THead*)((char*)p - sizeof(THead));
}

static bool is_live(void* p) {
    THead* h = head_of(p);
    return h->magic == 0x7EA1B10Cu && h->index >= 0 && h->index < live_count && live[h->index] == p;
}

void* t_malloc(size_t n) {
    if (should_fail() || live_count == (1 << 18)) return nullptr;
    THead* h = (THead*)malloc(sizeof(THead) + n);
    if (h == nullptr) return nullptr;
    h->index = live_count;
    h->magic = 0x7EA1B10Cu;
    void* p = (char*)h + sizeof(THead);
    live[live_count++] = p;
    alloc_calls++;
    return p;
}

void t_free(void* p) {
    if (p == nullptr) return;
    if (!is_live(p)) {
        bad_frees++;
        return;
    }
    THead* h = head_of(p);
    int i = h->index;
    live[i] = live[--live_count];
    if (i < live_count) head_of(live[i])->index = i;
    h->magic = 0;
    free(h);
}

void* t_realloc(void* p, size_t n) {
    if (p == nullptr) return t_malloc(n);
    if (!is_live(p)) {
        bad_frees++;
        return nullptr;
    }
    if (should_fail()) return nullptr;
    THead* h = (THead*)realloc(head_of(p), sizeof(THead) + n);
    if (h == nullptr) return nullptr;
    void* q = (char*)h + sizeof(THead);
    live[h->index] = q;
    alloc_calls++;
    return q;
}
// ---- End of the tracker.

struct Node {
    char* key;
    int value;
    Node* next;
};

struct HashMap {
    Node** buckets;
    int nbuckets;
    int size;
};

HashMap* hm_create() {
    return nullptr;
}

bool hm_put(HashMap* m, const char* key, int value) {
    return false;
}

bool hm_get(const HashMap* m, const char* key, int* out) {
    return false;
}

bool hm_erase(HashMap* m, const char* key) {
    return false;
}

void hm_destroy(HashMap* m) {
}
--- solution
#include <cstdint>
#include <cstdio>
#include <cstdlib>
#include <cstring>

// ---- The tracker from the malloc lesson, rebuilt to follow many blocks quickly. The checks watch
// ---- your blocks through it: use t_malloc, t_realloc and t_free exactly like malloc, realloc and
// ---- free. Do not change it.
static void* live[1 << 18];  // the live blocks, in no particular order
static int live_count = 0;   // blocks allocated and not yet freed
static int bad_frees = 0;    // frees of pointers that were not live blocks
static int alloc_calls = 0;  // successful allocations and reallocations
static int fail_after = -1;  // -1: never fail; k: k more succeed, then one fails

struct THead {               // hidden in front of every block
    int index;               // where the block is in live[]
    unsigned magic;
    long long pad;
};

static bool should_fail() {
    if (fail_after == 0) {
        fail_after = -1;
        return true;
    }
    if (fail_after > 0) fail_after--;
    return false;
}

static THead* head_of(void* p) {
    return (THead*)((char*)p - sizeof(THead));
}

static bool is_live(void* p) {
    THead* h = head_of(p);
    return h->magic == 0x7EA1B10Cu && h->index >= 0 && h->index < live_count && live[h->index] == p;
}

void* t_malloc(size_t n) {
    if (should_fail() || live_count == (1 << 18)) return nullptr;
    THead* h = (THead*)malloc(sizeof(THead) + n);
    if (h == nullptr) return nullptr;
    h->index = live_count;
    h->magic = 0x7EA1B10Cu;
    void* p = (char*)h + sizeof(THead);
    live[live_count++] = p;
    alloc_calls++;
    return p;
}

void t_free(void* p) {
    if (p == nullptr) return;
    if (!is_live(p)) {
        bad_frees++;
        return;
    }
    THead* h = head_of(p);
    int i = h->index;
    live[i] = live[--live_count];
    if (i < live_count) head_of(live[i])->index = i;
    h->magic = 0;
    free(h);
}

void* t_realloc(void* p, size_t n) {
    if (p == nullptr) return t_malloc(n);
    if (!is_live(p)) {
        bad_frees++;
        return nullptr;
    }
    if (should_fail()) return nullptr;
    THead* h = (THead*)realloc(head_of(p), sizeof(THead) + n);
    if (h == nullptr) return nullptr;
    void* q = (char*)h + sizeof(THead);
    live[h->index] = q;
    alloc_calls++;
    return q;
}
// ---- End of the tracker.

struct Node {
    char* key;
    int value;
    Node* next;
};

struct HashMap {
    Node** buckets;
    int nbuckets;
    int size;
};

static uint32_t fnv1a(const char* s) {
    uint32_t h = 2166136261u;
    for (; *s != '\0'; s++) {
        h ^= (unsigned char)*s;
        h *= 16777619u;
    }
    return h;
}

static int bucket_of(const char* key, int nbuckets) {
    return (int)(fnv1a(key) & (uint32_t)(nbuckets - 1));
}

static Node** new_buckets(int n) {
    Node** b = (Node**)t_malloc((size_t)n * sizeof *b);
    if (b != nullptr)
        for (int i = 0; i < n; i++) b[i] = nullptr;
    return b;
}

HashMap* hm_create() {
    HashMap* m = (HashMap*)t_malloc(sizeof *m);
    if (m == nullptr) return nullptr;
    m->buckets = new_buckets(8);
    if (m->buckets == nullptr) {
        t_free(m);
        return nullptr;
    }
    m->nbuckets = 8;
    m->size = 0;
    return m;
}

static Node* find(const HashMap* m, const char* key) {
    for (Node* n = m->buckets[bucket_of(key, m->nbuckets)]; n != nullptr; n = n->next)
        if (strcmp(n->key, key) == 0) return n;
    return nullptr;
}

// Doubles the bucket array and moves every node; false (and no change) if memory runs out.
static bool grow(HashMap* m) {
    int bigger = m->nbuckets * 2;
    Node** nb = new_buckets(bigger);
    if (nb == nullptr) return false;
    for (int i = 0; i < m->nbuckets; i++) {
        Node* n = m->buckets[i];
        while (n != nullptr) {
            Node* next = n->next;
            int b = bucket_of(n->key, bigger);   // the new size decides the bucket
            n->next = nb[b];
            nb[b] = n;
            n = next;
        }
    }
    t_free(m->buckets);
    m->buckets = nb;
    m->nbuckets = bigger;
    return true;
}

bool hm_put(HashMap* m, const char* key, int value) {
    Node* found = find(m, key);
    if (found != nullptr) {
        found->value = value;
        return true;
    }
    if ((m->size + 1) * 4 > m->nbuckets * 3 && !grow(m)) return false;
    size_t len = strlen(key);
    char* copy = (char*)t_malloc(len + 1);
    if (copy == nullptr) return false;
    Node* n = (Node*)t_malloc(sizeof *n);
    if (n == nullptr) {
        t_free(copy);
        return false;
    }
    memcpy(copy, key, len + 1);
    int b = bucket_of(key, m->nbuckets);
    n->key = copy;
    n->value = value;
    n->next = m->buckets[b];
    m->buckets[b] = n;
    m->size++;
    return true;
}

bool hm_get(const HashMap* m, const char* key, int* out) {
    Node* n = find(m, key);
    if (n == nullptr) return false;
    *out = n->value;
    return true;
}

bool hm_erase(HashMap* m, const char* key) {
    Node** link = &m->buckets[bucket_of(key, m->nbuckets)];
    while (*link != nullptr) {
        Node* n = *link;
        if (strcmp(n->key, key) == 0) {
            *link = n->next;
            t_free(n->key);
            t_free(n);
            m->size--;
            return true;
        }
        link = &n->next;
    }
    return false;
}

void hm_destroy(HashMap* m) {
    if (m == nullptr) return;
    for (int i = 0; i < m->nbuckets; i++) {
        Node* n = m->buckets[i];
        while (n != nullptr) {
            Node* next = n->next;
            t_free(n->key);
            t_free(n);
            n = next;
        }
    }
    t_free(m->buckets);
    t_free(m);
}
--- hint
Start with three helpers: `fnv1a`, a `bucket_of(key, nbuckets)` that masks the hash, and a `find` that walks one chain comparing keys with `strcmp`. `hm_get`, and the first step of `hm_put`, are then one call each.
--- hint
In `hm_put`, handle an existing key first (update, no allocation). For a new key: grow if the rule says so, then allocate the key copy, then the node; if the node fails, free the copy. Only after both succeed link the node in and increase `size`, so a failure changes nothing.
--- hint
To grow, allocate the new bucket array (all null) first; if that fails, return `false` with the old table intact. Then move each node, saving its `next` before relinking it, into the bucket computed with the **new** count, and free the old array. `hm_erase` uses the `Node**` walk from the lesson.
--- check test | Put and get, and absent keys
[] { HashMap* m = hm_create(); if (!m) return false; bool ok = hm_put(m, "batt.voltage", 28) && hm_put(m, "batt.temp", -5); int v = 0, w = 0, x = 7; bool got = hm_get(m, "batt.voltage", &v) && hm_get(m, "batt.temp", &w) && !hm_get(m, "batt", &x) && !hm_get(m, "batt.temp2", &x); bool sized = m->size == 2 && m->nbuckets == 8; hm_destroy(m); return ok && got && sized && v == 28 && w == -5 && x == 7; }()
--- check test | Putting an existing key updates it without adding
[] { HashMap* m = hm_create(); hm_put(m, "mode", 1); hm_put(m, "mode", 2); int v = 0; bool ok = hm_get(m, "mode", &v) && v == 2 && m->size == 1 && live_count == 4; hm_destroy(m); return ok; }()

--- check test | Erase removes a key and frees its memory
[] { HashMap* m = hm_create(); hm_put(m, "a", 1); hm_put(m, "b", 2); hm_put(m, "c", 3); int before = live_count; bool e = hm_erase(m, "b") && !hm_erase(m, "b") && !hm_erase(m, "d"); int v = 0; bool ok = e && !hm_get(m, "b", &v) && hm_get(m, "a", &v) && v == 1 && hm_get(m, "c", &v) && v == 3 && m->size == 2 && live_count == before - 2; hm_destroy(m); return ok; }()
--- check test | Grows from 8 to 16 buckets at the 7th key, and keeps every key
[] { HashMap* m = hm_create(); char k[8]; for (int i = 0; i < 6; i++) { snprintf(k, sizeof k, "k%d", i); hm_put(m, k, i); } int six = m->nbuckets; hm_put(m, "k6", 6); int seven = m->nbuckets; bool all = true; for (int i = 0; i < 7; i++) { snprintf(k, sizeof k, "k%d", i); int v = -1; if (!hm_get(m, k, &v) || v != i) all = false; } hm_destroy(m); return six == 8 && seven == 16 && all; }()
--- check test | 60000 keys: 131072 buckets, every key found, then all erased
[] { HashMap* m = hm_create(); char k[16]; for (int i = 0; i < 60000; i++) { snprintf(k, sizeof k, "p%d", i * 7); if (!hm_put(m, k, i)) return false; } if (m->size != 60000 || m->nbuckets != 131072) return false; for (int i = 0; i < 60000; i++) { snprintf(k, sizeof k, "p%d", i * 7); int v = -1; if (!hm_get(m, k, &v) || v != i) return false; } for (int i = 0; i < 60000; i += 2) { snprintf(k, sizeof k, "p%d", i * 7); if (!hm_erase(m, k)) return false; } int v; snprintf(k, sizeof k, "p%d", 14); bool gone = !hm_get(m, k, &v); snprintf(k, sizeof k, "p%d", 7); bool kept = hm_get(m, k, &v) && v == 1; bool sized = m->size == 30000; hm_destroy(m); return gone && kept && sized && live_count == 0 && bad_frees == 0; }()
--- check test | Destroy frees everything, and nullptr is allowed
[] { HashMap* m = hm_create(); for (int i = 0; i < 100; i++) { char k[8]; snprintf(k, sizeof k, "x%d", i); hm_put(m, k, i); } hm_destroy(m); hm_destroy(nullptr); return live_count == 0 && bad_frees == 0; }()

[] { fail_after = 1; HashMap* m = hm_create(); fail_after = -1; bool first = m == nullptr && live_count == 0; fail_after = 0; HashMap* n = hm_create(); fail_after = -1; return first && n == nullptr && live_count == 0; }()
--- check test | A failed put leaves the map exactly as it was
[] { HashMap* m = hm_create(); hm_put(m, "a", 1); int held = live_count; fail_after = 0; bool r1 = hm_put(m, "b", 2); fail_after = 1; bool r2 = hm_put(m, "c", 3); fail_after = -1; int v = 0; bool ok = !r1 && !r2 && live_count == held && m->size == 1 && !hm_get(m, "b", &v) && !hm_get(m, "c", &v) && hm_get(m, "a", &v) && v == 1; hm_destroy(m); return ok && live_count == 0; }()
--- check test | A failed grow leaves the map exactly as it was
