+++ practice | Insert and erase in a generic array
--- task
The starter holds the lesson's generic `Vec`: `vec_init`, `vec_at`, `vec_push` and `vec_free`. Add two functions to it. No `main`.

- `bool vec_insert(Vec* v, size_t i, const void* elem)`: insert a copy of the element at position `i` (0 ≤ i ≤ len), moving the elements from `i` onward one place up. `i` equal to `len` appends. Grow the same way `vec_push` does. Return `false` if `i` is greater than `len` or memory runs out, changing nothing.
- `bool vec_erase(Vec* v, size_t i, void* removed)`: remove the element at position `i` (i < len), moving the later elements one place down. If `removed` is not null, copy the removed element's bytes there first. Return `false`, changing nothing, if `i` is not below `len`.

The moved ranges overlap, so move them with `memmove`, which, unlike `memcpy`, is correct when source and destination overlap.
--- starter
#include <cstdlib>
#include <cstring>

struct Vec {
    void* data;
    size_t len;
    size_t cap;
    size_t elem_size;
};

void vec_init(Vec* v, size_t elem_size) {
    v->data = nullptr;
    v->len = 0;
    v->cap = 0;
    v->elem_size = elem_size;
}

void* vec_at(const Vec* v, size_t i) {
    return (char*)v->data + i * v->elem_size;
}

bool vec_push(Vec* v, const void* elem) {
    if (v->len == v->cap) {
        size_t new_cap = v->cap == 0 ? 4 : v->cap * 2;
        void* bigger = realloc(v->data, new_cap * v->elem_size);
        if (bigger == nullptr) return false;
        v->data = bigger;
        v->cap = new_cap;
    }
    memcpy(vec_at(v, v->len), elem, v->elem_size);
    v->len++;
    return true;
}

void vec_free(Vec* v) {
    free(v->data);
    vec_init(v, v->elem_size);
}

bool vec_insert(Vec* v, size_t i, const void* elem) {
    return false;
}

bool vec_erase(Vec* v, size_t i, void* removed) {
    return false;
}
--- solution
#include <cstdlib>
#include <cstring>

struct Vec {
    void* data;
    size_t len;
    size_t cap;
    size_t elem_size;
};

void vec_init(Vec* v, size_t elem_size) {
    v->data = nullptr;
    v->len = 0;
    v->cap = 0;
    v->elem_size = elem_size;
}

void* vec_at(const Vec* v, size_t i) {
    return (char*)v->data + i * v->elem_size;
}

static bool reserve_one(Vec* v) {
    if (v->len < v->cap) return true;
    size_t new_cap = v->cap == 0 ? 4 : v->cap * 2;
    void* bigger = realloc(v->data, new_cap * v->elem_size);
    if (bigger == nullptr) return false;
    v->data = bigger;
    v->cap = new_cap;
    return true;
}

bool vec_push(Vec* v, const void* elem) {
    if (!reserve_one(v)) return false;
    memcpy(vec_at(v, v->len), elem, v->elem_size);
    v->len++;
    return true;
}

void vec_free(Vec* v) {
    free(v->data);
    vec_init(v, v->elem_size);
}

bool vec_insert(Vec* v, size_t i, const void* elem) {
    if (i > v->len) return false;
    if (!reserve_one(v)) return false;
    // shift elements i .. len-1 up by one element
    memmove(vec_at(v, i + 1), vec_at(v, i), (v->len - i) * v->elem_size);
    memcpy(vec_at(v, i), elem, v->elem_size);
    v->len++;
    return true;
}

bool vec_erase(Vec* v, size_t i, void* removed) {
    if (i >= v->len) return false;
    if (removed != nullptr) memcpy(removed, vec_at(v, i), v->elem_size);
    // shift elements i+1 .. len-1 down by one element
    memmove(vec_at(v, i), vec_at(v, i + 1), (v->len - i - 1) * v->elem_size);
    v->len--;
    return true;
}
--- hint
Check the position first. For insert, make room exactly as `vec_push` does (a small helper that grows when `len == cap` lets both use it), then shift the tail up and copy the element into the gap.
--- hint
The tail from `i` has `len - i` elements, so it is `(len - i) * elem_size` bytes, moved from `vec_at(v, i)` to `vec_at(v, i + 1)`. Erase moves `len - i - 1` elements the other way, after copying the removed one out.
--- check test | Insert at the front, the middle and the end
[] { Vec v; vec_init(&v, sizeof(int)); int a = 1, b = 2, c = 3, d = 4; vec_push(&v, &b); vec_push(&v, &d); bool ok = vec_insert(&v, 0, &a) && vec_insert(&v, 2, &c) && vec_insert(&v, 4, &d); int* p = (int*)v.data; bool r = ok && v.len == 5 && p[0] == 1 && p[1] == 2 && p[2] == 3 && p[3] == 4 && p[4] == 4; vec_free(&v); return r; }()
--- check test | Insert past the end is refused
[] { Vec v; vec_init(&v, sizeof(int)); int a = 9; bool r = !vec_insert(&v, 1, &a) && v.len == 0 && vec_insert(&v, 0, &a) && v.len == 1; vec_free(&v); return r; }()
--- check test | Erase returns the removed element and closes the gap
[] { Vec v; vec_init(&v, sizeof(int)); for (int i = 0; i < 5; i++) vec_push(&v, &i); int out = -1; bool ok = vec_erase(&v, 1, &out) && out == 1 && vec_erase(&v, 3, nullptr) && vec_erase(&v, 0, &out) && out == 0; int* p = (int*)v.data; bool r = ok && v.len == 2 && p[0] == 2 && p[1] == 3; vec_free(&v); return r; }()
--- check test | Erase out of range changes nothing
[] { Vec v; vec_init(&v, sizeof(int)); int x = 5; vec_push(&v, &x); int out = 77; bool r = !vec_erase(&v, 1, &out) && out == 77 && v.len == 1; vec_free(&v); Vec e; vec_init(&e, sizeof(int)); bool r2 = !vec_erase(&e, 0, &out); return r && r2; }()
--- check test | 24-byte structs, inserted in the middle 1000 times
[] { struct R { double a; double b; int id; }; Vec v; vec_init(&v, sizeof(R)); for (int i = 0; i < 1000; i++) { R r = {i * 1.0, i * 2.0, i}; if (!vec_insert(&v, v.len / 2, &r)) return false; } bool ok = v.len == 1000; for (size_t i = 0; i < v.len; i++) { R* r = (R*)vec_at(&v, i); if (r->b != r->id * 2.0) ok = false; } R* mid = (R*)vec_at(&v, 500); R* first = (R*)vec_at(&v, 0); R* last = (R*)vec_at(&v, 999); bool r = ok && mid->id == 998 && first->id == 1 && last->id == 0; vec_free(&v); return r; }()
--- check test | Insert then erase at every position gives the same array back
[] { Vec v; vec_init(&v, sizeof(short)); for (short i = 0; i < 50; i++) vec_push(&v, &i); short x = -1; for (size_t i = 0; i <= 50; i++) { if (!vec_insert(&v, i, &x)) return false; short out = 0; if (!vec_erase(&v, i, &out) || out != -1) return false; } bool ok = v.len == 50; for (short i = 0; i < 50; i++) if (*(short*)vec_at(&v, i) != i) ok = false; vec_free(&v); return ok; }()

+++ practice | A set of strings with open addressing
--- task
Build a set of strings that stores its keys directly in one array of slots, with **linear probing**, the open addressing of the lesson's notes. No `main`.

```cpp fragment
struct StrSet {
    char** slots;   // cap slots; nullptr means empty
    int cap;        // a power of two
    int count;
};
```

Include the struct exactly as shown, and write:

- `bool set_init(StrSet* s, int cap)`: `cap` empty slots, from `malloc`; `cap` is a power of two. Return `false` if memory runs out.
- `int set_add(StrSet* s, const char* key)`: if the key is already in the set, return 0. If the set is full (`count == cap`), return -1. Otherwise store a copy of the key and return 1.
- `bool set_has(const StrSet* s, const char* key)`: whether the key is in the set.
- `void set_free(StrSet* s)`: free every copy and the slots.

A key's home slot is its FNV-1a hash (as in the lesson) `& (cap - 1)`. To add or find a key, look at its home slot, then the next, and so on, wrapping from the last slot to slot 0, until you find the key or an empty slot. The table never grows.
--- starter
#include <cstdint>
#include <cstdio>
#include <cstdlib>
#include <cstring>

struct StrSet {
    char** slots;
    int cap;
    int count;
};

bool set_init(StrSet* s, int cap) {
    return false;
}

int set_add(StrSet* s, const char* key) {
    return -1;
}

bool set_has(const StrSet* s, const char* key) {
    return false;
}

void set_free(StrSet* s) {
}
--- solution
#include <cstdint>
#include <cstdio>
#include <cstdlib>
#include <cstring>

struct StrSet {
    char** slots;
    int cap;
    int count;
};

static uint32_t fnv1a(const char* s) {
    uint32_t h = 2166136261u;
    for (; *s != '\0'; s++) {
        h ^= (unsigned char)*s;
        h *= 16777619u;
    }
    return h;
}

bool set_init(StrSet* s, int cap) {
    s->slots = (char**)malloc((size_t)cap * sizeof *s->slots);
    if (s->slots == nullptr) return false;
    for (int i = 0; i < cap; i++) s->slots[i] = nullptr;
    s->cap = cap;
    s->count = 0;
    return true;
}

// The slot holding key, or the first empty slot on its probe path, or -1 if neither exists.
static int probe(const StrSet* s, const char* key) {
    int i = (int)(fnv1a(key) & (uint32_t)(s->cap - 1));
    for (int steps = 0; steps < s->cap; steps++) {
        if (s->slots[i] == nullptr || strcmp(s->slots[i], key) == 0) return i;
        i = (i + 1) & (s->cap - 1);
    }
    return -1;   // every slot is full and none holds the key
}

int set_add(StrSet* s, const char* key) {
    int i = probe(s, key);
    if (i >= 0 && s->slots[i] != nullptr) return 0;
    if (i < 0) return -1;
    size_t len = strlen(key);
    char* copy = (char*)malloc(len + 1);
    if (copy == nullptr) return -1;
    memcpy(copy, key, len + 1);
    s->slots[i] = copy;
    s->count++;
    return 1;
}

bool set_has(const StrSet* s, const char* key) {
    int i = probe(s, key);
    return i >= 0 && s->slots[i] != nullptr;
}

void set_free(StrSet* s) {
    for (int i = 0; i < s->cap; i++) free(s->slots[i]);
    free(s->slots);
    s->slots = nullptr;
    s->cap = 0;
    s->count = 0;
}
--- hint
Write one `probe` helper that both functions use: start at the home slot and step forward, wrapping with `& (cap - 1)`, until the slot is empty or holds the key. Give up after `cap` steps, which only happens when the table is full and the key is not in it.
--- hint
`set_add`: if the probe found the key, return 0; if it found nothing at all, the set is full, return -1; otherwise copy the key into the empty slot it found. `set_has` is true only when the probe stopped on the key itself.
--- check test | Adds, duplicates and lookups
[] { StrSet s; if (!set_init(&s, 8)) return false; bool a = set_add(&s, "lox") == 1 && set_add(&s, "rp1") == 1 && set_add(&s, "lox") == 0; bool b = set_has(&s, "lox") && set_has(&s, "rp1") && !set_has(&s, "lh2") && s.count == 2; set_free(&s); return a && b; }()
--- check test | A full set refuses new keys but still knows its own
[] { StrSet s; set_init(&s, 4); char k[8]; for (int i = 0; i < 4; i++) { snprintf(k, sizeof k, "n%d", i); if (set_add(&s, k) != 1) return false; } bool r = set_add(&s, "extra") == -1 && set_add(&s, "n2") == 0 && set_has(&s, "n3") && !set_has(&s, "extra") && s.count == 4; set_free(&s); return r; }()
--- check test | Keys are copied
[] { StrSet s; set_init(&s, 16); char buf[16] = "orbit"; set_add(&s, buf); strcpy(buf, "deorbit"); bool r = set_has(&s, "orbit") && !set_has(&s, "deorbit"); set_free(&s); return r; }()
--- check test | A set of size 1
[] { StrSet s; set_init(&s, 1); bool r = set_add(&s, "only") == 1 && set_add(&s, "only") == 0 && set_add(&s, "other") == -1 && set_has(&s, "only") && !set_has(&s, "other"); set_free(&s); return r; }()
--- check test | 3000 keys in 4096 slots, with many collisions
[] { StrSet s; set_init(&s, 4096); char k[16]; for (int i = 0; i < 3000; i++) { snprintf(k, sizeof k, "tlm%d", i); if (set_add(&s, k) != 1) return false; } for (int i = 0; i < 3000; i++) { snprintf(k, sizeof k, "tlm%d", i); if (!set_has(&s, k) || set_add(&s, k) != 0) return false; } bool none = true; for (int i = 3000; i < 4000; i++) { snprintf(k, sizeof k, "tlm%d", i); if (set_has(&s, k)) none = false; } bool r = none && s.count == 3000; set_free(&s); return r; }()

+++ practice | The most frequent words
--- task
Mission controllers want the words that occur most often in a day's operator notes. The starter gives you a small counting table, `WordTable`, with `wt_init` and `wt_add`, which adds 1 to a word's count; its `entries` array holds `used` entries, each a word and its count. Leave that code as it is. Write one function. No `main`.

`int top_words(const char* text, int k, char words[][32], int* counts)`:

1. Split `text` into **words**: runs of letters `a` to `z` and `A` to `Z`. Every other character separates words. Convert each word to lower case before counting it. Words are at most 31 letters.
2. Count them with a `WordTable`.
3. Sort the distinct words with the library `qsort` and a comparator you write: by count, highest first, and words with equal counts in `strcmp` order.
4. Copy the first `k` words (or all of them, if there are fewer) into `words`, with their counts in `counts`, and return how many you copied.

For example, `"Go for launch. GO! go, for"` gives `go` 3 and `for` 2, then `launch` 1.
--- starter
#include <cstdlib>
#include <cstring>

// ---- A counting table. Do not change it.
struct WordCount {
    char word[32];
    int count;
};

struct WordTable {
    WordCount entries[2048];
    int used;
};

void wt_init(WordTable* t) {
    t->used = 0;
}

// Adds 1 to the count of `word` (at most 31 characters); a new word starts at 1.
void wt_add(WordTable* t, const char* word) {
    for (int i = 0; i < t->used; i++) {
        if (strcmp(t->entries[i].word, word) == 0) {
            t->entries[i].count++;
            return;
        }
    }
    if (t->used == 2048) return;
    strcpy(t->entries[t->used].word, word);
    t->entries[t->used].count = 1;
    t->used++;
}
// ---- End of the counting table.

int top_words(const char* text, int k, char words[][32], int* counts) {
    return 0;
}
--- solution
#include <cstdlib>
#include <cstring>

// ---- A counting table. Do not change it.
struct WordCount {
    char word[32];
    int count;
};

struct WordTable {
    WordCount entries[2048];
    int used;
};

void wt_init(WordTable* t) {
    t->used = 0;
}

// Adds 1 to the count of `word` (at most 31 characters); a new word starts at 1.
void wt_add(WordTable* t, const char* word) {
    for (int i = 0; i < t->used; i++) {
        if (strcmp(t->entries[i].word, word) == 0) {
            t->entries[i].count++;
            return;
        }
    }
    if (t->used == 2048) return;
    strcpy(t->entries[t->used].word, word);
    t->entries[t->used].count = 1;
    t->used++;
}
// ---- End of the counting table.

static bool is_letter(char c) {
    return (c >= 'a' && c <= 'z') || (c >= 'A' && c <= 'Z');
}

static int by_count_then_word(const void* a, const void* b) {
    const WordCount* x = (const WordCount*)a;
    const WordCount* y = (const WordCount*)b;
    if (x->count != y->count) return x->count > y->count ? -1 : 1;
    return strcmp(x->word, y->word);
}

int top_words(const char* text, int k, char words[][32], int* counts) {
    static WordTable table;   // about 72 KB: too big to put on a small stack
    wt_init(&table);
    char word[32];
    int len = 0;
    for (const char* p = text;; p++) {
        if (is_letter(*p)) {
            if (len < 31) word[len++] = (char)(*p >= 'A' && *p <= 'Z' ? *p - 'A' + 'a' : *p);
        } else {
            if (len > 0) {
                word[len] = '\0';
                wt_add(&table, word);
                len = 0;
            }
            if (*p == '\0') break;
        }
    }
    qsort(table.entries, table.used, sizeof table.entries[0], by_count_then_word);
    int n = table.used < k ? table.used : k;
    for (int i = 0; i < n; i++) {
        strcpy(words[i], table.entries[i].word);
        counts[i] = table.entries[i].count;
    }
    return n;
}
--- hint
Walk the text one character at a time, collecting letters (lower-cased) into a small buffer. At every non-letter, and at the terminating zero, if the buffer holds a word, end it with a zero and pass it to `wt_add`.
--- hint
The comparator gets two `WordCount*`s. Compare the counts first, returning a negative number when the first count is bigger, so the biggest come first; when the counts are equal, return `strcmp` of the words.
--- hint
Sort `table.entries` itself, `table.used` elements of `sizeof(WordCount)` bytes each, then copy out the first `k`. Make the table `static`: it is too big for a small stack.
--- check test | The example
[] { char w[5][32]; int c[5]; int n = top_words("Go for launch. GO! go, for", 5, w, c); return n == 3 && std::string(w[0]) == "go" && c[0] == 3 && std::string(w[1]) == "for" && c[1] == 2 && std::string(w[2]) == "launch" && c[2] == 1; }()
--- check test | Ties in strcmp order, and k smaller than the number of words
[] { char w[2][32]; int c[2]; int n = top_words("delta alpha charlie bravo alpha delta", 2, w, c); return n == 2 && std::string(w[0]) == "alpha" && c[0] == 2 && std::string(w[1]) == "delta" && c[1] == 2; }()
--- check test | No words at all
[] { char w[3][32]; int c[3]; return top_words("", 3, w, c) == 0 && top_words("123 -- 4.5 !!", 3, w, c) == 0; }()
--- check test | Digits and punctuation split words, and case is ignored
[] { char w[4][32]; int c[4]; int n = top_words("T+10s: ABORT? abort...Abort2abort", 4, w, c); return n == 3 && std::string(w[0]) == "abort" && c[0] == 4 && std::string(w[1]) == "s" && std::string(w[2]) == "t"; }()
--- check test | A long text of 20000 words
[] { static char text[200000]; int p = 0; const char* names[4] = {"nominal", "warning", "fault", "nominal"}; for (int i = 0; i < 20000; i++) { const char* s = names[i % 4]; if (i % 10 == 9) s = "safe"; for (int j = 0; s[j]; j++) text[p++] = s[j]; text[p++] = ' '; } text[p] = '\0'; char w[4][32]; int c[4]; int n = top_words(text, 4, w, c); return n == 4 && std::string(w[0]) == "nominal" && c[0] == 9000 && std::string(w[1]) == "fault" && c[1] == 5000 && std::string(w[2]) == "warning" && c[2] == 4000 && std::string(w[3]) == "safe" && c[3] == 2000; }()

+++ practice | A ring-buffer queue that grows
--- task
Build a first-in, first-out queue of elements of any size, stored in a **ring buffer**: an array in which the front of the queue can be anywhere, and the elements wrap round from the last slot to slot 0. No `main`.

```cpp fragment
struct Queue {
    void* data;       // cap slots of elem_size bytes
    size_t cap;
    size_t head;      // the slot of the front element
    size_t len;       // how many elements are queued
    size_t elem_size;
};
```

Include the struct exactly as shown, and write:

- `void q_init(Queue* q, size_t elem_size)`: an empty queue with no memory yet (`data` null, `cap` 0).
- `bool q_push(Queue* q, const void* elem)`: add a copy of the element at the back. When the queue is full, grow it first: capacity 4 if it was 0, otherwise double, into a **new** block from `malloc`, copying the queued elements into it **in queue order**, starting at slot 0, then freeing the old block. Return `false` if memory runs out, changing nothing.
- `bool q_pop(Queue* q, void* out)`: copy the front element into `out`, remove it, and return `true`; return `false` if the queue is empty.
- `void q_free(Queue* q)`: free the memory and make the queue empty.

The back of the queue is slot `(head + len) % cap`. After a pop, the front moves on one slot, wrapping round.
--- starter
#include <cstdlib>
#include <cstring>

struct Queue {
    void* data;
    size_t cap;
    size_t head;
    size_t len;
    size_t elem_size;
};

void q_init(Queue* q, size_t elem_size) {
    q->data = nullptr;
    q->cap = 0;
    q->head = 0;
    q->len = 0;
    q->elem_size = elem_size;
}

bool q_push(Queue* q, const void* elem) {
    if (q->len == q->cap) {
        size_t new_cap = q->cap == 0 ? 4 : q->cap * 2;
        void* bigger = realloc(q->data, new_cap * q->elem_size);
        if (bigger == nullptr) return false;
        q->data = bigger;
        q->cap = new_cap;
    }
    size_t back = (q->head + q->len) % q->cap;
    memcpy((char*)q->data + back * q->elem_size, elem, q->elem_size);
    q->len++;
    return true;
}

bool q_pop(Queue* q, void* out) {
    if (q->len == 0) return false;
    memcpy(out, (char*)q->data + q->head * q->elem_size, q->elem_size);
    q->head = (q->head + 1) % q->cap;
    q->len--;
    return true;
}

void q_free(Queue* q) {
    free(q->data);
    q_init(q, q->elem_size);
}
--- solution
#include <cstdlib>
#include <cstring>

struct Queue {
    void* data;
    size_t cap;
    size_t head;
    size_t len;
    size_t elem_size;
};

void q_init(Queue* q, size_t elem_size) {
    q->data = nullptr;
    q->cap = 0;
    q->head = 0;
    q->len = 0;
    q->elem_size = elem_size;
}

static void* slot(const Queue* q, size_t i) {
    return (char*)q->data + i * q->elem_size;
}

bool q_push(Queue* q, const void* elem) {
    if (q->len == q->cap) {
        size_t new_cap = q->cap == 0 ? 4 : q->cap * 2;
        char* bigger = (char*)malloc(new_cap * q->elem_size);
        if (bigger == nullptr) return false;
        // copy in queue order: the front element goes to slot 0
        for (size_t i = 0; i < q->len; i++)
            memcpy(bigger + i * q->elem_size, slot(q, (q->head + i) % q->cap), q->elem_size);
        free(q->data);
        q->data = bigger;
        q->cap = new_cap;
        q->head = 0;
    }
    memcpy(slot(q, (q->head + q->len) % q->cap), elem, q->elem_size);
    q->len++;
    return true;
}

bool q_pop(Queue* q, void* out) {
    if (q->len == 0) return false;
    memcpy(out, slot(q, q->head), q->elem_size);
    q->head = (q->head + 1) % q->cap;
    q->len--;
    return true;
}

void q_free(Queue* q) {
    free(q->data);
    q_init(q, q->elem_size);
}
--- hint
The starter grows with `realloc`, which keeps the bytes where they were. If the queue had wrapped round, its front is in the middle of the block and its back at the start, and the new slots at the end break the order. Try pushing 4, popping 2, pushing 3 by hand.
--- hint
Grow into a new block instead: element `i` of the queue (0 is the front) is in slot `(head + i) % cap` of the old block; copy it to slot `i` of the new one. Then free the old block and set `head` to 0.
--- check test | First in, first out
[] { Queue q; q_init(&q, sizeof(int)); for (int i = 1; i <= 3; i++) q_push(&q, &i); int a = 0, b = 0, c = 0, d = 99; bool ok = q_pop(&q, &a) && q_pop(&q, &b) && q_pop(&q, &c) && !q_pop(&q, &d); q_free(&q); return ok && a == 1 && b == 2 && c == 3 && d == 99; }()
--- check test | Popping an empty queue, new or used
[] { Queue q; q_init(&q, sizeof(int)); int x = 5; bool a = !q_pop(&q, &x) && x == 5; q_push(&q, &x); q_pop(&q, &x); bool b = !q_pop(&q, &x) && q.len == 0; q_free(&q); return a && b; }()
--- check test | Growing while wrapped keeps the order
[] { Queue q; q_init(&q, sizeof(int)); for (int i = 0; i < 4; i++) q_push(&q, &i); int x; q_pop(&q, &x); q_pop(&q, &x); for (int i = 4; i < 9; i++) q_push(&q, &i); bool ok = q.len == 7; for (int want = 2; want < 9; want++) { if (!q_pop(&q, &x) || x != want) ok = false; } q_free(&q); return ok; }()
--- check test | Single bytes and 24-byte elements
[] { Queue a; q_init(&a, 1); const char* s = "telemetry"; for (int i = 0; s[i]; i++) q_push(&a, &s[i]); char out[16] = {}; int n = 0; while (q_pop(&a, &out[n])) n++; q_free(&a); struct E { double t; double v; int id; }; Queue b; q_init(&b, sizeof(E)); for (int i = 0; i < 10; i++) { E e = {i * 1.0, i * 0.5, i}; q_push(&b, &e); } E e; q_pop(&b, &e); bool ok = e.id == 0 && b.len == 9; q_free(&b); return std::string(out) == "telemetry" && n == 9 && ok; }()
--- check test | 100000 pushes and pops, interleaved
[] { Queue q; q_init(&q, sizeof(int)); int next_in = 0, next_out = 0; unsigned x = 3u; for (int step = 0; step < 100000; step++) { x = x * 1664525u + 1013904223u; if ((x >> 16) % 3 != 0 || q.len == 0) { if (!q_push(&q, &next_in)) return false; next_in++; } else { int v; if (!q_pop(&q, &v) || v != next_out) return false; next_out++; } } int v; while (q_pop(&q, &v)) { if (v != next_out) return false; next_out++; } q_free(&q); return next_out == next_in; }()

+++ practice | Debug: lost after the table grew
--- task
This set of strings, a hash table with separate chaining, finds every key while it is small, but after it grows it loses about half of them: `set_has` returns `false` for keys that were added. Find the bug and fix it. No `main`.

The set starts with 4 buckets and doubles them when its count would exceed the number of buckets. The functions are `void cs_init(ChainSet* s)`, `bool cs_add(ChainSet* s, const char* key)` (returns `false` if the key was already there), `bool cs_has(const ChainSet* s, const char* key)`, and `void cs_free(ChainSet* s)`.
--- starter
#include <cstdint>
#include <cstdio>
#include <cstdlib>
#include <cstring>

struct CNode {
    char* key;
    CNode* next;
};

struct ChainSet {
    CNode** buckets;
    int nbuckets;
    int count;
};

static uint32_t fnv1a(const char* s) {
    uint32_t h = 2166136261u;
    for (; *s != '\0'; s++) {
        h ^= (unsigned char)*s;
        h *= 16777619u;
    }
    return h;
}

void cs_init(ChainSet* s) {
    s->nbuckets = 4;
    s->buckets = (CNode**)calloc(4, sizeof(CNode*));
    s->count = 0;
}

bool cs_has(const ChainSet* s, const char* key) {
    int b = (int)(fnv1a(key) & (uint32_t)(s->nbuckets - 1));
    for (CNode* n = s->buckets[b]; n != nullptr; n = n->next)
        if (strcmp(n->key, key) == 0) return true;
    return false;
}

static void grow(ChainSet* s) {
    int bigger = s->nbuckets * 2;
    CNode** nb = (CNode**)calloc(bigger, sizeof(CNode*));
    for (int i = 0; i < s->nbuckets; i++) {
        CNode* n = s->buckets[i];
        while (n != nullptr) {
            CNode* next = n->next;
            int b = (int)(fnv1a(n->key) & (uint32_t)(s->nbuckets - 1));
            n->next = nb[b];
            nb[b] = n;
            n = next;
        }
    }
    free(s->buckets);
    s->buckets = nb;
    s->nbuckets = bigger;
}

bool cs_add(ChainSet* s, const char* key) {
    if (cs_has(s, key)) return false;
    if (s->count + 1 > s->nbuckets) grow(s);
    CNode* n = (CNode*)malloc(sizeof *n);
    n->key = (char*)malloc(strlen(key) + 1);
    strcpy(n->key, key);
    int b = (int)(fnv1a(key) & (uint32_t)(s->nbuckets - 1));
    n->next = s->buckets[b];
    s->buckets[b] = n;
    s->count++;
    return true;
}

void cs_free(ChainSet* s) {
    for (int i = 0; i < s->nbuckets; i++) {
        CNode* n = s->buckets[i];
        while (n != nullptr) {
            CNode* next = n->next;
            free(n->key);
            free(n);
            n = next;
        }
    }
    free(s->buckets);
    s->buckets = nullptr;
    s->nbuckets = 0;
    s->count = 0;
}
--- solution
#include <cstdint>
#include <cstdio>
#include <cstdlib>
#include <cstring>

struct CNode {
    char* key;
    CNode* next;
};

struct ChainSet {
    CNode** buckets;
    int nbuckets;
    int count;
};

static uint32_t fnv1a(const char* s) {
    uint32_t h = 2166136261u;
    for (; *s != '\0'; s++) {
        h ^= (unsigned char)*s;
        h *= 16777619u;
    }
    return h;
}

void cs_init(ChainSet* s) {
    s->nbuckets = 4;
    s->buckets = (CNode**)calloc(4, sizeof(CNode*));
    s->count = 0;
}

bool cs_has(const ChainSet* s, const char* key) {
    int b = (int)(fnv1a(key) & (uint32_t)(s->nbuckets - 1));
    for (CNode* n = s->buckets[b]; n != nullptr; n = n->next)
        if (strcmp(n->key, key) == 0) return true;
    return false;
}

static void grow(ChainSet* s) {
    int bigger = s->nbuckets * 2;
    CNode** nb = (CNode**)calloc(bigger, sizeof(CNode*));
    for (int i = 0; i < s->nbuckets; i++) {
        CNode* n = s->buckets[i];
        while (n != nullptr) {
            CNode* next = n->next;
            int b = (int)(fnv1a(n->key) & (uint32_t)(bigger - 1));   // the new number of buckets
            n->next = nb[b];
            nb[b] = n;
            n = next;
        }
    }
    free(s->buckets);
    s->buckets = nb;
    s->nbuckets = bigger;
}

bool cs_add(ChainSet* s, const char* key) {
    if (cs_has(s, key)) return false;
    if (s->count + 1 > s->nbuckets) grow(s);
    CNode* n = (CNode*)malloc(sizeof *n);
    n->key = (char*)malloc(strlen(key) + 1);
    strcpy(n->key, key);
    int b = (int)(fnv1a(key) & (uint32_t)(s->nbuckets - 1));
    n->next = s->buckets[b];
    s->buckets[b] = n;
    s->count++;
    return true;
}

void cs_free(ChainSet* s) {
    for (int i = 0; i < s->nbuckets; i++) {
        CNode* n = s->buckets[i];
        while (n != nullptr) {
            CNode* next = n->next;
            free(n->key);
            free(n);
            n = next;
        }
    }
    free(s->buckets);
    s->buckets = nullptr;
    s->nbuckets = 0;
    s->count = 0;
}
--- hint
After `grow`, a lookup computes the bucket with the new number of buckets. Which number does `grow` use when it decides where each moved node goes?
--- hint
While moving nodes, `s->nbuckets` still holds the old count; it is only updated at the end. Every node lands in the lower half of the new array, but half of them belong in the upper half. Mask with `bigger - 1`.
--- check test | Small sets work
[] { ChainSet s; cs_init(&s); bool ok = cs_add(&s, "a") && cs_add(&s, "b") && !cs_add(&s, "a") && cs_has(&s, "a") && cs_has(&s, "b") && !cs_has(&s, "c"); cs_free(&s); return ok; }()
--- check test | Every key is still found after growing
[] { ChainSet s; cs_init(&s); char k[16]; for (int i = 0; i < 200; i++) { snprintf(k, sizeof k, "key%d", i); cs_add(&s, k); } bool ok = s.count == 200 && s.nbuckets == 256; for (int i = 0; i < 200; i++) { snprintf(k, sizeof k, "key%d", i); if (!cs_has(&s, k)) ok = false; } cs_free(&s); return ok; }()
--- check test | No duplicates after growing
[] { ChainSet s; cs_init(&s); char k[16]; for (int i = 0; i < 50; i++) { snprintf(k, sizeof k, "dup%d", i); cs_add(&s, k); } bool ok = true; for (int i = 0; i < 50; i++) { snprintf(k, sizeof k, "dup%d", i); if (cs_add(&s, k)) ok = false; } bool r = ok && s.count == 50; cs_free(&s); return r; }()
--- check test | Keys that were never added are still absent
[] { ChainSet s; cs_init(&s); char k[16]; for (int i = 0; i < 100; i++) { snprintf(k, sizeof k, "in%d", i); cs_add(&s, k); } bool ok = true; for (int i = 0; i < 100; i++) { snprintf(k, sizeof k, "out%d", i); if (cs_has(&s, k)) ok = false; } cs_free(&s); return ok; }()

+++ practice | An LRU cache
--- task
A ground station caches the most recently requested telemetry values. When the cache is full and a new key arrives, it evicts the **least recently used** key: the one whose last `get` or `put` was longest ago. Every operation must take O(1) expected time, whatever the capacity: the checks run a million operations on a cache of 20,000 keys, which a cache that scans its entries cannot finish in time. No `main`.

Write:

- `LRU* lru_create(int capacity)`: an empty cache holding at most `capacity` keys (capacity ≥ 1), or `nullptr` if memory runs out. You choose what the struct `LRU` contains; define it yourself.
- `bool lru_get(LRU* c, const char* key, int* out)`: if the key is cached, store its value in `*out`, make it the most recently used, and return `true`; otherwise return `false`.
- `void lru_put(LRU* c, const char* key, int value)`: set the key's value and make it the most recently used. If the key is new and the cache is full, first evict the least recently used key.
- `int lru_size(const LRU* c)`: the number of cached keys.
- `const char* lru_oldest(const LRU* c)`: the least recently used key, or `nullptr` if the cache is empty.
- `void lru_destroy(LRU* c)`: free everything.

Keys are texts of at most 31 characters; copy them. Use `malloc` and `free`. The usual design is a hash table from key to node, plus a doubly linked list of the nodes in order of use, so that moving a node to the front and removing the one at the back take O(1).
--- starter
#include <cstdint>
#include <cstdio>
#include <cstdlib>
#include <cstring>

struct LRU {
    int capacity;
};

LRU* lru_create(int capacity) {
    return nullptr;
}

bool lru_get(LRU* c, const char* key, int* out) {
    return false;
}

void lru_put(LRU* c, const char* key, int value) {
}

int lru_size(const LRU* c) {
    return 0;
}

const char* lru_oldest(const LRU* c) {
    return nullptr;
}

void lru_destroy(LRU* c) {
}
--- solution
#include <cstdint>
#include <cstdio>
#include <cstdlib>
#include <cstring>

struct Entry {
    char key[32];
    int value;
    Entry* prev;   // towards the most recently used
    Entry* next;   // towards the least recently used
    Entry* chain;  // the next entry in the same hash bucket
};

struct LRU {
    int capacity;
    int size;
    int nbuckets;       // a power of two, at least twice the capacity
    Entry** buckets;
    Entry* newest;      // the front of the use list
    Entry* oldest;      // the back
};

static uint32_t fnv1a(const char* s) {
    uint32_t h = 2166136261u;
    for (; *s != '\0'; s++) {
        h ^= (unsigned char)*s;
        h *= 16777619u;
    }
    return h;
}

static Entry** bucket(const LRU* c, const char* key) {
    return &c->buckets[fnv1a(key) & (uint32_t)(c->nbuckets - 1)];
}

LRU* lru_create(int capacity) {
    LRU* c = (LRU*)malloc(sizeof *c);
    if (c == nullptr) return nullptr;
    int nb = 8;
    while (nb < 2 * capacity) nb *= 2;
    c->buckets = (Entry**)calloc(nb, sizeof *c->buckets);
    if (c->buckets == nullptr) {
        free(c);
        return nullptr;
    }
    c->capacity = capacity;
    c->size = 0;
    c->nbuckets = nb;
    c->newest = nullptr;
    c->oldest = nullptr;
    return c;
}

static Entry* find(const LRU* c, const char* key) {
    for (Entry* e = *bucket(c, key); e != nullptr; e = e->chain)
        if (strcmp(e->key, key) == 0) return e;
    return nullptr;
}

static void unlink_entry(LRU* c, Entry* e) {
    if (e->prev) e->prev->next = e->next;
    else c->newest = e->next;
    if (e->next) e->next->prev = e->prev;
    else c->oldest = e->prev;
}

static void push_front(LRU* c, Entry* e) {
    e->prev = nullptr;
    e->next = c->newest;
    if (c->newest) c->newest->prev = e;
    c->newest = e;
    if (c->oldest == nullptr) c->oldest = e;
}

static void evict_oldest(LRU* c) {
    Entry* e = c->oldest;
    unlink_entry(c, e);
    Entry** link = bucket(c, e->key);
    while (*link != e) link = &(*link)->chain;
    *link = e->chain;
    free(e);
    c->size--;
}

bool lru_get(LRU* c, const char* key, int* out) {
    Entry* e = find(c, key);
    if (e == nullptr) return false;
    unlink_entry(c, e);
    push_front(c, e);
    *out = e->value;
    return true;
}

void lru_put(LRU* c, const char* key, int value) {
    Entry* e = find(c, key);
    if (e != nullptr) {
        e->value = value;
        unlink_entry(c, e);
        push_front(c, e);
        return;
    }
    if (c->size == c->capacity) evict_oldest(c);
    e = (Entry*)malloc(sizeof *e);
    if (e == nullptr) return;
    strncpy(e->key, key, 31);
    e->key[31] = '\0';
    e->value = value;
    Entry** b = bucket(c, key);
    e->chain = *b;
    *b = e;
    push_front(c, e);
    c->size++;
}

int lru_size(const LRU* c) {
    return c->size;
}

const char* lru_oldest(const LRU* c) {
    return c->oldest ? c->oldest->key : nullptr;
}

void lru_destroy(LRU* c) {
    if (c == nullptr) return;
    Entry* e = c->newest;
    while (e != nullptr) {
        Entry* next = e->next;
        free(e);
        e = next;
    }
    free(c->buckets);
    free(c);
}
--- hint
Give each entry two sets of links: `prev` and `next` for a doubly linked list in order of use (newest at the front), and `chain` for its hash bucket. The cache keeps pointers to the newest and oldest entries, and a bucket array big enough that chains stay short (at least twice the capacity, a power of two, so it never needs to grow).
--- hint
Write two list helpers: unlink an entry (fixing its neighbours, or the newest/oldest pointers when it is at an end), and push an entry at the front. A `get` or an update is find, unlink, push front.
--- hint
To evict, take the oldest entry, unlink it from the list, and remove it from its bucket's chain with the `Node**` walk from the lesson, then free it. Evict before adding a new key when the cache is full.
--- check test | Put, get, and eviction of the least recently used
[] { LRU* c = lru_create(2); lru_put(c, "a", 1); lru_put(c, "b", 2); int v = 0; bool g = lru_get(c, "a", &v) && v == 1; lru_put(c, "c", 3); bool evicted = !lru_get(c, "b", &v); bool kept = lru_get(c, "a", &v) && v == 1 && lru_get(c, "c", &v) && v == 3; bool sized = lru_size(c) == 2; lru_destroy(c); return g && evicted && kept && sized; }()
--- check test | Updating a key refreshes it and does not grow the cache
[] { LRU* c = lru_create(2); lru_put(c, "x", 1); lru_put(c, "y", 2); lru_put(c, "x", 10); lru_put(c, "z", 3); int v = 0; bool ok = lru_get(c, "x", &v) && v == 10 && !lru_get(c, "y", &v) && lru_size(c) == 2; lru_destroy(c); return ok; }()
--- check test | lru_oldest follows every get and put
[] { LRU* c = lru_create(3); bool e = lru_oldest(c) == nullptr; lru_put(c, "p", 1); lru_put(c, "q", 2); lru_put(c, "r", 3); bool a = strcmp(lru_oldest(c), "p") == 0; int v; lru_get(c, "p", &v); bool b = strcmp(lru_oldest(c), "q") == 0; lru_put(c, "q", 5); bool d = strcmp(lru_oldest(c), "r") == 0; lru_destroy(c); return e && a && b && d; }()
--- check test | A cache of one
[] { LRU* c = lru_create(1); lru_put(c, "one", 1); lru_put(c, "two", 2); int v = 0; bool ok = !lru_get(c, "one", &v) && lru_get(c, "two", &v) && v == 2 && lru_size(c) == 1 && strcmp(lru_oldest(c), "two") == 0; lru_destroy(c); return ok; }()
--- check test | Keys are copied
[] { LRU* c = lru_create(4); char buf[32]; strcpy(buf, "sensor.a"); lru_put(c, buf, 1); strcpy(buf, "sensor.b"); lru_put(c, buf, 2); int v = 0; bool ok = lru_get(c, "sensor.a", &v) && v == 1 && lru_get(c, "sensor.b", &v) && v == 2; lru_destroy(c); return ok; }()
--- check test | A million operations on 20000 keys agree with a slow model on a small cache, and finish in time
[] { LRU* c = lru_create(20000); char k[32]; unsigned x = 17u; long long hits = 0; for (int i = 0; i < 1000000; i++) { x = x * 1664525u + 1013904223u; int key = (int)((x >> 8) % 30000); snprintf(k, sizeof k, "t%d", key); int v; if (lru_get(c, k, &v)) { if (v != key * 3) return false; hits++; } else lru_put(c, k, key * 3); } bool sized = lru_size(c) == 20000; lru_destroy(c); LRU* s = lru_create(3); static int model[3]; int mn = 0; for (int i = 0; i < 20000; i++) { x = x * 1664525u + 1013904223u; int key = (int)((x >> 12) % 5); snprintf(k, sizeof k, "m%d", key); int pos = -1; for (int j = 0; j < mn; j++) if (model[j] == key) pos = j; int v; bool got = lru_get(s, k, &v); if (got != (pos >= 0)) return false; if (pos >= 0) { for (int j = pos; j > 0; j--) model[j] = model[j - 1]; model[0] = key; } else { lru_put(s, k, key); if (mn < 3) mn++; for (int j = mn - 1; j > 0; j--) model[j] = model[j - 1]; model[0] = key; } char want[32]; snprintf(want, sizeof want, "m%d", model[mn - 1]); if (strcmp(lru_oldest(s), want) != 0) return false; } lru_destroy(s); return sized && hits > 0; }()

+++ question | Copies made by doubling
--- ask
A dynamic array starts with capacity 1 and doubles whenever it is full. How many element copies do the growths make in total while 1,000 elements are pushed?
--- answer
1023
--- why
The array grows when it is full at sizes 1, 2, 4, …, 512, copying that many elements each time: 1 + 2 + 4 + … + 512 = 1023. That is less than 2 × 1000, as the amortised argument promises: O(1) copies per push on average.

+++ question | Why the table copies its keys
--- ask
Why does a hash table of strings in C usually copy each key into memory it owns, instead of storing the pointer it was given?
--- choice
Because `strcmp` only works on memory that came from `malloc`.
--- choice
Because copying makes lookups faster.
--- choice correct
Because the caller may later change or free the memory the pointer points at, and the table's key would then change or dangle, breaking the invariant that each key sits in the bucket of its hash.
--- choice
Because the hash function changes the characters of the key.
--- why
The table must be able to trust its keys for as long as they are stored. A key that changed after insertion would be in the chain for its old hash, so lookups for either the old or the new text could fail; a freed one would be read after free. Owning a copy removes both problems, at the cost of one allocation per key.
=== sys-gate | Systems Programming: mastery gate
--- teach
The gate covers the whole course: pointers and strings, the stack and the heap, allocators, bits and byte order, struct layout, function pointers, undefined behaviour, linking, files, error handling and data structures in C. Each problem combines several of these, as real systems code does, and none of them has hints. To get ready, redo the practice problems of the lessons that felt hard, especially the debug problems, and make sure you can explain why each of your solutions has no undefined behaviour and releases everything it acquires.
--- gate
pass 7
questions 9
minutes 132

+++ problem | Fields of a line, split in place
--- task
Split a line of text into fields, in place, without allocating. No `main`.

`int split_fields(char* line, char sep, char** fields, int max)` splits `line` at every `sep` character and stores a pointer to the start of each field in `fields`, in order, returning how many fields there are. Each field must end with a terminating zero, so replace the separators in `line` with zero bytes.

- There is always at least one field: an empty line has one empty field, `"a,,b"` has an empty middle field, and `"a,b,"` has an empty last field.
- A field that **starts** with `"` is quoted: its text runs to the next `"`, may contain `sep`, and does not include the quotes. The character after the closing quote must be `sep` or the end of the line. A `"` anywhere else is an ordinary character.
- Return -1 if a quote is never closed, if something other than `sep` or the end follows a closing quote, or if there are more than `max` fields. After -1, `line` and `fields` may hold anything.

`sep` is never `"` or the zero byte. For example, splitting `id,"Kiruna, SE",42` at `,` gives the three fields `id`, `Kiruna, SE` and `42`.
--- starter
#include <cstring>

int split_fields(char* line, char sep, char** fields, int max) {
    fields[0] = line;
    return 1;
}
--- solution
#include <cstring>

int split_fields(char* line, char sep, char** fields, int max) {
    int n = 0;
    char* p = line;
    while (true) {
        if (n == max) return -1;
        if (*p == '"') {
            char* start = p + 1;
            char* close = strchr(start, '"');
            if (close == nullptr) return -1;
            char after = close[1];
            if (after != sep && after != '\0') return -1;
            fields[n++] = start;
            *close = '\0';
            if (after == '\0') return n;
            p = close + 2;   // past the closing quote and the separator
        } else {
            fields[n++] = p;
            while (*p != sep && *p != '\0') p++;
            if (*p == '\0') return n;
            *p = '\0';
            p++;
        }
    }
}
--- check test | The example with a quoted field
[] { char line[] = "id,\"Kiruna, SE\",42"; char* f[8]; int n = split_fields(line, ',', f, 8); return n == 3 && strcmp(f[0], "id") == 0 && strcmp(f[1], "Kiruna, SE") == 0 && strcmp(f[2], "42") == 0; }()
--- check test | Fields point into the line itself
[] { char line[] = "ab;cd"; char* f[4]; int n = split_fields(line, ';', f, 4); return n == 2 && f[0] == line && f[1] == line + 3 && line[2] == '\0'; }()
--- check test | Empty line, empty fields in the middle and at the end
[] { char a[] = ""; char b[] = "x,,y"; char c[] = "p,q,"; char d[] = ","; char* f[8]; bool ok1 = split_fields(a, ',', f, 8) == 1 && f[0][0] == '\0'; bool ok2 = split_fields(b, ',', f, 8) == 3 && f[1][0] == '\0' && strcmp(f[2], "y") == 0; bool ok3 = split_fields(c, ',', f, 8) == 3 && strcmp(f[1], "q") == 0 && f[2][0] == '\0'; bool ok4 = split_fields(d, ',', f, 8) == 2 && f[0][0] == '\0' && f[1][0] == '\0'; return ok1 && ok2 && ok3 && ok4; }()
--- check test | Quoted fields: empty, last, and holding the separator twice
[] { char a[] = "\"\",\"a|b|c\""; char* f[4]; int n = split_fields(a, ',', f, 4); char b[] = "1|\"x|y|z\"|2"; char* g[4]; int m = split_fields(b, '|', g, 4); return n == 2 && f[0][0] == '\0' && strcmp(f[1], "a|b|c") == 0 && m == 3 && strcmp(g[1], "x|y|z") == 0 && strcmp(g[2], "2") == 0; }()
--- check test | A quote inside an unquoted field is ordinary

+++ problem | Every line of a file, on the heap
--- task
Read a whole text file into memory as an array of lines. The starter begins with the tracker from the malloc lesson: use `t_malloc`, `t_realloc` and `t_free` for all memory, and leave the tracker's code as it is. No `main`.

- `char** read_lines(const char* path, int* count)`: return an array of the file's lines, each a separate block holding the line's text without its `\n`, with a terminating zero, and set `*count` to the number of lines. A last line that does not end in `\n` still counts; an empty file has 0 lines, and then the function still returns a valid (non-null) array. Lines can be of any length.
- `void free_lines(char** lines, int count)`: free every line and the array.

If the file cannot be opened, or any allocation fails, return `nullptr` and set `*count` to -1, with nothing left allocated and the file closed.
--- starter
#include <cstdio>
#include <cstdlib>
#include <cstring>

// ---- The tracker. The checks watch your blocks through it: use t_malloc,
// ---- t_realloc and t_free exactly like malloc, realloc and free. Do not change it.
static void* live[1024];
static int live_count = 0;   // blocks allocated and not yet freed
static int bad_frees = 0;    // frees of pointers that were not live blocks
static int alloc_calls = 0;  // successful allocations and reallocations
static int fail_after = -1;  // -1: never fail; k: k more succeed, then one fails
static size_t last_size = 0; // bytes asked for by the last successful allocation

static bool should_fail() {
    if (fail_after == 0) {
        fail_after = -1;
        return true;
    }
    if (fail_after > 0) fail_after--;
    return false;
}

static int find_live(void* p) {
    for (int i = 0; i < live_count; i++) {
        if (live[i] == p) return i;
    }
    return -1;
}

void* t_malloc(size_t n) {
    if (should_fail() || live_count == 1024) return nullptr;
    void* p = malloc(n == 0 ? 1 : n);
    if (p != nullptr) {
        live[live_count++] = p;
        alloc_calls++;
        last_size = n;
    }
    return p;
}

void t_free(void* p) {
    if (p == nullptr) return;
    int i = find_live(p);
    if (i < 0) {
        bad_frees++;
        return;
    }
    live[i] = live[--live_count];
    free(p);
}

void* t_realloc(void* p, size_t n) {
    if (p == nullptr) return t_malloc(n);
    int i = find_live(p);
    if (i < 0) {
        bad_frees++;
        return nullptr;
    }
    if (should_fail()) return nullptr;
    void* q = realloc(p, n == 0 ? 1 : n);
    if (q != nullptr) {
        live[i] = q;
        alloc_calls++;
        last_size = n;
    }
    return q;
}
// ---- End of the tracker.

char** read_lines(const char* path, int* count) {
    *count = -1;
    return nullptr;
}

void free_lines(char** lines, int count) {
}
--- solution
#include <cstdio>
#include <cstdlib>
#include <cstring>

// ---- The tracker. The checks watch your blocks through it: use t_malloc,
// ---- t_realloc and t_free exactly like malloc, realloc and free. Do not change it.
static void* live[1024];
static int live_count = 0;   // blocks allocated and not yet freed
static int bad_frees = 0;    // frees of pointers that were not live blocks
static int alloc_calls = 0;  // successful allocations and reallocations
static int fail_after = -1;  // -1: never fail; k: k more succeed, then one fails
static size_t last_size = 0; // bytes asked for by the last successful allocation

static bool should_fail() {
    if (fail_after == 0) {
        fail_after = -1;
        return true;
    }
    if (fail_after > 0) fail_after--;
    return false;
}

static int find_live(void* p) {
    for (int i = 0; i < live_count; i++) {
        if (live[i] == p) return i;
    }
    return -1;
}

void* t_malloc(size_t n) {
    if (should_fail() || live_count == 1024) return nullptr;
    void* p = malloc(n == 0 ? 1 : n);
    if (p != nullptr) {
        live[live_count++] = p;
        alloc_calls++;
        last_size = n;
    }
    return p;
}

void t_free(void* p) {
    if (p == nullptr) return;
    int i = find_live(p);
    if (i < 0) {
        bad_frees++;
        return;
    }
    live[i] = live[--live_count];
    free(p);
}

void* t_realloc(void* p, size_t n) {
    if (p == nullptr) return t_malloc(n);
    int i = find_live(p);
    if (i < 0) {
        bad_frees++;
        return nullptr;
    }
    if (should_fail()) return nullptr;
    void* q = realloc(p, n == 0 ? 1 : n);
    if (q != nullptr) {
        live[i] = q;
        alloc_calls++;
        last_size = n;
    }
    return q;
}
// ---- End of the tracker.

void free_lines(char** lines, int count) {
    for (int i = 0; i < count; i++) t_free(lines[i]);
    t_free(lines);
}

// Appends a copy of text[0..len) to the array, growing it; false if memory runs out.
static bool push_line(char*** lines, int* n, int* cap, const char* text, int len) {
    if (*n == *cap) {
        int bigger_cap = *cap * 2;
        char** bigger = (char**)t_realloc(*lines, (size_t)bigger_cap * sizeof *bigger);
        if (bigger == nullptr) return false;
        *lines = bigger;
        *cap = bigger_cap;
    }
    char* s = (char*)t_malloc((size_t)len + 1);
    if (s == nullptr) return false;
    memcpy(s, text, len);
    s[len] = '\0';
    (*lines)[(*n)++] = s;
    return true;
}

char** read_lines(const char* path, int* count) {
    *count = -1;
    FILE* f = fopen(path, "rb");
    if (f == nullptr) return nullptr;
    int n = 0, cap = 4;
    int len = 0, bcap = 64;
    char** lines = (char**)t_malloc((size_t)cap * sizeof *lines);
    char* buf = (char*)t_malloc((size_t)bcap);
    bool ok = lines != nullptr && buf != nullptr;
    int c;
    while (ok && (c = getc(f)) != EOF) {
        if (c == '\n') {
            ok = push_line(&lines, &n, &cap, buf, len);
            len = 0;
            continue;
        }
        if (len == bcap) {
            char* bigger = (char*)t_realloc(buf, (size_t)bcap * 2);
            if (bigger == nullptr) {
                ok = false;
                break;
            }
            buf = bigger;
            bcap *= 2;
        }
        buf[len++] = (char)c;
    }
    if (ok && len > 0) ok = push_line(&lines, &n, &cap, buf, len);
    t_free(buf);
    fclose(f);
    if (!ok) {
        if (lines != nullptr) free_lines(lines, n);
        return nullptr;
    }
    *count = n;
    return lines;
}
--- check test | Three lines, the last without a newline

--- check test | An empty file: a valid array of 0 lines

--- check test | A line of 20000 characters and 300 short ones

--- check test | Every allocation failure in turn leaves nothing behind
[] { FILE* f = fopen("sysgate_lines_e.txt", "w"); for (int i = 0; i < 9; i++) { for (int j = 0; j < 40 * i; j++) fputc('z', f); fputc('\n', f); } fclose(f); int successes = 0; for (int k = 0; k < 60; k++) { fail_after = k; int n = 0; char** L = read_lines("sysgate_lines_e.txt", &n); fail_after = -1; if (L == nullptr) { if (n != -1 || live_count != 0) return false; } else { if (n != 9 || strlen(L[8]) != 320) return false; free_lines(L, n); successes++; } if (live_count != 0 || bad_frees != 0) return false; } return successes > 0; }()

+++ problem | A slab allocator with a bitmap
--- task
Write a slab allocator: it hands out fixed-size slots from one block of memory, and records which slots are taken in a bitmap. No `main`.

```cpp fragment
struct Slab {
    unsigned char* mem;   // nslots * slot_size bytes
    int slot_size;
    int nslots;           // 1 to 256
    uint32_t used[8];     // slot i is taken when bit (i % 32) of used[i / 32] is 1
};
```

Include the struct exactly as shown, with `<cstdint>`, and write:

- `void slab_init(Slab* s, unsigned char* mem, int slot_size, int nslots)`: every slot free.
- `void* slab_alloc(Slab* s)`: take the **lowest-numbered** free slot and return its address, `mem + i * slot_size`; return `nullptr` if every slot is taken.
- `bool slab_free(Slab* s, void* p)`: free the slot starting at `p` and return `true`. Return `false`, changing nothing, if `p` is null, is not inside the slab's memory, is not the start of a slot, or is the start of a slot that is already free (a double free).
- `int slab_used(const Slab* s)`: how many slots are taken.

Compare addresses as integers, through `uintptr_t`, since comparing pointers into different objects with `<` is not defined.
--- starter
#include <cstddef>
#include <cstdint>

struct Slab {
    unsigned char* mem;
    int slot_size;
    int nslots;
    uint32_t used[8];
};

void slab_init(Slab* s, unsigned char* mem, int slot_size, int nslots) {
    s->mem = mem;
    s->slot_size = slot_size;
    s->nslots = nslots;
}

void* slab_alloc(Slab* s) {
    return s->mem;
}

bool slab_free(Slab* s, void* p) {
    return true;
}

int slab_used(const Slab* s) {
    return 0;
}
--- solution
#include <cstddef>
#include <cstdint>

struct Slab {
    unsigned char* mem;
    int slot_size;
    int nslots;
    uint32_t used[8];
};

void slab_init(Slab* s, unsigned char* mem, int slot_size, int nslots) {
    s->mem = mem;
    s->slot_size = slot_size;
    s->nslots = nslots;
    for (int w = 0; w < 8; w++) s->used[w] = 0;
}

static bool taken(const Slab* s, int i) {
    return (s->used[i / 32] >> (i % 32)) & 1u;
}

void* slab_alloc(Slab* s) {
    for (int w = 0; w * 32 < s->nslots; w++) {
        uint32_t free_bits = ~s->used[w];
        if (free_bits == 0) continue;
        int bit = 0;
        while (((free_bits >> bit) & 1u) == 0) bit++;   // lowest free bit of this word
        int i = w * 32 + bit;
        if (i >= s->nslots) return nullptr;             // bits past the last slot are not slots
        s->used[w] |= 1u << bit;
        return s->mem + (size_t)i * s->slot_size;
    }
    return nullptr;
}

bool slab_free(Slab* s, void* p) {
    if (p == nullptr) return false;
    uintptr_t start = (uintptr_t)s->mem;
    uintptr_t at = (uintptr_t)p;
    uintptr_t size = (uintptr_t)s->nslots * (uintptr_t)s->slot_size;
    if (at < start || at >= start + size) return false;
    uintptr_t offset = at - start;
    if (offset % (uintptr_t)s->slot_size != 0) return false;
    int i = (int)(offset / (uintptr_t)s->slot_size);
    if (!taken(s, i)) return false;
    s->used[i / 32] &= ~(1u << (i % 32));
    return true;
}

int slab_used(const Slab* s) {
    int count = 0;
    for (int w = 0; w < 8; w++) {
        uint32_t x = s->used[w];
        while (x != 0) {
            x &= x - 1;
            count++;
        }
    }
    return count;
}
--- check test | Slots are handed out in order

--- check test | A full slab returns nullptr
[] { static unsigned char mem[2 * 8]; Slab s; slab_init(&s, mem, 8, 2); slab_alloc(&s); slab_alloc(&s); return slab_alloc(&s) == nullptr && slab_used(&s) == 2; }()
--- check test | A freed slot is the lowest free one, and is reused
[] { static unsigned char mem[5 * 12]; Slab s; slab_init(&s, mem, 12, 5); for (int i = 0; i < 5; i++) slab_alloc(&s); bool f1 = slab_free(&s, mem + 36) && slab_free(&s, mem + 12); void* a = slab_alloc(&s); void* b = slab_alloc(&s); return f1 && a == mem + 12 && b == mem + 36 && slab_alloc(&s) == nullptr; }()

[] { static unsigned char mem[4 * 16 + 16]; Slab s; slab_init(&s, mem, 16, 4); slab_alloc(&s); slab_alloc(&s); int before = slab_used(&s); bool a = !slab_free(&s, nullptr); bool b = !slab_free(&s, mem + 5); bool c = !slab_free(&s, mem + 64); bool d = !slab_free(&s, mem + 32); bool e = slab_free(&s, mem + 16) && !slab_free(&s, mem + 16); return a && b && c && d && e && before == 2 && slab_used(&s) == 1; }()

[] { static unsigned char mem[33]; Slab s; slab_init(&s, mem, 1, 33); for (int i = 0; i < 33; i++) if (slab_alloc(&s) != mem + i) return false; bool full = slab_alloc(&s) == nullptr && slab_used(&s) == 33; bool fr = slab_free(&s, mem + 32) && slab_alloc(&s) == mem + 32; return full && fr; }()

--- check test | init clears an old bitmap
[] { static unsigned char mem[64]; Slab s; for (int w = 0; w < 8; w++) s.used[w] = 0xFFFFFFFFu; slab_init(&s, mem, 8, 8); return slab_used(&s) == 0 && slab_alloc(&s) == mem; }()

+++ problem | Varints and zigzag
--- task
Many binary formats, such as Protocol Buffers and the DWARF debugging format, store integers as **varints**: small numbers take fewer bytes. Write the encoding, the decoding with full error checks, and the zigzag mapping for signed numbers. No `main`.

A varint stores an unsigned 64-bit value 7 bits per byte, **lowest 7 bits first**. Every byte except the last has its top bit (0x80) set; the last byte has it clear. So 1 is `01`, 127 is `7F`, 128 is `80 01`, and 300 is `AC 02`.

- `int varint_encode(uint64_t v, unsigned char* out)`: write the shortest varint for `v` and return its length (1 to 10).
- `int varint_decode(const unsigned char* in, int n, uint64_t* out)`: read one varint from the `n` bytes at `in`, store its value, and return how many bytes it used. Return -1 if the `n` bytes end before a byte with the top bit clear. Return -2 if the value would not fit in 64 bits: a 10th byte greater than 1, or an 11th byte. On an error leave `*out` unchanged, and never read `in[n]` or beyond.
- `uint64_t zigzag(int64_t x)`: map signed to unsigned so small magnitudes stay small: 0, -1, 1, -2, 2, … become 0, 1, 2, 3, 4, …, that is 2x for x ≥ 0 and −2x − 1 for x < 0.
- `int64_t unzigzag(uint64_t u)`: the inverse.

All four must work for every value, `INT64_MIN` included, without undefined behaviour.
--- starter
#include <cstdint>

int varint_encode(uint64_t v, unsigned char* out) {
    out[0] = (unsigned char)v;
    return 1;
}

int varint_decode(const unsigned char* in, int n, uint64_t* out) {
    *out = in[0];
    return 1;
}

uint64_t zigzag(int64_t x) {
    return (uint64_t)(x * 2);
}

int64_t unzigzag(uint64_t u) {
    return (int64_t)(u / 2);
}
--- solution
#include <cstdint>

int varint_encode(uint64_t v, unsigned char* out) {
    int len = 0;
    while (v >= 0x80) {
        out[len++] = (unsigned char)((v & 0x7F) | 0x80);
        v >>= 7;
    }
    out[len++] = (unsigned char)v;
    return len;
}

int varint_decode(const unsigned char* in, int n, uint64_t* out) {
    uint64_t value = 0;
    for (int i = 0; i < 10; i++) {
        if (i >= n) return -1;
        unsigned char b = in[i];
        if (i == 9 && b > 1) return -2;          // only one more bit fits, and it must end here
        value |= (uint64_t)(b & 0x7F) << (7 * i);
        if ((b & 0x80) == 0) {
            *out = value;
            return i + 1;
        }
    }
    return -2;   // unreachable: a 10th byte of 0 or 1 always ends the varint
}

uint64_t zigzag(int64_t x) {
    uint64_t u = (uint64_t)x;              // two's complement bit pattern, defined
    return (u << 1) ^ (x < 0 ? ~(uint64_t)0 : 0);
}

int64_t unzigzag(uint64_t u) {
    uint64_t v = (u >> 1) ^ (0 - (u & 1));  // all ones when the low bit is 1
    return (int64_t)v;                      // defined since C++20: the same bit pattern
}
--- check test | The examples encode as stated
[] { unsigned char b[10]; bool a = varint_encode(1, b) == 1 && b[0] == 0x01; bool c = varint_encode(127, b) == 1 && b[0] == 0x7F; bool d = varint_encode(128, b) == 2 && b[0] == 0x80 && b[1] == 0x01; bool e = varint_encode(300, b) == 2 && b[0] == 0xAC && b[1] == 0x02; bool f = varint_encode(0, b) == 1 && b[0] == 0; return a && c && d && e && f; }()

--- check test | Truncated input gives -1 and keeps *out

--- check test | Too big for 64 bits gives -2

+++ problem | Where a key would go
--- task
Write a generic binary search over a sorted array of any type, like C++'s `std::lower_bound`, and use it to count a range. No `main`.

- `void* lower_bound_any(const void* key, const void* base, size_t n, size_t size, int (*cmp)(const void* key, const void* elem))`: the `n` elements of `size` bytes at `base` are sorted so that `cmp(key, element)` never goes from `<= 0` back to `> 0` as you move along them. Return the address of the **first** element for which `cmp(key, element) <= 0`, that is, the first element not less than the key, or `base + n * size` (one past the end) if there is none. It must make at most ⌈log₂(n + 1)⌉ calls to `cmp`; the checks count them.
- `size_t count_in_range(const void* lo, const void* hi, const void* base, size_t n, size_t size, int (*cmp)(const void*, const void*))`: the number of elements `e` with `lo ≤ e < hi` in the order of `cmp`, using two searches. If `hi` is not greater than `lo`, it is 0.

For example, in the ints `{1, 3, 3, 3, 8}`, the lower bound of 3 is the element at index 1, of 4 is index 4, and of 9 is one past the end; the count from 3 up to (not including) 8 is 3.
--- starter
#include <cstddef>

void* lower_bound_any(const void* key, const void* base, size_t n, size_t size, int (*cmp)(const void* key, const void* elem)) {
    const char* p = (const char*)base;
    for (size_t i = 0; i < n; i++)
        if (cmp(key, p + i * size) <= 0) return (void*)(p + i * size);
    return (void*)(p + n * size);
}

size_t count_in_range(const void* lo, const void* hi, const void* base, size_t n, size_t size, int (*cmp)(const void*, const void*)) {
    return 0;
}
--- solution
#include <cstddef>

void* lower_bound_any(const void* key, const void* base, size_t n, size_t size, int (*cmp)(const void* key, const void* elem)) {
    const char* first = (const char*)base;
    size_t count = n;   // the answer is in [first, first + count * size]
    while (count > 0) {
        size_t half = count / 2;
        const char* mid = first + half * size;
        if (cmp(key, mid) > 0) {          // mid is less than the key: the answer is after it
            first = mid + size;
            count -= half + 1;
        } else {                          // mid is not less: the answer is mid or before
            count = half;
        }
    }
    return (void*)first;
}

size_t count_in_range(const void* lo, const void* hi, const void* base, size_t n, size_t size, int (*cmp)(const void*, const void*)) {
    const char* a = (const char*)lower_bound_any(lo, base, n, size, cmp);
    const char* b = (const char*)lower_bound_any(hi, base, n, size, cmp);
    if (b <= a) return 0;
    return (size_t)(b - a) / size;
}

+++ problem | Fixed-point arithmetic without overflow
--- task
Small flight computers often have no floating-point hardware, so they compute with **fixed-point** numbers. In the Q16.16 format, an `int32_t` holding the integer v stands for the real number v / 65536: `65536` is 1.0, `98304` is 1.5, and `-32768` is −0.5. Write multiplication and division. No `main`.

- `bool q_mul(int32_t a, int32_t b, int32_t* out)`: the product of the two numbers they stand for, in Q16.16.
- `bool q_div(int32_t a, int32_t b, int32_t* out)`: the quotient a / b of the numbers they stand for, in Q16.16. Division by zero gives `false`.

The exact result is usually between two Q16.16 values: **round to the nearest**, and when it is exactly half way, **away from zero** (so 2.5 units becomes 3 and −2.5 becomes −3). If the rounded result does not fit in an `int32_t`, return `false`. On `false`, leave `*out` unchanged; on success store the result and return `true`. Use 64-bit integers for the intermediate values; there must be no undefined behaviour for any inputs.

For example, 1.5 × 2.0 is 3.0 (`q_mul(98304, 131072, &r)` gives 196608), and 1.0 / 3.0 is 21845, since 65536 / 3 = 21845.33….
--- starter
#include <cstdint>

bool q_mul(int32_t a, int32_t b, int32_t* out) {
    *out = (a * b) >> 16;
    return true;
}

bool q_div(int32_t a, int32_t b, int32_t* out) {
    *out = (a << 16) / b;
    return true;
}
--- solution
#include <cstdint>

// n / d rounded to nearest, halves away from zero; false if it does not fit in int32_t.
static bool round_div(int64_t n, int64_t d, int32_t* out) {
    int64_t q = n / d;                 // truncated towards zero
    int64_t r = n % d;                 // has the sign of n
    int64_t twice_r = r < 0 ? -2 * r : 2 * r;
    int64_t abs_d = d < 0 ? -d : d;
    if (twice_r >= abs_d) q += ((n < 0) != (d < 0)) ? -1 : 1;   // move away from zero
    if (q < INT32_MIN || q > INT32_MAX) return false;
    *out = (int32_t)q;
    return true;
}

bool q_mul(int32_t a, int32_t b, int32_t* out) {
    // |a * b| is at most 2^62, so the product fits in int64_t
    return round_div((int64_t)a * b, 65536, out);
}

bool q_div(int32_t a, int32_t b, int32_t* out) {
    if (b == 0) return false;
    return round_div((int64_t)a * 65536, b, out);
}

+++ problem | Laying out and relocating a program
--- task
Write the last two steps of a linker: laying the object files out in memory, and patching every reference with a real address. No `main`.

```cpp fragment
struct Sym {
    const char* name;
    int offset;          // where it is, from the start of its object's bytes
};

struct Reloc {
    int offset;          // where the 4-byte address goes, in this object's bytes
    const char* target;  // the symbol whose address goes there
};

struct Obj {
    const unsigned char* bytes;
    int size;
    int align;           // a power of two
    const Sym* syms;
    int nsyms;
    const Reloc* relocs;
    int nrelocs;
};
```

Include these definitions exactly as shown, with `<cstdint>`, and write `int link_image(const Obj* objs, int n, uint32_t base, unsigned char* image, int cap, const char** bad)`:

1. **Layout.** Object 0 starts at address `base`. Each later object starts at the end of the one before, rounded up to its own `align`. (`base` is a multiple of every alignment.) The image covers the addresses from `base` to the end of the last object; its size is that end minus `base`, and `image[i]` holds the byte at address `base + i`. Gaps between objects hold 0.
2. **Symbols.** A symbol's address is its object's start plus its offset.
3. **Relocation.** After copying every object's bytes into the image, for each relocation write the address of its target as 4 bytes, **little-endian**, at its object's start plus its offset.

Check for errors in this order: if any name is defined by two symbols (anywhere), return -2 and set `*bad` to it (the one whose second definition comes first, scanning objects and their symbols in order); if the image size is greater than `cap`, return -3; if any relocation's target is not defined, return -1 and set `*bad` to the first such target, in order. Otherwise return the image size. With `n` equal to 0, the image is empty: return 0.
--- starter
#include <cstdint>
#include <cstring>

struct Sym {
    const char* name;
    int offset;
};

struct Reloc {
    int offset;
    const char* target;
};

struct Obj {
    const unsigned char* bytes;
    int size;
    int align;
    const Sym* syms;
    int nsyms;
    const Reloc* relocs;
    int nrelocs;
};

int link_image(const Obj* objs, int n, uint32_t base, unsigned char* image, int cap, const char** bad) {
    int at = 0;
    for (int i = 0; i < n; i++) {
        memcpy(image + at, objs[i].bytes, objs[i].size);
        at += objs[i].size;
    }
    return at;
}
--- solution
#include <cstdint>
#include <cstring>

struct Sym {
    const char* name;
    int offset;
};

struct Reloc {
    int offset;
    const char* target;
};

struct Obj {
    const unsigned char* bytes;
    int size;
    int align;
    const Sym* syms;
    int nsyms;
    const Reloc* relocs;
    int nrelocs;
};

// The address of `name`, found by scanning every object; false if it is not defined.
static bool address_of(const Obj* objs, int n, const uint32_t* start, const char* name, uint32_t* addr) {
    for (int i = 0; i < n; i++)
        for (int j = 0; j < objs[i].nsyms; j++)
            if (strcmp(objs[i].syms[j].name, name) == 0) {
                *addr = start[i] + (uint32_t)objs[i].syms[j].offset;
                return true;
            }
    return false;
}

int link_image(const Obj* objs, int n, uint32_t base, unsigned char* image, int cap, const char** bad) {
    // duplicate definitions: a symbol whose name appeared earlier in the scan
    for (int i = 0; i < n; i++)
        for (int j = 0; j < objs[i].nsyms; j++)
            for (int a = 0; a <= i; a++)
                for (int b = 0; b < (a < i ? objs[a].nsyms : j); b++)
                    if (strcmp(objs[a].syms[b].name, objs[i].syms[j].name) == 0) {
                        *bad = objs[i].syms[j].name;
                        return -2;
                    }
    // layout
    static uint32_t start[1024];
    uint32_t end = base;
    for (int i = 0; i < n; i++) {
        uint32_t a = (uint32_t)objs[i].align;
        start[i] = (end + a - 1) & ~(a - 1);
        end = start[i] + (uint32_t)objs[i].size;
    }
    int size = (int)(end - base);
    if (size > cap) return -3;
    // copy, with zeros in the gaps
    memset(image, 0, size);
    for (int i = 0; i < n; i++) memcpy(image + (start[i] - base), objs[i].bytes, objs[i].size);
    // relocate
    for (int i = 0; i < n; i++)
        for (int k = 0; k < objs[i].nrelocs; k++) {
            uint32_t addr;
            if (!address_of(objs, n, start, objs[i].relocs[k].target, &addr)) {
                *bad = objs[i].relocs[k].target;
                return -1;
            }
            unsigned char* p = image + (start[i] - base) + objs[i].relocs[k].offset;
            for (int b = 0; b < 4; b++) p[b] = (unsigned char)(addr >> (8 * b));
        }
    return size;
}
--- check test | Two objects: main calls thrust, with alignment padding between them

--- check test | A symbol in the middle of an object, and a reference to data

+++ problem | Merging two sorted record files
--- task
Two ground stations each recorded telemetry to a file of 8-byte records, sorted by time. Merge them into one sorted file, reading each input only once, from start to end. No `main`.

Each record is 8 bytes: a 32-bit unsigned **key** (the time), little-endian, then a 32-bit signed **value**, little-endian. Within a valid input file the keys never decrease.

Write `long long merge_files(const char* a, const char* b, const char* out)`. It writes to `out` (replacing any old file) every record of both inputs, ordered by key, with records of equal key from `a` before those from `b`, and within one file in their original order. It returns the number of records written.

- Return -1 if any of the three files cannot be opened.
- Return -2 if an input's size is not a whole number of records, or if an input's keys decrease somewhere. (What `out` then holds does not matter.)

Close every file you open, on every path.
--- starter
#include <cstdint>
#include <cstdio>

long long merge_files(const char* a, const char* b, const char* out) {
    return -1;
}
--- solution
#include <cstdint>
#include <cstdio>

struct Input {
    FILE* f;
    unsigned char rec[8];
    uint32_t key;
    bool has;       // rec holds an unread record
    uint32_t last;  // the previous key, to check the order
    bool started;
    bool bad;
};

static uint32_t get32(const unsigned char* p) {
    return (uint32_t)p[0] | ((uint32_t)p[1] << 8) | ((uint32_t)p[2] << 16) | ((uint32_t)p[3] << 24);
}

// Loads the next record of `in`, checking that keys never decrease.
static void advance(Input* in) {
    in->has = fread(in->rec, 8, 1, in->f) == 1;
    if (!in->has) return;
    in->key = get32(in->rec);
    if (in->started && in->key < in->last) in->bad = true;
    in->last = in->key;
    in->started = true;
}

static bool whole_records(FILE* f) {
    if (fseek(f, 0, SEEK_END) != 0) return false;
    long size = ftell(f);
    if (fseek(f, 0, SEEK_SET) != 0) return false;
    return size >= 0 && size % 8 == 0;
}

long long merge_files(const char* a, const char* b, const char* out) {
    long long result = -1;
    Input x = {}, y = {};
    FILE* o = nullptr;
    x.f = fopen(a, "rb");
    y.f = fopen(b, "rb");
    o = fopen(out, "wb");
    if (x.f == nullptr || y.f == nullptr || o == nullptr) goto done;
    result = -2;
    if (!whole_records(x.f) || !whole_records(y.f)) goto done;
    advance(&x);
    advance(&y);
    result = 0;
    while (x.has || y.has) {
        if (x.bad || y.bad) {
            result = -2;
            goto done;
        }
        Input* take = (!y.has || (x.has && x.key <= y.key)) ? &x : &y;   // ties: a first
        if (fwrite(take->rec, 8, 1, o) != 1) {
            result = -1;
            goto done;
        }
        result++;
        advance(take);
    }
    if (x.bad || y.bad) result = -2;
done:
    if (x.f) fclose(x.f);
    if (y.f) fclose(y.f);
    if (o) fclose(o);
    return result;
}
--- check test | Two small files interleave, ties from a first
[] { auto put = [](FILE* f, uint32_t k, int32_t v) { unsigned char r[8]; for (int i = 0; i < 4; i++) { r[i] = (unsigned char)(k >> (8 * i)); r[4 + i] = (unsigned char)((uint32_t)v >> (8 * i)); } fwrite(r, 8, 1, f); }; FILE* f = fopen("sysgate_merge_a1.bin", "wb"); put(f, 1, 10); put(f, 5, 11); put(f, 9, 12); fclose(f); f = fopen("sysgate_merge_b1.bin", "wb"); put(f, 2, 20); put(f, 5, 21); put(f, 10, 22); put(f, 11, 23); fclose(f); long long n = merge_files("sysgate_merge_a1.bin", "sysgate_merge_b1.bin", "sysgate_merge_o1.bin"); FILE* g = fopen("sysgate_merge_o1.bin", "rb"); unsigned char buf[80]; size_t got = fread(buf, 8, 10, g); fclose(g); int want_v[7] = {10, 20, 11, 21, 12, 22, 23}; if (n != 7 || got != 7) return false; for (int i = 0; i < 7; i++) if (buf[8 * i + 4] != want_v[i]) return false; return true; }()
