+++ problem | Interning strings
--- task
Compilers and interpreters **intern** strings: they give every distinct string a small integer id, the first string seen getting 0, the next new one 1, and so on, so that later comparisons are integer comparisons. Write an interner. You define the struct `Interner` yourself. Use `malloc` and `free`. No `main`.

- `Interner* interner_create()`: an empty interner, or `nullptr` if memory runs out.
- `int intern(Interner* in, const char* s)`: the id of `s`: its existing id if it was interned before, otherwise the next unused id. Keep a copy of the string. Return -1 if memory runs out.
- `const char* name_of(const Interner* in, int id)`: the interner's copy of the string with that id, or `nullptr` if no string has it. The pointer must stay valid until the interner is destroyed, even while more strings are interned.
- `int interned_count(const Interner* in)`: how many distinct strings there are.
- `void interner_destroy(Interner* in)`: free everything.

Both `intern` and `name_of` must take O(1) expected time: the checks intern 100,000 distinct strings and look each one up again, which a linear scan cannot finish in time.
--- starter
#include <cstdint>
#include <cstdio>
#include <cstdlib>
#include <cstring>

struct Interner {
    int count;
};

Interner* interner_create() {
    return nullptr;
}

int intern(Interner* in, const char* s) {
    return -1;
}

const char* name_of(const Interner* in, int id) {
    return nullptr;
}

int interned_count(const Interner* in) {
    return 0;
}

void interner_destroy(Interner* in) {
}
--- solution
#include <cstdint>
#include <cstdio>
#include <cstdlib>
#include <cstring>

struct Interner {
    char** names;     // names[id]: the copy of each string, in id order
    int count;
    int names_cap;
    int* slots;       // open addressing: id + 1 of the string in each slot, 0 when empty
    int nslots;       // a power of two, kept at least twice count
};

static uint32_t fnv1a(const char* s) {
    uint32_t h = 2166136261u;
    for (; *s != '\0'; s++) {
        h ^= (unsigned char)*s;
        h *= 16777619u;
    }
    return h;
}

Interner* interner_create() {
    Interner* in = (Interner*)malloc(sizeof *in);
    if (in == nullptr) return nullptr;
    in->count = 0;
    in->names_cap = 16;
    in->nslots = 32;
    in->names = (char**)malloc(in->names_cap * sizeof *in->names);
    in->slots = (int*)calloc(in->nslots, sizeof *in->slots);
    if (in->names == nullptr || in->slots == nullptr) {
        free(in->names);
        free(in->slots);
        free(in);
        return nullptr;
    }
    return in;
}

// The slot where s is, or the empty slot where it would go.
static int find_slot(const Interner* in, const int* slots, int nslots, const char* s) {
    int i = (int)(fnv1a(s) & (uint32_t)(nslots - 1));
    while (slots[i] != 0 && strcmp(in->names[slots[i] - 1], s) != 0) i = (i + 1) & (nslots - 1);
    return i;
}

static bool grow_slots(Interner* in) {
    int bigger = in->nslots * 2;
    int* ns = (int*)calloc(bigger, sizeof *ns);
    if (ns == nullptr) return false;
    for (int id = 0; id < in->count; id++) ns[find_slot(in, ns, bigger, in->names[id])] = id + 1;
    free(in->slots);
    in->slots = ns;
    in->nslots = bigger;
    return true;
}

int intern(Interner* in, const char* s) {
    int i = find_slot(in, in->slots, in->nslots, s);
    if (in->slots[i] != 0) return in->slots[i] - 1;
    if (2 * (in->count + 1) > in->nslots) {
        if (!grow_slots(in)) return -1;
        i = find_slot(in, in->slots, in->nslots, s);
    }
    if (in->count == in->names_cap) {
        char** bigger = (char**)realloc(in->names, 2 * in->names_cap * sizeof *bigger);
        if (bigger == nullptr) return -1;
        in->names = bigger;
        in->names_cap *= 2;
    }
    size_t len = strlen(s);
    char* copy = (char*)malloc(len + 1);   // its own block, so it never moves
    if (copy == nullptr) return -1;
    memcpy(copy, s, len + 1);
    int id = in->count++;
    in->names[id] = copy;
    in->slots[i] = id + 1;
    return id;
}

const char* name_of(const Interner* in, int id) {
    if (id < 0 || id >= in->count) return nullptr;
    return in->names[id];
}

int interned_count(const Interner* in) {
    return in->count;
}

void interner_destroy(Interner* in) {
    if (in == nullptr) return;
    for (int id = 0; id < in->count; id++) free(in->names[id]);
    free(in->names);
    free(in->slots);
    free(in);
}
--- check test | Ids in first-seen order, and repeats get the same id
[] { Interner* in = interner_create(); if (!in) return false; int a = intern(in, "thrust"), b = intern(in, "pitch"), c = intern(in, "thrust"), d = intern(in, "yaw"); bool ok = a == 0 && b == 1 && c == 0 && d == 2 && interned_count(in) == 3; interner_destroy(in); return ok; }()
--- check test | name_of gives the interner's own copy
[] { Interner* in = interner_create(); char buf[16] = "roll"; int id = intern(in, buf); strcpy(buf, "XXXX"); const char* n = name_of(in, id); bool ok = n != nullptr && strcmp(n, "roll") == 0 && n != buf; interner_destroy(in); return ok; }()
--- check test | Unknown ids give nullptr
[] { Interner* in = interner_create(); intern(in, "a"); bool ok = name_of(in, 1) == nullptr && name_of(in, -1) == nullptr && name_of(in, 0) != nullptr; interner_destroy(in); Interner* e = interner_create(); bool ok2 = name_of(e, 0) == nullptr && interned_count(e) == 0; interner_destroy(e); return ok && ok2; }()
--- check test | The empty string is a string too
[] { Interner* in = interner_create(); int a = intern(in, ""), b = intern(in, "x"), c = intern(in, ""); bool ok = a == 0 && b == 1 && c == 0 && name_of(in, 0)[0] == '\0'; interner_destroy(in); return ok; }()
--- check test | 100000 distinct strings, each looked up again
[] { Interner* in = interner_create(); char k[24]; for (int i = 0; i < 100000; i++) { snprintf(k, sizeof k, "param.%d", i * 13); if (intern(in, k) != i) return false; } const char* first = name_of(in, 0); for (int i = 99999; i >= 0; i--) { snprintf(k, sizeof k, "param.%d", i * 13); if (intern(in, k) != i) return false; if (strcmp(name_of(in, i), k) != 0) return false; } bool ok = interned_count(in) == 100000 && strcmp(first, "param.0") == 0 && name_of(in, 0) == first; interner_destroy(in); return ok; }()

+++ problem | An intrusive list
--- task
The Linux kernel links its objects into lists without allocating list nodes: each object **contains** its links, and code gets from a link back to the object around it with pointer arithmetic. Build such a list. No `main`.

```cpp fragment
struct ListNode {
    ListNode* prev;
    ListNode* next;
};

struct Task {
    int id;
    int priority;
    ListNode link;     // the task's place in a list
    char name[16];
};
```

A list is a **circular** doubly linked list with a sentinel: a `ListNode` called the head, which belongs to no task. An empty list's head points to itself both ways. Include the definitions exactly as shown, and write:

- `void list_init(ListNode* head)`: an empty list.
- `void list_push_back(ListNode* head, ListNode* node)`: add `node` at the end.
- `void list_remove(ListNode* node)`: unlink `node` from its list, and make it point to itself both ways.
- `Task* task_of(ListNode* node)`: the `Task` that contains `node`, computed from the node's address and `offsetof(Task, link)`.
- `int total_priority(const ListNode* head)`: the sum of the priorities of the tasks in the list, found through `task_of`.
- `void list_sort(ListNode* head, int (*cmp)(const Task* a, const Task* b))`: reorder the list so that `cmp` never returns a positive number for a task and the one after it, **stably** (tasks that compare equal keep their order). Relink the nodes; never copy or move the tasks themselves.

Tasks live wherever the caller keeps them, in arrays, on the stack or on the heap: the list never allocates or frees anything.
--- starter
#include <cstddef>

struct ListNode {
    ListNode* prev;
    ListNode* next;
};

struct Task {
    int id;
    int priority;
    ListNode link;
    char name[16];
};

void list_init(ListNode* head) {
    head->prev = nullptr;
    head->next = nullptr;
}

void list_push_back(ListNode* head, ListNode* node) {
}

void list_remove(ListNode* node) {
}

Task* task_of(ListNode* node) {
    return (Task*)node;
}

int total_priority(const ListNode* head) {
    return 0;
}

void list_sort(ListNode* head, int (*cmp)(const Task* a, const Task* b)) {
}
--- solution
#include <cstddef>

struct ListNode {
    ListNode* prev;
    ListNode* next;
};

struct Task {
    int id;
    int priority;
    ListNode link;
    char name[16];
};

void list_init(ListNode* head) {
    head->prev = head;
    head->next = head;
}

// Links node in between a and b, which are neighbours.
static void link_between(ListNode* node, ListNode* a, ListNode* b) {
    node->prev = a;
    node->next = b;
    a->next = node;
    b->prev = node;
}

void list_push_back(ListNode* head, ListNode* node) {
    link_between(node, head->prev, head);
}

void list_remove(ListNode* node) {
    node->prev->next = node->next;
    node->next->prev = node->prev;
    node->prev = node;
    node->next = node;
}

Task* task_of(ListNode* node) {
    return (Task*)((char*)node - offsetof(Task, link));
}

int total_priority(const ListNode* head) {
    int total = 0;
    for (ListNode* n = head->next; n != head; n = n->next) total += task_of(n)->priority;
    return total;
}

void list_sort(ListNode* head, int (*cmp)(const Task* a, const Task* b)) {
    // insertion sort by relinking: take the nodes off one by one, in order, and insert
    // each after the last sorted node that is not greater than it, which keeps it stable
    ListNode sorted;
    list_init(&sorted);
    while (head->next != head) {
        ListNode* n = head->next;
        list_remove(n);
        ListNode* after = sorted.prev;
        while (after != &sorted && cmp(task_of(after), task_of(n)) > 0) after = after->prev;
        link_between(n, after, after->next);
    }
    if (sorted.next == &sorted) return;
    // move the sorted chain back under head
    head->next = sorted.next;
    head->prev = sorted.prev;
    sorted.next->prev = head;
    sorted.prev->next = head;
}
--- check test | Push, iterate through task_of, and total the priorities
[] { static Task t[3] = {{1, 5, {}, "gyro"}, {2, 7, {}, "star"}, {3, 2, {}, "sun"}}; ListNode head; list_init(&head); for (int i = 0; i < 3; i++) list_push_back(&head, &t[i].link); bool order = task_of(head.next) == &t[0] && task_of(head.next->next) == &t[1] && task_of(head.prev) == &t[2] && head.prev->next == &head; return order && total_priority(&head) == 14; }()
--- check test | task_of finds the task around its link
[] { Task a = {42, 1, {}, "x"}; Task* p = task_of(&a.link); return p == &a && p->id == 42; }()
--- check test | An empty list points to itself, and totals 0
[] { ListNode head; list_init(&head); return head.next == &head && head.prev == &head && total_priority(&head) == 0; }()
--- check test | Removing the first, middle and last

--- check test | Sorting by priority is stable and never moves the tasks
[] { static Task t[6] = {{1, 3, {}, ""}, {2, 1, {}, ""}, {3, 3, {}, ""}, {4, 2, {}, ""}, {5, 1, {}, ""}, {6, 3, {}, ""}}; ListNode head; list_init(&head); for (int i = 0; i < 6; i++) list_push_back(&head, &t[i].link); auto by_pri = [](const Task* a, const Task* b) -> int { return (a->priority > b->priority) - (a->priority < b->priority); }; list_sort(&head, by_pri); int want[6] = {2, 5, 4, 1, 3, 6}; int k = 0; for (ListNode* n = head.next; n != &head; n = n->next) { Task* p = task_of(n); if (k >= 6 || p->id != want[k] || p != &t[want[k] - 1]) return false; if (n->next->prev != n) return false; k++; } return k == 6 && head.prev == &t[5].link; }()
--- check test | Sorting an empty list and a list of one

--- check test | 2000 tasks on the heap, sorted by id descending
[] { Task* ts = (Task*)malloc(2000 * sizeof(Task)); ListNode head; list_init(&head); unsigned x = 1u; for (int i = 0; i < 2000; i++) { x = x * 1664525u + 1013904223u; ts[i].id = (int)(x >> 16); ts[i].priority = 1; list_push_back(&head, &ts[i].link); } auto desc = [](const Task* a, const Task* b) -> int { return (a->id < b->id) - (a->id > b->id); }; list_sort(&head, desc); int count = 0; int last = 1 << 30; bool ok = true; for (ListNode* n = head.next; n != &head; n = n->next) { int id = task_of(n)->id; if (id > last) ok = false; last = id; count++; } bool total = total_priority(&head) == 2000; free(ts); return ok && count == 2000 && total; }()

+++ question | The size of a struct
--- ask
On a machine where `int` has size and alignment 4 and `short` has size and alignment 2, what is `sizeof(S)`?

```cpp fragment
struct S {
    char tag;
    int count;
    short id;
};
```
--- answer
12
--- why
`tag` is at 0, `count` needs a multiple of 4 so it goes at 4, and `id` goes at 8 and ends at 10. The struct's alignment is 4, so the size is rounded up from 10 to 12. Declaring `count` first would give 8.

+++ question | Pointer arithmetic
--- ask
What does this print?

```cpp fragment
int a[5] = {10, 20, 30, 40, 50};
int* p = a + 1;
printf("%d %d\n", *(p + 2), (int)(&a[4] - p));
```
--- answer
40 3
--- why
`p` points at `a[1]`, so `p + 2` points at `a[3]`, which holds 40. Subtracting pointers counts elements, not bytes: from `a[1]` to `a[4]` is 3 elements.

+++ question | A byte that wraps
--- ask
What does this print?

```cpp fragment
unsigned char level = 200;
level += 100;
printf("%d\n", level);
```
--- answer
44
--- why
The sum is computed as an `int`, 300, and storing it in an `unsigned char` keeps it modulo 256: 300 − 256 = 44. Conversion to an unsigned type is always defined; nothing here is undefined behaviour.

+++ question | The copy that is one byte short
--- ask
What is wrong with this function?

```cpp fragment
char* duplicate(const char* s) {
    char* copy = (char*)malloc(strlen(s));
    if (copy == nullptr) return nullptr;
    strcpy(copy, s);
    return copy;
}
```
--- choice
`strcpy` cannot copy into memory from `malloc`.
--- choice correct
The block has room for the characters but not the terminating zero, so `strcpy` writes one byte past its end.
--- choice
It should check `strlen(s) == 0` first, since `malloc(0)` always fails.
--- choice
The caller cannot free the result, because it was made inside another function.
--- why
`strlen` does not count the terminating zero, but `strcpy` copies it. The block needs `strlen(s) + 1` bytes; as written it is a heap buffer overflow of one byte, which may corrupt the allocator's data and crash later, far from the bug.

+++ question | Returning a local array
--- ask
A function builds a label in `char buf[32]`, a local array, and returns `buf`. The caller prints the result right away and it usually looks correct. What is actually going on?
--- choice
Nothing is wrong: arrays are always returned by copying.
--- choice
The compiler moves `buf` to the heap because it is returned.
--- choice correct
`buf` stops existing when the function returns, so the caller holds a dangling pointer; the bytes are often still there until another call reuses that stack memory, which is why it seems to work.
--- choice
The caller must `free` the pointer when it has finished with it.
--- why
A local array lives in the function's stack frame, and the frame is gone after the return. Reading through the pointer is undefined behaviour. Pass a buffer in from the caller, or allocate on the heap and document that the caller frees it.

+++ question | Why realloc goes into a second variable
--- ask
Why do careful programmers write `double* bigger = (double*)realloc(data, n * sizeof *data); if (bigger) data = bigger;` rather than `data = (double*)realloc(data, n * sizeof *data);`?
--- choice
Because `realloc` always returns a new address, so the old pointer must be kept to free it.
--- choice correct
Because when `realloc` fails it returns null and leaves the old block alone; assigning straight to `data` would lose the only pointer to that block, leaking it and its contents.
--- choice
Because assigning the result of `realloc` to the same variable is undefined behaviour.
--- choice
Because the second form grows the block twice.
--- why
On failure `realloc` keeps the old block valid and unchanged, which is only useful if you still have its address. On success the old pointer must not be used again, which is why `data` is updated then.

+++ question | Freeing twice
--- ask
A program calls `free(p);` and later, by mistake, `free(p);` again, with no allocation in between. What does the C standard say happens?
--- choice
The second call does nothing, because the block is already free.
--- choice
The second call returns an error code the program can check.
--- choice correct
It is undefined behaviour: the allocator may corrupt its free lists, crash at once or much later, or hand the same block out twice.
--- choice
The program always stops with a "double free" message.
--- why
Only `free(nullptr)` is defined to do nothing. Freeing a block that is not currently allocated breaks the allocator's invariants; some allocators detect it and abort, but nothing guarantees that, and attackers exploit it. Setting `p = nullptr` after freeing makes a second `free` harmless.

+++ question | The check that disappeared
--- ask
A function reads `int n = cfg->count;` and then, two lines later, tests `if (cfg == nullptr) return -1;`. In the optimised build the test is gone. Why is the compiler allowed to remove it?
--- choice
Because tests for null are always removed in release builds.
--- choice correct
Because reading `cfg->count` through a null pointer would be undefined behaviour, so in every execution the standard cares about, `cfg` is not null at the test, which is therefore always false.
--- choice
Because `cfg` is a parameter, and parameters can never be null.
--- choice
Because the compiler checks the pointer itself when the function is called.
--- why
A compiler only has to be correct for executions without undefined behaviour. After a dereference, "the pointer is not null" is a fact it may use. Test pointers before using them.

+++ question | Declared but never defined
--- ask
`main.cpp` includes `radio.h`, which declares `int radio_send(const char* msg);`, and calls it. The program is built from `main.cpp` alone; `radio.cpp`, which defines the function, is forgotten. What happens?
--- choice
The compiler reports an error when it compiles `main.cpp`, because it cannot find the body of `radio_send`.
--- choice correct
`main.cpp` compiles, since the declaration is enough for the compiler; the linker then fails with an undefined reference to `radio_send`.
--- choice
The program builds, and the call does nothing when it runs.
--- choice
The preprocessor copies `radio.cpp` in automatically because the header has the same name.
--- why
The compiler sees one translation unit at a time and only needs a declaration to compile a call; it leaves an undefined symbol for the linker. Only the linker, looking at all the object files together, finds that no one defines it.

+++ question | A short read
--- ask
A program calls `size_t got = fread(rec, 16, 10, f);` and `got` is 3. What does that tell it?
--- choice
3 bytes were read.
--- choice correct
3 whole records of 16 bytes were read; then the end of the file came, or an error happened, which `feof(f)` and `ferror(f)` can tell apart.
--- choice
The file is corrupt and must be closed at once.
--- choice
`fread` failed completely, and nothing was stored in `rec`.
--- why
`fread` returns the number of whole items read, not bytes. A number smaller than asked for means it stopped early; a final partial record is not counted, and may mean a torn file.

+++ question | Rows or columns
--- ask
A loop reads one 8-byte field from each of 1,000,000 records, stored as an array of 64-byte structs. Memory is fetched in 64-byte cache lines. About how many bytes does the CPU fetch from memory, and how many if the field were kept in its own array instead?
--- choice
8 MB in both cases, since the loop reads 8 MB of data.
--- choice correct
About 64 MB for the array of structs, since every record's line is fetched, against about 8 MB for the separate array.
--- choice
About 64 MB in both cases, because every access fetches a whole line.
--- choice
About 8 MB for the array of structs and 64 MB for the separate array.
--- why
With a 64-byte stride, each field sits in a different cache line, so all 1,000,000 lines, 64 MB, are fetched to use 8 bytes of each. In a separate array the fields are packed 8 to a line, so 125,000 lines, 8 MB, are fetched, and every byte is used.

+++ question | A table where every key collides
--- ask
A hash table with separate chaining holds n keys, and a bad hash function sends every key to the same bucket. What does one lookup of a key that is not in the table cost?
--- choice
O(1), because the bucket is found in one step.
--- choice
O(log n), because the chain is kept sorted.
--- choice correct
O(n), because the lookup must compare the key with every node in that one chain.
--- choice
O(n²), because every key must be rehashed.
--- why
Finding the bucket is O(1), but the chain holds all n keys and an absent key must be compared with each of them. The O(1) expected cost of hash tables depends on the hash spreading keys evenly.
